<meta>This is the title page of a highly controversial 17th-century work. The text is primarily in Latin, with a Biblical quotation in Greek and a library stamp in Dutch. The publication location "Eleutheropolis" (Greek for "City of Liberty") is a known pseudonym for Amsterdam, often used by authors to bypass censorship for radical or heterodox works. This specific work is historically attributed to Lodewijk Meyer, a close associate of the philosopher Baruch Spinoza.</meta>

## PHILOSOPHY
# INTERPRETER OF THE HOLY SCRIPTURE;
### *A Paradoxical Exercise,*

->In which it is demonstratively shown that true Philosophy is the<-
->infallible rule for interpreting the Holy Scriptures, and<-
->opinions differing from this are<-
->examined and refuted.<-

->Test all things; hold fast to that which is good. <note>original Greek: πάντα δοκιμάζετε, τὸ καλὸν κατέχετε.</note><-

->*1 Thessalonians 5, verse 21.*<-

<insert>LIBRARY OF THE UNITED MENNONITE CONGREGATION IN AMSTERDAM. <note>original Dutch: "BIBLIOTHEEK DER VEREEN. DOOPSG. GEMEENTE TE AMSTERDAM."</note></insert>

->AT ELEUTHEROPOLIS, <note>Literally "City of Liberty," a pseudonym for Amsterdam often used for heterodox works to avoid censorship.</note><-

<summary>This is the title page of Lodewijk Meyer's radical 1666 treatise, which argues that reason and philosophy, rather than religious tradition, are the only reliable tools for interpreting the Bible. Published anonymously in Amsterdam under a pseudonym, it represents a landmark moment in the Early Enlightenment and the "Spinozist" movement.</summary>

# P R O L O G U E .

We have no doubt that as soon as theologians cast their eyes upon the title and name of this <term>little book</term> <note>Latin: *libelli*</note>, they will not be kindly or fairly disposed toward its author; indeed, for some, their bile will be stirred. For their minds have become so hardened and secure in their preconceived opinions—or those received from their teachers—that they think their views are entirely in harmony with the truth and should be held by everyone as if they were divine Oracles. Therefore, they take it very badly and are greatly indignant if anyone dares to think differently from them, to profess it openly, or to enter the arena to challenge their views.

But such is the happiness of the age we live in that we need care little for the anger and indignation of these men. If they are of such a weak mind as to allow themselves to be carried away, they can do nothing else than slander the author's name among the common folk and the unlearned rabble to make him hated, should his identity become known. To keep ourselves safe and sound from this trouble, we have thought it wise to step onto the stage <term>wearing a mask</term> <note>The author is explaining their choice to publish anonymously to avoid personal persecution.</note>.

And although by this means not every opportunity for slander is taken away from them—indeed, it gives them a handle to attack us with insults, calling us a <term>shunner of light</term> <note>Latin: *Lucifugæ*</note> or a <term>dweller in shadows</term> <note>Latin: *Tenebrionis*</note>, as if we were someone condemned by our own conscience who dared not reveal our name because we could expect no honor from this writing nor any praise from the pious. We preferred, however, to undergo this light danger, since they will strike only at the air like <term>blindfolded gladiators</term> <note>original: *Andabatarum*; these were ancient Roman gladiators who fought in helmets without eye-holes</note> and will hurl their weapons in vain at an unknown person, rather than expose our head to the certain strikes of their tongues by revealing our name. As for the honor and praise that might result from the publication of this <term>treatise</term> <note>Latin: *Tractatûs*</note>, we want them to know that we are so constituted that neither insults touch us, nor

<meta>catchword: glo-</meta>

# P R O L O G U E .

...[not so that we might] capture a little glory, since we have devoted our labor in this work solely to Truth. From this, we are more than sufficiently conscious that if our intention had been to pursue good fame and reputation, we would undoubtedly have obtained it—if not among those [critics] and the common folk who depend on their every nod, then at least among the wise, who care for the truth at heart, however paradoxical it may be (and whom alone we judge worthy of the name of the truly pious). But that we have set as our goal in this treatise not the pursuit of vain glory, nor the hunting for human approval, but the propagation of truth, the benefit of our neighbor, and the reconciliation of a divided and disagreeing Christianity, will be clearer than the noonday sun to any candid reader who does not intend to interpret our efforts perversely, both from the whole context of the work and from what we are about to say.

For after completing the course of academic studies—having been imbued with a knowledge of both the <term>Higher Faculties</term> <note>In the early modern university, the "Higher Faculties" were Theology, Law, and Medicine, which one studied after completing the "Lower Faculty" of Liberal Arts/Philosophy.</note>, as they are commonly called, and the remaining philosophical disciplines, such as would satisfy our own wishes and those of our friends—we returned home. There, in our leisure, we intended to ruminate anew and seriously examine on the <term>balance of Truth</term> <note>Latin: *Veritatis trutinam*. A common metaphor for objective, rational judgment.</note> all that we had drawn in during that whole period—both from the learned lectures of our distinguished teachers and from reading the most erudite books and inspecting the records of famous authors. It seemed best to turn our mind first to Theology, as it excels all other disciplines by many <term>parasangs</term> <note>A Persian unit of distance (approx. 3.5 miles), used here metaphorically to mean "by a vast distance."</note>, since it shows mortals the way of living well and blessedly and is able to lead them to the eternal salvation of souls, than which nothing is more excellent or desirable. 

While we were putting all our effort into this, and attentively weighing and considering the various sects of the Christian religion and their mutually clashing opinions, comparing and distinguishing them, we found it to be almost no different today than it was in ancient times with Philosophy, which was compared...

<meta>The catchword "ratum" at the bottom of the page suggests the next page begins with "comparatum" (compared) or "comparatur" (is compared).</meta>

<summary>The author clarifies that his goal is the reconciliation of Christian sects rather than personal fame, and describes his transition from general academic studies to the specific pursuit of Theology.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on his academic journey. Having returned home to examine his studies, he begins to categorize the conflicting schools of thought he encountered, comparing the divisions in modern theology to the ancient divisions in philosophy.</meta>

-> # P R O L O G U E . <-

<sig>* 3</sig>

...seemed established. For just as all the philosophers who once existed can be most conveniently divided into two classes—the first being the **Dogmatists**, who claimed to teach and hand down to their disciples a certain and indubitable knowledge of things drawn from true principles; and the second being the **Skeptics**, who, on the contrary, calling everything into doubt, pronounced that nothing could be known for certain—so also in this century, two similar kinds of **Theologians** are found, where one corresponds to the former group and the other to the latter. 

For the greatest part of them boldly proclaim that they possess a true and evident understanding of theological matters drawn from the **Holy Scriptures**, and they preach that they are teaching this to others; whereas others, on the contrary, ingenuously confess that if not all, then at least as many as possible of their views concerning the Christian religion are only *probably* sought and deduced from the Scriptures. Therefore, they submit these views, as if they were uncertain and doubtful, to the better and healthier judgment of others, speaking, as they say, *under correction* <note>original: "sub correctione" — a humble scholarly formula indicating the author is open to being corrected by higher authorities.</note>, as if proposing them to be amended by those others.

And just as in ancient times the whole class of **Dogmatic** philosophers did not unite into one body, but split into diverse, disagreeing, and clashing opinions and sects—such as the **Pythagoreans**, **Platonists**, **Epicureans**, **Stoics**, **Peripatetics** <note>These represent the major schools of Greek philosophy: followers of Pythagoras (mathematics/mysticism), Plato (ideal forms), Epicurus (atomism/pleasure), the Porch/Zeno (duty/logic), and Aristotle (observation/logic).</note>, etc.—similarly in our own time, the Dogmatic theologians differ much among themselves regarding the main points of the Christian religion. They have forged, and even now cherish, various and contrary opinions, and this with such heat of spirit and fervor that the Christian world is, as it were, torn and distracted into different factions and diverse Churches. Its inhabitants are not only discordant in spirit and customs, but some have become mortal enemies—such as there are in Europe (to say nothing of those in Asia): **Catholics** <note>original: "Pontificii" — literally "those following the Pontiff/Pope."</note>, **Reformed**, **Lutherans**, **Anabaptists**, **Socinians** <note>A group that rejected the Trinity and emphasized rational interpretation of the Bible.</note>, **Arminians** <note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius who argued against strict predestination in favor of free will.</note>, and others of that kind. 

And finally, just as those ancient philosophers did, so also do these theologians; although any given sect of either side boasts and commends itself above the others, and [claims to rest] upon unshakable truth...

<meta>catchword: nixam (resting/supported)</meta>

<summary>The author compares the fractured state of modern Christianity to ancient philosophical schools, distinguishing between "Dogmatists" who claim absolute certainty and "Skeptics" who admit to only probable knowledge. He lists various contemporary sects to illustrate how these certainties lead to bitter divisions and social discord.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of contemporary scholarship. Having previously divided thinkers into Dogmatists and Skeptics, he now focuses on the failure of Dogmatic theologians to provide certain proof for their claims, setting the stage for his adoption of the Cartesian method.</meta>

-> # P R O L O G U E . <-

...glory and promise that they possess a well-founded knowledge and teach it; if not all, they nevertheless profess as many things as possible, and thrust upon their followers dogmas which either are not built upon the firm and stable foundation of Truth, or, if perhaps they are, they are by no means able to demonstrate or certainly show them to be so.

That this is true of **Theologians** (to say nothing of **Philosophers**, since they do not pertain so much to the plan of our undertaking) will be clear to anyone who wishes to look more deeply with us into their **Method** and way of demonstrating. For when some controversy arises among them concerning the establishment of a dogma of **Faith** and **Morals**, they immediately—as befits men of such a profession—betake themselves and flee to the **Holy Scriptures**, the infallible Word of God, as if to a "sacred anchor" <note>original: "sacram anchoram" — a common classical and biblical metaphor (Hebrews 6:19) for a final refuge or ultimate security.</note>. From there they summon and pile up as many passages as possible to support their opinion, and they busy themselves showing that these passages mean what they contend; but almost always with such trivial little reasons and petty interpretations <note>original: "ratiunculis interpretatiunculisque" — the author uses these diminutive Latin forms to mock the perceived weakness and insignificance of his opponents' arguments.</note> that an adversary is soon able to refute them with the greatest of ease, either by adapting the cited passages to strengthen his own opinion or by twisting them into another sense. Then another person, equipped with the same weapons, later evades and turns aside the force and strength of that argument with equal facility: so much so that everyone fights, but no one conquers or carries off a victory over the Enemy.

When I observed that **Theology**, the queen of the sciences <note>original: "disciplinarum principe" — literally "the leader of disciplines," reflecting the traditional view of theology as the highest field of academic study.</note>, was in such a wretched state, and that its Doctors, practitioners, and students were reduced to such straits—and that I myself, along with them, was immersed in such waves of uncertainty and doubt—I began to turn over in my mind whether the source of these evils could be uncovered, and whether some hope of emerging from them might shine forth, or a way of escape might be found anywhere. While I was fixing all my thought on investigating these things, there suddenly occurred to me that most successful and excellent **Method** by which that most noble and incomparable man, **René Descartes**,

> *Was the first to rescue the Truth, inaccessible for so many centuries,*
> *From the foul shadows of long-lasting darkness,*
<note>original: "Primus inaccessum, qui per tot secula Verum / Eruit è tetris longæ caliginis umbris." This poetic tribute credits Descartes with breaking through the intellectual obscurity of the Middle Ages.</note>

<meta>catchword: Philo-</meta>

<summary>The author laments the state of theology, arguing that traditional methods of biblical interpretation lead to endless, inconclusive disputes. He introduces René Descartes as the figure who provided a new method for discovering certain truth.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the author's critique of traditional dogmatism to his proposal for a new method in theology. # P R O L O G U E .

He <note>The author is referring to René Descartes (1596–1650), whose "Method" of systematic doubt revolutionized 17th-century thought.</note> restored **Philosophy** from its very foundations, cleared it of the so many and such great gaps with which it swarmed, and restored it to its own native splendor; namely, through a serious renunciation of all **Prejudices**, and the assent to and assertion of nothing unless it is clearly and distinctly perceived. I have often and diligently considered within myself whether, just as it was permitted to him in **Philosophy**, so it might be allowed and beneficial for me in **Theology** to call into doubt whatever can be called into doubt, and to reject it continually as if it were false, until at last I might arrive at something firm and stable in **Theology**, where I could safely plant my foot. But while I pause here for a little while, and [turn] my mind—lest I should be like the "struck fisherman" who only learns wisdom after the event <note>original: "piscator ictus saperem" — an ancient proverb suggesting it is better to be cautious than to learn through painful experience.</note>—

-> *I pull my mind into various parts, and turn it through all things,* <-
<note>original: "In partes rapio varias, perque omnia verso" — a quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, describing a mind in deep, restless deliberation.</note>

after a long and sufficiently serious deliberation, I discovered that no danger, no loss, and no inconvenience threatened me from such doubting. Indeed, when I called the **Theologians** themselves into counsel, I found that some of them of no low rank <note>i.e., highly respected authorities.</note> determine—and clearly persuade beginners—that this is most conducive to the right understanding and learning of **Holy Scripture**, and thus of the **Theology** drawn from it. For this is what **Zanchius** <note>Girolamo Zanchius (1516–1590), a prominent Italian Protestant theologian.</note> teaches in his *Tract on Holy Scripture (found in Volume 8 of his Works), question 12, chapter 1, rule 3,* and **Scarpius** <note>Johannes Scarpius, a 17th-century theologian known for his "Cursus Theologicus."</note> in his *Course on Theology, section on Holy Scripture, Controversy 8, Rule 3*:

> *That we should approach the study of those (namely, the Scriptures) without any prejudice, whether conceived in our own heads or from the teachings of others; but with a candid and pure mind, eager to learn. For prejudice is the greatest hindrance to progress.*

Therefore **Christ** commanded his followers to beware of the *leaven of the Pharisees*. This is also what **Polanus** <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610), a late Reformation theologian who systematized Reformed doctrine.</note> has in view when, in his *Syntagma of Theology, book 1, chapter 45, question 6,* he lists "Unteachableness due to prejudices, with which many are imbued" in the fifth place among the hindrances to finding the true sense of **Holy Scripture**. And **Ravanellus** <note>Petrus Ravanellus (1585–1660), author of a massive biblical encyclopedia, the *Bibliotheca Sacra*.</note> in his *Sacred Library, Title: On Scripture,* requires a mind [free] from perverse...

<summary>The author proposes applying René Descartes’ method of systematic doubt to Theology, arguing that setting aside all prejudices is essential for finding divine truth. He supports this radical approach by citing established Protestant authorities like Zanchius and Polanus, who warned that preconceived notions hinder the understanding of Scripture.</summary>

# P R O L O G U E .

...to possess a well-founded knowledge, and boasts and promises to teach it; if they do not profess all things, they certainly profess as many as possible, and thrust upon their followers dogmas which either are not built upon the firm and stable foundation of **Truth**, or if by chance they are, they are least able to demonstrate and show them to be such with certainty.

That this is true of **Theologians** (to say nothing of **Philosophers**, since they do not pertain so much to the scope of our project) will be clear to anyone who wishes to look more deeply with us into their **Method** and way of demonstrating. For when any controversy arises among them about establishing a dogma of **Faith** and **Morals**, immediately—as befits men of such a profession—they betake themselves and flee to **Holy Scripture**, the infallible **Word of God**, as if to a **sacred anchor** <note>original: "sacram anchoram" — a common 17th-century metaphor for a final, unbreakable authority or refuge.</note>. From it, they summon and accumulate as many passages as possible to build up their opinion, and they busy themselves showing that these passages mean what they contend. Yet, they do this almost always with such **trivial reasons** and **petty interpretations** <note>The author uses the Latin diminutives "ratiunculis interpretatiunculisque" to mock the weakness of these arguments.</note> that an adversary can soon refute them with the greatest ease, either by adapting the cited passages to confirm his own opinion or by twisting them into another sense. The force and power of these arguments are, in turn, eluded and turned aside with the same ease by another person equipped with those same weapons. The result is that everyone fights, but no one conquers or carries off the victory over the Enemy.

Therefore, when I noticed that **Theology**—the queen of the sciences <note>original: "disciplinarum principe"</note>—was in such a wretched state, and that its Doctors, practitioners, and students were reduced to such straits, and that I, along with them, was immersed in such waves of uncertainties and doubts, I began to revolve in my mind whether the source of these evils could be uncovered, and whether a hope of emerging from them might shine forth, and a way of escape might appear anywhere. While I fixed all my thought on investigating these things, there occurred to me that most happy and excellent **Method** by which the most noble and incomparable man **René Descartes**,

> Who first rescued the Truth, inaccessible for so many centuries,
> From the foul shadows of long darkness,

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the lack of a stable foundation in contemporary theology. The author now proposes a new method inspired by the restoration of philosophy, moving from a critique of others toward his own proposed solution.</meta>

# P R O L O G U E .

He restored **Philosophy** from its very foundations, purged it of the many and great gaps with which it overflowed, and restored it to its own native brilliance; namely, the serious rejection of all **Prejudices** <note>The author is referring here to René Descartes and his method of systematic doubt, which revolutionized 17th-century thought.</note>, and the assent to and assertion of nothing unless it is clearly and distinctly perceived. For a long time and with great diligence, I considered within myself whether, just as he did in **Philosophy**, it might be permissible and beneficial for me in **Theology** to call into doubt whatever could be called into doubt, and to immediately reject it as if it were false, until I finally arrived at something firm and stable in **Theology** where I could safely set my foot. While I paused here for a little while, and turned the matter over in my mind—lest I should become "wise only after being struck" <note>original: "piscator ictus saperem" — a Latin proverb referring to a fisherman who only learns to be careful after being bitten or stung; it means learning a lesson the hard way.</note>—

->I am pulled in various directions and turn through all things,<-

after a sufficiently long and serious deliberation, I discovered that no danger, no loss or inconvenience threatened me from such doubt. Indeed, when I called the **Theologians** themselves into counsel, I found that some of them of no low rank <note>meaning "of high status" or "highly respected."</note> determined, and clearly persuaded beginners, that this method is most conducive to rightly understanding and learning **Holy Scripture**, and thus the **Theology** drawn from it. For thus **Zanchius** <note>Girolamo Zanchi (1516–1590), a prominent Italian Protestant Reformation clergyman and educator.</note> in his *Tractate on Holy Scripture (which is in Volume 8 of his Works) question 12, chapter 1, rule 3* and **Scarpius** in *Course of Theology, section on Holy Scripture, Controversy 8, Rule 3* prescribe: *That we should approach the study of those (namely, the Scriptures) without any prejudice, whether conceived in our own head or from the doctrines of others; but with a candid, pure mind, eager for learning. For prejudice is the greatest obstacle to progress.* Therefore *Christ ordered his followers to beware the leaven of the Pharisees.* **Polanus** also looks to this when, in *Syntagma of Theology, book 1, chapter 45, question 6, among the obstacles to finding the true sense of Holy Scripture, he lists in fifth place "Unteachableness due to prejudices, with which very many are imbued."* And **Ravanellus** in *Sacred Library, Title: "On Scripture"*, when among the *means necessary for digging out and attaining the true and legitimate sense of Scripture, and thus for interpreting Scripture*, he requires *a mind [free] from wicked affecti-* <note>The text cuts off here due to the page break; the catchword "affecti-" likely completes to "affections" or "passions."</note>

<summary>The author contemplates applying Cartesian systematic doubt to theology to find a firm foundation for faith, citing respected Protestant theologians to argue that stripping away prejudices is essential for interpreting Holy Scripture.</summary>
<keywords>methodology, Cartesian doubt, René Descartes, prejudice, Girolamo Zanchi, Amandus Polanus, biblical interpretation, systematic theology</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument for applying a Cartesian method of doubt to Theology. Having introduced the idea on the previous page, he now justifies this approach by citing major theological authorities who warned against bringing personal prejudices to the study of the Bible.</meta>

...void of emotions and preconceived opinions. Nor does the **Confession of the Remonstrants** <note>The Remonstrants were a Dutch theological movement based on the ideas of Jacobus Arminius; they often emphasized a more rational and less dogmatic approach to scripture.</note> disagree, when it says in Chapter 1, Section 14: *“Not only learned Readers, but even common people <note>original: "idiotas" — in the 17th century, this referred to laypeople or those without formal academic training rather than its modern insulting meaning.</note> are able to grasp the meaning of those things which are necessary for eternal salvation, provided they do not allow themselves to be blinded by prejudice, vain confidence, and other perverse emotions.”*

Most gravely of all, however, **Zwingli** <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531) was a primary leader of the Reformation in Switzerland.</note> speaks in this manner in his booklet *On the Certainty and Clarity of the Word of God* (which is found in the first volume of his Works), Chapter 3: *“You will confess that you have frequently read through the Scriptures for this purpose: to pick out certain words that would support an opinion already conceived beforehand, with which you might confirm your own view, which you desire to hold onto tooth and nail <note>original: "mordicùs" — literally "with a bite," meaning to hold on tenaciously.</note>. This, this is the ulcer of all human Traditions, which it pains one to have touched with a needle <note>A Latin idiom meaning to hit upon the exact point of a problem or a sensitive matter.</note>. For what else is this but wishing to prove one’s own sense by the testimony of Scripture, to force a foreign meaning into the Scriptures, and to seek the protection and patronage of the Scriptures for one’s own error; so that you may drag passages of Scripture—even if they resist and are unwilling—into your own opinion, and force them to say that which you have already persuaded yourself of? For our prejudices are already broad, which we carry in our hand, just as if someone asks for bread from his neighbor with an armed hand; if he does not give it, he feels that violence is being done to him.”* To which he adds an example, and afterwards concludes: From which it can now be gathered *“how little it is fitting to approach the Scriptures with a violent prejudice of our mind.”*

And to this also seems to belong that warning of **Hilary** <note>St. Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent early Christian theologian.</note>, so often repeated by **Theologians**: *“Meaning is not to be brought into the Scriptures, but sought from the Scriptures.”*

Moved and supported therefore by the authority of such great men, and by the truth of the matter itself, I wished to set my hand to the work, and, following in the footsteps of **Descartes**, to try whether—by acting as a **Theologian**, and by removing whatever can be removed as doubtful and uncertain in **Theology**—one could finally arrive at something within it that cannot be removed without the whole thing collapsing and being removed at once, and which would be the principle and foundation of all other **Theological** dogmas.

While I applied myself to this business, I did not for long or much [scrutinize]...

<meta>catchword: tatus</meta>

<summary>The author justifies his use of systematic doubt in theology by quoting the Remonstrants, Zwingli, and Hilary of Poitiers, all of whom warn against interpreting Scripture through the lens of preconceived prejudice. He proposes to find an unshakable foundation for faith by stripping away everything uncertain, mirroring René Descartes' philosophical method.</summary>
<keywords>René Descartes, Huldrych Zwingli, Hilary of Poitiers, Remonstrants, Systematic Doubt, Biblical Interpretation, Theological Foundations, Prejudices</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's reflection on his search for a firm starting point in theological study. He transitions from the previous discussion on avoiding prejudice to identifying the primary axiom of the field.</meta>

->### PROLOGUE <-

**

...I had meditated, but that there soon presented itself the first of all Propositions <term>Enunciatorum: in logic and theology, a basic statement or axiom</term> in Theology, namely: *that the Books of the Old and New Testament are the infallible Word of God, the Best and Greatest.* <note>original: "Dei Opt. Max." (Deo Optimo Maximo), a title meaning "To God, the Best and Greatest," originally used for Jupiter but adopted by Christian scholars.</note> For if anyone should wish even to remove and reject this, he would already leap outside the limits of Theology, and would have to be restrained and convinced not by theological reasons—that is, authorities sought from Scripture—but by historical arguments and those brought from elsewhere; and upon him would fit that Aristotelian saying, *One must not argue against someone who denies first principles.* And just as the whole of Jurisprudence <term>Jurisprudence: the study and theory of law</term> rests upon the Body of Law <note>original: "Corpori juris" — referring to the *Corpus Juris Civilis*, the fundamental collection of Roman law which served as the basis for legal systems in Europe.</note>, and a Jurist blushes to speak without the Law: so also all Theology is built upon the Word of God, and nothing must be brought forward or admitted by a Theologian unless it is deduced from there.

This foundation of all Theology having therefore been found and laid, it pleased me to proceed further, and to investigate what could and should be built upon it next. With no great or particularly difficult mental effort, I noticed and discovered that this next step is the infallible Interpretation of Holy Scripture. For because Theologians have lacked this, it has stood as the sole fountain and origin from which such a great discrepancy of opinions has gushed forth among the Dogmatists <note>Dogmatists: scholars who focus on established church doctrines or "dogmas."</note>, and why no one has been able to demonstrate certainly and infallibly that his own opinion is true above the others. That this is the only remedy for these evils, and the unique hinge upon which the whole of Theology turns—immediately after the foundation has been established—is clear from this: that all its dogmas, both concerning faith and morals, must be both sought and proved from Scripture; yet they can neither be sought nor proved from there unless its meaning is certainly known and understood. Whence also in their disputes of all kinds, of all ages, and of all regions, Christian Theologians have always uniquely applied themselves to this, and have strained every nerve of their intellect and their learning, so that they might persuade and show that Scripture intends to signify what they themselves say, and not what their adversaries say. Once I perceived these things, immediately

# PROLOGUE

...I applied my mind to the investigation of a **Method** by which the true sense of **Holy Scripture** might be drawn out, and demonstrated to have been drawn out; and by which the false interpretations of others might be detected and shown to be such. And indeed, I sought to do all this certainly and infallibly; for it was my intention to build the **Palace of Most Holy Theology** not upon sand, but upon rock, so that when the storm-cloud descends, the rivers come, and the winds blow and rush upon our building, it should not collapse, fall, or totter, but remain unshaken and stable, fearlessly awaiting and despising all the injuries of the weather and the attacks and insults of enemies.

I therefore consulted **Theologians** of every kind, from all places and centuries; I weighed what they established regarding the Method of interpreting Holy Scripture and investigated what norm of exposition they themselves applied. But I could find nothing that fully satisfied my mind. Wherefore, leaving all those behind, it pleased me to make a trial of what I could do by my own efforts <note>original: "propriô marte" — literally "by my own Mars," a classical idiom meaning by one's own strength or skill without outside help</note>, and how far my own powers might reach. At last, after an arduous and laborious inquiry, I happened upon this [method], in which I fully rest, which I have no doubt is certain and infallible, and which I now set before the public examination of theologians to see and await what judgment they will pass upon it.

I am therefore an advocate to all and each of my readers, that they should accurately and diligently inspect and scrutinize everything which we are about to propose to the theological world in this little treatise, subjecting it to the most rigid examination, and weighing it against the **balance of Truth** <term>trutina: a scale or balance used to determine weight; here used metaphorically for a standard of judgment</term>. I ask that they acknowledge and profess it with me if I have succeeded, but if otherwise, that they do not err with me; for it is no

> *solace to the wretched to have had companions in misfortune,* <note>original: "Solamen miſero ſocios habuiſſe malorum" — a medieval proverb. The author is saying that if he is wrong, he does not want the "comfort" of knowing others followed him into error; he prefers to be corrected.</note>

since my only wish is that, should I err, the right way be shown to me by those who do not. Hence, I proceed not with the elegance of voices, nor the ornate composition of words, nor the flowery rhetoric and embellishments of orators

...pect <note>The text continues from the previous page's final word, "inspect."</note> [these matters], I did not wish to entice you, Reader, to my opinion, but rather to place the whole matter nakedly and simply before your eyes, and to demonstrate it with apodictic <term>apodictic: a logical term for a demonstration that is absolutely certain, leaving no room for doubt or denial</term> reasons. My intention is not to move your emotions, but to teach. For this reason, I have not sought to soothe you at the threshold of this preface with any flattery or sweet-sounding names, as is the custom of many writers, nor have I studied to win your favor. For it is all the same to me whether you are fair-minded or hostile <note>original: "candidus, an ater" — literally "white or black," a classical metaphor for a friendly or unfriendly witness</note>, since I desire you to be most hostile—indeed, most sharp.

Indeed, this writing goes out into the public for my sake as much as for yours. For if you recognize the Truth demonstrated here, that will turn entirely to your benefit; but if you reject it, that will be to your loss; however, if you should discover and show that I have slipped into error, that will be a gain for me, and I will rejoice that you were so "black" <note>meaning a harsh or unforgiving critic</note>. For Truth alone is the one and only thing I aim at here: If you do not seek this, do not proceed; but if you do seek it, read boldly, read through, read again, air it out, and examine it, and finally:

> Live, be well, and if you know anything better than these principles,
> Fairly share it; if not, join me in using these. <note>original: "Vive, vale, &, siquid novisti rectius istis, Candidus imperti; si non, his utere mecum." — A famous closing from the Roman poet Horace (*Epistles* 1.6.67–68).</note>

# PHILOSOPHY,

# THE INTERPRETER OF HOLY SCRIPTURE.

## A PARADOXICAL DISQUISITION.

### CHAPTER I.

->1. *The importance of the question.* 2. *The main points of the disquisition.*<-
->*Headings.*<-

<margin>I.</margin> Ever since the very beginnings of the Reformation, a grave and weighty question has flourished between the Catholics <note>original: "Pontificios," referring to those loyal to the Pope</note> and the Evangelicals <note>original: "Evangelicos," the term used for the emerging Protestant churches</note> concerning the right and the infallible rule for interpreting the most holy Scriptures. And this is certainly not without cause. For since the books of the New and Old Testament are recognized and held as the starting point <note>original: "principio"</note> from which all <term>dogmas</term> <note>established religious doctrines or beliefs</note> of both Faith and Morals must be sought by all who have taken the name of Christ as the Word of God and the unique source (excepting only the Catholics, who add on Traditions and the decrees of the Church); no one who has even a passing acquaintance with <term>Theology</term> <note>literally: "one who has saluted Theology from the threshold"</note> will deny that whatever difficulties may subsequently emerge concerning these matters tend toward and converge upon this single point of Interpretation, and that the hinge of all disagreements and debates among Christians turns on this alone. And from this it follows, clearer than the midday sun, that to him alone who is able to indicate the true and genuine sense of the divine Writings and demonstrate it certainly and undoubtedly, it is granted to establish something firm and immovable regarding Faith and Morals, and to refute and convince those who think otherwise. Whereas, on the contrary, those to whom this prerogative is denied—the rest—build upon sand and merely pour out words in vain; or if perhaps they persuade this or that person who is not at all stubborn and far too gullible, it is supported by such weak and failing foundations that it can be cast down and destroyed by even the slightest effort of those supporting a different view. But because, being destitute of this gift of interpretation, they [have taken it upon] themselves to explain the sacred books...

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>This opening page introduces the central thesis of the work: that the most critical issue in post-Reformation Christianity is determining the infallible rule for interpreting Scripture, as all theological certainty depends on this interpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Holy Scripture, Reformation, Catholics, Evangelicals, Dogmas, Interpretation, theological authority</keywords>

# O F   T H E   D I S C O U R S E

...the **Theologians** <note>original: "Theologi"</note> have taken into their hands, it has stood as the root and the fountain from which so many differing opinions have sprouted among Christians, so many sects have gushed forth with opposing fronts, and so many schisms have flowed. These have continually infested the Christian world since its foundations were laid, and they infest it even more every day. As a result, its inhabitants are wretchedly forced to misuse against one another's breasts those weapons that should have been hurled at the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicos"</note>, Jews, and Muslims <note>original: "Mahumetanos"</note>—those common enemies of the **heavenly doctrine** <note>original: "caelestis doctrinae"</note>—to the great detriment of the truth. It seems to me that the divided members of a torn **Christianity** <note>original: "Christianismi"</note> cannot be reunited, nor can so many minds be brought back to the same consensus by any better remedy than that very same one by which the disease could have been prevented and guarded against: namely, by a **standard** <note>original: "Normâ"</note> and a certain, unerring rule. By this rule—as if by a **Lydian stone** <term>touchstone</term> <note>original: "Lydium lapidem"; a stone once used to test the purity of gold, serving here as a metaphor for an infallible standard of truth</note>—the truth of every **Interpretation** <note>original: "Interpretationis"</note> should be measured, explored, and judged. Therefore, the work of those who have strained every nerve of their intellect to find it and have consecrated a part of their studies to it is not at all to be despised, much less attacked; rather, it is to be promoted and extolled with the highest praise.

For this reason, even though I am a new and untrained **athlete** <note>original: "rudis athleta"</note> in this **arena** <term>palaestra</term> <note>originally a Greek wrestling school; here referring to the field of theological debate</note>, I will not be ashamed to have spent some time and labor in this investigation. I will not fear to explain to the world in this present theological **Little Treatise** <note>original: "Tractatulô"</note> what I have accomplished and achieved in this matter. Indeed, it was not a lust for quarreling or contradicting, nor a greed for profit, nor a desire for glory that drove me; rather, just as the pure love of Truth impelled me to investigate, so it has moved me to write. I hope and trust that all those who deign to examine this matter diligently with an attentive mind—free from the clouds of prejudice—and who read through these few pages, will be moved by that same love.

2. Now, so that they may proceed through these pages with a surer step and greater profit, I wish for three main heads to be considered and attended to, by which I shall complete the whole business. The first will explain certain preliminaries concerning the **subject and predicate of the Controversy**, and from those, my own opinion, **demonstratively** <note>original: "ἀποδεικτικῶς"; a Greek term for a logical proof that is certain and necessary</note> proven. The second will provide a **defense** <note>original: "vindicias"</note> of our demonstration against the objections that could be made against it. The last will set before one's eyes a refutation of the **Opinions** that differ from ours, and a solution to the arguments by which those opinions are usually established.

### CHAPTER II.

<margin>ii</margin>

<meta>This page transitions from the general necessity of a "standard" for interpretation (the Lydian stone mentioned on page 2) to a specific linguistic analysis of the word "Interpreter" itself. The author begins Chapter II by defining his terms through classical authorities.</meta>

### ->PARADOXES CHAPTER II.<-

1. *The Multiple Meanings of Interpreter.* 2. *What it signifies to us.* 3. *Its material object.* 4. *What a word is.*
5. *What a discourse is.* 6. *The division of both.*
7. *The formal object of an interpreter.*

<margin>1.</margin>
The word **Interpreter**—to begin with the term itself—from which the words *to interpret*, *interpretation*, and *explanation* <note>original: "interpretamentum"</note> derive their origin, is used with multiple meanings among various Authors. The more common meanings are as follows: first, it denotes one who reconciles and brings together disagreeing parties. In this sense, they are called **interpreters** by Asconius <note>Asconius Pedianus was a Roman grammarian known for his commentaries on Cicero's speeches</note> in his *Commentary on Cicero’s Second Oration against Verres*, through whom a **pact** <note>original: "pactio"</note> is introduced. Virgil also uses the word in this sense in the 4th book of the *Aeneid*:

> *And you, Juno, the interpreter of these cares, and the witness.* <note>original: "Tuque harum interpres curarum, & conscia Juno."</note>

On this passage, Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian famous for his detailed commentary on Virgil</note> remarks: *Interpreter, that is, Mediator and reconciler.* Likewise, Cicero says in his *5th Oration against Verres*: *Those who are usually either agents or interpreters for the purpose of corrupting a judgment, etc.* 

And because such **Mediators** <note>original: "Mediatores"</note> examine and investigate the minds of those for whom they act as interpreters, and afterwards explain and open those minds to others, it has come about that, secondly, an **interpreter** is understood as one who explains obscure things. Thus Virgil writes in the 3rd book of the *Aeneid*:

> *O son of Troy, interpreter of the gods, etc.* <note>original: "Trojugena interpres divûm &c."</note>

Regarding this passage, Servius notes: *Interpreter, a prophet <note>original: "vaticinator"</note> who makes the mind of the Gods public to mortals.* And in the 10th book of the *Aeneid*:

> *— Asylas, the interpreter of men and gods.* <note>original: "Hominum Divumque interpres Asylas."</note>

Where Servius again notes: *He is called an interpreter of the gods whom he interprets, and of the men to whom he interprets and indicates the divine minds.* Cicero applies this more specifically to the obscurity and ambiguity of speech in his *14th Oration for Aulus Cluentius*: *The Magistrates are the ministers of the laws; the Judges are the interpreters of the laws; finally, we are all servants of the laws for this reason: so that we may be free.* So also in his *On the Divisions of Oratory*: *So that from the deed of each person, the Judge might interpret the mind of the law.* And in the 1st book of *On Duties*: *Injustice often arises through a kind of trickery, and through a too clever and malicious interpretation of the Law.*

Furthermore, since one who transfers something from an unknown language into a known one also makes an obscure discourse clear and transparent, the word **Interpreter** is, thirdly, used for one who translates something into another language, whether in writing or by the living voice. Horace uses this meaning of the word in his *Art of Poetry* when he says:

> *Nor will the faithful interpreter care to render word for word.* <note>original: "Nec verbum verbô curabit reddere fidus Interpres."</note>

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page explores the etymology and various classical definitions of the word "Interpreter," categorizing it as a reconciler of parties, an explainer of mysteries, or a translator of languages.</summary>
<keywords>Interpreter, etymology, Cicero, Virgil, Servius, Horace, mediation, translation, legal interpretation, terminology</keywords>

### OF THE EXERCISE

And Cicero, in *On the Ends of Good and Evil*, book 3: *Yet it will not be necessary to translate word for word, as unskillful interpreters are accustomed to do, when there is a word that expresses the same thing in a more or less common way.* <note>original: "Nec tamen exprimi verbum è verbô necesse erit..."</note> And in *On the Best Kind of Orators*: *I have translated the most noble speeches of the two most eloquent men: and I translated them not as an interpreter, but as an orator.* <note>original: "Converti duorum eloquentissimorum nobilissimas orationes: nec converti ut interpres, sed orator." Here Cicero distinguishes between a literal translator and one who captures the spirit and style of the original.</note> etc.

<margin>2.</margin>
However, although this last meaning of the word—namely translation—is also encountered and in use among Theologians, specifically when it is debated between the Papists <note>Roman Catholics</note> and those who have broken away from them <note>Protestants</note> regarding the interpretation or translation of Holy Scripture into vernacular or common languages. You may see Chamier in *The Catholic Panstratia*, Volume 1, book 11; Polanus in *The System of Theology*, book 1, chapter 39; and others. Nevertheless, for us in this place, the word is used in a second sense, distinguishing this meaning from the former along with the same Polanus in the passage cited, chapter 45, in these words: *The interpretation of Holy Scripture (to advise on the meaning of the word at the outset) is understood in this chapter not as a version or translation from one language into another, but as the showing of the true sense and use of Scripture*; And with Chamier in *Panstratia*, Vol. 1, book 15, chapter 1, section 1, when he says: *Having thoroughly examined the controversies pertaining to that Interpretation of Holy Scripture which regards the words, and is usually called translation or version, it follows that we should treat that part which pertains to the very meaning of the words.* Concerning this same subject, the distinguished Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer, a 17th-century theologian and pioneer in hermeneutics</note> also treats it in general in his *Idea of the Good Interpreter and the Malicious Slanderer*, exhibiting a complete System of it, making it a part of Logic, and calling it **Hermeneutics** <note>The science or art of interpretation</note>: As also the most learned and Reverend man Johann Clauberg, Professor of Philosophy and Theology in the University of Duisburg, in his *Old and New Logic*, who calls it *Analytical Hermeneutics*, and says it deals with *investigating the true sense of obscure Speech.*

<margin>3.</margin>
From these things it becomes manifest that the Interpreter, of whom we must speak, is occupied and engaged with external speech, so that he may draw out and bring forth from it the internal speech. That is, he considers the individual words and the sentences composed from them, so that he may search out and extract their sense, exactly agreeing with and responding to the mind of the Author.

<margin>4.</margin>
Furthermore, as Cicero has it in his *Topics*, these words *are signs of things, or rather*, as we think, *of concepts.* <note>original: "sunt rerum notæ, aut potiùs... conceptuum"</note> For we use them primarily so that we may make known to others what we have conceived in our mind; and since concepts are nothing but representations of things in the intellect, those same things are also, in a secondary way, denoted and designated by them. However, this entire system of denotation does not take its origin from Nature, but from the free will of men. We do not, indeed, deny that it flows from the nature of man—and is thus natural to man—to emit sounds and voices; indeed, to speak, which is to utter words. But it must be credited solely to the pleasure and institution of men that these sounds and these voices designate this concept and not that one, or this thing and not another. For since whatever is within the scope of Nature...

<meta>Catchword: bitu</meta>

<summary>This page defines "interpretation" not merely as linguistic translation (version) but as the intellectual process of uncovering an author's true meaning (hermeneutics). The author argues that words are conventional signs used to communicate internal concepts rather than being naturally fixed to specific objects.</summary>

<keywords>hermeneutics, translation, Cicero, Daniel Chamier, Amandus Polanus, Johann Clauberg, linguistics, signs and concepts, theology, external vs internal speech</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 4, which discussed the different theological and philosophical definitions of "interpretation," this page transitions into the mechanical and logical origins of human communication—how individual thoughts are externalized through words and writing.</meta>

# PARADOXES CHAPTER II.

[...the mind] is contained within its own limits, and is not always nor everywhere present, and since it has been granted by Nature to no mortal to look into another's mind and soul as if into a mirror; and yet, because the needs and benefits of humans living in the same society require that they communicate their thoughts and feelings to one another, they were forced to compensate by their own industry for what nature had denied. They did this by fashioning vocal sounds at will and attaching specific meanings to them. Nor did this sagacity of human wit stop with sounds and words alone—by which it is only permitted for those present to converse with one another—but progressing further, it even made it possible for the absent to talk with the absent, as it were, by fabricating marks for words and sounds. These are, of course, letters; by their manifold and changeable composition, whatever vocabulary strikes the ears can also be presented to the mind through the eyes. Thus, by this "silent speech," we are no less able to make the feelings of our soul known to others than by the living voice.

<margin>5.</margin>
Furthermore, from words, as if from component parts, **speech** <note>original: *oratio*. In this logical context, *oratio* refers to a "proposition" or a complete sentence rather than a public address.</note> is forged, or a declaration, **in which something is said and pronounced about another thing**. There are therefore two things in every statement: namely, something which is said about another, and something about which the other thing is said. The latter is called the **Subject** and the former the **Predicate** by Logicians; they are joined in an affirmation by the little word **is**, and separated in a negation by **is not**. Indeed, these elements are so essential that without these particles, no Proposition can stand; therefore, they are not undeservedly called the "soul" of the sentence.

<margin>6.</margin>
Since, therefore, words compose all speech, and these can not only be pronounced by the mouth but also painted and designated by letters, the same rule will hold true for speech itself. Thus, both words and speech will exist in two forms: either brought forth by the living voice or expressed in writing. Our **Interpreter** <note>The author is defining the role of a "Hermeneutist" or one who practices the art of understanding and explaining texts.</note> will be occupied with both kinds, both of words and of speeches, though in different ways. They are concerned with speeches primarily, as the "proximate object" <note>the immediate thing being studied</note>; but with words secondarily, as the "remote object"—namely, the material and parts from which all speech is produced and consists. For the first thing an interpreter intends is to investigate the sense of the speech; and since this sense mostly arises from the words forging it, and therefore must be drawn out from those words, he can in no way hit the mark he set for himself unless he also attentively considers, diligently weighs, and examines those words.

<margin>7.</margin>
For although Speech is the immediate and primary object of Interpretation, yet that is not the whole of what must be observed in this place. Grammar, Rhetoric, and other parts of Logic also consider speech, but not in the same way as this study. Hence, Philosophers best distinguish the objects of disciplines and their parts into **material and formal**. The <term>material object</term> <note>The "raw material" or general subject matter.</note> is common to many arts; the <term>formal object</term> <note>The specific "angle" or perspective from which a subject is studied.</note> is peculiar to one. Thus, speech is also treated by Grammarians, but insofar as it follows precepts and...

...it conforms to the rules of art; it is treated by Rhetoricians insofar as it is ornamented, and by Logicians insofar as concepts are expressed through it. Here, however, we treat speech insofar as it is **obscure** and its sense must be investigated. For if all speech were clear and transparent, and none was obscure, there would be no need for any **Interpretation** <note>original: *Interpretatione*. The author argues that interpretation is a corrective tool used only when communication fails to be immediately clear.</note> to make it clear—that is, to reveal its sense. Therefore, since obscurity subjects speech to our interpreter and makes it the formal object of his work, we must conduct a broader discussion about it.

## CHAPTER III.

1. *What is the sense of speech?* 2. *What is clarity, and what is obscurity?* 3. *That these are relative, not absolute.* 4. *How many types of sense are there, and their definitions?* 5. *How many types of clarity and obscurity?* 6. *The distinction between the truth of a statement and its true sense.* 7. *Three things to be considered in every statement, the neglect of which has led Theologians into error?* 8. *What is obscurity properly so called, and what is ambiguity?* 9. *How many types of this obscurity are there?* 10. *How many types of obscurity exist for a single word?* 11. *What is the strangeness of words?* <note>original: *insolentia*. What "signification" is to individual words, "**sense**" is to speech—namely, that which is indicated and denoted by it. And since any speech, as we have said, is forged from and consists of words, its sense will also consist of and be forged from the significations of those words.

### CHAPTER III. OF THE PARADOXES

<margin>2.</margin> In this regard, speech is called either **clear** or **obscure**. *Speech is **clear** when its meaning is evident at first glance, or at first intuition, to the listener or reader. It is **obscure**, however, when its meaning is hidden at first glance, or at first intuition, to the listener or reader.* We use the phrases *at first glance* or *at first intuition*, and *to the listener or reader*, so that we might encompass the distinction between both types of speech—those delivered by the living voice as well as those expressed in writing—by referring "at first glance" to the listener and "at first intuition" to the reader.

<margin>3.</margin> From these definitions, it becomes manifest that both clarity and obscurity concern the intellect of the listener or reader; thus, the entire nature of both consists in a **relationship to another**. <note>The author argues that clarity is not an inherent property of a text, but a "relative" one that depends on the person reading it.</note> This is further confirmed by the fact that it very often happens that a statement which is obscure to one person is clear to another, and conversely, what is clear to one is obscure to another; this could not happen if these were absolute qualities. Hence, there is a grave error among those **Theologians** who speak of these not as relative terms, but as if they were absolute things when they dispute the clarity and obscurity of Holy Scripture, explaining them by comparison to the Sun and its light. For instance, a great **Reformed Theologian** <note>original: *Reformatos Theologus*. This refers to the Protestant tradition, which often argued for the "perspicuity" or inherent clarity of the Bible.</note> says: *The clarity of Scripture is a property that exists within Scripture itself, which it always retains, whether a person is enlightened or not; even though the unenlightened person does not see that clarity. In a similar way, the Sun is bright in itself and remains so, whether a person opens or closes their eyes, and whether they see or are blind; for that variation in the person can make the Sun neither bright nor dark.* And as it makes no difference (*these are the words of another distinguished Reformed theologian*) *to the light of the Sun what kind of eye looks at it: because whatever eye it may be, it cannot see the Sun except through the light of that same Sun; and therefore the Sun is always bright as long as it is seen through its perceived light, and not otherwise. Thus, Scripture will be clear, because whether they have more or less sharp minds, people understand the Scriptures from the Scriptures, whatever the measure or degree of their intelligence may be.*

<margin>4.</margin> Furthermore, the **sense** <note>original: *Sensus*. In this context, "sense" refers to the underlying meaning or direction of a sentence.</note> of one and the same statement—for various and manifold reasons soon to be mentioned and enumerated—can be various and manifold, and can be divided in many ways. The most fitting and convenient division regarding the present matter is that it is either **simply so called**, which the words display on the surface, or it is the **true sense**, which the Author intends. That these senses occur in speech is evident from the fact that the meaning which the words of a sentence display—according to how they commonly and popularly sound—is not immediately the sense which the Author had in mind while writing or pronouncing those words for the sake of expressing his mind. To demonstrate this with one or two examples from Scripture: when we read "**the arm of God**" or "**the finger of God**" <note>These are "anthropomorphisms"—using human body parts to describe God's power metaphorically.</note>; and when Christ says, "**this is my body**," or "**I am the way, the truth, and the life**"; no one is ignorant of what those words...

He introduces the distinction between the surface meaning of words and the "true sense" intended by the author, using Biblical metaphors as examples.</summary>

# EXERCISES

...designate in common usage, and thus collect a sense from them according to that meaning. However, no <term>Theologian</term><note>A scholar of divinity and religious doctrine.</note> of sound mind would say that such a sense is the true one intended by the Author. From this, it is clear that these sentences, and therefore speech itself, admit a twofold meaning. We do not deny that these meanings can sometimes converge, so that the meaning from the Author’s mind and the meaning offered to the listener or reader from common usage coincide, even if the speech might still carry another sense or explanation. Nor do we deny that sometimes only a simple and unique sense, or perhaps even more than two senses, can exist in a text. Nevertheless, since there can be two senses—and different ones, as has been proven—this distinction of meanings within speech will hold its place.

<margin>5.</margin> And since, as we hinted above, speech is called **clear** or **obscure** in respect to its sense, and that sense is twofold—namely, sense "simply stated" and "true" sense—clarity and obscurity will also be twofold, depending on whether they refer to one or the other. The definitions of these can very easily be compiled from what has come before.

<margin>6.</margin> From what has been said, it is clear that the "true sense" we are discussing here must by no means be confused with that "true sense" from which speech is called "true" in a factual way. That factual truth refers not to the understanding and mind of the speaker, but to the things being spoken about. It is known to anyone even lightly brushed by <term>Scholastic disciplines</term><note>The traditional system of theology and philosophy taught in European universities, characterized by rigorous logical analysis.</note> that a manifold naming of "Truth" is found among the Philosophers. Among others, there are two types attributed to external speech: one called **Logical**, the other **Ethical**. Upon the former depends the *truth of the speech*; upon the latter depends the *true sense* of that same speech. From the former, speech is called "true"; from the latter, the sense is called "true." For the former is the agreement of speech with the things themselves as they exist in nature outside the speaker’s intellect. The latter, however, is the conformity of speech with the ideas and concepts held in the mind of the one who produced it, whether in writing or by the living voice, without regard for whether it matches the things themselves or not. **Augustine** seems to note this same difference between the truth of speech and the true sense in *Confessions, Book 12, Chapter 23*:

> *I see that two kinds of disagreements can arise when something is announced through signs by truthful messengers: one, if the disagreement is about the truth of the things; the other, if it is about the will of the one who announces it. For we seek one way to know what is true regarding the condition of the Creation, but another way to know what **Moses**, that excellent servant of your faith, wished the reader or listener to understand in these words.* 
> <note>original Latin: "Duo video dissensionum genera oboriri posse... Aliter enim quærimus de Creaturæ conditione, quid verum sit: aliter autem quid in his verbis Moses... intelligere lectorem, auditoremve voluerit."</note>

The distinction of **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a prominent French Reformed theologian.</note> is not dissimilar, which he proposes in *Panstratia Catholica, Volume 1, Book 15, Chapter 3, Section 21*, in these words:

> *I distinguish "true sense": for it is spoken of either absolutely and in itself—namely, that which proposes nothing false or impious—or relatively to the passage in question, so that it is truly that literal sense which the passage requires.*
> <note>original Latin: "Distinguo verum sensum: dicitur enim absolutè & ad se... vel relatè ad locum, de quô est quæstio."</note>

### PARADOXES. CHAPTER III.
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: if one,</meta>

This distinct consideration of clarities and obscurities will bestow no small light upon the goal of our **Exercise**; conversely, the neglect of these has led interpreters of Holy Scripture and Theologians into labyrinths of error and meanders of delusion. For they have thought that many passages of Scripture were clear and transparent—specifically with the transparency of the true sense—when they could gather a sense from known words at first glance without any effort. They immediately persuaded themselves that the meaning which offered itself so spontaneously agreed with the mind of the Holy Writer. Meanwhile, the writer’s intent was far different; thus, those passages are actually obscure and difficult in respect to their true sense. These interpreters have wretchedly deceived themselves by not noticing, or by disregarding, the distinction between senses, seizing upon what is "simply stated" as if it were the truth. They failed to inquire further, as was their duty, whether the Author actually recorded the text in the same sense in which they were explaining the passage offered to them.

Thus the **Roman Catholics** <note>original: "Pontificii" — a term used to refer to those loyal to the Pope.</note> (to illustrate my words with examples) cry out and write that the words of Christ with which he instituted the **Holy Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Communion.</note> in *Matthew 26, verses 26, 27, 28*—"Take, eat, this is my body, and drink from it all of you, for this is my blood of that new Testament, etc." <note>original: "Accipite, comedite, hoc est corpus meum, & bibite ex eo omnes, hoc enim est sanguis meus novae illius Testamenti &c."</note>—are clear and transparent, as indeed they are. From this, they construct a meaning without any trouble, just as anyone might. But this meaning is only the "simply stated" one; it is not the **true** one, as the **Reformers** <note>Protestant theologians of the Reformation.</note> contend and demonstrate. Therefore, this passage is indeed clear in respect to the sense "simply stated," but it is not clear in respect to the true sense; rather, it is obscure.

So also, when in the Scriptures God is called "**Lord of heaven and earth**" (*Matthew 11:25, Luke 10:21, Acts 17:24*), "**King of nations**" (*Jeremiah 10:7*), "**King of Kings**" (*1 Timothy 6:15*), is said to "**Reign over the nations**" (*Psalm 47:9*), to have "**Begotten a Son**" (*Psalm 2:7*), or to have "**Loved the world**" (*John 3:16, etc.*), these are words whose significance escapes no one. From these, anyone can easily gather the sense of these speeches, but only as they are "simply stated." For no one who wishes to be wiser than the common crowd would assert that God is a lord, or a king, or begets, or loves in the same manner as human beings. Therefore, the true senses will require a deeper investigation; in respect to that investigation, these things predicated of God are not clear, but obscure. Hence, it is established that the Christian common-folk are "entirely in the clouds" <note>An idiom meaning "completely mistaken."</note> when they take these things everywhere just as the words sound at first glance—as are all the Interpreters of Holy Scripture who side with them in this matter. Therefore, our distinction of the senses—and the clarities and obscurities regarding them—must be especially maintained by those who do not wish to constantly slip in a most disgraceful way and crash into the reefs of the most absurd interpretations.

8. We establish, therefore (to return to where we diverted), a twofold **Obscurity**: namely, either of the sense "simply stated"—which is properly and simply called **Obscurity**—or of the true sense, which is again twofold. For either no sense occurs in the speech, or one occurs, or many. If none, it is simply obscure; <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the logical division of obscurity on the next page.</note>

and the distinct consideration of these [clarities] and obscurities will yield no small light for the goal of our **Exercise**; whereas, conversely, the neglect of them has led interpreters of **Holy Scripture** and theologians into labyrinths of error and the twisting meanders of delusion. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the clarity and obscurity of texts. The author is moving from the general distinction between the "literal surface meaning" and the "intended true meaning" to a specific taxonomy of why texts become difficult to understand, categorizing types of obscurity at the level of words, sentences, and entire works.</meta>

<header>OF THE EXERCISE</header>

whether there be one or many [senses], the passage is certainly clear with respect to the **meaning as simply stated** <note>original: "sensus simpliciter dicti"; the literal or dictionary meaning of the words regardless of context.</note>; yet with respect to the **true meaning**, it is obscure. Indeed, one may doubt whether that one sense, or those many senses, are actually the true ones. Thus, obscurity regarding the true sense exists both in a speech where one meaning presents itself, and in a speech where many offer themselves. If that one meaning offered is also the *only* possible one, and the speech admits no other, then it will indeed be the true one. If not—which is usually the case, if not always—it will not immediately be the true sense, even if it could be. One must investigate (just as in a speech where many senses offer themselves) which one it is, if there is one, or which ones, if many were intended by the Author. Whether this can actually be done, we shall inquire near the end of this chapter. This multitude of senses in a single speech is commonly called **Ambiguity**, and since it renders a speech obscure regarding its true meaning, we must consider it alongside "obscurity" in the simple sense.

9. This **Obscurity** can be fourfold: it may involve a single word or phrase; an entire single speech; many speeches combined into a single period <note>In classical rhetoric, a "period" is a long, complex sentence composed of several balanced clauses.</note>; or an entire written work. Specifically: 
I. A single word or a single phrase can make the entire meaning of a speech unknown. 
II. Even if the meaning of every individual word is understood, the speech may turn out obscure because of how they are composed and constructed. 
III. Individual speeches, each of which might be clear and easy if viewed alone, can—when connected and joined together—render the whole **Period** they compose obscure and difficult. 
IV. Finally, an entire written work can be fashioned from periods that are clear and intelligible in themselves, but which are arranged so confusedly and shifted about so chaotically that the work cannot be understood. Cicero touches upon some, if not all, of these in the third book of *On the Orator* with these words:
> *Those men who bring their cases to us usually teach us so well that you would not wish for them to speak more plainly. However, as soon as Fusius or Pomponius begins to handle the same matters, I do not equally understand what he is saying unless I pay the closest attention. His speech is so confused, so disordered, that nothing comes first, nothing second; and there is such an oddity and a crowd of words that the speech, which ought to bring light to things, instead brings obscurity and darkness—and they seem, in a way, to be drowning out their own voices while speaking.*

10. The obscurity of a single word or phrase is either native and vernacular to the language in which we speak or write, or it is foreign. The latter is called **Barbarism**, while the former is called
- **Insolence**.

11. An **Insolent** <note>original: "Insolens"; in a rhetorical context, this refers to words that are "un-accustomed" or rare, rather than "rude."</note> and unusual word or phrase is one which, due to its age, is not just ancient but outmoded and obsolete; or one which, due to excessive novelty, has not yet been introduced or accepted into common usage. For words, like men, grow old and perish; and others are pushed into the light and succeed those who have passed away, according to that passage from Horace in his *Art of Poetry*:

<meta>catchword: As</meta>

<summary>This page defines "Ambiguity" and outlines four levels of linguistic obscurity, ranging from individual words to the structure of an entire book. It uses classical authorities like Cicero and Horace to explain how language can become "insolent" (obsolete or too new) or "barbarous" (foreign).</summary>
<keywords>Ambiguity, Obscurity, Cicero, Rhetoric, Insolence, Barbarism, Horace, Philology, Linguistics, Textual Interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on obscurity in language, specifically moving into the first category mentioned on the previous page: obscurity caused by the use of individual words or phrases that are either too old, too new, or foreign to the language being used.</meta>

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER III.

> As forests change their leaves as the years glide by,
> The first fall; so the old generation of words perishes,
> And like the youth, those newly born flourish and grow strong.
> We and our works are owed to death.
<note>original: Horace, *Ars Poetica*, lines 60–63. A classic Roman reflection on the "life cycle" of language.</note>

And a little later,

> Mortal works shall perish;
> Much less can the honor and living grace of speech endure.
> Many words shall be reborn which have now fallen, and those
> Which are now in honor shall fall, if usage wills it,
> In whose power is the decision, the law, and the rule of speaking.
<note>original: Horace, *Ars Poetica*, lines 68–72.</note>

Anyone who uses speech so ancient that he seems to be speaking not with us, but with our great-great-grandfather or even more distant ancestor—or speech so new that he seems to be speaking with our descendants—does nothing other than ensure he is not understood by his contemporaries. Quintilian also recognized this in his *Institutes of Oratory*, Book 8, Chapter 2, with these words: *Obscurity also arises from words removed from common usage: as if someone, having scrutinized the commentaries of the Priests, the most ancient treaties, and obsolete authors, seeks this very thing from them: that the things he has pulled from there should not be understood.* <note>original: "Obscuritas fit etiam verbis ab usu remotis..." Quintilian was a famous Roman teacher of rhetoric who emphasized clarity.</note> Indeed, Gerardus Vossius <note>A 17th-century Dutch scholar and influential rhetorician.</note> says this in his *Institutes of Oratory*, Book 4, Chapter 1, Section 8: *New words are repugnant to clarity. For it can hardly happen that things first given birth by the speaker are known to everyone.* **New** words are of almost the same coin. In the time of Augustus <note>The first Roman Emperor (r. 27 BC – 14 AD).</note>, such obsolete words and phrases among the Latins were: *mis* for *mei* <note>original: "of me"</note>; *sam* for *eam* <note>original: "her"</note>; *nenum*, *nenu*, or *neno* for *non* <note>original: "not"</note>; *topper* for *citò* <note>original: "quickly"</note>; *perbitare* for *perire* <note>original: "to perish"</note>; *bovinari* for *tergiversari* <note>original: "to delay or shuffle"</note>; *dapatilia comisse* for *comedisse opipara* <note>original: "to have eaten sumptuously"</note>; *apludam edere* and *floccos bibere* for *furfure vesci* and *fæcem bibere* <note>original: "to eat chaff and drink dregs"</note>, about which Aulus Gellius disputes in Book 11, Chapter 7. New words that gained favor, however, were *urbanus* <note>original: "refined/urban"</note>, *reatus* <note>original: "the state of being accused"</note>, *munerarius* <note>original: "a giver of public shows"</note>, *piratica* <note>original: "piracy"</note>, and others, concerning which see Quintilian, Book 8, Chapter 3. Also *irritare* for *irritum reddere* <note>original: "to make void or invalid"</note>, in Law 2 of the Code concerning Holidays: *Whatever shall be done against this, is made void [irritatur] in every way*; and *absentare* for *absentem facere* <note>original: "to make absent"</note>, in Claudian’s third book *On the Rape of Proserpina*:

> As soon as he made himself absent from his father's stars.
<note>original: "Patriis simul absentaverit astris."</note>

12. A **barbarism** <note>In a linguistic context, this refers to using foreign words or incorrect forms that "corrupt" the purity of a language.</note> is a word or phrase that, being taken from a foreign language, is introduced into the native tongue and used contrary to the custom of approved writers. There are two kinds of these: some are native neither by usage nor by origin; others are native by origin, but not by usage. Those that are native by usage but not origin vary according to the variety of languages from which they arise; for this reason, a Barbarism is either a **Hebraism**, a **Græcism**, a **Latinism**, etc. Examples of barbaric and foreign words of every kind, sought from everywhere, can be seen in the Latin language in Vossius's book *On the Vice of Speech and Barbarous Latin Glosses*. In the New Testament, among others, Latinisms and especially Hebraisms occur. Latinisms <note>Latin words written with Greek letters or following Latin grammar within the Greek text of the Bible.</note> are: *Assarion* <note>original: Ασσάριον; a small copper coin.</note>, *census* <note>original: κῆνσος; a tax.</note>, *centurion* <note>original: κεντυρίων; a Roman officer.</note>, *custodia* <note>original: κουστωδία; a guard or watch.</note>, *denarion* <note>original: δηνάριον; a silver coin.</note>, *legion* <note>original: λεγεών; a military unit.</note>, *lention* <note>original: λέντιον; a linen cloth or towel.</note>, *titlos* <note>original: τίτλος; an inscription or title.</note>, and *give effort* <note>original: δὸς ἐργασίαν; a Greek phrasing of the Latin idiom "da operam," meaning "try hard" or "take care."</note>, Luke 12.

<summary>This page discusses how language becomes obscure through the use of archaic words (archaisms) or overly modern inventions (neologisms), using classical authorities like Horace and Quintilian. It also introduces the concept of "barbarisms"—foreign loanwords that can confuse the meaning of a text, such as the Latin-derived terms found in the Greek New Testament.</summary>
