### DISSERTATIONS

verse 58, and others of that kind. There are Hebraisms <note>Words or idioms derived from Hebrew but used in another language—in this case, the Greek of the New Testament.</note> such as *Amen* <note>original: "Amen," meaning "Truly" or "So be it."</note>, *Emmanuel* <note>original: "Emmanuel," meaning "God with us."</note>, *Hosanna* <note>original: "Hosanna," a cry for help or salvation.</note>, *Maranatha* <note>original: "Marana-tha," an Aramaic phrase meaning "Our Lord has come" or "Come, Lord."</note>, *Messiah* <note>original: "Messias," meaning "The Anointed One."</note>, *Raca* <note>original: "Raca," a term of contempt meaning "empty-headed."</note>, *Sebaoth* <note>original: "Sebaoth," meaning "of Hosts" or "of Armies."</note>, and many other words of that sort, besides which there occur innumerable phrases and modes of speaking derived from the Hebrew language. These are, as the saying goes, "known even to the bleary-eyed and to barbers" <note>original: "lippis & tonforibus notum." A Latin idiom from Horace meaning something is common knowledge or "everyone knows it."</note>; yet they must be considered accurately, for neglecting them can lead an interpreter of Holy Scripture into error.

I will add one example out of many, which is encountered everywhere and is very well known. Theologians seem to "find a laurel in a laurel-cake" <note>original: "laureolam in muftaceo." A Latin proverb meaning to seek glory in trivial things or to make a grand discovery out of something small.</note> when they so anxiously distinguish between these phrases: **to believe [that there is a] God**, **to believe in God**, and **to believe God**.

They say that **to believe [that there is a] God** is to believe that someone is God, and that He is all-knowing, all-powerful, omnipresent, the creator, preserver, director, just, merciful, eternal, etc. 

But **to believe in God**, they say, is not only to believe that God exists and is indeed all-powerful, omnipresent, merciful, good, etc., but moreover to place all hope and trust for one's salvation in Him; and thus, for the believer, He is good, for the believer He is all-powerful, toward the believer He is merciful, and He directs all things for that person’s benefit and salvation. 

However, **to believe God** is to assent to His words and to give Him credit, which is the faith one should show to someone speaking and professing the truth. 

They claim the first type belongs to all men, even to the demons themselves, based on James 2:19. The second belongs only to the Faithful; and they say it is a sin to "believe in" a mere creature. But this restriction does not apply to the third type, for it is permitted to "believe" men when they bring forth the truth. 

Thus almost everyone speaks with one voice. Meanwhile, I have never encountered the phrase **to believe God** [as a statement of existence] <note>original: "Credere Deum." The author is arguing about the specific Latin grammatical construction here.</note> in the Scriptures, and it cannot be said unless the little word "to be" is understood or added by ellipsis, as in the passage cited from the Epistle of James. Furthermore, **to believe in God** is a Hebraism which has the same value as the Greek and Latin phrase **to believe God** [to believe His word]. Hence, in Holy Scripture, "to believe in God" and "to believe God" are used interchangeably. See, if you will, John 14:1, Romans 10:14, 1 Peter 1:21 and 2:6, Romans 4:3 and 4:17, Galatians 3:6, and 2 Timothy 1:12; as also "to believe in Christ" and "to believe Christ." There are very many passages; let them be looked at: John 2:11, 4:39, 5:46, 6:35, 6:40, 6:47, and chapter 8 verses 30, 31, 45, 46, and chapter 10:37, 38, 42.

From that second-to-last cited chapter <note>John 10.</note>, our opinion appears clearer than the midday sun, for both phrases are used regarding the same people. For after the Evangelist had said in verse 30, *While he was speaking these things, many believed **in** him*, he immediately adds in the following verse 31, *Jesus therefore said to those Jews who had believed **him**.* 

That the latter—"believed him"—is the same as the former—"believed in him"—is admitted in their own version by the Theologians who, by order of the High and Mighty Lords of the States General of the United Netherlands, translated the Holy Scriptures into the native tongue <note>This refers to the *Statenvertaling*, the first official Dutch translation of the Bible from the original languages, completed in 1637.</note>; for they translate "those who had believed him" <note>original: τοὺς πεπιστευκότας αὐτῷ (Greek).</note> as those who believed <insert>[in]</insert> him. 

Finally, so that it may be abundantly clear that "to believe in God" is a Hebraism, and that the phrase "to believe in a creature" is used in the Scriptures with the same meaning, it is pleasing to bring forward just two passages from the Old Testament. One is Exodus 14:31: *The people themselves feared*

<summary>The author argues that many theological distinctions between "believing God" and "believing in God" are based on a misunderstanding of Hebrew idioms. He demonstrates through scripture and recent translations that these phrases are often used interchangeably to mean the same thing.</summary>

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER III.

[...they believed in] the Lord, and they believed in the Lord and in His servant Moses. Another 
<margin>2 Chronicles 20:20</margin>
instance is when Jehoshaphat, standing, said: *Hear me, O Jews and inhabitants of Jerusalem: believe firmly in the Lord your God, and you shall be established; believe firmly in His prophets, and you shall prosper.* <note>Jehoshaphat was a King of Judah known for his religious reforms.</note> The theologians I just mentioned have translated this differently, namely: *they believed in the Lord and his servant Moses,* and also: *believe in the Lord your God, and believe his prophets.* However, in the commentary which they placed in the first section, and even where they cite the second (for the reasoning behind both is the same), they openly admit that the manner of speaking in the Hebrew is identical, but that the difference in translation is drawn from the "nature of the subject." From this, one can easily judge how remarkably those men have performed their duty—those who are always shouting and writing that the meaning of Scripture should not be brought into it, but drawn out from it. <note>The author is pointing out a contradiction: these scholars claim to follow the literal text, but here they change the translation to fit their own theological ideas about who is worthy of "belief in."</note>

13. Obscurity in speech arises either from the <term>transposition</term> <note>original: "trajectio." A rhetorical device where the natural order of words is changed for effect.</note> of its words, or from their omission. This transposition occurs when their natural order is disturbed. This is called a *mixing of words* by Quintilian <note>A famous Roman educator and rhetorician.</note> in the cited passage, and an example is provided here from this verse of Virgil, in the first book of the *Aeneid*:

->*Rocks call the Italians which in the waves' midst Altars.*<-
<note>original: "Saxa vocant Itali mediis quæ in fluctibus aras."</note>

The natural order of this is: *Which rocks in the midst of the waves the Italians call Altars.* Omission occurs when certain words necessary to the speech are kept silent, as in that passage of Virgil, in the first book of the *Aeneid*:

->*Whom I... But it is better to calm the troubled waves.*<-
<note>original: "Quos ego... Sed motos præstat componere fluctus." This is a famous example of "aposiopesis," where Neptune stops himself mid-threat.</note>

And in this passage from Lucan, in the second book of the *Pharsalia*:

->*Do they seek, with such a crop of new*<-
->*crimes, which of the two shall rule the City?*<-
->*It was hardly worth the cost of stirring up civil wars,*<-
->*just so that neither might.*<-
<note>original: "Tantône novorum... Ut neuter." The poet Lucan is lamenting the bloody civil war between Caesar and Pompey.</note>

This skipping over of words is found not only in poetry but also in prose, as in that passage from Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author and naturalist.</note> in book 18, chapter 7: *In Transpadane Italy, I know that modii of grain weigh twenty-five pounds; around Clusium, also twenty-six.* He means twenty-six *pounds*. It is also found in that passage from Luke in the Gospel, chapter 13, verse 9: *And if indeed it should bear fruit [well]; but if not, in the future you shall cut it down.* <note>original: "Κἂν μὲν ποιήσῃ καρπόν· εἰ δὲ μήγε εἰς τὸ μέλλον ἐκκόψεις αὐτήν."</note> Erasmus translated it as: *And if indeed it shall have produced fruit: but if not, you shall cut it down hereafter.* <note>Desiderius Erasmus was a major Renaissance scholar who produced a famous Greek-to-Latin translation of the New Testament.</note> Beza, in his version, fills it in this way: *And if indeed it shall have borne fruit, you shall leave it; but if not, etc.* <note>Theodore Beza was a Protestant Reformer and successor to John Calvin.</note> Similar to this is the passage from Paul in 2 Corinthians, chapter 5, verse 13: *For whether we are beside ourselves, it is for God; or whether we are of sound mind, it is for you.* <note>original: "Εἴτε γὰρ ἐξέστημεν, θεῷ· εἴτε σωφρονοῦμεν, ὑμῖν."</note> That is to say: *whether we are insane, it is for God; or whether we are sane, it is for you.* Erasmus and Beza filled this in by repeating the words *insane* and *sane*. Very many instances of this kind are encountered in the Scriptures.

14. The obscurity of an entire <term>period</term> <note>A long, complex sentence composed of several clauses.</note> arises from many causes. The first is *Brevity*, according to that famous line by Horace in his *Art of Poetry*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the causes of obscurity in writing. While the previous page touched upon the transposition and omission of words, this page expands on how brevity, sentence length, and poor logical connections can hinder a reader's understanding.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>EXERCISES</header>

---
->I strive to be brief<-

### I become obscure.
<note>original: "Brevis esse laboro, obscurus fio." This is a famous line from Horace's *Ars Poetica*, warning that trying too hard to be concise can lead to being misunderstood.</note>

Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, a celebrated Roman educator and rhetorician.</note> criticizes this fault in the place cited as follows: *Others, in their rivalry for brevity, withdraw even those words necessary for the speech; as if it were enough that they themselves know what they wish to say, they think it matters nothing how it pertains to others. But I would call that speech idle which the listener cannot understand by his own intellect.* Cicero noted this cause of obscurity in the writings of Thucydides <note>A Greek historian known for his dense and difficult prose.</note> in his book *Brutus*, to whom can be added among the Latin writers Tacitus and Persius. The second cause, and indeed the opposite of the first, is the excessive length of <term>Periods</term> <note>original: "Periodorum." In classical rhetoric, a "period" is a long, complex, and carefully balanced sentence.</note>. Hence the same Quintilian, in the passage already frequently praised regarding obscurity in the context and continuation of speech, says: *Let it not be so long that the mind's focus cannot follow it; nor so slow in its <term>transposition</term> <note>original: "trajectione." The act of moving words out of their natural grammatical order.</note> that its conclusion is delayed by a <term>Hyperbaton</term>.* <note>original: "Hyperbaton." A figure of speech where the natural order of words is disrupted, often by placing a long phrase between words that belong together.</note> And to this also seems to belong that "multiloquence" <note>Talkativeness or wordiness.</note> which the same author attacks in that same place as follows: *There is also in some people a crowd of empty words; while they dread the common way of speaking, led by a show of elegance, they circle around everything they wish to say with a copious talkativeness. Then, joining and mixing that very series with another similar one, they extend it beyond what any breath can endure.*

The third cause of obscurity in Periods is the connection of the speeches and pronouncements of which they consist—either there is no connection, or it is poor. For it is a habit for some writers to accumulate many sentences, but to connect them neither with the preceding ones nor among themselves with those small words usually used for joining, which are therefore called <term>Conjunctions</term> by grammarians. Others, however, do use them, but incorrectly, and for a purpose that is foreign or discordant to the context.

The fourth is the confusion and mixing of speeches, namely when their natural order is broken and disturbed, and a <term>the latter becomes the first</term> <note>original: "ὕστερον πρότερον" (hysteron proteron). A figure of speech where the natural chronological or logical order of events is reversed.</note> occurs. A famous example occurs in Virgil's second book of the *Aeneid*:

> I begin upon these things: Youths, hearts most brave in vain,
> if you have a certain desire to follow me as I dare the extremes:
> you see what fortune there is for our affairs.
> All the gods have departed, leaving their shrines and altars,
> the gods by whom this empire had stood; you come to the aid of a city
> in flames: let us die, and rush into the midst of arms.
<note>Virgil, *Aeneid* 2.347–353. The "hysteron proteron" is in the last line: one must rush into battle before one can die, but Virgil reverses the order for dramatic effect.</note>

Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian famous for his commentary on Virgil.</note> clarifies the order of these verses in his commentary on this passage: *Youths, most brave hearts, you come to the aid of a burning city in vain, because all the gods have departed. Therefore, if you have a certain desire to follow me as I dare the extremes; let us die and rush into the midst of arms.*

The fifth cause is a silent <term>Prosopopoeia</term> <note>original: "Prosopopœia." A rhetorical device where an absent or imaginary person is represented as speaking.</note> or dialogue, when either one person making a speech or several conversing among themselves are introduced not expressly, but secretly. An example of the former is in that passage from Juvenal's *Satire 6*:

> If some God, or someone like the Gods and better than the fates, 
> were to give you four hundred thousand, little man, how great 
> you would become from nothing! How great a friend of Virro!
<note>Juvenal, *Satires* 6.202–205. The poet suddenly addresses the "little man" directly without a formal introduction of the speaker.</note>

<meta>catchword: Give</meta>

<summary>This page outlines five primary causes of obscurity in writing: excessive brevity, overly long and complex sentences, poor use of conjunctions, inverted word order (hysteron proteron), and unannounced shifts in speaker (prosopopoeia).</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, obscurity, Quintilian, Virgil, Hysteron Proteron, Hyperbaton, brevity, sentence structure, classical literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of causes for obscurity in writing, moving from the length of sentences to the specific rhetorical device of "interposition" or parenthesis. It uses classical examples from Juvenal, Horace, and Virgil to show how interrupting a thought can confuse a reader.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER III: ON PARADOXES</header>

> "Give to Trebius, set it before Trebius. Would you like a piece of these entrails, brother?" O money, it is you who grants him this honor; it is you who are the brothers.
<note>original: "Da Trebio, pone ad Trebium..." These lines are from Juvenal’s *Satires* (5.135-137), mocking how a poor man is suddenly treated with "brotherly" respect only when he acquires wealth.</note>

And later in the same satire by the same author:

> "Put a cross for the slave." "For what crime did the slave deserve this punishment? What witness is present? Who reported him? Listen: no delay is ever too long when the death of a human being is at stake." "O Madman! So a slave is a human being? Suppose he did nothing; this is what I want, so I command, let my will stand in place of reason."
<note>original: "Pone crucem servo..." From Juvenal’s *Satires* (6.219-223). It depicts a tyrannical wife ordering the execution of a slave without cause, famously asserting that her whim is sufficient justification.</note>

Also in that passage of Horace, Book 1, Epistle 16, which he concludes thus:

> A good and wise man will dare to say: "Pentheus, Ruler of Thebes, what indignity will you force me to bear and suffer?" "I will take away your goods." "You mean my cattle, property, beds, and silver: you may take them." "I will hold you in handcuffs and shackles under a cruel guard." "God himself, as soon as I wish, will release me." I think he means this: "I will die." Death is the final line of all things.
<note>original: "Vir bonus & sapiens..." This passage refers to a scene from Euripides' *Bacchae* where the god Dionysus, disguised as a man, defies the king. Horace uses it to show that a truly free man does not fear worldly loss, as death is the ultimate escape.</note>

Finally, the sixth cause is **Interposition**, which occurs when one or more connected sentences are inserted into another. Quintilian also recognized this as a cause of obscurity in the passage already so often mentioned, when he says: *Even by interjection—which both orators and historians use frequently to insert some thought in the middle of a speech—the understanding is usually hindered, unless what is interposed is brief.* For *Virgil*, in that passage where he describes a young horse, after he had said:

-> He does not fear empty noises— <-
<note>original: "Nec vanos horret strepitus." From Virgil’s *Georgics* (3.79).</note>

having inserted several lines, returns at last in the fifth verse with a different figure:

-> —Then, if some arms sound from afar, <-
-> He knows not how to stand still; he pricks up his ears, his limbs tremble; <-
-> And pressing back the gathered fire, he rolls it beneath his nostrils. <-

Moreover, this Interposition occurs either with or without the marks of Parenthesis. It is without them in the example cited above; but it is with them in this passage from *Aeneid* Book 3:

> "Speak now—(for a favorable religion told me my whole course, and all the gods urged me with their divine power to seek Italy and try remote lands; only the Harpy Celaeno sings a new portent, an abomination to tell of, and denounces sad wraths and foul hunger)—which dangers do I avoid first, or by following what might I overcome such great labors?"
<note>original: "Fare age (namque omnem cursum...)" Virgil uses this long parenthesis to show Aeneas's anxiety and the complexity of the prophecy he received.</note>

Examples of this kind are encountered everywhere among authors.

15. The obscurity of an entire piece of writing can proceed from no other—

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>D I S P U T A T I O N S</header>
<meta>catchword: nis,</meta>

we see, how it arises from a confused and disturbed **Arrangement** and **Ordering** of the things being discussed; specifically when the highest things are mixed with the lowest in a backward fashion, and those things which should have been placed in the first, middle, and last positions are muddled together in a chaotic way without any order. Some titles of the *Digest* or *Pandects* <note>original: "Digestorum seu Pandectarum." The *Digest* (also called the *Pandects*) is a massive 6th-century collection of Roman legal juristic writings compiled under Emperor Justinian I. It was notoriously difficult to navigate.</note> exhibit a specimen of this obscurity.

16. Thus far we have spoken of Obscurity; it remains for us to speak of Ambiguity. This exists and arises in speech either from its parts or from the complexity and combination of the whole: the former is called **Homonymy**, while the latter is called **Amphiboly**; although both denote the same thing as "Ambiguity." In this sense, **Fabius** <note>This refers to Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the famous Roman rhetorician.</note> says in *The Institutes*, Book 7, Chapter 9: *The species of amphiboly are innumerable; so much so that to some philosophers, there seems to be no word that does not signify more than one thing: but the types are quite few. For ambiguity happens either in individual words or in joined words.*

17. **Homonymy** occurs when some part of a speech (which is either a single vocabulary word or a phrase) has several diverse meanings. Thus the word **Taurus** in Quintilian denotes an animal [a bull], a mountain [Mount Taurus], a sign in the sky [the constellation Taurus], the name of a man, and the root of a tree. However, **Homonymy** is commonly divided by Logicians into that which occurs by chance and that which occurs by design.

18. **A word is said to be a homonym by chance** when it signifies different things fortunately and without a specific reason; and this can happen in almost innumerable ways. It occurs either without or with a change in the word's inflection. Without inflection, a homonymous word may fall under the same category of words or a different one. For example, under the category of **nouns** is **Jus**, denoting both that which is the opposite of injury [law/right] and that which can be sipped [broth/soup]; **Malum**, denoting a type of fruit [an apple] and misfortune [evil/harm], etc. Under the category of Verbs, **edere**, signifying to bring forth into the light [to publish] and to chew with the mouth [to eat]; **Pendere**, to explore weight [to weigh] and to pay a debt; **Cernere**, to see, to judge, and to sift; etc. Under the category of Participles, **Passus** from *pando* [to spread/outstretched] and *patior* [having suffered]; **succensus**, from *succenseo* [to be angry] and *succendo* [to be set on fire]. Under the category of Adverbs, **Ubi** [where/when] and **Hactenus** [thus far], denoting circumstances of place and time. Under the category of prepositions, **In**, denoting against, among, motion toward a place, and being in a place. **Super**, meaning for, besides, beyond, concerning, and among. Under the category of Conjunctions, **Ut**, signifying a cause, and a concession [meaning "although"]. Under different categories are **Latro**, a noun [a robber] and a verb [I bark]; **Decursus, neglectus, impulsus, latus** which are both nouns and participles; **Penitus** a Noun and an Adverb; **Versus**, and **Adversus**; nouns and prepositions; **Qui**, and **hic** pronouns and adverbs; **Certo, intro, eo, adeo, continuo** Verbs and adverbs; **Cum** an adverb, a preposition, and a conjunction; **Ut**, and **modo** adverbs and conjunctions, etc. That which arises from the inflection of a word is a **Homonymy** of either one or many things. This is not so much a matter of the meaning itself as of the same grammatical circumstance: and it is quite multifaceted; for how many cases in declensions, how many endings of genders, and how many persons of tenses in conjugations run together? This is the meaning itself—

# CHAPTER III OF THE PARADOXES.

...[inflectio]nis <note>The text begins mid-word from the previous page’s catchword, completing "inflection."</note>, and it happens when, through **inflection** <note>The process of changing a word's ending to express grammatical functions like tense, case, or gender.</note>, several forms coincide into the same word. These forms can belong to the same category of words or different ones, and this change can affect only one word or both simultaneously. We add examples here:

*   *Tela* <note>original: "Tela"</note> is the nominative singular feminine <note>meaning "a web" or "loom"</note>, and also the nominative, accusative, and vocative plural of the neuter noun *Telum* <note>meaning "weapons" or "missiles"</note>.
*   *Avis* <note>original: "Avis"</note> is the nominative singular <note>"a bird"</note>, and also the dative and ablative plural from *avus* <note>"grandfathers"</note>.
*   *Turba* <note>original: "Turba"</note> is a noun <note>"a crowd"</note>, and also the second-person imperative of the verb *Turbare* <note>"to disturb!"</note>.
*   *Amor* <note>"love"</note> and *canor* <note>"melody"</note> are nouns, but also the first-person present indicative passive forms of the verbs *amo* <note>"I am loved"</note> and *cano* <note>"I am sung"</note>.
*   *Is* <note>original: "Is"</note> is a pronoun <note>"he"</note>, and also the second-person present indicative of the verb *eo* <note>"you go"</note>.
*   *Minime*, *maxime*, and *plane* are vocative cases of the adjectives *minimus* <note>"smallest"</note>, *maximus* <note>"greatest"</note>, and *planus* <note>"flat"</note>, but they also serve as adverbs for denying and affirming <note>"not at all," "certainly," and "clearly"</note>.
*   *Imo* <note>original: "Imo"</note> is an adverb <note>"on the contrary"</note>, and also the ablative from *imus* <note>"from the lowest part"</note>.
*   *Circum* <note>original: "Circum"</note> is a preposition <note>"around"</note>, and also the accusative from the noun *circus* <note>"a circle" or "the circus arena"</note>.
*   *Adversum* and *Secundum* are prepositions <note>"against" and "following"</note>, and also the neuter forms of the adjectives *adversus* and *secundus*.
*   *Quoque* is a conjunction <note>"also"</note>, and also the ablative form of *quique* <note>"from each"</note>.
*   *Verum* and *vero* are conjunctions <note>"but" or "truly"</note>, and also the neuter and ablative forms of *verus* <note>"true"</note>.
*   *Porro* <note>"furthermore"</note> and *modo* <note>"just now"</note> are conjunctions, and also ablative forms of *porrum* <note>"a leek"</note> and *modus* <note>"a measure"</note>.
*   *Eo*, *quo*, *aliquo*, *quocunque*, *hac*, *ea*, and *qua* are Adverbs, but also the ablative forms of the pronouns *is*, *qui*, *aliquis*, *quicunque*, *hæc*, *ea*, and *quæ* <note>These function as markers of place or direction, like "there," "where," or "by this way."</note>.
*   *Uti* is an Adverb <note>"as" or "that"</note>, and the infinitive of the verb *utor* <note>"to use"</note>.
*   *Age* and *agite* are adverbs of encouragement <note>"Come on!"</note>, and also the second-person singular and plural imperatives of the verb *ago* <note>"Do!" or "Act!"</note>.
*   *Intra* <note>"inside"</note> and *sine* <note>"without"</note> are prepositions, and also the second-person imperatives of the verbs *intro* <note>"Enter!"</note> and *sino* <note>"Allow!"</note>.
*   *Cis* <note>original: "Cis"</note> is a preposition <note>"on this side of"</note>, and the second-person present indicative of *cio* <note>"you summon"</note>.
*   *Fili* <note>original: "Fili"</note> is the genitive of *filum* <note>"of a thread"</note>, and the vocative of *filius* <note>"O son!"</note>.
*   *Telis* <note>original: "Telis"</note> is the dative and ablative plural from both *tela* <note>"for the webs"</note> and *telum* <note>"by the weapons"</note>.
*   *Suum* <note>original: "Suum"</note> is the genitive plural from *sus* <note>"of the pigs"</note>, and the neuter of the possessive *suus* <note>"his own"</note>.
*   *Turbas* <note>original: "Turbas"</note> is the accusative plural of *turba* <note>"crowds"</note>, and the second-person present of *turbo* <note>"you disturb"</note>.
*   *Ducis*, *Legis*, and *Regis* are genitive singulars from the nouns *Dux* <note>"of the leader"</note>, *Lex* <note>"of the law"</note>, and *Rex* <note>"of the king"</note>, and also the second-person present indicative of the verbs *duco* <note>"you lead"</note>, *lego* <note>"you read"</note>, and *rego* <note>"you rule"</note>.
*   *Vestrum* and *Nostrum* are genitive plurals from the pronouns *tu* <note>"of you all"</note> and *ego* <note>"of us"</note>, and also the neuter forms of the adjectives *vester* <note>"your thing"</note> and *noster* <note>"our thing"</note>.
*   *Meas* and *eas* are accusative plurals from the pronouns *mea* and *ea*, and also second-person present forms of the verbs *meo* <note>"you wander"</note> and *eo* <note>"you go"</note>.
*   *Constiti* is the past tense from both *consto* <note>"I have stood firm"</note> and *consisto* <note>"I have halted"</note>; *crevi* is from *cerno* <note>"I have discerned"</note> and *cresco* <note>"I have grown"</note>; *pependi* is from *pendeo* <note>"I have hung down"</note> and *pendo* <note>"I have weighed/paid"</note>.
*   *Egero*, *ris*, *rit*, *rimus*, *runt* <note>These are conjugated verb endings</note> represent the present indicative, whose infinitive is *egerere* <note>"to carry out"</note>, and also the future and the perfect subjunctive from *ago* <note>"to do"</note>.

These should suffice; for one could bring forth six hundred of this kind, were it not that it would create boredom for both the Reader and ourselves. We therefore make an end to them with these little verses, which display one or two examples:

> What will you do (*facies*), when you come (*veneris*) before the face (*facies*) of Venus (*Veneris*)?
> Lest you sit (*sedeas*), but go (*sed eas*), lest you perish (*pereas*) through them (*per eas*).

And these:

> If you sit (*sedes*) in any seat (*sede*), if the seat (*sedes*) be comfortable to you,
> Sit (*sede*) in that seat (*sede*), nor from that seat (*sede*) depart.

19. **By Design** <note>original: "A Consilio"</note> is a type of Homonymy that is attributed for a certain reason to signify diverse things. This reason is found either in ourselves or in the things themselves. It is in us when, for a specific cause, we give our children the name of another person, either someone illustrious or dear to us. # EXERCISES

...recognizes Quintilian in his *Institutes of Oratory, Book 7, Chapter 9*, with these words: *“When the same name applies to several things, or even several people, it is called homonymy”* <note>original: "ὁμωνυμία" (homonymia); the Greek term for words that sound the same but have different meanings</note>, and he adds an example: *“Ajax, son of Telamon, or the son of Oileus”* <note>In Greek mythology, there are two distinct heroes named Ajax; Quintilian uses this to show how one name can refer to two different people</note>. In things, the underlying reason for this is found either in **Grammar** or **Rhetoric**. It pertains to **Grammar** when an Adjective is used in the neutral sense either as a Noun, an Adjective, or an Adverb; when a Verb is taken for a noun or a verb; and when a Participle is used as an Adjective or a Participle. The reason pertaining to **Rhetoric** is the **Trope** <note>A figurative use of a word</note>, under which are included **Metaphor**, **Metonymy**, **Synecdoche**, and **Irony**, and their various types and categories. Those who desire a broad explanation of these should consult the rhetoricians, especially the leader among them, **Gerardus Johannes Vossius** <note>A famous 17th-century Dutch scholar and linguist</note>, in his *Institutes of Oratory, Book 4*, who provides a most brilliant treatment of them from chapter five to the end of the book. 

Moreover, it is manifest that there is ambiguity in every trope, because a speech expressed with tropical <note>figurative</note> words has a double sense. For example, when **“Soul”** <note>original: "Anima"</note> is used for a human being, **“Stern”** <note>original: "puppis"; the back part of a ship</note> for a ship, **“Author”** for a work, **“Shepherd”** <note>original: "pastor"</note> for a minister of the church, and so on. For it is uncertain and doubtful whether those words are being used in their literal or their figurative meaning. The same judgment applies to **Phrases** <note>Idioms or expressions</note>, which for that reason must be referred to here. For **“to cast a pebble”** <note>original: "scrupulum injicere"</note> is either literally *to throw a small stone*, or figuratively *to cause a difficulty*. Also of this kind are: **“to mark with black coal”** <note>original: "atró carbone notare"; meaning to condemn or mark as bad</note>, **“to greet from the threshold”** <note>original: "à limine salutare"; meaning to touch upon a subject only briefly</note>, and many others of that type. 

Finally, it seems to me that no class is more suitable to include those words which Philosophers call **Technical Terms** <note>original: "Termini artis"</note>; they denote one thing in common speech and another in specific disciplines, and sometimes different things in different disciplines. Thus, **“Genus”** <note>Type or kind</note> sounds like one thing to the common people, another to Grammarians, and another to Logicians. So **“Conversion”** <note>original: "Conversio"</note> means one thing among the common folk, another among Logicians, and another among Theologians. Likewise, **“Inflection”** <note>original: "declinatio"</note> denotes one thing in common use and another in Grammar. **“Ellipsis”** expresses one thing in Geometry and another in Grammar. **“Figure, Hyperbole, and Parabola”** <note>original: "Schema, Hyperbole, & Parabole"</note> mean one thing in Rhetoric and another in Geometry. **“Ray”** <note>original: "Radius"</note> denotes, among other things in common speech and for Opticians, the brightness flashing out from a luminous body; for Geometers, it is half of a Diameter; and for Anatomists, it is the smaller bone of the arm <note>the radius bone</note>, which lies upon the larger bone between the wrist and the middle curve of the arm.

20. To the ambiguity of words, in our judgment, also belong their various **Acceptions** <note>The different senses in which a word can be understood</note>, although others may feel differently. For they produce a double sense in speech because of the double meaning they possess. They are called *Acceptions*, as well as **Suppositions**, because through them that which the word denotes is "supposed" <note>placed under or substituted</note> and "accepted." Quite a few of these are listed. The **First** is that a word is taken either **materially** or **formally**. It is taken **materially** for itself, or its "matter"—namely the letters and syllables of which it consists. It is taken **formally** for the thing it signifies. Thus, when we say **“mouse”** <note>original: "mus"</note> is a monosyllable of the third declension and masculine gender; or **“Beast”** <note>original: "Bestia"</note> is a trisyllable of the first declension,

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 18 regarding the different ways words can be understood—known as "acceptions"—before moving into the concept of structural ambiguity, or amphiboly.</meta>

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER III.

In the feminine gender, the words **mouse** and **beast** are taken materially <note>original: "materialiter"; referring to the words themselves as linguistic objects rather than the animals</note>. But when we say, *“a mouse is a beast,”* they are taken formally <note>original: "formaliter"</note>, for the actual things they were established to designate. 

**The second way** is that a word may be taken either broadly or strictly: *broadly* indeed in a wider sense, and *strictly* in a more narrow meaning. Thus, the word **Philosophy** sometimes denotes all liberal and philosophical disciplines in their entirety; at other times, it refers only to theoretical and practical dispositions, excluding the "instrumental" arts of **Grammar**, **Logic**, and **Rhetoric**. 

**The third way** is when a word is taken truly or improperly <note>original: "abuſivè"; literally 'abusively,' meaning a word used away from its strict, true definition</note>. It is said to be taken *truly* for the actual thing it denotes; but *improperly* for that which merely presents the appearance of the true thing. Thus, the name of **God** and of **Philosophy** or **Wisdom** is sometimes taken for the true God and true philosophy or wisdom, and at other times for the idols of the heathens and the erroneous opinions of men. 

**The fourth way** is that a word may be taken either as a proper name or as a common name <note>original: "appellativè"</note>. It is taken *properly* when it designates a singular and individual thing; *common* when it designates a thing shared by many. Thus, **Academy** signifies properly the wooded place near Athens where Plato was born; but as a common name, it refers to that place in any city where gymnasiums of study are celebrated. 

**The fifth and final way** is that a word is taken collectively or distributively. It is taken *collectively* when it notes many things joined together, such that the statement cannot be applied to the individuals beneath the subject; for example, *“all the Apostles are twelve”* or *“all the Evangelists are four.”* <note>While the group of Apostles is twelve, it is not true to say "Peter is twelve."</note> It is taken *distributively* when the statement can be applied to each individual. Since the sub-categories of a subject are either species or individuals, this distribution is two-fold: it is either *into the types of each* <note>original: "in genera ſingulorum"</note>, when it is predicated only of species—such as *“God wants all men to be saved”* or *“all animals were in Noah’s ark”*—or it is *into each individual* <note>original: "in ſingula generum"</note>, when the statement can be made of every single individual, such as *“every man is an animal.”* 

These are the principal types of acceptions; the rest may be seen among the **Scholastics** <note>Medieval university philosophers known for their highly technical and detailed logical distinctions</note>, who dispute about them at great length, and generally with more subtlety than utility.

21. Thus far we have looked at **Homonymy** <note>words with multiple meanings</note>; let us now proceed to **Amphiboly**, which **Vossius** <note>Gerardus Johannes Vossius, a renowned 17th-century scholar mentioned on the previous page</note> defines as *“ambiguity of construction.”* However, for us, as is clear from what we have said above, this term has a broader scope in this context: namely, it designates every ambiguity that occurs in a whole sentence which does not originate from a single homonymous word or phrase. Thus, it contains within it that which arises from construction as a specific type. This happens in many ways, according to the many ways words are arranged in a sentence's syntax. We shall list a few. 

A possessive word <note>a noun in the genitive case</note> placed between two names so that it could refer to either one creates an **Amphiboly**; for example: *“The brother of the wife killed the son.”* <note>original Latin: "Frater Uxoris filium occîdit"</note> Here it is doubtful whether **“of the wife”** belongs with **“brother”** or with **“son.”** <note>Does it mean "The wife's brother killed the son" or "The brother killed the wife's son"?</note> **Valerius Flaccus** <note>A Roman poet of the 1st century AD</note> falls into a similar ambiguity when he begins his *Argonautica* <note>An epic poem about Jason and the Argonauts</note> thus:

> *We sing of the first straits of the Gods made passable for great sailors.* <note>original Latin: "Prima Deûm magnis canimus freta pervia nautis"</note>

Where the learned dispute whether the possessive **“of the Gods”** <note>original: "Deûm"</note> should be referred to **“the first straits”** or...

### EXERCISES

...for great sailors. **Amphiboly** <note>original: "Amphibolia"; a grammatical term for a sentence that can be interpreted in two ways</note> also arises when the relative pronoun **Who** <note>original: "Qui"</note> appears in such a form that it could refer to either of two nouns in the preceding sentence; as in that passage from Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, Book 1:

> *One was the face of Nature in the whole world,*
> *Which they called Chaos.* <note>original: "Unus erat totô Naturæ vultus in orbe, / Quem dixere Chaos."</note>

The word **Which** <note>original: "Quem"</note> can refer both to the **world** and to the **face**. Examples of this kind occur so frequently everywhere that there is no need to add more. Furthermore, two accusative nouns used with an infinitive verb, where one denotes the person acting and the other the person being acted upon, make a sentence ambiguous. This is seen in that well-worn oracle of Ennius found in Cicero’s *On Divination*, Book 2:

> *I say that you, son of Aeacus, the Romans can defeat.* <note>original: "Ajo te, Æacida, Romanos vincere posse." This famous oracle was given to King Pyrrhus; it could mean "You can defeat the Romans" or "The Romans can defeat you."</note>

It is left entirely unclear here who will be the victor and who will be the defeated. Similar to this is the line from Plautus’s *The Merchant*, Act 2, Scene 4:

> *They say the Bacchants tore Pentheus apart.* <note>original: "Pentheum diripuisse ajunt Bacchas." Because of Latin grammar, this could also be read as "They say Pentheus tore the Bacchants apart."</note>

And this one from Quintilian’s *Institutes of Oratory*, Book 7, Chapter 9:

> *I heard that Laches struck Demea.* <note>original: "Lachetem audivi percussisse Demeam." Again, it is unclear who did the striking and who was struck.</note>

Some also claim there is a certain kind of ambiguity in that line from Terence’s *The Mother-in-Law*, Act 2, Scene 1:

> *And so, with one mind, all mothers-in-law hate daughters-in-law.* <note>original: "Itaque adeò unô animô omnes socrus oderunt nurus."</note>

For the word **mothers-in-law** <note>original: "socrus"</note> can be the subject and **daughters-in-law** <note>original: "nurus"</note> the object, or vice versa. However, we prefer to call this **Homonymy** <note>original: "Homonymiam"</note>. The words for "mother-in-law" and "daughter-in-law" are homonymous because of their grammatical endings; since they belong to the fourth declension, their singular nominative, genitive, and vocative forms, as well as their plural nominative, accusative, and vocative forms, all share the same ending. Thus, the ambiguity of that sentence comes from the words themselves, not from the sentence structure as in the previous examples, or in this one:

-> —— *He runs down from the open sky*, —— <- <note>original: "Cœlô decurrit apertô"; from Virgil's Aeneid</note>

Where it is doubtful whether he runs *through* the open sky, or *at the time when* the sky was open, as Quintilian notes in the passage cited above. He adds one or two more examples which, because they are relevant to our current topic, I will include here. He says: *“Where the ambiguity lies in what a word refers to, the meaning itself is doubtful: 'Let my heir be bound to give him all his own things.' In this category falls Cicero (in the Brutus) speaking of the practice of his father-in-law, C. Fannius: 'Whom, because he had not been co-opted into the college of Augurs, he did not particularly like; especially since he had preferred Q. Scaevola, his younger son-in-law, to him.' For the word 'he' can refer both to the father-in-law and to Fannius.”* Thus says Fabius <note>The Roman rhetorician Quintilian was also known as Fabius</note>; to which should also be added the remark of Octavius, who said to Cicero as he was approaching him that he was the "last of his friends to arrive." Furthermore, the **Order** or placement of words can be related to sentence construction, and the ambiguity arising from that also belongs here. Regarding this, Quintilian says in the same place: *“It happens through Placement; where it is doubtful what should be referred to what—”*

<summary>This page discusses "amphiboly" and "homonymy," explaining how sentence structure or identical word endings in Latin can create multiple, often contradictory, interpretations. It uses classical examples from Ovid, Virgil, and Cicero to illustrate these grammatical puzzles.</summary>

<meta>The previous page discussed how structural ambiguity, known as Amphiboly, can arise from the placement of relative pronouns or the use of double accusatives with infinitive verbs, illustrating this with examples from Ovid, Cicero, and Quintilian.</meta>

### PARADOXES CHAPTER III.

...most frequently, when that which is in the middle can be pulled toward either side, as Virgil says of Troilus in Book 1 of the *Aeneid*:

->Yet holding the reins —<- <note>original: "Lora tenens tamen"</note>

Here it can be questioned: is it because he holds the reins, or although he holds them, he is nevertheless dragged? From this arises that legal controversy: *A certain man ordered in his will that a golden statue holding a spear be erected.* The question is whether the statue holding the spear ought to be golden, or whether the spear should be golden on a statue made of another material. Beyond these examples, <term>Amphiboly</term> <note>structural ambiguity where a sentence can be interpreted in two ways</note> also arises from a sense of speech that is too broad and not sufficiently determined, as in the Oracle given to a very wealthy King, which Herodotus reports in Book 2:

*Croesus, by crossing the Halys, will overthrow a great power.* <note>original Greek: "Κροῖσος Ἅλυν μεταϐὰς μεγάλην ἀρχὴν καταλύσει."</note>

this is,

->*Croesus, penetrating the Halys, will overthrow a great force of wealth.*<- <note>original Latin: "Cræſus, Halym penetrans, magnam pervertet opum vim."</note>

Deceived by this, he thought he would overthrow the power of his enemies, but he overthrew his own, as Cicero notes in Book 2 of *On Divination*. Not unlike this is the example from Fabius <note>The rhetorician Quintilian</note> in a place often praised. Often, it is in doubt to which of two preceding subjects the following speech is joined: *Hence the controversy: "My heir shall be bound to give my wife one hundred pounds of silver, whichever he chooses."* The question is: which of the two should choose <note>the heir or the wife</note>? 

Furthermore, Amphiboly also occurs when a single person is silently introduced as speaking, or several people are in dialogue; for it is often doubtful whether the Author himself, or a persona, or which of the personae is holding the discourse. This kind of <term>Prosopopoeia</term> <note>a figure of speech in which an imaginary or absent person is represented as speaking</note> or character-speech and <term>Dialogism</term> <note>a dialogue in which the speaker represents the various voices of a conversation</note> occur in *Ecclesiastes* and the *Song of Songs*; as also in the Prophets and the Psalms, as Chamier acknowledges in his *Panstratia Catholica*, Volume 1, Book 15, Chapter 15, Section 27. It is also found among the Satirists. Thus Horace, in *Satires* Book 2, Satire 3, introduces Damasippus accusing Horace himself of writing nothing, when he himself might seem to be accusing another: for he begins thus:

> *You write so rarely that not four times in a whole year*
> *Do you ask for parchment, unravelling everything you have written,*
> *Angry with yourself because, being generous with wine and sleep,*
> *You sing nothing worthy of mention.* etc. <note>original: "Sic rarò ſcribis..."</note>

To these lines he later attaches a dialogue between himself and Damasippus, in which it is often ambiguous who is speaking. In our judgment, both types of ambiguity are also found in Juvenal’s *Satire 5*, in these verses:

> *Will you, as a bold guest, be filled from the usual baskets,*
> *And know the color of your bread?*
> *Surely this was why I often left my*
> *Wife and ran across the opposing hill and the cold*
> *Esquiline, when the springtime Jupiter grumbled with cruel hail*
> *And my cloak dripped with much rain?* <note>original: "Vin tu conſuetis..."</note>

And in those lines from *Satire 6*:

->Please tell me of some defense, tell me, Quintilian.<- <note>original: "Dic aliquem ſodes, dic Quintiliane colorem." The term "color" here refers to a rhetorical "gloss" or legal excuse used to justify a person's behavior.</note>

> *We are at a loss: she says, "It was once agreed,"*
> *"That you would do what you wanted, and I too could"*
> *"Indulge myself. You may shout, and the sea with the sky"*
> *"Confound: I am human. Nothing is bolder than those"*
> *"Caught in the act; they take their anger and courage from their crime."*
<note>original: Juvenal, *Satires*, 6.281–285. This passage describes a wife caught in adultery who audaciously defends her actions by claiming a mutual right to indulge in desires.</note>

Finally, it seems that <term>Amphiboly</term> <note>a type of ambiguity where a sentence can be interpreted in more than one way due to its structure</note> should also be applied when an entire speech or proposition admits of a different meaning in common usage in a language than what the words literally sound like. There is a memorable example—worthy of being brought forward because it is so perversely misunderstood by the public—found in 1 Kings, chapter 3, verse 12. There, God responds to Solomon, who was asking for an understanding heart to judge the people: *Behold, I act according to your word: behold, I give you a wide and understanding heart: so that there has been none like you before you, nor shall any like you arise after you.* Almost the entire troop of theologians, followed with blind momentum by the common church-goers, takes these latter words literally; as if they signify that Solomon was elevated to such a peak of knowledge and wisdom of all things that

> *None was similar to him, much less did any surpass him*
> *In his clever heart, whether they lived in past ages,*
> *Or if they should exist hereafter in coming years:*

However, this is a <term>Hebraism</term> <note>a linguistic style or idiom peculiar to the Hebrew language</note> and a way of speaking most common to the Hebrews, by which nothing else is signified than excellence and preeminence. Thus, the holy Author in that passage only intended to express that God had bestowed upon Solomon a truly extraordinary and clearly uncommon wisdom. 

That this is the case is revealed quite clearly from two other passages by the same Writer. One is in 2 Kings, chapter 18, verses 5, 6, and 7, where it is said of Hezekiah: *He had trust in the Lord God of Israel, and there was none like him among all the Kings of Judah who were after him, or who were before him. For he clung to the Lord: he did not depart from following Him, but observed His commandments, which the Lord had commanded Moses,* etc. The other is found in chapter 23, verse 25 of the same book, and it is about Josiah in this manner: *And there was no king like him before him, who turned to the Lord with all his heart, and with all his soul, and with all his strength, entirely according to the law of Moses; nor after him did any arise like him.* 

Since it is said of both of these Kings of Judah that no other king was or would be like them in piety, the Text must either be accused of falsehood or be explained figuratively. For the same thing cannot be literally true of both: if the former excelled all who lived after him, and the latter excelled all who lived before him, then each must have excelled the other, yet the contrary is explicitly asserted. It is therefore a <term>Hebrew idiom</term> <note>original: "Hebræus idiotismus"</note> by which the Author intended to indicate that these Kings excelled in piety and reverence toward God.

<summary>This page discusses how common linguistic idioms, specifically "Hebrew idioms," can create structural ambiguity (Amphiboly) when interpreted literally. The author uses the biblical descriptions of Solomon, Hezekiah, and Josiah to show that phrases like "none like him before or after" are expressions of great excellence rather than literal historical uniqueness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ambiguity from the previous page, moving from the historical example of Solomon's wisdom to the formal logical classification of structural ambiguity (Amphiboly). # PARADOXES. CHAPTER III.

22. Just as we previously related the various *Meanings* of words to *Homonymy* <note>original: *homonymia*; the phenomenon where a single word has multiple meanings, like "bank" (a river edge) and "bank" (a financial institution)</note>, so now, for the same reason, we believe that the meanings of *Sentences* should be referred to <term>Amphiboly</term> <note>original: *amphibolia*; structural ambiguity where a whole phrase can be interpreted in more than one way</note>. There are three main types of these: in the composite or divided sense, in the signified or exercised act, and in the identical or formal sense. A sentence is taken **in the composite sense** when the parts of its Subject or Predicate are taken together; but it is taken **in the divided sense** when they are taken separately. Depending on these different interpretations, a sentence is either true or false.

For example, this sentence, *Two and three are five*, is true in the composite sense—that is, if the parts of the subject *Two and Three* are taken together. However, it is false in the divided sense if they are taken separately: for it is false to say that *Two are five, and three are five*. Similarly, the statement *Every animal is either rational or irrational* is true in the composite sense—if the predicate *rational or irrational* is taken together as a single unit. But if taken separately, in this way—*every animal is rational, or every animal is irrational*—it will be false, specifically in the divided sense.

Furthermore, this distinction between the composite and divided senses is especially relevant in propositions involving the word <term>possible</term> <note>original: *possibile*; logical possibility</note>, and in those where words occur that signify several things at once, or where some word has been omitted; such as *it is possible for a laughing man to weep*. In this sentence, either the word *man* has been omitted, or the word *laughing* has two meanings: one is material—namely, the person who laughs, which coincides with the omitted word; the other is formal—namely, the very action of laughing itself.

Therefore, when this statement is taken in the composite sense, the *man* is joined with the *laughing*, or (which is the same thing) the formal meaning of the word *laughing* is taken and *weeping* is attributed to it; then it is false. For it is not possible for one who is laughing to weep at the same time, such that laughter and weeping are joined together. But when it is taken in the divided sense, the *man* and the *laughing* are separated, or the material meaning of the word *laughing* is used (which denotes the man), and "to weep" <note>original: τὸ *flere*</note> is predicated of him; then the sentence is true. For it is possible that he who laughs now may weep at another time, so that weeping is predicated of the man, not insofar as and when he is weeping, but insofar as he is a man. It was in this sense that Christ responded to the disciples sent to him by John in Luke, chapter 7, verse 22: *The blind see, the lame walk, and the deaf hear.* <note>These are true in the "divided sense" because those who were once blind now possess sight; it does not mean they are blind and seeing simultaneously.</note>

The second distinction of a sentence is in the signified act or the exercised act. It is taken **in the signified act** when words are used under a common name and definition; but **in the exercised act** when they are used in a particular instance and applied to things themselves. For example, this common saying among logicians, *The defined and the definition are the same*, must be understood in the exercised act, and then it is true: for *A beast is an irrational animal, and an irrational animal is a beast*. However, in the signified act, it would be false; for the "Defined" is that whose essence is explained, and the "Definition" is that by which the essence of the defined is explained <note>The labels "Definition" and "Defined" represent different logical roles, even if the things they refer to are the same.</note>. Finally, the last distinction is in the identical or formal sense. An **identical sense** is when things...

# OF EXERCISES

...ing, although they are designated by us with different names and represented by different concepts, they are stated of each other. The **formal sense**, however, occurs when those things themselves are not predicated, but rather the different concepts by which they are represented are predicated of each other. Thus, when Theologians determine that the attributes of God can be predicated of one another—for example, *The Intellect of God is His Will; the Mercy of God is His Justice*, etc.—they admit this only in the **identical sense** <note>Meaning that in reality, God's nature is one and indivisible; Mercy and Justice are the same thing in Him.</note>, but not in the **formal sense**: for formally, or by rational definition, they are distinguished from each other.

Wherefore Suarez <note>Francisco Suárez (1548–1617), a highly influential Spanish Jesuit priest, philosopher, and theologian.</note> in *Metaphysical Disputations* 30, section 6, paragraph 13, dealing with these statements, says: *If the speech is about the things themselves as they are conceived, those expressions are true: because the meaning is that the thing which is conceived under the rationale of Justice, or Intellect, formally and essentially includes the rationale of Mercy, Will, etc. But if the speech is about things as they are distinctly and expressly conceived by us, then those expressions are false. For our mind, through inadequate concepts, divides a thing which in itself is completely indivisible: and then, although the thing in itself is completely the same, yet it does not fall into our mind under individual concepts according to its total adequate rationale: and therefore if a reduplication is made in order to our concepts, that which is attributed to one cannot be attributed to the other: because the same thing is not conceived under the same expressed relationship through one concept and another.*

23. We have so far surveyed the sources of Obscurities and Ambiguities common to both spoken and written Orations; we now move to those which are proper and peculiar to one or the other. Regarding Ambiguities that specifically occur in speech delivered by the living voice, only two occur to us. One is that which arises from the similar pronunciation of two different letters; this varies according to the different pronunciations of various nations. 

For instance, among us and nearly all other Europeans, the consonants ***c*** and ***s*** before ***e*** and ***i*** are uttered in the same way, whence they agree in sound. Examples include:
*   *ceu* (as if) and *seu* (or)
*   *cedens* (yielding) and *sedens* (sitting)
*   *cedebam* (I was yielding) and *sedebam* (I was sitting)
*   *cella* (chamber) and *sella* (seat)
*   *cellaria* (pantry) and *sellaria* (sitting room)
*   *cera* (wax) and *sera* (late/bolt)
*   *cero* (I smear with wax) and *sero* (I sow/late)
*   *cervus* (stag) and *servus* (slave)
*   *cibi* (foods) and *sibi* (to oneself)
*   *cingulus* (belt) and *singulus* (single)
*   *cinis* (ash) and *sinis* (you allow)
*   *cis* (on this side) and *sis* (may you be)
*   *citus* (swift) and *situs* (situated)
*   *cive* (by a citizen) and *sive* (or)

Among the Frisians and Germans, the letters ***f*** and ***v*** do not differ in pronunciation, whence they utter in a similar way: *feritas* (wildness) and *veritas* (truth), *fidebam* (I trusted) and *videbam* (I saw), etc. The Spanish pronounce ***b*** and ***v*** with the same sound, for which reason they confuse *bibere* (to drink) and *vivere* (to live), *belle* (beautifully) and *velle* (to wish), *betula* (birch tree) and *vetula* (old woman), *vilis* (cheap) and *bilis* (bile), etc. You may find similar instances among other nations as well. 

So also the vowel ***e*** and the diphthongs ***æ*** and ***œ*** agree in sound when they are uttered; thus, in pronunciation, there is no difference between *ædis* (temple) and *edis* (you eat), *ædilis* (magistrate) and *edilis* (edible), *æquis* (just/equal) and *equis* (horses). From this comes the little verse:

->*This man spoke what was just, but the other took the horse,*<-
<note>original: *Dixerat hic æquum, sed tulit alter equum*; a pun on *æquum* (the just/equal thing) and *equum* (the horse).</note>

From this type of ambiguity, a testator <note>someone making a will</note> sowed a lawsuit. He had two sons, one named Leon and the other named Cunctaleon. He testified as follows: "I give all my goods to Leon" <note>original: "Bona do mea cuncta leonti."</note>. A doubt was left behind: whether he had given his goods to his son Cunctaleon, or whether he had truly given all (*cuncta*) his goods to his son Leon (*Leonti*). This is expressed in the following Epigram:

> *Leon battles his brother Cunctaleon with the weapons of the court,*
> *And wages war with the outcome of the struggle in doubt.*
> *The cause of the war is the father’s misunderstood will,*
> *Namely, to which of the two he had given his wealth:*
> *For with these words—"I give all my goods to Leon"—*
> *The parent had spoken before he died.*
> *Hence Cunctaleon claims the father’s goods as heir,*
> *And Leon himself demands all the paternal goods.*
> *You, O most learned Priest of the Laws, tell us*
> *Upon which of them the parent's wealth should rightfully remain.*

<margin>24.</margin> Regarding those ambiguities that specifically occur in written speeches, they originate from the fact that all the variations observed by a listener in speech and pronunciation cannot be represented by writing and characters. For although individual words may be distinguished by a small space, and the length of their syllables noted by an accent; and though sentences formed from them may be distinguished by commas, semicolons, colons, and periods; and though certain of their tones are expressed by signs of interrogation, exclamation, and parentheses—many other things are still lacking. For instance, there are no marks for abrupt sentences (to designate which some today use several dots placed one after another, in this manner . . . . ), nor for ironic, commanding, or praying sentences, and the like. The lack of these marks very often leaves readers in doubt as to the sense in which the letters were recorded. Nor is the lack of these signs and marks the only cause of ambiguity, but also their neglect, as well as their improper use. The Catholic <note>original: "Pontificii," referring to the Roman Catholic Church.</note> Doctors acknowledge this when they list among the causes of the obscurity of Scripture the fact that, as it is undetermined, it can be pulled into different meanings, whether from varying punctuation or from a different way of writing or pronouncing; so that it is doubtful whether it should be read affirmatively, ironically, or interrogatively. Nor does Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Huguenot theologian. His "Panstratia Catholica" was a major work of Reformed theology.</note> disagree in *Panstratia* Volume 1, book 15, chapter 15, paragraph 32, when he says: *I confess there are ambiguous passages which different punctuation can change; I also confess that all can be perverted by writing or twisted by pronunciation.* However, so that ambiguity—both that which arises from defect as well as from neglect—

## E X E R C I S E S

view of the signs and marks that can occur in written speech, I shall review them in some order. It must be considered whether speech is viewed on its own, or insofar as it is accompanied by the movements and gestures of the limbs. The diversity of all speech, when viewed alone, depends on the modulation of the voice, which applies either to a single word or to an entire sentence. The pronunciation of a single word is performed with the proper accent, or with emphasis, or followed by a certain pause; therefore, these three things ought to also be expressed in writing.

The *accent mark*, although employed by some more accurate writers, is nevertheless neglected by most; then ambiguity arises, as in the distinction between *to weigh* and *to hang*, *he stifles* and *he strangles*, or *a hare* and *wit*, which are distinguished by these little verses:

->*The just man wishes to pay, the wicked does not wish to hang.*<-
<note>original: "Pendere vult justus, non vult pendêre malignus." The joke relies on the vowel length: péndere (to weigh out money/pay) vs. pendēre (to be hanged).</note>
->*One stifles and extinguishes; the other strangles the throat.*<-
<note>original: "Suffocat extinguit, suffôcat guttura stringit."</note>
->*Seek a hare in the woods, but seek wit in words.*<-
<note>original: "In sylvis leporem, ast in verbis quære lepôrem." Leporem (short 'o') is a rabbit; lepōrem (long 'o') is charm or elegance.</note>

And this takes place most especially in the Greek language, since accents and <term>breathings</term><note>The "Spiritus" or breathing marks in Greek indicate whether an initial vowel is aspirated (pronounced with an 'h' sound) or not.</note> create a great diversity and distinction in its words, as is very well known to anyone who has even "greeted that language from the threshold" <note>A Latin idiom meaning to have even a basic or introductory knowledge of a subject.</note>. The sign for *Emphasis* is lacking in writing, which leaves it doubtful whether a certain word was spoken emphatically or otherwise. Thus, the Catholics and the Lutherans pronounce the little word *is* in this sentence, *The bread is the body of Christ*, emphatically, as if some mystery lies hidden within it. So also others raise their voice on the word *all* in *God wills all men to be saved*, so as to deceive the unlearned into thinking all men are the "elect" <note>A theological term for those predestined by God for salvation.</note>; as noted by Keckermann in his *System of Logic*, book 3, chapter 1.

Finally, the *brief pause* <note>original: "Morula"</note> which follows the pronunciation of a word in a sentence not yet finished—and by which that word is denoted as belonging to what preceded it—is expressed by few (specifically with a comma mark). It is neglected by most, and left in doubt whether it should refer to what comes before or what follows. Let an example be that of Cicero, from book 2 of *On Duties*: *Philosophers, with the greatest authority, certainly severely and honorably, distinguish these three types, confused, by thought.* <note>original: "Summâ quidem auctoritate philosophi, severè sanè atque honestè, hæc tria genera confusa, cogitatione distinguunt."</note> Here, if a comma is not inserted after "confused," it is ambiguous whether that word should be joined to "types" or to "thought."

In this class of ambiguities arising from defects in writing, we must also place those which occur in the Hebrew language. This is due to the fact that the writing of the ancient Jews formerly lacked the <term>vowel points</term><note>The "nikkud" system of dots and dashes added to Hebrew consonants to indicate vowel sounds.</note> which are today added to the characters. For reason truly escapes those who think that *Moses wrote the Pentateuch with them; or that Ezra at least, and the men of the Great Synagogue, used them.* In fact, those marks were eventually invented by the Jewish critics known as the *Masoretes*, specifically around the year 500 AD, as the famous Vossius holds in book 1 of *The Art of Grammar*, chapter 31. Because the ancients did not use them, a word which was not ambiguous when it was spoken acquired that fault when it was written. For when a word consists of consonants alone—

### PARADOXES CH. III.
D 2
<meta>catchword: dispo-</meta>

...marked with consonant letters, different vowel points—or the same points in different places—are attached or added, something different will be produced, and consequently something different will be denoted by it. You may find various examples of this ambiguity in the *Old Testament* and its versions, indeed in no small number; see some in *Vossius* <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649) was a celebrated Dutch scholar and theologian known for his works on grammar and history.</note> in the place just cited.

*In the pronunciation of a whole speech, Distinction and Variation must be considered.* Distinction is achieved by breathing, through which we sustain, suspend, or bring our speech to a close at intervals. The *Comma* sustains, the *Colon* suspends, and the *Period* brings to a close, as *Lipsius* <note>Justus Lipsius (1547–1606) was a famous Flemish humanist whose works on Latin style and Stoicism were widely influential.</note> states in his *Letter to Hubert Audejantius concerning Distinction and Punctuation*. In the comma there is the smallest breath, in the colon a middle breath, and in the period a full breath. To these three, many add the Semicolon, in which they establish a breath slightly greater than in the comma, but smaller than in the colon. 

To this distinction in speech corresponds *punctuation* in writing, which is, according to *Lipsius’s* definition, *a fixed method of marking by which we cut and divide writing.* Indeed, this distinction is necessary both for speech expressed in writing and for speech uttered by mouth: *For by Hercules (these are his words), the whole of reading and understanding is confused, unless their marks divide them and, as it were, lead them by the hand.* <note>original: "Nam hercules... confusa tota lectio & intellectio, nisi notæ suæ dividant, & quasi manuducant."</note> From this it is clear that both Obscurity and Ambiguity can arise in writing from the lack or neglect of punctuation, or from it being applied incorrectly. 

And this especially takes place in the writings of the Ancients: for those Ancients lacked the punctuation marks that we use; nor are those varying marks of distinction found in their books: but whatever of them is in those books today must be considered to have been added by more recent hands. This would also hold true for the *New Testament* itself; for it must not be thought that the Evangelists and Apostles punctuated their writings in such a way, or distinguished them with marks and signs of the comma, colon, and period, as they are presented to us today. Rather, Christian theologians performed this work a long time later, once these marks had already been invented. Hence it happens that where one person uses a punctuation mark, another may decide that either none or a completely different one should be used, according to whether that passage seems to favor his own opinion more or less. And it would not be difficult to bring forward very many examples of this matter, were it not that we consider it superfluous, since they are encountered everywhere by anyone scrutinizing the writings of theologians.

We proceed, therefore, to the *variation of pronunciation*, which is accomplished by the raising and lowering of the voice, and its other inflections, and it can be quite multifaceted. Through this, various <term>accidents</term><note>In the grammatical tradition of this era, "accidents" (accidentia) refer to the non-essential qualities or properties of speech, such as tone, pitch, or emphasis.</note> of speech are expressed, such as interrogation, wonder, interposition, irony, entreaty, command, threats, and other things of that kind. All of these things ought to be placed before the eyes of readers in writing by certain marks intended for that purpose, if all the differences of speeches uttered by mouth were to be shown to them in letters. We admit, indeed, that these differences are often [understood] from the meaning of the words of the speech, as well as from their arrangement, or...

<summary>The author argues that punctuation and vowel pointing are modern additions to ancient texts, including the Bible, and that their placement often depends on the theological bias of the editor. He then begins to discuss how vocal inflection (like irony or commands) changes a sentence's meaning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of punctuation and its necessity for clarity. The previous page established that punctuation marks like commas and periods were developed to indicate breathing and rhythm in speech. Here, the author transitions to how the lack of specific punctuation (like question marks) in ancient texts leads to multiple possible interpretations of sacred scripture.</meta>

### EXERCITATIONS
<meta>catchword: let</meta>

...can be seen from the arrangement; likewise the first three—namely interrogation <note>questioning</note>, admiration <note>exclamation</note>, and interposition or parenthesis—are today represented by peculiar signs and marks in writing. Yet everyone knows the opposite sometimes happens; and it has already been said that the ancients did not have marks for these three. So it is that the same difficulty arises in interpreting their writings regarding all marks as occurs in modern books regarding the rest. Since, therefore, so many and such great differences in speeches and sentences occur, and characters and marks are lacking in writing to express and exhibit them, it is impossible that many things which were neither obscure nor ambiguous when spoken aloud do not contract these defects when committed to letters. I do not intend to run through examples of every kind; it will suffice to have enumerated a few. 

Christ, in John’s Gospel, chapter 21, verses 15, 16, and 17, addressing Peter three times, says: *Simon son of Jonah, you love me.* <note>original Latin: "Simon Jonæ, diligis me."</note> These words can be accepted in two ways: either as if Christ were asking him whether he loved Him, as they are commonly taken and then marked with a question mark; or as if Christ were affirming to Peter that he loved Him. Likewise, in the same author, chapter 5, verse 39, responding to the Jews who were persecuting Him and seeking to kill Him, He says: *You search the Scriptures, because you think that in them you have eternal life; and it is they that testify of me.* <note>original Latin: "Scrutamini Scripturas..."</note> The first words of this verse, *search the scriptures* <note>original Greek: ἐρευνᾶτε τὰς γραφάς</note>, can be taken either in the indicative mood <note>stating a fact: "You are searching"</note>, as Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria, a 5th-century Church Father</note> prefers and the commentators on the *New Dutch Version* <note>The Statenvertaling, the official Dutch Bible translation first published in 1637</note> allow, or in the imperative <note>giving a command: "Search!"</note>, as Tertullian, Chrysostom, Augustine, and Theophylact judge. 

If they are taken indicatively, it can be a question, as if Christ said, *Do you search the Scriptures, which testify of me, yet you will not come to me?* or a rebuke, as if charging them with stupidity in this way: *You indeed search the Scriptures, and they testify of me, yet you do not wish to come to me,* etc. If they are taken imperatively, Christ could again either be commanding them or asking them to *search the Scriptures* etc. 

Similarly, when he returned for the third time to his disciples who were heavy with sleep in the Garden and found them sleeping, he says in Matthew chapter 26, verse 45 and Mark chapter 14, verse 41: *Are you still sleeping and resting?* <note>original: "Dormitis jam & requiescitis"</note> in the indicative, or *Sleep on now and take your rest* <note>original: "Dormite jam, & requiescite"</note> in the imperative. If the former, it will be a question, as if to say: In this danger hanging over me and you, is there such security and tranquility of mind, when you ought most to be watching? If the latter, it is an ironic permission of this kind: So far, while I exhorted you not to sleep, I wasted my words; sleep now, therefore, and rest; the enemies are here who will not permit this to you, but will force you to wakefulness against your will. Melanchthon <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a key German Reformer and educator</note> also thinks there is irony in the 4th book of his *Dialectics* in the words of Pilate when he asked Christ: *What is truth?* <note>original: "Quid est veritas?"</note>; as if he were to say, "You are a great fool, who wants to speak the truth in a court, where [the power] prevails..."

<summary>This page discusses how the absence of specific punctuation marks in ancient manuscripts leads to interpretive ambiguity in the Bible, specifically whether certain passages should be read as questions, commands, or ironic statements.</summary>
