<meta>This page continues the exploration of how the lack of punctuation and context in written texts leads to multiple interpretations. It moves from discussing punctuation marks to the role of physical gestures in conveying meaning, citing both biblical examples and classical rhetoricians to show that written words alone are often insufficient for perfect clarity.</meta>

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER III.

...and first that famous saying: *truth produces hatred, compliance produces friends;* <note>original Latin: "veritas odium parit, obsequium amicos"</note> since these same words can also be taken interrogatively, just as Pilate asked for it to be explained to him what truth was. Paul, when he writes to the Colossians in chapter 2, verse 21, *Do not touch, do not taste, do not handle,* <note>original Latin: "Ne attigeris, neque gustaveris, neque contrectaveris"</note> is to be understood not as asserting and teaching these things himself, but as recounting the sayings of others, as Melanchthon <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a German reformer and educator</note> argues in the place cited; and so these are to be interpreted mimetically <note>original Greek: "μιμητικῶς" — imitatively or through representation</note> and as a recital, as examples of superstitious traditions which some inattentive readers nonetheless take for Paul’s own precepts. But enough of examples; let us move to the consideration of Speech, insofar as the movements and gestures of the limbs accompany it, which is the second part of our general division proposed at the beginning of this paragraph. These gestures and external acts either merely *help* the understanding of speech, or they *complete* it entirely. They *help* and promote meaning in speeches where the nuances of delivery are expressed by variations in tone, which we recently considered: for you would hardly grasp an ironic sentiment unless you knew the speaker’s gesture; likewise, you would not understand a threatening speech if you did not know the movement of the hand accompanying it. They *complete* it entirely in speeches where a thing is designated by a demonstrative pronoun <note>words like "this" or "that"</note> or some similar particle—things not previously mentioned by any other word in the speech—and at the same time are pointed out by the speaker's finger or the movement of another limb. Thus these sentences, *I do not value you this much* or *at such a price*, can in no way be understood if you do not imagine that "this" or "so much" was shown by the speaker with some gesture. And from this, the Jews wrongly interpreted the words of Christ in John’s Gospel, chapter 2, verse 19: when they asked for a sign because He was casting the merchants out of the temple with a whip, He replied: *Destroy this temple, and in three days I will raise it up;* <note>original Latin: "Destruite templum hoc, & tribus diebus erigam illud"</note> they understood those words as referring to the Temple in Jerusalem, whereas He was speaking of the Temple of His body, which He perhaps even pointed to with a finger or another limb; since it is likely that it was His custom to join gestures to His words, as seen in John 20, verses 19 and 20, where in addressing the disciples, He showed them His hands and His side.

25. From all of which we think it is clear that Fabius <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the Roman rhetorician</note> is not so far from reason when he begins the penultimate chapter of the eighth book of his *Institutions* in this manner: *The types of ambiguity are innumerable; so much so that to some of the philosophers there seems to be no word that does not signify several things.* <note>original Latin: "Amphiboliæ species sunt innumerabiles..."</note> Nor is G. J. Vossius, who supports him in his *Institutions of Oratory*, book 4, chapter 1, section 10. From these Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer (1603–1666), a Lutheran theologian known for his work on hermeneutics</note> does not differ, when in the *Idea of a Good Interpreter*, he begins to close the second section of the first part with these words (Section 47): *The equivocation of terms and speech is nothing other than a sea of infinite perplexities. For who shall explain the various meanders of ambiguities, or what paper will embrace even the principal examples?* From which the Poet says,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ambiguity in communication. Having previously established how gestures and tone affect meaning, the author now transitions to a formal classification of "Ambiguity" (Ambiguitas), using classical poetry to illustrate the density of meaning before diving into the logical distinctions between speaker-intent and reader-interpretation.</meta>

# EXERCISES

> As many as the leaves that fall and drop in the woods at the first frost of autumn; or as many as the birds that flock toward land from the deep abyss, when the cold season drives them across the sea and sends them to sunny lands,
> <note>original Latin: "Quàm multa in sylvis Autumni frigore primô / Lapsa cadunt folia..." — A famous simile from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book 6), describing the vast number of souls gathered at the banks of the River Styx.</note>

this imagery seems to us to fit this subject matter perfectly; indeed, it seems that no speech can occur that is not, to some degree, liable to ambiguity.

26. Before we transition from these points to others, we consider it wise to give one further warning in this place regarding **Ambiguity** <term>ambiguitas: the quality of having more than one possible meaning</term>, which will be of use in the following sections. It is this: a speech may be called "ambiguous" either in itself and absolutely, or relatively and in respect to us. It is ambiguous *in itself* when, without us being aware of it or paying attention, the speech admits multiple meanings. However, when we do notice these meanings, it is called "uncertain" *with respect to us*. Since we are capable of either speaking and writing, or hearing and reading, this relative ambiguity is again twofold: namely, either *with respect to the speaker or writer*, or *with respect to the listener or reader*. A speech is uncertain *with respect to the speaker or writer* when they knowingly and willingly speak or write in such a way that their speech has multiple senses. It is uncertain *with respect to the listener or reader*, however, when they discover multiple senses within it. Both of these cases can be considered in two ways: the speaker or writer may act either with or without the intention of making the listener or reader doubtful as to which of those many meanings was intended; and the listener or reader may be in doubt (or not) about what was intended.

27. Furthermore, some of these types of Ambiguities can occur simultaneously, while others cannot, and this happens in many different ways, as is clear to anyone paying attention and comparing them. We shall touch upon the most important ones, which seem likely to be useful, with a light finger <note>A Latin idiom meaning to touch upon a subject briefly or superficially.</note>. Thus, a speech can be ambiguous both in itself and with respect to us at the same time, or it can be ambiguous in itself but not with respect to us. But it can by no means be ambiguous with respect to us if it is not also ambiguous in itself. Furthermore, a speech can be ambiguous in itself and yet not be noticed by the speaker or writer, nor even by the listener or reader; or it may be noticed by both, or by one of them. Finally, the Listener or Reader of an ambiguous speech can be in doubt about the meaning or not; indeed, they might not even notice the ambiguity itself, even when the speaker intended to make them doubt—or even when the speaker did not intend it, and was himself unaware that the speech was ambiguous.

28. Among the points just mentioned, the possibility arises that someone may produce a speech, whether orally or in writing, which they both know and intend to admit multiple senses; I do not think anyone will call this into doubt. However, whether multiple meanings can be intended by an Author who is both wise and good—that is, one who knows what he is saying and does not wish to deceive on purpose—is a matter of sharp debate, especially among **Theologians** <note>Scholars of religion and divinity.</note>. The **Cabalists** <term>Cabalists: followers of the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism that seeks hidden, multiple layers of meaning in sacred texts</term> defend the affirmative view, as reported by Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a Renaissance philosopher famous for his "Oration on the Dignity of Man" and his interest in combining various schools of thought, including Jewish mysticism.</note>...

<summary>The author defines the logical categories of ambiguity, distinguishing between inherent ambiguity in a text and relative ambiguity based on whether the speaker or the reader notices it. He begins to address whether a "good and wise" author—specifically in a religious context—can intentionally use multiple meanings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of ambiguity, moving from a general linguistic theory toward a specific application: the interpretation of Holy Scripture. The author contrasts the Catholic and Jewish traditions of multiple layers of meaning with the Protestant "single literal sense" before beginning Chapter IV, which focuses on why the Bible is intentionally difficult to understand.</meta>

# PARADOXES CHAPTER IV.

Giovanni Pico della Mirandola in his *Apology* <note>original Latin: "Apologia" — Pico’s 1487 defense of his "900 Theses," some of which were deemed heretical.</note>, as well as the Jewish Rabbis, certain Church Fathers, and a great number of Catholics, hold this view. On the other hand, all those theologians who have broken away from the Roman See hold the negative position, establishing and professing with one open voice that there is only one true, genuine literal sense (as they call it) intended by the author for any given passage of Scripture. For our part, to offer our own judgment on this matter, we frankly admit that it is indeed very common and consistent for writers to intend and signify only one thing with a single statement. However, we do not see why they might not occasionally be able to denote several things with the same words. Nor can we hide the fact that the arguments brought forward by the patrons of the opposing view to strengthen their position—at least those we have happened to see—prove nothing at all; indeed, they are so flimsy and worthless <note>original Latin: "ficulnea" — literally "of a fig tree," a classical idiom for something weak or brittle.</note> that we do not think it worth the effort to spend time listing and refuting them. As for what we think about the multiple senses of Holy Scripture—specifically, whether several literal senses can apply to a single passage—a more convenient place for discussion will be provided in the progress of this Treatise <term>Exercitatio: a formal academic exercise or written essay</term>.

## CHAPTER IV.

1. *That Scripture is obscure and ambiguous. 2. Who is the true Interpreter? 3. And specifically of the Holy Scriptures? 4. And how might one properly perform their duty? 5. That all interpretations of Scripture opposing the truth are spurious. 6. That truths of Scripture do not always seem to be the true senses. 7. Who is the best interpreter, if not an infallible one? 8. That all truths of Scripture are true senses is proved by reasons. 9. Confirmed by authorities. 10. That the conditions required by the Reformed <note>The "Reformed" refers to Protestant theologians, specifically those in the Calvinist or Zwinglian traditions.</note> for a true interpreter of the Holy Scriptures also fit our own.*

But we have already said enough, and indeed quite extensively, in the previous chapter regarding the general object of the Interpreter—namely, obscure and ambiguous speech. Let us now descend to the more specific object to which our interpreter is restricted in this controversy we have at hand: Holy Scripture. That this is both obscure and ambiguous in many places is a fact that escapes no one (no matter how little they have practiced with it), and is abundantly clear from the examples we have interspersed elsewhere. This is also shown and testified by so many of the most learned and sharpest minds, from the very foundations of the Christian Church...

<summary>The author transitions from a general discussion of linguistics to the specific problem of interpreting the Bible, arguing against the strict Protestant view that every verse has only one "literal" meaning. He introduces Chapter IV, which sets out to define the role of a true interpreter in navigating the inherent obscurities of sacred texts.</summary>
<keywords>Pico della Mirandola, literal sense, Holy Scripture, ambiguity, Reformers, biblical interpretation, hermeneutics</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the "single literal sense" of Scripture. The catchword from the previous page, "investigationis" (or "investigandis"), likely completed a sentence about the history of biblical scholarship.</meta>

->OF THE EXERCITATION<-

foundations up to this day by theologians, notes—both on specific parts and on the whole of Scripture—have been written and published, along with explanations, observations, commentaries, paraphrases, interpretations, and many other works of that kind, some of which far exceed the size of the very books they are meant to clarify. And not only do all the Papists <note>original: "Pontificii"; a term used by the author to refer to those loyal to the Pope</note> expressly state this; even those who have defected from them <note>referring to the Protestant reformers</note> admit it. Thus Luther, in his book *On the Bondage of the Will*:

> *I certainly admit that there are many places in the Scriptures that are obscure and hidden, not because of the majesty of the subjects, but because of our ignorance of vocabulary and grammar; yet these do not hinder the knowledge of all things in the Scriptures. All the realities within the Scriptures have been brought into the light, even though some places remain obscure because of unknown words.*

And Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a prominent French Reformed theologian</note> in his *Universal Panstratia*, Volume 1, Book 15, Chapter 4, Section 5, the Catholics <note>The author likely means "Universal" or "Orthodox" believers in the Protestant sense here, or is quoting a standard position</note> declare clearly and with a full voice:

> *First, there are not a few very obscure places in the Scriptures, from which—when considered alone and by themselves—no certain sense can be proven. And this happens in many ways. Sometimes because of the obscurity of the subjects, as in prophecies of future things, etc. Sometimes the words of a passage are so woven together that they produce different meanings due to <term>homonymy</term> <note>original Greek: ὁμωνυμίαν; when one word has multiple meanings</note> or <term>amphiboly</term> <note>original Greek: ἀμφιβολίαν; ambiguity in grammatical structure</note>; for example, "I and the Father are one" was understood by the Arians <note>A 4th-century group who believed Jesus was a created being, not co-equal with God the Father</note> as a union of will, just as when Christ prayed that his disciples might be one. But the Catholics referred it to being <term>of one substance</term> <note>original Greek: ὁμοούσιον; the orthodox doctrine that Father and Son share the same essence</note>. etc.*

Testimonies of this kind occur everywhere. It is not only that Scripture is obscure and ambiguous, but also—since it consists of words—there is no doubt that every kind of obscurity and ambiguity we have listed can find a place within it. Indeed, it would not be difficult to prove that if not all, then at least the greatest part, and indeed the principal types of ambiguity occur here—along with others of no small importance, as theologians themselves admit: such as mystical preaching (both typical and sacramental), <term>anthropopathy</term> <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποπάθεια; attributing human emotions or body parts to God</note> and <term>theoprepeia</term> <note>original Greek: θεοπρέπεια; attributing to God what is fitting to His divine nature</note>, <term>prolepsis</term> <note>original Greek: πρόληψις; a "flash-forward" or describing a future event as if it had already happened</note>, reversal or anticipation, and variant readings of both the Old and New Testament, and the like—if the current topic required it and if the true method of interpretation were already established. But since we are inquiring into that method, and since it is only necessary for now to show that certain places of Scripture are obscure and ambiguous—and therefore that it has need of interpretation (which even the theologians themselves do not deny)—it will suffice for our purpose to have pointed this out for the time being.

2. When, however, theologians dispute in this controversy about the "Interpreter of Holy Scripture," they are speaking of a *true* interpreter, just as we are doing here. He is not—as can easily be deduced from what was said before—someone who merely explains *some* sense or truth of a speech; rather, he is one who draws out the *true* sense (that is, the one agreeing and corresponding with the mind and intention of the Author) and shows it to be such. And so, *true interpretation will be nothing other than the drawing out and indication of the true and genuine sense of those speeches which are heard or read;* or as Chamier defines it in his *Panstratia*, Volume 1, Book 15, Chapter 1, Section 3.

<summary>The author argues that both Catholic and Protestant theologians admit the Bible contains many obscure and ambiguous passages. He defines "true interpretation" as the act of uncovering the original author's intended meaning, rather than simply finding any possible meaning.</summary>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from Chapter III regarding the "literal sense" of the Bible. Having previously distinguished between the literal meaning of words and the objective truth of the content, the author now begins Chapter IV to define the specific duties of a biblical interpreter.</meta>

# PARADOXES, CHAPTER IV.

->Investigation of that thing which the *Author* signifies in individual places.<-

From this, it is clear that a true interpreter ought not to be so concerned with the truth or falsity of the speech being interpreted, but rather with its true or false sense. He who has investigated and pointed these out—refuting the one and confirming the other (that is, proving it conforms to the Writer’s understanding)—should be judged to have abundantly satisfied his duty and fulfilled all its requirements, no matter how much it might disagree with right reason or contradict the truth. <note>The author is arguing that an interpreter's job is to find what the biblical writer *meant*, even if what they meant seems scientifically or logically "untrue" to a modern reader.</note>

3. Therefore, the true *Interpreter of Holy Scripture* will be the one who can draw out their true senses and demonstrate them to be such, as well as detect and refute false ones. Thus, his duty is completed and consists of two parts: namely, the investigation and proof of true interpretations, and the detection and refutation of false ones. It will certainly not be enough to have performed these with some degree of probability or likelihood, as is commonly the case in secular writings <note>original: "scriptis profanis"; referring to non-religious or "profane" literature</note>. Instead, it must be done with unshakable certainty and invincible arguments, in which a mind desiring solid truth can and should find full and complete rest. For since this does not concern trivial matters, but the weightiest ones; not earthly things, but heavenly; not fleeting and temporary goods, but the eternal salvation of our souls—than which nothing more precious or desirable can be imagined for man—the entire edifice of our faith must be placed not upon unstable sand, nor upon a weak and failing foundation, but upon an unshakable and immovable rock.

4. Furthermore, this province of interpreting the sacred Books (aside from others, if there happen to be any, which we do not deny) will be worthily and properly upheld by the one who is able to fish out <note>original: "expiscari"; a metaphorical term for searching out or "hooking" the truth</note> the truths of the passages to be explained, detect the falsities of explanations made by others regarding them, and establish by no doubtful arguments that he has detected them. We say *truths and falsities*, which will undoubtedly seem strange, since in the previous chapter we so anxiously and diligently distinguished the truths of speeches from their true senses, and in section 2 of this chapter we established that an interpreter should be called "true" based on the latter rather than the former. But indeed, anyone who chooses to observe with us will cease to wonder and will notice that we rely on reason: for it is one way with secular and human writings, and another with sacred and divine ones. In the latter, truths and true senses are everywhere joined by an indissoluble bond, which is not the case in the former. And so, he who has unearthed those truths has at the same time unearthed these senses, and he who has demonstrated that the explanations of others contain falsities has also shown those same explanations to be false.

That things are so is manifest from this: that *Holy Scripture* has *God* himself as its author. He made use of such *Scribes* <note>original: "Amanuensibus"; literally "hand-servants," referring to the prophets and apostles who acted as God's secretaries</note> whom he led, as if by the hand, into the way of truth, and to whom the *Spirit of Truth* was always present during the writing—into whom not even a shadow of falsity or deception can fall.

The author argues that because God is the source of Scripture, the "true sense" of the text and "objective truth" are uniquely and perfectly linked, unlike in secular literature.</summary>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dual duties of a biblical interpreter: identifying the true meaning intended by the author and refuting false interpretations. The author is arguing that while Scripture is always true, not every "true" statement one can make about a passage is necessarily what the original author intended.</meta>

### EXERCISES

it is most certain that whatever is found written in them <note>referring to the Holy Scriptures mentioned on the previous page</note> contains nothing but the purest truth, and is entirely free from any association with falsehood or error; thus, the true meanings of the divine Utterances always agree with the truth, while false meanings, on the other hand, continually conflict with it. From this, it most evidently follows that all expositions of Scripture that oppose the truth also oppose its true meaning, and are therefore false, alien, spurious, and to be removed as far as possible from the mind of God. Therefore, whoever can detect and show that an interpretation of any passage of Holy Writ is not in harmony with the truth, but is discordant with it, will simultaneously find and prove that interpretation to be erroneous, spurious, and false, and far removed from the true sense. Thus, he will neither foist it upon others as true nor allow it to be foisted upon himself. In this way, he will be able to worthily and properly fulfill the second part of the Interpreter's duty, which we said consists in the detection and exposure of the false explanations of others.

6. But the matter is not so easily settled with the first part of the duty: namely, the investigation and demonstration of the true meanings. For although we have already placed it beyond any gamble of doubt that *every true sense of any passage of Holy Scripture is also a truth*; yet the converse of this statement does not seem to be of the same standing—namely, that *every truth is also a true sense.* For it could happen that in the same passage of Scripture, which admits several meanings, several truths are contained; yet the sacred Author of that passage had only one of them in mind, to the exclusion of the others, and intended to signify only that one while he was writing those words. Therefore, even if one, or several, or indeed all the truths of an ambiguous passage that could be contained in it are drawn out and demonstrated, it will still remain doubtful and uncertain whether the sacred Writer intended that one, or which of them (if only one is signified), or which ones (if several are signified—a possibility we hinted at above) to be understood. From this, it seems to follow that the Interpreter of Holy Writ whom we have described is more than sufficient for the second part of his task, but perhaps not in the same way for the first part—which, however, anyone will judge (and we ourselves freely acknowledge) to be the most important part.

7. But truly, even if the powers of our Interpreter did not rise higher than what we have said, nor extend beyond these set limits, it could by no means be denied that he far excels all others. He ought to be placed many parasangs <note>original: "parasangis"; an ancient Persian unit of distance, used here to mean "miles ahead" or "by a great distance"</note> ahead of others in these times, when it is not permitted to seek the true, genuine, and native sense of Scripture from the Prophets and Apostles themselves, nor can anyone prove beyond doubt that the light of the Holy Spirit shines upon them sufficiently to draw out that sense. For while others are unable to certainly detect and show the false interpretations of others, nor to investigate and demonstrate the truths of passages of Holy Scripture (let alone their true senses), our interpreter—if not eliciting the infallibly true senses—at least elicits the truths...

<meta>catchword: re, &</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while every true meaning of Scripture must be factually true, not every factual truth is the specific meaning intended by the biblical author. He concludes that a skilled interpreter is vital for at least ruling out false interpretations, which is a major advantage over those who lack a systematic method.</summary>

<keywords>biblical interpretation, hermeneutics, truth vs. sense, authorial intent, Holy Scripture, error detection, Prophets and Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the interpreter's duty. The previous page ended mid-sentence, suggesting that even if an interpreter cannot find the specific historical meaning of a passage, any truth they uncover that aligns with the wider Scripture is still valuable.</meta>

### PARADOXES CHAPTER IV.

[of Scriptu]re, and will be able to refute false interpretations, preventing us in that way from giving assent to erroneous explanations as if they were genuine. Thus, if we are blocked from finding the true intended senses, we are at least led toward truths (which is not forbidden); in this, there is certainly minimal danger, as Augustine teaches in *On Christian Doctrine* <note>original: "De Doctrin. Christian."</note>, book 3, chapter 27, in this manner:

> *When from the same words of Scripture not one thing alone, but two or more things are understood: even if it remains hidden what he who wrote them intended, there is no danger if any of them can be shown from other places of the Holy Scriptures to be in harmony with the truth,* etc.

And in *The City of God* <note>original: "De Civitate Dei"</note>, book 11, chapter 19:

> *Whether, when many things are discussed, that which the writer intended is reached, or whether that remains hidden: provided that, on the occasion of treating a profound obscurity, certain other true things are said.*

8. But come, let us see whether we can show that these limits placed upon our Interpreter can be broken through, and that he can worthily and properly satisfy both the former and the latter parts of his duty. We will easily accomplish this if only we prove that all truths of any passage that occur or can occur to the reader are also **true senses**. For then, he who has brought forth only one truth will also have brought forth a true sense; and the more truths one brings forth, the more senses; and the more excellent the interpreter will be. Furthermore, so that it may be clear that all truths of Scripture are also true senses, two things especially regarding God, the author of the Sacred Books, come to be considered. 

The first is that He is **All-Knowing** <term>Omniscient; from the Latin 'omnis' (all) and 'scire' (to know)</term>, and therefore knew perfectly all the meanings of the words delivered by Him—both the true and the false. He not only foreknew that those truths could occur to hearers and readers, but also foresaw that they *would* occur; and, since nothing happens outside His providence, He provided that they would occur as truths; as Augustine writes in book 3 of *On Christian Doctrine*, chapter 27, in these words:

> *For that Author [God] saw in those same words which we wish to understand that very meaning; and certainly the Spirit of God, who worked these things through him [the human author], without doubt foresaw that this very meaning would occur to the reader or hearer—nay, He provided that it should occur, because it also is supported by the truth.*

The second point is that He is **Truthful**, and thus can neither deceive nor be deceived. Therefore, He never intended—through His ministers—to utter anything aloud or have it recorded in writing for the sake of deceiving (that is, leading people into a sense other than what He wished to signify with the words). Nor did He intend to leave readers or hearers doubtful as to which of the several occurring meanings He intended. Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610), a prominent Reformed theologian</note> seems to wish the same in his *Synthesis of Theology* <note>original: "Syntagm: Theolog."</note>, book 1, chapter 45, question 2, when he says:

> *No word, and therefore no statement uttered by God, is ambiguous from His perspective, or as far as it pertains to Him. For God does not speak ambiguously, as if wishing by design to make men uncertain and doubtful, or as if wishing the sense of His word to be doubtful and uncertain. The Holy Spirit never once falls into the vice of ambiguity. He who [formed] the mouth and in-* <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword suggests "intelligence" or "understanding" follows.</note>

# EXERCISES

...[He who] gave understanding to man, neither wishes nor is able to fall into such a state. Nor do I believe anyone is so **brazen-faced** <note>original: "perfrictæ frontis," literally "of a rubbed forehead," an idiom for shamelessness</note> or of such an impious mind that they would dare to ascribe such an enormous crime to **God the Greatest and Best** <note>original: "Deo Optimo Maximo," a title often abbreviated as D.O.M.</note>, the most perfect Being—upon whom not even a shadow of falsehood or deceit can fall. For we blame men when they knowingly and willingly compose their words, and further use nods, gestures, and other bodily movements to strain every nerve of a deceitful mind, so that readers or listeners fall into a meaning not intended by the speaker, or into several meanings where it is doubted which one they wanted. 

Therefore, since such a great imperfection must be removed as far as possible from Him, and since a reader or listener encounters no truths in Holy Scripture that the Spirit of God did not foresee—indeed, provided for—and since one can encounter all of them, it follows that all those truths were intended and signified in that place by God himself; and thus they are also true senses. This same point is also proven by that common **Axiom** among Philosophers, *it is done in vain by more which can be done by fewer* <note>A principle of parsimony, often called Ockham’s Razor</note>; from which another is deduced, which is admitted by everyone: namely, that he speaks and writes better who says many things in few words than he who says few things in many words. Thus, he who teaches two truths in one simple speech writes better than he who teaches only one to the exclusion of the other. It is, therefore, the best and most excellent thing if someone signifies all the truths they can encompass in a single statement. Since, as they say, God and nature do nothing in vain, and all confess with one voice that the Holy Spirit wrote in the best possible way, no one can deny or cast doubt that He maintained this habit in writing the oracles of God.

9. And this opinion of ours is also supported by the authorities of many most learned men of every age. The first of these, to begin with the most ancient, are the **Jewish Rabbis**, who, to explain this idea, compare the word of God to glowing iron. For just as iron, heated by fire and continuous flames, if placed on an anvil and struck with a hammer, scatters and throws out sparks and glowing slag in every direction: so also they say, all senses agreeing with the truth, which are drawn and extracted from the sacred Letters by interpretation, are like sparks and torches with which God wished to free the souls of men from the mists of ignorance and the darkness of lack of knowledge, and to illuminate their minds with the light of saving wisdom and cognition. And they confirm this opinion of theirs with **Psalm 62**, where verse 12 says, *Once God spoke, twice I heard the same*, which is the strength of God. That is, as they interpret it, God produced one simple speech, but one that contained a manifold sense; for I understood the same thing "twice," that is, in many ways (using a **certain number for an uncertain one** <note>A rhetorical device where a specific number represents an indefinite plurality</note>), by drawing more senses from it. In this is situated the strength of God: that He can so compose the words of His speech that by one simple utterance, He teaches the listener or reader many truths...

# PARADOXES CHAPTER IV.

truths. In this area, Augustine seems to follow the Rabbis. His most illustrious passage is from the *Confessions*, book 12, chapter 31, which reads as follows: *“Thus, when one person says, ‘He <note>Referring to Moses as the author of Genesis</note> meant what I think,’ and another says, ‘No, he meant what I think,’ I consider it more pious to say: why not both rather, if both are true? And if anyone sees a third truth, or a fourth, or any other truth at all in these words, why should it not be believed that he <note>Moses</note> saw them all, through whom the one God has tempered the Holy Scriptures to the senses of many who would see truths that are both real and diverse? I certainly declare intrepidly from my heart: if I were writing something to reach the summit of authority, I would prefer to write in such a way that my words would echo whatever truth anyone could grasp concerning these matters, rather than setting down one true meaning so clearly as to exclude the others, whose falsity could not offend me.”* Therefore, my God, I do not wish to be so headlong as to not believe that this man <note>Moses</note> deserved this from You. I have said too little for the merit of the volume: all praise is inferior; in individual words multiple layers of understanding lie hidden.”* And on the 3rd chapter to the Ephesians: *“Individual speeches, syllables, strokes, and points in the divine Scriptures are full of meanings.”* Thus Basil, in chapter 1 of his work *On the Holy Spirit*, teaches that the very tips of the letters <note>original: "apices," the small marks or serifs</note>, the letters themselves, and the syllables should be explained by theological men, not to mention the words and sentences. And finally, Chrysostom in his 21st Homily on Genesis: *“I,” he says, “beseech you all not to pass over simply those things contained in the sacred Scriptures. # OF THE DISQUISITION

...gave understanding to man, He neither *wants* nor is able to fall into such a state. Nor do I believe anyone is of such a shameless <note>original: "perfrictæ frontis," literally "a scrubbed forehead," a Roman idiom for someone incapable of blushing</note> and impious mind that—while it is considered a vice in men to knowingly and willingly compose their words, and furthermore to strain every nerve of a deceitful soul through nods, gestures, and other bodily movements, so that readers or listeners fall into a meaning they did not intend, or into several meanings where it is doubted which one they wanted—they would dare to attribute such an enormous crime to God the Best and Greatest, the most perfect Being, upon whom not even a shadow of falsehood or deception can fall.

Therefore, since such a great imperfection must be removed far from Him, and since a reader or listener encounters no truths in Holy Scripture which the Spirit of God did not foresee—indeed, which He provided—and since he can encounter all of them, it follows that all those truths were intended and signified by Him in that place; and thus, they are also true senses.

This same point is also proven from that well-worn axiom among the Philosophers: *it is done in vain through many things, what can be done through fewer* <note>original: "frustrà fieri per plura, quod potest per pauciora," a version of the principle of parsimony or Occam’s Razor</note>. From this is deduced another point, which is admitted by everyone: namely, that he speaks and writes better who says many things in few words, rather than he who says few things in many words. And so, he who teaches two truths in a single and simple speech is better than he who teaches only one, to the exclusion of the other. It is, therefore, best and most excellent if one signifies all the truths one can grasp in a single [utterance]. Since, as they say, God and nature do nothing in vain, and since all confess with one voice that the Holy Spirit wrote in the best possible way, no one can deny or call into doubt that He maintained this custom in writing the oracles of God.

9. And this opinion of ours is also supported by the authorities of many most learned men of every age. The first of these, to begin with the most ancient, are the Jewish Rabbis, who, to explain this, liken the word of God to glowing iron. For just as iron, heated by fire and continuous flames, if placed on an anvil and struck by a hammer, scatters and throws off sparks and glowing dross in every direction: so also they say that all senses consistent with truth, which are drawn and extracted from the Holy Scriptures by interpretation, are like sparks and torches. By these, God wished to free the souls of men from the mists of lack of skill and the darkness of ignorance, and to illustrate and illuminate their minds with the light of saving wisdom and knowledge. 

And they strengthen this opinion of theirs with Psalm 62, where in verse 12 <note>Verse 11 in many English Bibles</note> it is said: *God has spoken once, twice I have heard the same, that strength belongs to God* <note>original: "Semel Deus locutus est, bis idem audivi, quod Dei robur est"</note>. That is, as they interpret it, God brought forth one simple speech, but one that contained a manifold sense; for I understood the same in two ways—that is, in many ways (a definite number being used for an indefinite one)—by eliciting multiple senses from it. In this is situated the "strength of God": namely, that He can so compose the words of His speech that through one simple [utterance], He teaches the listener or reader many truths...

...truths. Augustine seems to follow the Rabbis in this regard. His most famous passage is in the *Confessions*, book 12, chapter 31, which reads as follows:

> *Thus, when one person says, "He meant what I do," and another says, "No, he meant what I do," I think it more pious to say: why not both instead, if both are true? > *Indeed, I would wish that if I had been Moses then—for we all come from the same lump of clay, and what is man except that You are mindful of him?—I would wish, I say, if I were what he was then, and the task of writing the book of Genesis were laid upon me by You, that such a faculty of speaking be given to me, and such a way of weaving my discourse, that those who cannot yet understand how God creates would not reject the words as exceeding their strength; and those who already can understand would find that whatever true opinion they reached by thinking was not passed over, but was to be found in the few words of Your servant; and if another person saw another truth in the light of truth, that very thing would not be failing to be understood in those same words.*

A similar sentiment occurs in *On Christian Doctrine*, book 3, chapter 27:

> *What could be provided more generously and abundantly in the Divine Words <note>original: "Divinis Eloquiis"</note> than that the same words be understood in several ways, which other no less divine testimonies may cause to be approved? And from this comes his own Rule: Ambiguous things in sacred Scripture, if they cannot be explained <note>i.e., narrowed down to one definitive meaning</note>, can be understood in both ways, provided the rule of faith does not stand in the way.*

> *The Revelation of John has as many mysteries <note>original: "Sacramenta," here meaning sacred secrets or symbols</note> as it has words. I have said too little for the merit of the volume: all praise is inferior; in individual words, multiple layers of understanding lie hidden.*

And on the 3rd chapter of the Letter to the Ephesians:

> *Individual words, syllables, <term>apices</term> <note>the small strokes, flourishes, or "tittles" used in writing</note>, and points in divine Scripture are full of meanings.*

> *I beseech you all not to simply pass over those things contained in sacred Scripture. For nothing is written here that does not have many riches of meaning.*

<header>OF THE EXERCISE</header>

The Prophets spoke having been inspired by the Spirit; therefore, because they were written by the Spirit, they contain within themselves a vast *treasure*. And do not wonder that, in this list of names, I promise that I will show you many hidden riches of meaning. For there is not even a syllable, or a tiny stroke of a pen <note>original: *apiculus*, referring to the smallest mark in a letter, similar to the "jot or tittle" mentioned in the Gospels</note> in the Holy Scriptures, in whose depth there is not some great treasure. St. Thomas Aquinas asserts the same in several places, such as in the *Summary of Theology* <note>original: *Summa Theologiæ*</note>, part 1, question 1, article 10, and in the second book of the *Sentences*, Distinction 12, article 2, response 7, and elsewhere. 

But he speaks most eloquently of all in the *Questions on the Power of God* <note>original: *Quæstionibus de Potentiâ Dei*</note>, which are found in the eighth volume of his works. There, in question 4, article 1, he says: *One thing is (to be avoided): that no one should wish to force the Scripture toward a single sense in such a way that other senses—which contain truth and can be adapted to the Scripture while preserving the literal context—are excluded. For it belongs to the dignity of divine Scripture that it contains many senses under one literal text, so that it may thus suit the diverse understandings of men, and that each person might marvel that he can find in divine Scripture the truth which he has conceived in his own mind. And through this, it may also be more easily defended against unbelievers <note>original: *infideles*</note>; for if something that someone wishes to understand from Holy Scripture should appear to be false, recourse can be had to another of its senses. Hence it is not incredible that it was divinely granted to Moses and the other authors of Scripture that they themselves should know the various truths that men might understand, and designate them under a single sequence of text, so that each of them would be the author’s meaning. Thus, even if certain truths are adapted to the text by expositors of Holy Scripture which the Author did not understand <note>Referring here to the human author's conscious mind</note>, there is no doubt that the Holy Spirit understood them, who is the principal author of divine Scripture. Therefore, every truth that can be adapted to divine Scripture while preserving the literal context is its meaning.* 

Not far from these ideas is the "Caution" which the most famous and learned Philosopher as well as Theologian, and most worthy Professor of both faculties at the University of Duisburg, Johann Clauberg, teaches should be observed in interpreting the writings of others in his *Old and New Logic*, part 3, chapter 7, question 43: *Do not easily restrict the words of any speech if the profound wisdom of the Author allows for them a broader significance, and other laws of interpretation do not forbid it. This rule,* he continues, *has a place especially in explaining the Oracles of God; for because He is supremely wise, supremely good, and powerful, His words and works must be thought of in the mind as majestic and broad rather than narrow and limited.*

10. Finally, add to all of this that the conditions which Theologians require in a supreme judge of controversies also apply to this Interpreter of ours; these conditions can be adapted to this business not inappropriately. These are listed as three by some, and four by others. The first is that he cannot err, and that this must be clearly evident. For since all Dogmas of Theology must be of undoubted truth, and these can only be built from interpretations; and since, moreover, no [interpretation], which [is contrary] to the sacred Text, which is infallibly true,

<header>PARADOXES CH. V.</header>
<meta>catchword: H. Scrip-</meta>

is established, and if it does not fail to correspond to the truth, it becomes genuine; it is clear that such a thing is entirely free from all error, and its Author must show it to be so. The **second condition** is that no appeal can be made from it; that is, its interpretation is not to be examined against something else. For an "appeal" <note>original: *provocatio*, a legal term for referring a case to a higher judge</note> properly belongs to a Judge. The **third** is that it must not be biased, leaning more toward one side than the other. The **fourth** and final condition is that it must be able to compel assent, or as others say, to imbue men with certain knowledge. These three conditions are founded upon the first and are derived from it. For since the first condition requires that a true interpreter be immune from all error entirely, and that this must be clearly perceived; once perceived and known, the truth of that exposition will also be established. Therefore, it need not be measured against anything else—for what more do we seek than to know the true meaning with certainty?—nor can it be led by partisan zeal. Instead, it alone can wring assent from minds and instill infallible knowledge. And we believe it is manifest to anyone willing to apply and adapt them that all four of these conditions belong to the interpreter we have designated; hence, we consider it superfluous to pursue this at greater length.

But indeed, with what faculties must one be endowed, with what aids assisted, and with what rule and method instructed, so that he might truly explain Holy Scripture himself, defend it against the false interpretations of others, and thus be sufficient for the duty of a genuine interpreter? <meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter V. Having defined the four conditions of a true interpreter on the previous page, the author now challenges the conventional views of both Catholics and Protestants to propose his own "paradoxical" thesis: that true Philosophy is the ultimate tool for biblical interpretation.</meta>

# EXERCISES

...Holy Scripture is the unique and best rule, and the infallible norm, according to which every interpretation must be directed. However, while we reject both this view and the other <note>referring to the Catholic and Protestant positions discussed previously</note> for reasons to be reviewed below, it is our intention for the present to establish a new and **paradoxical** <note>In the 17th-century sense: an opinion that goes against common belief, not necessarily a logical contradiction.</note> position. Namely, that this duty belongs to **true Philosophy**, and that this is the certain and least fallible norm, both for explaining the sacred Books and for testing the explanations given of them. And in this sense, we wish the Title of our Exercise to be understood; just as Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> theologians claim that Scripture is both its own interpreter and the judge of theological controversies—that is, the norm and rule for interpreting and judging.

2. Regarding the **Philosophy** of which we speak here (to begin by sampling the subject of our controversy, for we believe we have already sufficiently examined the premise), by the **name of Philosophy**, I say, we do not understand the opinions and conjectures about the *nature of things* published under that name by the Divine Plato, the great Aristotle, or any others. Rather, we mean that true and indubitably certain knowledge which **Reason**—free from every covering of prejudice, supported by the natural light and sharpness of the intellect, and cultivated by study, diligence, practice, experiments, and the use of things—draws out from unshakable and self-evident principles through legitimate consequences and apodictic <note>demonstrably certain</note> demonstrations, clearly and distinctly perceived, and places in the most certain light of truth.

3. Moreover, it is known to all that this Philosophy owes its origin to God, the Greatest and Best, the Father of lights and the fountain of wisdom. For *every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights, with whom there is no change or shadow of turning*, as Saint James has it in his Epistle, chapter 1, verse 17. And *God gives wisdom to the wise, and knowledge to those who know and understand.* Daniel 2:21. And *the Lord gives wisdom; from his mouth come knowledge and understanding.* Proverbs 2:6. The same is also easily gathered from 2 Chronicles chapter 1, verse 12. And *all wisdom is from the Lord, and is with him forever.* Ecclesiasticus 1:1. Nor are obscure testimonies of this matter found only among sacred writers, but also among profane and indeed Heathen writers. Thus Plato says in the *Timaeus* that *no gift more excellent than Philosophy has ever been, or will hereafter be, granted by God to mortals.* And Lucretius, addressing Memmius concerning Wisdom, begins his fifth book *On the Nature of Things* in this manner:

> Who has the power to build with a mighty heart a song
> Worthy of the majesty of things and these discoveries?
> Or who is so strong in words that he can paint praises
> Matching the merits of him who left such prizes to us,
> Conceived in his own heart and sought by his own effort?

## PARADOXES: CHAPTER IV.

<note>original: "Nemo, ut opinor, erit mortali semine cretus..." From Lucretius, *De Rerum Natura* (On the Nature of Things), Book 5. The "God" referred to here by Lucretius is Epicurus.</note>

Similarly, **Cicero**, the Parent of Roman eloquence, says in the first book of his *Tusculan Disputations* that **Philosophy is a gift and invention of the Gods.** And all Christian Philosophers support these heathens. **Clement of Alexandria**, in the first book of his *Stromata* <note>A 2nd-century work titled "Miscellanies," which argued that Greek philosophy prepared the way for the Gospel.</note>, says: *God is the cause of all good things; but of some principally, such as the Old and New Testament; and of others by consequence, such as Philosophy.* **Melanchthon**, in his commentary on Paul's Epistle to the Colossians, chapter 2, verse 8, writes: *It is manifest that there are true natural notions, implanted in the minds of men at creation; these are norms of truth and certainty, such as those regarding numbers, order, figures, natural laws concerning Morals, and physical principles, from which many arts are built. Indeed, Paul calls natural laws the truth of God, and says that the Law is written on men's hearts.* And it is manifest that counting, writing, reading, architecture, and the medical art are approved by God; and of the Architect <note>Bezalel, the builder of the Tabernacle.</note> it is said in **Exodus 31**: *God filled him with this wisdom.* And God both instituted and revealed the learned reckoning of the year, and God commands us to count. The *History of the Apostles* [Acts] shows that languages are a gift of the Holy Spirit, and of the Medical Art it is said in **Sirach 38**: *God gave men knowledge, so that he might be glorified in his wonders.*

Let us recognize, therefore, that the arts constructed from the notions implanted in the minds of men at creation are good things and gifts of God; and indeed let us know that they are reminders and testimonies of God, because they show that God is wise. Indeed, the law of nature testifies that God exists and teaches what he is like, etc. On this, see the **Conimbricenses** <term>The "Conimbricenses" were Jesuit professors at the University of Coimbra in Portugal, famous for their comprehensive commentaries on Aristotle in the late 16th century.</term> in their introduction to *Logic*; Masius in his introduction to *Physics*, section 1, question 6; Keckermann in his *Introduction to Philosophy*, book 1, chapter 3; Alsted in his *Encyclopedia*, book 3, chapter 2, and his *Introduction to Philosophy*, part 1, chapter 13; Timpler in his *Philosophical Exercises*, section 2, question 4; Magirus in his *Prolegomena to Aristotle's Ethics*; Casmann in his *Christian Philosophy*, chapter 4; and others in no small number.

4. But truly, in this matter—namely, that God is the first and principal cause of Philosophy—there does not occur as much difficulty as there is regarding the **light of nature**, or human **reason**. This is established by some as an "instrumental" cause, and by others as a "remote" and "less principal" or "secondary" cause. For some of today's theologians make reason so meager, so thin and small, that it is completely blind in perceiving and knowing the essences and natures of things; they claim that in place of the marrow and the kernels, it gathers nothing but husks and shells, and so...

->### EXERCISES<-

...on a dry diet, with which the hunger for solid knowledge can by no means be satisfied, they must feed themselves. Indeed, some have gone so far as to not blush when asserting with a full mouth that philosophy is entirely nothing. But how erroneous this opinion is, how far it wanders from the truth, and how much it ought to be rejected is shown by the entire "Choir" of the most noble Mathematical disciplines. These disciplines—as is well known to those who study them—penetrate into the very essences of the things they treat, and into the properties necessarily flowing from them, demonstrating them apodictically <note>original: "apodicticè." In logic, this refers to a demonstration that is absolutely certain and necessary, leaving no room for doubt.</note>.

That the same can be achieved in Metaphysics as in Physics is proven by that most illustrious and brilliant new star which has risen in this our age for the Philosophical World: the most noble **René Descartes**. His writings are most worthy of cedar <note>original: "cedrô digniſſimis." A Latin idiom meaning "worthy of immortality," referring to the ancient practice of using cedar oil to preserve important scrolls from decay.</note>. Indeed, those for whom such clear and open truths cannot illuminate the eyes of the mind have need of hellebore <note>A plant used in antiquity as a medical treatment for madness or to clear "sticky humors" from the brain.</note>. This would purge the sticky humors of stubborn prejudices and preconceptions with which their brains are everywhere flooded and besieged, and dispel the thick vapors of preoccupation that cast clouds over their intellect.

For this incomparable man shows by his own example what Reason (or the human mind) can do in the investigation of things when it is freed from the impediments and obstacles of understanding, and equipped with its own strengths and faculties. This is possible if the mind assumes nothing unless it is **clearly and distinctly perceived**, and if it proceeds step-by-step from the simplest and best-known things to the complex and more unknown, according to the true *Method* for directing the intellect.

And truly, if the "light of nature" or reason were as meager or non-existent as those people claim, what, I ask, would make us human and distinguish us from irrational animals? But I seem to observe here the same thing that usually happens in all matters, which has even become a proverb: **Art has no enemy or scorner except the ignorant.** 

For the less I find any of these Theologians to be a Philosopher, and the less they use natural reason, the more I find them to be a despiser of Philosophy and reason. On the contrary, many excellent and outstanding Theologians—who are also Philosophers—continually celebrate human reason and its offspring, Philosophy, with the highest praise, raising them to the heavens with the most ample tributes. However much some Theologians may belittle and disparage reason, it has long been demonstrated how many things reason perceives clearly and distinctly. In asserting those things which it has perceived clearly and distinctly, reason cannot err, but must attain the truth. And since God (as they themselves admit) is the source and origin of all truth, He will also be the author and preserver of all true Philosophy or Wisdom.

5. And since every clear and distinct perception has certainty joined to itself alongside Truth, whatever this true Philosophy has taught...

### CHAPTER V. OF PARADOXES.

...osophy or <term>wisdom</term> <note>original: "sapientia." In this context, it refers to the highest form of knowledge that combines intellect and virtue.</note>, it will be firm and established, nor can it be called into doubt by any arguments; so much so that the soul eager for truth will be able, and indeed ought to, rest plainly and fully in its persuasion, as if it were beyond all exception, nor should any further certainty be sought.

6. Furthermore, since there is also no clear and distinct perception that does not produce an intimate <term>consciousness</term> <note>original: "conscientia." While this can mean "conscience" in a moral sense, here it refers to the internal awareness of one's own thoughts.</note> of itself within us; and since, as has been said, God is the cause of every clear and distinct perception, He will also be the cause of this intimate consciousness. And because this consciousness undoubtedly persuades, dictates, testifies, and inspires us that the thing perceived is true, it can—not undeservedly or inappropriately—be called the persuasion, dictate, testimony, and inspiration of God or of the **Holy Spirit**. 

From this it is clear how far off the mark they were, and with what empty labor those Theologians tortured themselves, who, considering <term>Reason</term> or the <term>natural light</term> <note>original: "lumen naturale." A philosophical term for the innate human capacity to discover truth without divine revelation.</note> to be so small or even non-existent, sought something else to be designated by those names. They substituted a different concept in its place and called it "supernatural light," though they themselves do not know how to explain clearly what that actually is, as will be taught at length below in Chapter 14. 

This opinion of theirs seems to me to have originated from the fact that they held as indubitable that <term>Aristotelian dogma</term>: <note>original: "Nihil esse in intellectu, quod non antea fuerit in sensu."</note> *Nothing is in the intellect that was not previously in the senses.* Because they never called the mind away from the senses, they noticed that the knowledge drawn from them was quite mutilated, imperfect, or even non-existent. Assuming that natural reason was nothing more than this sensory data, they declared it to be meager or null. However, because these same people occasionally used the <term>pure intellect</term> by chance when perceiving and knowing certain things, they tasted a ray of true and solid knowledge. Finding this to be of a completely different quality than the knowledge drawn from the senses, they distinguished it far from the former. And since they had firmly persuaded themselves that sensory knowledge was the only thing belonging to their own nature, they thought this higher knowledge must be a divine thing of a higher nature, and so they named it "supernatural light." 

Certainly, whatever the case may be, we think it is clear enough that what we have said can be conveniently understood under these names. Therefore, we will accept these terms as such, until those men can prove that something else—specifically what they claim—is denoted by them; provided it is not a pure and simple fiction, of which you can find neither hide nor hair <note>original: "nec vola nec vestigium." A Latin idiom meaning "neither a palm-print nor a footprint," i.e., no trace at all.</note> in the nature of things outside of their own brains.

7. From what has been said before, it can also easily be gathered how futile is the opinion of those who wish for the nature of <term>grace</term> to be opposed to science, <term>revelation</term> to be opposed to knowledge, and ordinary truth to be opposed to extraordinarily revealed truth—not only regarding their supposed certainty, but as opposites. In reality, both must be held in one and the same place and order. For **Truth** cannot be contrary to itself, nor can it excel itself, even if it is produced and acquired in a different way.

We shall clarify the matter with an example. If it has been revealed to one person through the extraordinary light of grace that God is **all-knowing**; but to another the same truth is <term>apodictically</term> <note>original: "apodicticè." This refers to a logical demonstration that is absolutely certain and necessary, such as a mathematical proof.</note> demonstrated by the **light of nature**: both will perceive the same truth clearly, distinctly, certainly, and infallibly, since it is the same proposition. This truth will neither contradict itself, nor will it produce a greater certainty in one person than in the other, since neither can doubt it in any way. See also some points regarding this in Chapter 8.

## CHAPTER VI.

### *Philosophy is shown to be the Infallible Norm for interpreting Holy Scripture, by 1. Arguments, 2. The continuous practice of Theologians, and 3. Examples.*

<margin>1.</margin>
Thus far we have explained the Subject and Predicate of the controversy and have laid the foundations upon which we shall now build the entire structure of our demonstration. This shall be a simple task; for the facts suggest themselves naturally to anyone who recalls what has been said and pays close attention. For we demonstrated in Chapter 4 that all the truths in the passages of Scripture are also their true meanings, and that all interpretations of them which do not agree with the truth are false. Consequently, a true interpreter of the Holy Scriptures is one who can draw out the truths of the statements contained within them and show that they have been drawn out, while also detecting and demonstrating that interpretations of those same passages prepared by others are not in harmony with the truth. 

Since, therefore, as we taught in the preceding chapter, **Philosophy** is the true, certain, and indubitable knowledge of Things, deduced from principles known by the light of nature and <term>apodictically</term> demonstrated: it follows that the truths of Scriptural passages can be certainly drawn out by this knowledge, and demonstrated once they are drawn out. Furthermore, explanations made by others can be explored to see whether they agree with the truth or conflict with it, and can be shown beyond doubt to be of one character or the other. From this it follows most evidently that Philosophy is a certain and infallible <term>Norm</term> <note>original: "Norma." A standard, rule, or measuring stick used to judge the correctness of something.</note> both for interpreting the Holy Scriptures and for testing interpretations. Moreover, the person who is equipped with this knowledge, and upon whom its true and sincere light has shone, is able to fulfill all the roles of an interpreter of the divine word most rightly and worthily. 

By this, we certainly do not mean that someone who has only "greeted Philosophy at the doorstep" <note>original: "à limine solùm salutavit." A Latin idiom for having only a superficial or introductory acquaintance with a subject.</note> and has tasted it only with the "tips of their lips," and who is thus only slightly or moderately stained with the knowledge of the liberal arts and disciplines, could penetrate, understand, and explain everything that occurs in the Holy Scriptures—even the more difficult and abstruse matters. Rather, we mean only those things of which they have already [acquired] philosophical...

# CHAPTER VI. OF THE PARADOXES.

<note>original: "phandô" — this is likely a fragment of "explicandô" (explaining) or a similar gerund continuing from the previous page.</note> compared it, and indeed found more and higher things the more frequently and deeply he descended into the secrets of Nature and its innermost recesses by the light and sharpness of his intellect, and the greater and more inward progress he made in the knowledge of things. It is common knowledge among everyone that the same thing happened to all theologians, as many as have applied their minds to unraveling the sacred Books from the foundations of Christianity down to these times. For not everyone was endowed with the same measure of the faculty of interpreting; rather, the more each person excelled in genius, learning, and knowledge, the more worthily he sustained the role of an Interpreter of Holy Scripture, and the more correctly and better he untied many of the more difficult passages. Hence, it will suffice to accept our argument with this limitation (for it is not necessary to go any further).

Next, it is also clear that every interpretation adorned with the help of Philosophy, or explored and approved by its scrutiny, is true and genuine. This is evident because none of the requirements listed by the theologians at the end of Chapter 3, which are desired in a Rule of interpretation, fail to correspond perfectly to Philosophy. For since Philosophy is true, it is free from all danger of error; and since it cannot deceive, there will be absolutely no need to examine an interpretation tested by this standard against any other standard. Furthermore, since it rests upon immovable foundations that do not know how to bend, it will not be led by <term>partisanship</term> <note>original: "partium studiô." This refers to bias or favoring one religious sect over another.</note>. And finally, since it is certain and indubitable, it can force assent from the minds of men and provide firm knowledge.

Moreover, there is an old and true saying, frequently used by theologians when they dispute concerning the Interpreter of the Scriptures: *“Everyone is the best interpreter of their own words.”* They also attribute this to God, and indeed with the best right, since it fits Him better than any mortal. For men are both liars and deceivers, and are very often carried away by passions, or, looking out for their own utility and advantages, they utter words in one sense and soon explain them in another. But He, upon whom not even a shadow of a lie or deceit falls, is and remains always one and the same, both in speaking and in interpreting. Therefore, since—as we said in the preceding chapter—God is the author of Philosophy, every interpretation brought forward under its guidance and approval must also be ascribed to Him. And consequently, the name of a true Rule of interpretation can be denied to it for no reason.

<margin>2.</margin>

For most of the ancient Fathers, such as **Justin Martyr**, **Dionysius the Areopagite**, **Origen**, and others, being devoted to **Platonic philosophy**, used no other rule than that whenever a knot in the Holy Scriptures presented itself to be untied. The rest, who were almost all <term>Eclectics</term> <note>The Eclectics were philosophers or theologians who did not stick to one single school of thought but picked the best elements from various systems (Platonism, Stoicism, etc.).</note>, in the more difficult passages of the Scriptures,

# OF THE EXERCISE

...and they applied their own dogmas, drawn from the sects of all the philosophers, to unraveling the more obscure points. After these, with a long period of Barbarism <note>The author likely refers to the Middle Ages here, a common rhetorical stance in the 17th century.</note> intervening, there finally arose the Doctors known as the **Scholastics**. Swearing by the words of **Aristotle**, whenever a dispute arose concerning the meaning of the divine utterances, they brought nothing onto the stage that was not supported by **Peripatetic** <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> doctrines. They did this with such zeal and such momentum that **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German Benedictine abbot and polymath.</note>, speaking of these doctors, says: *secular philosophers have defiled Theology with their useless curiosity*; and **Antonius Sadeël** <note>Antoine de la Roche Chandieu (1534–1591), a Huguenot theologian.</note>, in his preface to the book *On the Written Word of God*, says: *those Scholastics, because of the confusion of Philosophy with Theology, should be called Philosophers rather than Theologians.* Subsequently, all those Christians who broke away from the Roman See followed in their footsteps, though not quite so closely; until finally, in this century, the light of **Cartesian Philosophy** <note>The philosophy of René Descartes, which emphasized reason and doubt as the starting points for knowledge.</note> shone upon certain Theologians. To these men, those interpretations of Holy Scripture that were not illuminated by the rays of this new light began to seem foul and worthless.

3. Finally, numerous examples of the more difficult texts of Holy Scripture, explained by the help of Philosophy, testify to and confirm this same point quite clearly. Indeed, they are so many that if I were to busy myself with bringing them all forward, this Exercise of ours would grow into a massive volume. Let it suffice to have brought forward one or two of the principal ones.

First, let us consider the subject of **God**. Speaking of Him as if of a man, the Holy Book attributes to Him human members—a right hand, eyes, ears, a heart, and so on; it assigns to Him mortal emotions—anger, repentance, love, etc.; and it ascribes to Him both the operations of the senses and various types of movement. Yet a theologian of sound mind will determine that none of these things apply to God. For that most perfect and infinite Being is not truly and properly determined or circumscribed by such a bodily mass as we carry about. Hence, He is neither agitated by the movements of the mind, nor does He use the service of the senses in perceiving things, nor does He employ the movement of limbs to transport Himself anywhere. For this reason, theologians also rightly warn, by unanimous consensus, that such things are said of God <term>in a human manner<note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποπαθῶς (anthropopathōs). The attribution of human feelings or characteristics to God.</note></term>, but must be understood and explained <term>in a manner worthy of God<note>original Greek: θεοπρεπῶς (theoprepōs). Interpreting divine descriptions in a way that fits God’s nature as a perfect, non-physical being.</note></term>.

But truly, who taught them these things? Who showed them what is <term>worthy of God<note>original Greek: θεοπρεπές (theoprepes).</note></term>? Not Scripture; for nowhere does it explain which things are attributed to God through <term>human passion<note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποπάθειαν (anthropopatheian).</note></term> and which are attributed properly; nor which things should be interpreted <term>in a manner worthy of God<note>original Greek: θεοπρεπῶς</note></term> and which simply as written. Therefore, it is **Nature**, and therefore **Philosophy**, which evidently demonstrates by the light of nature both that God exists and what must be ascribed to Him or denied of Him. But indeed, I seem to hear the theologians here objecting with great fervor and asserting that Holy Scripture does indeed speak <term>in a human manner</term> in certain places, but in many others it speaks <term>in a manner worthy of God</term>; and from these latter passages, they claim they can be sufficiently taught what is <term>worthy of God</term>, and thus they have no need of the assistance of philosophy in unraveling these points. But to me, [he] calmly respon- <meta>catchword: respon- (continues as "respondetur" or "respondeo" on the next page: "is responded" or "I respond")</meta>

# CHAPTER VI OF THE PARADOXES

...let them answer a questioner; for one would like to know from them how they have perceived—since certain passages seem to attribute a human body to God, while others take it away—that the latter speak literally <note>original: "propriè"</note> and are to be understood as such, while the former speak metaphorically <note>original: "impropriè"</note>. For if someone were to maintain the direct opposite and assert that all those passages in which they place <gloss>human-like passions</gloss> <note>original: "ἀνθρωποπάθειαν" (anthropopatheian); the attribution of human emotions or physical form to God.</note> should be understood literally, and conversely, those in which they recognize <gloss>divine fittingness</gloss> <note>original: "θεοпρέπειαν" (theoprepeian); attributes or descriptions that are suitable to the majesty of God.</note> should be understood metaphorically; and that in this way they become easier and clearer to explain; by what reasoning, I ask, would they convict this interpreter of error? By what arguments would they prove his explanation false? Surely by none except those sought from **Philosophy**, to which, as to a "sacred anchor" <note>An ancient metaphor for a final refuge or the last resort in an argument.</note>, just as their predecessors fled, so they too would have to flee, unless they wished to pour out words in vain and waste both their oil and their labor <note>A Latin idiom, *oleum et operam perdere*, meaning to waste time and resources on a fruitless task.</note>.

Let the second example be the **Sacrament of the Eucharist**, whose words of institution, *This is my body*, are twisted into various meanings by Christians. For the **Papists** explain it thus: *This is transubstantiated into the body of Christ*; the **Lutherans**, however, understand it in this way: *This bread is locally joined to the body of Christ*; finally, the **Reformed** <note>Referring to the Calvinist or Zwinglian tradition, which favored a symbolic interpretation.</note> interpret it so: *This bread is a sign of the body of Christ*. But among these three differing explanations, which is true? Which is false? Which corresponds to the sense that Christ had in mind while he uttered these words? **Philosophy** has taught this. For the Reformed, with its help, established their own position and showed the opinion of the Pontiffs and Lutherans to be absurd; they demonstrated from **Physics** that bread, while its **accidents** <term>accidents: In Aristotelian philosophy, these are the external qualities of a thing (like color, shape, or taste) that can change without changing the essential nature of the thing itself.</term> remain unchanged, cannot be transubstantiated into another body, nor can one and the same body be in several places at once, nor two bodies in one and the same place, along with other absurdities that necessarily follow from their opinions.

Let the last example to come upon the stage be the **Most Holy Trinity**, which among certain Theologians is called a "Mystery," but among others a "monster" filled with a jumble of all absurdities. To defend this against the most fierce attacks of the latter, and to keep it "under a sound roof" <note>A legal metaphor meaning "perfectly intact" or "well-protected."</note>—though unknown and not understood—what monsters of words, what trifles of distinctions have they not sought from the innermost shrines and sanctuaries of **Metaphysics**? Or if they could not find them, what have they not newly invented? How much better it would have been not to hold it as a Mystery, and with the help of **Philosophy**, before they decided *that* it was, to have investigated *what* it was according to the precepts of true **Logic**, alongside the renowned **Keckermann** <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (1572–1609), a German Reformed theologian and philosopher known for his systematic and logical approach to divinity.</note>; rather than retreating with such fervor and labor into the deepest caves and the darkest recesses of metaphysical speculations and fictions, just to place their opinion in safety from the arrows of their adversaries. Truly, that great man, thoroughly learned in all parts of **Aristotelian Philosophy**, in his *System of Theology*, book 1, chapter 3, [treats] that dogma, how-
