### CHAPTER VII.

1. *The duty of one who proves something by the authority of any writing.*
2. *What true wisdom is, and what is apparent wisdom.* 
3. *That **Philosophy** and true wisdom are not condemned in the **Scriptures** by **Paul** in certain passages occurring in the **1st Epistle to the Corinthians**, chapters 1, 2, and 3.* 
4. *Neither is it condemned in chapter 2, verse 14.* 
5. *Finally, nor in the **Epistle to the Colossians**, chapter 2, verse 8.*

It follows now that we examine and dissolve those objections that could be raised against our view. These are of two kinds: they are either sought from the **Holy Scriptures**, or they present themselves from outside of them. We shall respond to the latter in the following chapter, and to the former in this one which is now at hand. Many passages are found in the Word of God in which **Philosophy** or **Wisdom** (for these words, although they differ, nevertheless designate the same thing) seems to be condemned and rejected by the **Sacred Writers**, not only as useless to heavenly doctrine, but even as harmful. All of these, poorly understood and explained, could provide **Adversaries** a handle for attacking our opinion and exploding it as if it were contrary to the **Divine Oracles**. Lest they seize upon this too improvidently and precipitously, we have deemed it our duty to prevent it; and that this may be done conveniently, we wish to begin the matter from a slightly deeper point.

1. Whoever contends to firmly establish his opinion by the authority of any book, as is proper, must by no means be thought to have "hit the mark" <note>A metaphor for succeeding in an argument.</note> if he merely brings forward certain passages from it in which the same or similar words are found as those with which he expressed his own mind, or even those which seem to imply that which he himself has stated. Rather, it is additionally incumbent upon him to demonstrate that by those words, the author intended to imply and express the very thing he is striving to prove...
<meta>The text cuts off here; the catchword "luisse" is the end of the word "voluisse" (intended).</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the first section of the treatise, arguing that even theologians who dislike philosophy must use it to explain the Trinity. It then begins Chapter VII, which aims to refute the common argument that the Apostle Paul condemned philosophy in the New Testament.</summary>

The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how many passages from Scripture are used as objections.</meta>

->PARADOXES CHAPTER VII.<-

...intended by the author of that writing <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought that a reader must prove the original author's intent.</note>. For the dispute is not about words or mere probability, but about the matter itself and its unshaken certainty. For this reason, all those **Theologians** seem to me to be trifling—and, as the proverb goes, "plowing the shore with oxen that will achieve nothing" <note>original: "non profecturis littora bobus arare"; a classical idiom for wasted effort</note>—who, in order to show the truth of their own dogmas or to refute the falsehood of others, scrape together and heap up as many passages of **Holy Scripture** as possible in which the same words (or words not much different from their own opinion) occur. Meanwhile, they omit the most important thing: a **demonstration** <term>demonstratio: a logical proof that leads to a certain conclusion from necessary premises</term> which clearly teaches that the divine writers of those passages had the same thing in mind as what these theologians are contending. Therefore, they seem to have no right to deserve or demand any other refutation of their proofs, or solution to their objections, from their opponents than a bare and simple denial of their arguments. With this, they ought to admit they have been fully answered, unless they wish to show that they are far removed from all reason in disputing and are plainly ignorant of the genuine method of proof. Since this path has now been paved, if we were so inclined, we could by our own right decline a long-winded response here, simply by denying that any texts of the **Holy Books** oppose or contradict our assertion, since this has not yet been proved by any **Theologian**, nor can it ever be proved. But for the present, it pleases us to be more generous and to voluntarily prepare ourselves for a defense, showing that none of the passages commonly cited by **Theologians** from the **Word of God** either conflict with our opinion or weaken its proof.

2. However, so that we may perform this duty properly, it must first be considered that the term **Philosophy**, or **wisdom**, is ambiguous and is applied everywhere both to the true and the apparent. **True wisdom** (which was discussed in Chapter 5) is that which consists in a solid knowledge of **God** and created things, and in the tireless pursuit of the true good. On the other hand, **apparent wisdom** is that which peddles vain opinions and conjectures about **God** and created things—conjectures which the human mind has devised when blocked by prejudices drawn from the senses and from bad education and upbringing. Such wisdom is aided by one or two superficial observations and is built up only through plausible-sounding reasons that are perceived obscurely and confusedly; it finds it necessary to defend itself with empty distinctions <term>distinctiunculis: small, often hair-splitting logical divisions used in Scholastic debate</term> and the subtleties of cleverness. Furthermore, it follows and pursues with blind impulse and persistent labor not true goods, but apparent ones—those pertaining to the fleeting comforts of this life, such as honors, riches, pleasures, fame, and the like. And since this apparent wisdom chases after empty things, it requires a great apparatus of words and needs much grandiloquence of speech to deceive the eyes of the mind and entice people to itself. All these things **true wisdom** scorns, since naked truth longs to be seen; true wisdom rejects such things as useless and as future obstacles, and—refusing to be adorned—is content only to be taught.

-> E X E R C I S E S <-

3. With these things established, let us move on to those passages of Scripture in which either **Philosophy** or **Wisdom** appears to be condemned. Among these, we shall give the primary place to those found in the first **Epistle of Paul to the Corinthians**, chapters 1, 2, and 3—both because several similar points coincide here, and because once these are cleared up, the explanation of the remaining passages will follow naturally. At the beginning of this Epistle, the Apostle censures the **schisms** <note>schisms: divisions or factions within the early Christian community</note> that had arisen among the Corinthians. Explaining their cause and origin, he rebukes the vanity and ambition of certain Teachers who, under the name of **Wisdom** and eloquence, were seeking and striving for primacy in that Church. At the same time, he rebukes the folly of the listeners who, admiring such Teachers, were split into opposing parties and pursuits. To lead them back to the path of salvation, he discourages them from following those dizzying and schismatic men; to that end, he demonstrates at length the difference between the heavenly doctrine of salvation and worldly **Wisdom**, between the spiritual man and the **animal or carnal man** <term>animalis/carnalis: in Paul's usage, this refers to a "natural" person who relies solely on human reason and physical senses, lacking spiritual insight</term>, and between divine, simple eloquence and human, turgid eloquence. But let us see and examine the specific passages that might cause us trouble. In Chapter 1, verse 19, these words are found: *“I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the understanding of the prudent I will take away.”* <note>original: "Abolebo sapientiam sapientum, & intelligentiam intelligentium tollam è mediô."</note> and verse 20: *“Where is the wise man? where is the scribe? where is the seeker of this age? Has not God made foolish the wisdom of this world?”* <note>original: "Ubi sapiens? ubi scriba? ubi conquisitor seculi istius? nonne infatuavit Deus sapientiam mundi hujus?"</note> and Chapter 2, verse 6: *“We do not speak the wisdom of this age, nor of the princes of this age, who are being brought to nothing.”* <note>original: "Sapientiam non loquimur seculi hujus neque principum seculi hujus, qui abolentur."</note> and Chapter 3, verses 19, 20: *“The wisdom of this world is foolishness with God: For it is written, he catches the wise in their own craftiness. And again, The Lord knows the thoughts of the wise are vain.”* <note>original: "Sapientia mundi hujus stultitia est apud Deum... carpit sapientes cum versutiâ ipsorum... Dominus novit cogitationes sapientium esse vanas."</note> In these places, almost all interpreters cry out that **Philosophy** is understood by the terms *wisdom of the wise, understanding of the prudent, wisdom of this world, of this age, and of the princes of this age*, and that it is thereby condemned. But they are mistaken: for it is clear that the Apostle is not designating or attacking **true wisdom**, but only **apparent wisdom**. This is evident first from his purpose, which we have already stated: because the cause of schisms cannot be "true" wisdom, since it is true and pursues a true good. Furthermore, it is clearly demonstrated by what is said about that wisdom by the Apostle. For God will not destroy true wisdom, nor take it away, nor make it foolish, nor hold it as foolishness, since He is its author, and it teaches nothing but truth and goodness, which are eternal. Rather, he speaks of **apparent wisdom**, which, having proceeded from the human brain, catches at vain likelihoods and pursues empty and fleeting goods. Hence it is also called "human," "of this world," "of this age," and "of the princes of this age" by the Apostle—either in respect to its cause, insofar as it was invented and cultivated by the men of this world and age; or in respect to its object, insofar as it is concerned with the things of this world and age. And those who are endowed with this wisdom, *“God catches in their own craftiness, and knows their thoughts to be vain”*; things which can by no means apply to the possessors of true wisdom. For the excellent [gift] of God...

# PARADOXES, CHAPTER VII.

<note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of 1 Corinthians 1:19: "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise..."</note> [Wisdom] is a gift, whose sincere praise is so often proclaimed in the Holy Scriptures, and whose tireless study is so frequently commended; so much so that the Apostle James even expressly commands us to seek it from God through prayers in Chapter 1, verse 5, with these words: *If any of you lacks wisdom, let him ask of God, who gives generously to all without finding fault, and it will be given to him.* Our explanation of these passages is consistent with that of the most authoritative Doctor of the ancient Church, **Clement of Alexandria**, in the first book of his *Stromata* <note>original: "Stromateis," meaning "Miscellanies," a major work of early Christian philosophy</note>, when he says: *When Scripture calls them (the Greeks) wise, it does not rebuke those who are truly wise, but those who seem wise to themselves. Regarding these, however, it says: "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the prudence of the prudent I will abolish." Therefore the Apostle adds: "Where is the wise man? Where is the scribe? Where is the investigator of this age?" etc.* Similarly, in Book 5: *I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the intelligence of the intelligent I will reject—namely, those who seem wise to themselves and are contentious.* And again in Book 1: *The Lord alone knows the disputes of the wise, that they are vain.* Here, Scripture is calling those men **Sophists** <term>Sophists: ancient teachers of rhetoric often criticized for using clever but fallacious arguments to win debates rather than seeking truth</term> who occupy themselves with mere words and superfluous arts; and a little later: *Regarding these and similar people, who have exercised themselves in an empty style of speaking, the Divine Scripture most beautifully says, "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise," etc.*

The **Venerable Bede** also contributes to this point when, in his exposition of the 1st Epistle to the Corinthians, chapter 3 (which he compiled from the works of **St. Augustine**), he has these words: *The Lord knows the thoughts of men, that they are vain. Sometimes in the Scriptures, "Wisdom" is used for "shrewdness" by a misuse of the word, not reflecting the true nature of the thing. Hence it is also said: "Where is the wise man? Where is the scribe? Where is the investigator of this age? Has not God made the wisdom of this world foolish?"* And **Anselm** <note>Anselm of Canterbury, an influential 11th-century theologian</note> on 1st Corinthians, chapter 1, verse 19: *The Apostle insults the wisdom and shrewdness that has been proven false and rejected when he says: "Where is the wise man, where is the scribe, where is the investigator of this age?" It is as if he were saying, the shrewdness of such men is worth nothing. And it is an attack against the Jews and Gentiles, etc.* See also what he has in chapter 2.

**St. Thomas Aquinas** does not differ from all of these, commenting on the first chapter of this Epistle, verse 19, in this way: *It must be considered that men, however evil they may be, are not totally deprived of God's gifts, nor are the gifts of God rejected in them; rather, what is rejected and destroyed in them is that which proceeds from their own malice. Therefore, He does not simply say "I will destroy wisdom"—because all wisdom is from the Lord God—but "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise," that is, the wisdom which the wise of this world have invented for themselves against the true wisdom of God. For as it says in James 3: "This is not the wisdom descending from above, but is earthly, animal, and demonic."* <note>original: "animalis," meaning "of the natural soul" or unspiritual</note> *Similarly, He does not say "I will reject prudence," for the wisdom of God teaches true prudence; but He says "the prudence of the prudent," that is, that which those who judge themselves prudent in worldly matters consider to be prudence—namely, so that they may cling to the goods of this world. Or because, as it says in Romans 8: "The **prudence of the flesh** is death," etc.* <term>prudence of the flesh: (Latin: prudentia carnis) a mindset entirely focused on material survival and worldly desires, rather than spiritual truths</term>

**Thomas de Vio Cajetan** <note>a prominent 16th-century Catholic theologian and commentator on Aquinas</note> writes in the same sense on this passage with these words: *Significantly, He does not say "I will destroy wisdom and intelligence," but adds,*

# OF THE EXERCISE

3. With these premises established, let us proceed to those passages of Scripture in which either **Philosophy** or **Wisdom** seems to be condemned. Among these, we shall give the primary place to those which occur in Paul’s first Epistle to the Corinthians, chapters 1, 2, and 3—both because many similar points converge here, and because once these are cleared up, the explanation of the remaining passages will follow naturally. At the beginning of this Epistle, the Apostle censures the <term>schismata: divisions or factions</term> that had arisen among the Corinthians. Explaining their cause and origin, he rebukes the vanity and ambition of certain Teachers who, in the name of **Wisdom** and eloquence, were seeking and affecting a position of primacy in that Church. At the same time, he rebukes the foolishness of the listeners who, admiring such Teachers, were split into opposing parties and factions. To lead them back to the way of salvation, he discourages them from following those dizzying and schismatic men. To that end, he extensively demonstrates the difference between the heavenly doctrine of salvation and worldly **Wisdom**; between the spiritual man and the <term>animalem: the "soulish" or natural man, guided by earthly instincts rather than the Spirit</term> or carnal man; and between divine, simple eloquence and that which is human and turgid. But let us look at and examine the individual passages that might cause us trouble.

In Chapter 1, verse 19, we find these words: *I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the intelligence of the intelligent I will cast aside.* <note>original Latin: "Abolebo sapientiam sapientum, & intelligentiam intelligentum tollam è medio."</note> And in verse 20: *Where is the wise man? where is the scribe? where is the debater of this age? Has not God made foolish the wisdom of this world?* <note>original: "Ubi sapiens? ubi scriba? ubi conquisitor seculi istius? nonne infatuavit Deus sapientiam mundi hujus?"</note> And in Chapter 2, verse 6: *We do not speak the wisdom of this age, nor of the rulers of this age, who are coming to nothing.* <note>original: "Sapientiam non loquimur seculi hujus neque principum seculi hujus, qui abolentur."</note> And in Chapter 3, verses 19 and 20: *The wisdom of this world is foolishness with God. For it is written: He catches the wise in their own craftiness. And again: The Lord knows that the thoughts of the wise are vain.* <note>original: "Sapientia mundi hujus stultitia est apud Deum: Scriptum est enim, carpit sapientes cum versutiâ ipsorum. Et rursum, Dominus novit cogitationes sapientium esse vanas."</note>

In these passages, nearly all interpreters noisily assert that **Philosophy** is meant by the "wisdom of the wise," "intelligence of the intelligent," and the "wisdom of this world, of this age, and of the rulers of this age," and that it is therefore condemned. But they are mistaken. It is clear from the Apostle’s purpose, as already stated, that he is designating and criticizing not true wisdom, but apparent wisdom. True wisdom cannot be the cause of schisms, since it is true and pursues the true good. This is also clearly demonstrated by what the Apostle says about that wisdom. For God will not destroy, nor cast aside, nor make foolish, nor consider as folly the **true** wisdom, since He is its author, and it teaches nothing but the true and the good, which are eternal. Rather, he refers to **apparent** wisdom, which, proceeding from the human brain, catches at vain probabilities and pursues empty and fleeting goods. Hence, it is also called by the Apostle "human," "of this world," "of this age," and "of the rulers of this age"—either in respect to its cause, insofar as it was invented and cultivated by the men of this world and age; or in respect to its object, insofar as it is concerned with the things of this world and age. And regarding those who are endowed with this, *God catches them in their own craftiness, and knows that their thoughts are vain*—things which can in no way apply to those who possess true wisdom. For the [gift] of God is excellent...

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER VII.

[Wisdom] is a gift, whose sincere praise is so often proclaimed in the Holy Scriptures, and whose tireless pursuit is so often commended; so that even the Apostle James, in chapter 1, verse 5, expressly commands us to ask for it from God in these words: *If any of you lacks wisdom, let him ask of God, who gives generously to all and does not reproach, and it will be given to him.* And with this explanation of ours for these passages, that most weighty Doctor of the ancient Church, Clement of Alexandria, <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD) was a Christian theologian who sought to unite Greek philosophy with Christian doctrine.</note> agrees in Book 1 of his *Miscellanies* <note>original: "Stromat." referring to the *Stromata*, a work on Christian life and philosophy.</note> when he says: *In calling them (the Greeks) "wise," the Scripture does not rebuke those who are truly wise, but those who seem wise to themselves. Regarding these, it says: "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the prudence of the prudent I will abolish." Thus the Apostle concludes: "Where is the wise man? Where is the scribe? Where are the researchers of this age?" etc.* Similarly in Book 5: *"I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the understanding of the understanding I will reject"—namely, of those who seem wise to themselves and are contentious.* And again in Book 1: *The Lord alone knows the disputes of the wise, that they are vain; for the Scripture calls "wise" those who are <term>Sophists: philosophers who used clever but fallacious arguments for show or profit</term>, who occupy themselves with mere words and superfluous arts.* And a little later: *Regarding these and similar people, who have exercised themselves in a hollow style of speaking, the divine Scripture says most beautifully: "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise," etc.*

The Venerable Bede <note>An English monk and scholar (672–735 AD), known as the "Father of English History."</note> also supports this point when, in his exposition of the First Epistle to the Corinthians, chapter 3 (which he compiled from the works of Saint Augustine), he has this to say: *"The Lord knows the thoughts of men, that they are vain." Sometimes in the Scriptures, "Wisdom" is used to mean "cunning"—by an abuse of the word, not according to the true nature of the thing. This is also why it is said: "Where is the wise man? Where is the Scribe? Where is the investigator of this world? Has God not made the wisdom of this world foolish?"* And Anselm <note>Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109), a philosopher and theologian famous for the ontological argument for God's existence.</note> on 1 Corinthians chapter 1, verse 19: *The Apostle insults the wisdom and cunning that has been proven false and rejected, when he says: "Where is the wise man, where is the scribe, where is the investigator of this age?" As if to say, the cunning of such people is worth nothing. And it is an invective against the Jews and Gentiles, etc.* See also what he has to say on chapter 2.

From all of these, Saint Thomas Aquinas <note>The most influential scholastic philosopher and theologian (1225–1274).</note> does not differ in his commentary on the first chapter of this Epistle, verse 19, writing thus: *It must be considered that humans, however evil they may be, are not totally deprived of God's gifts, nor are the gifts of God rejected in them; rather, what is rejected and destroyed in them is what proceeds from their own malice. Therefore, he does not say simply "I will destroy wisdom," because all wisdom is from the Lord God. Rather, he says, "I will destroy the wisdom of the wise," that is, the wisdom which the wise of this world have invented for themselves against the true wisdom of God; because, as it is said in James 3: "This is not the wisdom descending from above, but is earthly, <term>animalis: "soulish" or natural, relating to the lower human instincts rather than the spirit</term>, and demonic." Similarly, he does not say "I will reject prudence," for the wisdom of God teaches true prudence; but he says "the prudence of the prudent," that is, the prudence of those who judge themselves prudent in worldly matters so that they may cling to the goods of this world—or because, as it says in Romans 8, "The prudence of the flesh is death," etc.* In the same sense, Thomas de Vio Cajetan <note>A 16th-century Italian cardinal and philosopher known for his commentaries on Aquinas.</note> writes on this passage in these words: *Significantly, he does not say "I will destroy wisdom and intelligence," but he adds,*

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of philosophy against theological critics. Having previously cited Clement of Alexandria, the author now addresses specific Pauline texts (1 Corinthians) often used to discredit human reason, arguing that "human wisdom" in the Bible refers to sophistry and pride, not to the pursuit of truth through logic or science.</meta>

# OF THE EXERCISE

[The Apostle] joins the wise and the intelligent, because neither wisdom nor intelligence is contrary to the cross of Christ; since in truth it does not oppose it: But the wisdom of the wise (that is, the way men possessing it use it) and similarly the intelligence of the intelligent (that is, the way men possessing it use it), who are called wise and intelligent in the world, is what is contrary to the Gospel; it is what God destroys and rejects. For no part of **Philosophy** or science is contrary to the Gospel in itself for the reason stated: because no truth is contrary to another truth. Moreover, sciences are true in themselves, and the Gospel is true: but the presumption of men to extend propositions and means outside their proper limits creates a contradiction with the truth of the Christian faith. And that this is the mind of Saint Paul is confirmed by what he adds about his own style of speaking, when he says in Chapter 2, verse 4: *His speech and preaching were not concerned with the persuasive words of human wisdom*; and in verse 13: *He speaks not in words taught by human wisdom*. For by these words, criticizing and plucking at the vain eloquence of the Teachers among the Corinthians, he teaches that in handing down true and saving doctrine, he did not use a parade of words, ambiguous speech, grandiloquence, or the flowers and "pigments" <note>original: "pigmentis," referring to rhetorical colors or artificial embellishments</note> of orators, which human and apparent wisdom has need of in order to persuade people of its erroneous dogmas and to thrust them upon the gullible as truths—as has also been noted by us above. And thus, we believe it has been shown more than sufficiently that these passages in no way oppose our opinion.

4. And so, following the thread of the Apostle, we come to a passage which not only the <term>Theologasters: a derogatory term for petty or superficial theologians</term>, but even the weightiest theologians have constantly on their lips, and which they continually throw at the evaluators and defenders of Philosophy as an indissoluble and inextricable knot. It occurs in chapter 2, verse 14, and reads thus: *The <term>animal man: from the Latin "animalis homo," a literal translation of the Greek "psychikos," referring to a person governed by the soul/passions rather than the spirit</term> is not capable of those things which are of the spirit of God: for they are foolishness to him, nor can he know them, because they are spiritually judged.* From this passage they think it can be proven that the Philosopher and the wise man are incapable of spiritual things: For by the <note>original Greek: "anthrōpon psychikon"</note> **animal man**, as they translate it, they contend the Apostle designates a man endowed with no other knowledge than what can be acquired by the light of Nature when equipped with the proper aids. Whence even the Theologians who, by order of the Mighty Estates of the Federated Netherlands <note>This refers to the "Statenvertaling," the first official Dutch Bible translation commissioned in 1618 by the States-General.</note>, translated the Holy Scriptures into the vernacular tongue, interpreted the word <note>original Greek: "psychikos"</note> **natural**; although in the notes placed in the margin they concede it properly signifies "Animal." But they are all mistaken: For besides the fact that the little word **psychikos**, among both sacred and profane authors, should never be interpreted by the word "natural," but always "animal"; the Apostle is not speaking here of a man who has prepared for himself a true knowledge of the true and the good by natural light alone; since

### CHAPTER VII. OF PARADOXES

<page-num>53</page-num>

...it would not be the mark of a truly wise man, but of a vain and foolish one, to pass judgment on things unknown to him. But here the discourse is expressly about the man to whom those things he does not grasp and cannot know are foolishness; therefore, it is by no means about the true Philosopher. For the philosopher, until he perceives a thing clearly and distinctly, suspends judgment. 

Rather, [the Apostle] speaks either of a man who is plainly unrefined and devoid of all knowledge, who follows the blind impulse of his own passions—as Erasmus explains in his *Annotations* on this passage—or of one who lives "animally" <note>original: "animaliter," referring to living by biological drives rather than reason or spirit</note>, or who thinks of God carnally, imagining that the things he sees in physical bodies also exist in God—as Cardinal Hugh <note>Hugo de Sancto Caro, a 13th-century biblical commentator</note> has it in this place—or of the Pseudo-philosophers, for whom it is a custom to immediately condemn and hold as foolishness whatever escapes them and whatever they are incapable of. 

And by opposing these to "Spiritual" people, the Apostle quite significantly calls them "Animal men" <note>original: "Animales," from the Latin 'anima' (soul), often translated in Bibles as the 'natural man'</note>, inasmuch as they are constantly occupied with those things that look toward this animal life—that is, the vegetative and sensitive life—such as food and clothing, as well as honors, riches, pleasures, and the like. They have never called their minds away from the senses and sensible things; but being totally immersed in them, they neglect, remove far from themselves, and cast away spiritual things—that is, those things which pertain to the rational soul, which is spirit, such as the knowledge of God and of one's own self. Saint Jerome seems to intend the same thing regarding this passage:

> *He who does not have the spirit of God is an animal man, because he conducts himself in the manner of animals, thinking there is nothing after death: And therefore, as an animal, he commits himself to his belly and to lust, destined to perish in eternal death.*

And Bede, in his *Exposition* of this passage collected from the works of Saint Augustine:

> *Those who have carnal wisdom, and hope for and desire carnal things from God whether in this life or after this life, are animal men. And a little later: What carnal and animal man does not wander through the phantasms of his own heart, and fashion for himself a God such as might please him according to his carnal sense, and thus believe only in something far different from what God is—as much as vanity differs from truth? And again: He who lives from the body is called a carnal or animal man. Carnal, because he pursues carnal things. But animal, because he is carried away by the unrestrained wantonness of his soul, which the spirit does not rule, nor restrain within the boundaries of the natural order, because he does not submit himself to be ruled by God.*

Thus also Dionysius the Carthusian <note>a 15th-century theologian</note> on this passage: *The animal man, that is, the carnal and vicious man, whose affection and intellect are held fast in sensible things.* With whom Saint Anselm agrees in this place, inasmuch as almost the same words occur in his work. And Erasmus, who says thus in his *Annotations* on this passage: <term>psychikos</term> <note>original: "ψυχικός," the Greek term for 'soulish' or 'natural'</note> *as if following the soul, that is, human affects. For since Paul divides man into three parts—flesh, soul, and spirit—here he seems to have used the word for 'soul' in place of 'flesh'.* Nor does Zwingli <note>Huldrych Zwingli, the Swiss Reformer</note> seem to depart from these when he comments on this place: *He is called "animal" here from the soul,* <term>psychikos</term>. *But it has the same force as if he said "carnal." For man (insofar as he is not reborn by the spirit of God) is not only flesh in body, but also...*

<sig>G 3</sig>

# DISQUISITIONS

<page-num>54</page-num>

even with the **soul**. *For the flesh of itself thinks nothing. And the passions of the flesh are in the soul, not in the flesh. Therefore, the man alone who is led by his passions is "flesh," whom [the Apostle] calls "animal" <note>original: "animalis," often translated as "natural" or "sensual" man</note> here.* Furthermore, that this is the true sense of this passage is confirmed not only by the preceding parts (upon which we commented above) and by what follows—for in Chapter 3 he calls those same people **carnal**, *in whom there is envy, and contention, and discord, which cannot befall truly wise men*—but also by what James says in Chapter 3, verses 14, 15, and 17. There, opposing true wisdom to that which only appears to be so, he says: *This is the wisdom descending from above: pure, peaceable, moderate, compliant, full of mercy and good fruits, without judging, without hypocrisy. But that other wisdom is earthly, animal, demonic, the effects of which are Envy, irritation, tumult, and every wicked work.* 

It should be noted here that in the Greek the word **psychikē** <note>original: ψυχικὴ, meaning "soulish" or pertaining to the natural life of the soul as opposed to the spirit</note> occurs; thus the same epithet that Paul applied to the man incapable of spiritual things is attributed by James to "apparent" wisdom. Finally, all these things would turn out much clearer if, as the matter requires, we were to examine and show at length *what the Apostle meant by "those things which are of the Spirit of God," and by "spiritual discernment."* But the nature of our project—the requirements of which we believe we have fully satisfied—does not permit us to enter so vast a sea, since we have already lingered too long on the explanation of these points. However, we did not think this inappropriate, both because the matter is treated here professionally <note>original: "ex professô," meaning with expertise or as a primary subject</note>, and because, following this example, anyone will be able to navigate the other passages of Holy Scripture dealing with this material with the greatest ease. Therefore, we shall waive further explanation of them for the present.

5. But truly, before we "take our hand off the tablet" <note>A Latin idiom meaning to finish a task or stop writing</note>, we must still briefly unravel a passage—for it does not require a laborious interpretation—where the very word "Philosophy" is found expressed. For this reason, those who are not indeed the more perceptive Theologians, but those among the church masses—who, while they truly know nothing solid, yet seem to themselves to be wonderfully wise—frequently rattle off this verse with great emphasis as proof. Whenever a mention of Philosophy occurs, they immediately and quite thoughtlessly vomit it out. It is found in Paul’s Epistle to the Colossians, Chapter 2, verse 8, and it sounds like this: *See to it that no one prey upon you through Philosophy and empty deception, according to the tradition of men, according to the elements of the world, and not according to Christ.* They think that here the Apostle is exhorting the Colossians to beware of Philosophy; but they are hallucinating quite crudely. For many very weighty Theologians believe that by that word, the "knowledge of things" is not designated, but rather the various sects of the Jews. Among others, Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer (1603–1666), a Lutheran theologian known for his work on hermeneutics</note> explains and teaches this excellently in his *Idea of the Good Interpreter and the Malicious Slanderer*, Part 1, Section 3, Article 7, verse 79; for that reason, I am pleased to transcribe his entire passage here. *The question is*, he says, *what is the meaning of the word Philoso-*

# PARADOXES CHAPTER VII.

<page-num>55</page-num>

...philosophy, <note>The text begins mid-word: "philosophiæ."</note> *Colossians* 2:8? Without a doubt, it is that which was the meaning of that word in the age when the Apostle wrote. For among the Jews at that time, "Philosophy" signified nothing other than the various Jewish sects. Indeed, the contemporary historian Josephus expressly calls those three sects—the Pharisees, the Sadducees, and the Essenes—the "three philosophies" in *Book 18 of the Antiquities, Chapter 2*, and "the philosophies of the capture" <note>original: ἁλώσεως φιλοσοφίας; referring to Josephus's work "The Jewish War"</note> in *Book 2, Chapter 7*. Eusebius distinguishes the sects that existed before the times of the Hasmoneans <note>The Hasmoneans were a ruling dynasty of Judea (c. 140–37 BCE) who led the Maccabean revolt.</note> into two families: one consisting of those who studied the Law alone, and the other of those who accepted additions to the Law. He calls the former (in *Book 8 of the Gospel Preparation*) **Philosophy according to the literal sense**; <note>original: Φιλοσοφίαν κατὰ τὴν ῥητὴν διάνοιαν</note> and the latter
<margin>theoretical.</margin> <note>original: θεωρητικήν</note>
Indeed, the "Golden-Tongued Father" <note>Referring to St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), a famous early Christian preacher.</note> speaks of **the matters of philosophy** in his *64th Homily on Matthew*, saying **"since after the coming of Christ"**; <note>original: ἐπειδὴ μετὰ τὴν τοῦ Χριστοῦ παρουσίαν</note> that is, according to the interpreter Casaubon, after the advent of Christ the rule of living well according to the law was intensified. Compare the works of Joseph Scaliger in his *Refutation of the Three Sects of Nicolaus Serrarius, Chapter 22*, Drusius in his *Response to Serrarius, Book 1, Chapters 2 and 18, and Book 4, Chapter 3*, Franz’s *Oracles 106*, and Affelman in *Selected Miscellany 3*. The "philosophical points" of this Philosophy were legalistic contentions. *For example: Is it permitted on the Sabbath to eat an egg which a hen laid on the Sabbath? Or should one’s feet be lifted high from the earth, or should one instead strike against stones?* Compare *Matthew* 6:5, 9:11, 23:14, 23:23, and 12:2; *Luke* 5:33 and 18:12; *Mark* 7:3 and 4. These are the views of Dannhauer, whose opinion is confirmed by the entire context of this chapter. 

Furthermore, even if it were conceded that in this passage the word "Philosophy" denotes "knowledge of things," Paul does not say that one must beware of Philosophy itself, but rather of **plundering through Philosophy**. From this it evidently follows that he does not mean true philosophy, but apparent philosophy—or, as others hold, not philosophy properly, in itself, and absolutely, but that which is called philosophy improperly and accidentally. For true philosophy (as is clear from what was said above) can plunder no one. Calvin feels this way when he comments on this passage as follows:

> Because many have falsely thought that Philosophy is condemned here by Paul, we must define what he understands by this name. In my judgment, he understands whatever men devise of themselves when they wish to be wise by their own sense—and that not without a plausible pretext of reason, so that it appears probable in outward form.

And a little later:

> But let us remember that Paul, under the name of Philosophy, condemned only all those adulterated doctrines which are born from the human head, whatever color of reason they may have.

Saint Ambrose sides with Calvin, writing on this passage:

> He calls it "earthly philosophy," through which those who desire to be judged prudent in this world are usually seduced. This is composed of arguments and the subtlety of certain minutiae <note>Refers to trivial details or "hair-splitting" arguments.</note>.

And a little later:

> He who falls into these disputes will scarcely, if ever, escape. For he is detained by plausible causes and made-up things, estimating nothing to be as true as that which is seen and understood in the elements.

In almost the same way, Saint Anselm says on this passage: *I warn you to walk in Christ; and "see," that is, beware with cautious circumspection,*

The author supports this by citing early Church Fathers and Reformation theologians like Calvin and Ambrose.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Colossians 2:8, specifically focusing on how various theologians and commentators have interpreted the "philosophy" warned against by the Apostle Paul. # D I S P U T A T I O N S
<page-num>56</page-num>

...lest anyone deceive you, leading you into some error through *earthly Philosophy*, <note>original: "Philosophiam terrenam"</note> which is composed of arguments and subtlety: and it deceives while, through plausible causes and fictitious things, nothing is estimated to be as true as what is observed in the physical elements. And a little later: *Either beware, lest anyone deceive you through Philosophy, that is, through the philosophical interpretation of idols, and through empty—that is, void of all utility and all truth—deceit of such sayings, which is according to the tradition of men, that is, of those who are as if they were wise, who more prudently (as it were) explain idols according to the elements of the world*, etc. Similarly also Thomas de Vio Cajetan <note>Thomas de Vio (1469–1534), also known as Gaetanus or Cajetan, was a leading Catholic theologian and cardinal who famously debated Martin Luther.</note> says on this passage: *He joins empty deceit to Philosophy regarding the task of preying upon men* <note>The Latin "praedandi" refers to the act of plundering or taking someone captive as "prey" or "spoil."</note> *away from the Christian faith, because it is not the office of Philosophical truth to prey upon Christians, unless deceit is added to it. Because this deceit is added to Philosophy in a twofold way, he therefore adds: "According to the traditions of men, according to the elements of the world." For either empty deceit is joined to Philosophy from the tradition of men, such as from the sect of the Stoics, or the Peripatetics,* <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle.</note> *and such like; or it is from the elements of the world, that is, from measuring and judging those things which belong to the Christian faith according to the elements of the world—namely, celestial and elementary causes, and things composed of them. For this belongs to empty deceit, insofar as the judgment which can be had from causes of this kind does not extend itself to the lofty mysteries of Christ, which we hold by faith.* 

Besides these, one may look to Andreas Hyperius, Benedictus Aretius, Augustin Marlorat, Willem Estius, Johannes Piscator, and Nicolaus Hemmingius <note>These were prominent 16th-century Protestant and Catholic biblical commentators.</note> on this passage; the last of whom, when he had run through almost all parts of Philosophy and had celebrated their utility and merit, finally concludes thus: *Since therefore all parts of Philosophy are useful and excellent gifts of God, it is a kind of sacrilege to condemn Philosophy in its entirety.* 

And the subsequent explanation confirms that this was the Apostle's mind, for he adds *empty deception according to the tradition of men, according to the elements of the world*, that is, according to human wisdom handed down by "soulish" men, <note>original: "hominibus animalibus"; a reference to those living by natural instinct rather than the Holy Spirit.</note> and according to opinions about the elements and principles of natural things, which at that time had invaded the Greek Philosophers in many diverse forms: and *not according to Christ*, who is the truth and the true wisdom of God. Hence Calvin subjoins to the former points: *What follows next concerning empty deception, I resolve thus: beware of Philosophy, which is nothing else than empty deception; so that this is added appositively.* <note>An "appositive" implies that "empty deception" is a definition or clarification of the specific "philosophy" Paul is attacking, rather than a condemnation of all philosophy.</note> 

And Johannes Piscator says on this passage that the Apostle declares what he understands by the word *Philosophy* through the phrase "empty deceit." <note>original Greek: κενῆς ἀπάτης (kenēs apatēs)</note> Therefore, the particle "and" will not have the force of a copulative conjunction, but of an interpretive "or"; and the former will be declared by the latter, as if it were held: "through *Philosophy*, or *empty deception*." The sense, therefore, is (these are the words of Keckermann <note>Bartholomaeus Keckermann (1572–1609) was a famous German writer and philosopher known for his systematic approach to logic and theology.</note> in *Preliminary Knowledge of Philosophy, book 1, chapter 4*): *Beware of empty deceptions, and the fictions of various opinions, which are sold under the name of Philosophy by men bearing the title of Philosophers.*

<meta>This page continues the defense of philosophy within a theological context, moving from the specific interpretation of Colossians 2:8 to a broader discussion of how true philosophy and theology are never actually in conflict. <page-num>57</page-num>
<header>P A R A D O X E S,  C H A P. VII.</header>

Paul therefore warns the Colossians against the fictions of the Platonists, Epicureans, Stoics, and especially the Sophists, who flourished greatly in Greece at that time. These Sophists would argue at length on both sides of any given subject, and by their opinions—which were proposed with such cleverness and eloquence that they appeared to be true wisdom—they attempted to lead the Colossians away from the saving doctrine of Christ. It is in this sense and for this purpose that he also writes in verse 4: *I say this so that no one may deceive you with persuasive speech.* <note>original: "probabilitate sermonis"</note> This also aligns with the view of Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–458 AD) was an influential theologian and bishop in the early Church.</note> on this passage: *That which he previously called persuasive and fit for convincing speech, he here calls "Philosophy."* However, he does not mean true Philosophy, as that does not rob us of heavenly truth; rather, it leads us toward acquiring it and arms us to defend it. Therefore, let those good men—and the unlearned <note>The original "Idiotæ" refers to those lacking formal education or technical training in a subject.</note> who follow them with blind impulse—cease from now on to use this passage to disparage Philosophy or to defend their own erroneous opinions. For not only does reason stand on our side, but also a great number of the most serious theologians of every kind. Among these, besides those already cited, are Clement of Alexandria in books 1 and 5 of the *Stromata*; Zanchi <note>Girolamo Zanchi (1516–1590), a prominent Italian Protestant Reformation theologian.</note> and Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a French Protestant theologian and successor to John Calvin.</note> on this passage; Melchior Cano in *Theological Commonplaces*, book 9, chapter 9; Alsted in *Precognition of Theology*, book 2, chapter 10; and Casmann in *Christian Philosophy*, part 2. To all of these we add Augustine, and with his words from *On Order*, book 1, chapter 11, we conclude this chapter: *The Divine Scriptures do not command us to avoid all philosophers, but only the "philosophers of this world." Likewise: For whoever thinks that all philosophy should be fled, wishes nothing else than that we do not love Wisdom.*

Since most of these are of very little importance and easily solved by anyone using the distinction provided in the preceding chapter, we would pass over them all in silence, were it not for one that seems to present some difficulty. It is this: that Philosophy establishes many dogmas which seem to fight against Theology and Holy Scripture...

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 7 and Chapter 8, refuting the claim that philosophy is inherently hostile to faith. It specifically tackles the "double truth" theory—the idea that something can be true in theology while being false in philosophy.</meta>

### OF THE EXERCISES

...seem to be far from the original sources: from which one could easily infer that such a discipline should by no means be considered a genuine and legitimate interpreter of the Word. For how can that discipline extract the true sense from the Divine Writings, and demonstrate that sense with firm arguments, when it occasionally teaches and asserts opinions here and there that are not only far removed from Holy Scripture, but even—what is more—contrary to it? This is done according to that common saying: *Many propositions are given which are true Theologically, but false Philosophically.* <note>The "double truth" theory was a controversial medieval and Renaissance idea suggesting that faith and reason could reach contradictory conclusions that were nonetheless both "true" within their own fields.</note> 

But we respond by conceding that an apparent and erroneous Philosophy can indeed dictate and establish certain things that oppose the Holy Utterances; yet, regarding *true* Philosophy, we deny this entirely. And so we say: whatever is true or false in Theology is also true or false in true Philosophy. Conversely, whatever is true or false in the latter is also true or false in the former. Never can what is true in one be false in the other; rather, both agree and consent in truth and falsehood everywhere in the most delightful harmony.

Furthermore, we cannot marvel enough at the addition that some things are true Theologically and false Philosophically. For truth always exists as one and the same, perpetually consistent with itself; it never changes its face, nor does it put on one countenance and then another, regardless of the discipline or writing in which it is delivered. How, I ask—since truth is nothing other than the agreement of speech with reality <note>original: "sermonis cum rebus convenientia"</note>—could one and the same speech agree with reality in this book, yet differ from reality when delivered in another? Surely, since God is immutable and is the author of both Philosophy and Theology, it is most absurd to think that He could teach something to be true in the latter which He teaches to be false in the former, or conversely, that something is false in this one which He teaches is true in that one. 

So it is that what the <term>light of nature</term> <note>The human capacity for reason and logic, independent of divine revelation.</note> dictates to be true, the <term>light of grace</term> <note>Truth revealed by God through Scripture or spiritual insight.</note> cannot reject as false. And what Scripture asserts as harmonious with truth, nature cannot reject as discordant. Otherwise, one and the same thing would be both true and false at the same time—and nothing is more contrary to reason than that. Mornay <note>Philippe de Mornay (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat known as the "Huguenot Pope."</note> expresses this same point in very clear words when he says the following in the preface to his examination of the book *On the Truth of the Religious*:

> *The axioms and principles of nature—of nature, I say, which God made—cannot be contrary to God Himself. Moreover, God is reason itself, truth itself. Every other reason, every other truth depends on Him, refers back to Him, and cannot even exist outside of Him. It is so far from being the case that what is true and rational in nature is false and irrational in Theology; for Theology is certainly not against nature, but against the corruption of nature, which is itself against true and genuine nature.*

Therefore, let it be firmly established and remains: between Philosophical and Theological truth, there can be no conflict at all.

2. However, perhaps our adversaries would still be <term>stuck</term> <note>original: "aqua hæreret," a Latin idiom literally meaning "the water would stop," used to describe someone who is at a loss or has run out of arguments.</note> unless it is shown more particularly that those propositions which are held as true Theologically and...

<summary>This page argues for the total harmony between faith and reason, rejecting the idea that a concept can be true in theology but false in philosophy. It asserts that because God is the author of both nature and scripture, the "light of nature" and the "light of grace" cannot contradict one another.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the "double truth" theory, where scholars argue that a proposition can be false in philosophy but true in theology. Having established in the previous chapter that true philosophy and true theology must agree, the author now begins to examine specific examples commonly used to support this supposed conflict, such as creation from nothing and the resurrection of the body.</meta>

### PARADOXES, CHAPTER VIII.

H 2

Those things commonly listed as philosophically false are, in fact, not so. To prove their own opinion, these scholars bring certain examples onto the stage, such as: *nothing comes from nothing*; *the same individual thing cannot be reproduced numerically*; *the world is eternal*; *a virgin cannot give birth*; *one cannot be three*; *a heavy body is carried downward*; *fire burns material placed near it*; *there is no return from a state of loss to a state of possession* <note>original: "à privatione ad habitum non detur regressus." This is an Aristotelian principle suggesting that once a natural state, such as life or sight, is lost, it cannot be regained through natural means.</note>; and other things of that kind, which no one doubts are of this character. So come, let us indulge them; let us examine one or two of these examples and show that these men are utterly mistaken.

First, then, they put forward the idea that *Nothing is made from nothing* <note>original: "Ex nihilô nihil fieri"</note>. They say this is philosophically true—and indeed correctly so, since it is a principle known through itself and counted among common notions—but they claim it is theologically false. For the Holy Pages teach that God established this visible world and everything contained within it out of nothing. 

To this we respond by saying no. For with whatever diligence and care we have unrolled those pages, we have nowhere been able to find anything that bore the appearance of that claim, except in the second book of Maccabees, chapter 7, verse 28. Since that book is <term>Apocryphal<note>The "Apocrypha" refers to books found in some versions of the Bible (like the Catholic Vulgate) but rejected as non-canonical by others (like many Protestant traditions). The author suggests its authority is not universal.</note></term>, and therefore does not hold divine authority among most Christians—and moreover, since it allows for an explanation that favors philosophy—it causes no trouble here. 

But does not Moses (for I seem to hear them retorting with great fervor) teach this clearly enough when he begins his books thus: *In the beginning God created the heaven and the earth*, which is to say, he made them out of nothing? For is that not how the word "Creation" must be interpreted? 

But truly, I would like to ask: who taught them that? In many other places in the early chapters of Genesis, "to create" means to produce something from some pre-existing matter; why then, at the very threshold of the book, does it denote production out of nothing? From where did they draw such an interpretation? Surely not from Scripture, for no passage holds this; and not from Philosophy, for she establishes the contrary, as they themselves admit. It must be, then, either something that came from their own brains or was accepted through traditional instruction. Therefore, until they have demonstrated that this axiom is theologically false, let it remain true and beyond doubt in both Theology and Philosophy.

The second example they produce is: *The same thing cannot be reproduced numerically* <note>original: "Idem non posse reproduci numericè." In philosophy, "numerical identity" means being the exact same individual, not just a similar copy.</note>. They grant that this is philosophically true, because the procreation and conservation of every individual (which is nothing other than continuous procreation) requires a specific confluence of individual partial causes. Yet in Theology, they cry out that it is false; for Theology teaches that the very same bodies in number will rise again on the last day of the Final Judgment, and it proves this from Job, chapter 19, verses 25, 26, and 27, and 1 Corinthians, chapter 15. 

But truly, it does not sufficiently occur to me how to wonder enough that very serious men—who are otherwise quite perceptive—should so adhere to opinions either drunk in from infancy, or acquired through instruction, or seized upon by blind impulse, that they hold, teach, and defend them tooth and nail without an accurate and unbiased examination of the proofs. For this dogma concerning the same...

<summary>The author argues against the "double truth" theory by examining the concept of creation out of nothing, asserting that neither Scripture nor philosophy actually requires the belief that God created the universe from absolute nothingness. # OF THE DISPUTATION

...the resurrection of the flesh, and it is repugnant to sound reason, as we have said, and can by no means be inferred from the cited passages of Scripture. For the first passage, drawn from the Old Testament, is found to be so confused, disturbed, and obscure in the Hebrew text that not even a suitable meaning can be derived from it, much less the resurrection of the same flesh. Moreover, if the latter passage is examined even slightly, it will be found to teach the resuscitation of new bodies rather than the same ones. <note>The author is likely referring to the debate over whether the physical atoms of the deceased are gathered back together (the "same flesh") or if God provides a transformed, spiritual body (a "new body").</note> Therefore, if they busy themselves with convicting this statement of falsehood in Theology, they will have to bring forward other arguments to prove it. Since none occur in the Word of God, it shall remain firm and settled in Theology just as in Philosophy.

Regarding the remaining points we reviewed above, the reasoning is similar, as shown at length by Keckermann in book 1 of *Introduction to Philosophy*, the penultimate chapter, and Alsted in *Theological Introductions*, book 2, chapter 10. <note>Bartholomaeus Keckermann (1572–1609) and Johann Heinrich Alsted (1588–1638) were influential German Reformed scholars who sought to harmonize logic and theology.</note> There, Alsted also lists and weakens many arguments by which the **Haters of Philosophy** <note>original: "Misophilosophi"</note> attempt to construct a battle between Philosophy and Theology. We respond briefly—for we do not think it worth the effort to pursue them at length—that all those statements, if they are freed from all ambiguity of words and are correctly and justly distinguished and limited, and are legitimately demonstrated to be true by philosophical reasons, can in no way contradict Theology or Holy Scripture. Rather, they must agree most perfectly: *For Holy Scripture (as Keckermann records from the Third Lateran Council, celebrated in the year 1180, session 8) does not conflict with the truth of Nature, because truth does not contradict truth.* <note>The Third Lateran Council actually took place in 1179. The author’s point is a cornerstone of Scholasticism: since God is the author of both the Bible and the natural world, the two cannot truly disagree.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.

1. *The Church, the Fathers, the Councils, and the Roman Pope are not the infallible norm for interpreting Scripture.* 2. *They are neither led by the Spirit of God, nor can they certainly extract and demonstrate the truths contained in the Holy Letters.* 3. *They are subject to errors.*

<margin>1.</margin>
Thus far, we have built up our own Opinion like a fortress; we have surrounded it with walls, fortified it with ramparts, defended it from the insults and invasions of enemies, and preserved it in good repair. Finally, there remains the last part of our undertaking: that we now descend into the lands of the enemies to invade, capture, and demolish their fortresses, so that our own may emerge more secure and more illustrious. These enemies, however, fall into two categories. The first is that of the Roman Catholics <note>original: "Pontificiorum"</note>, the second is that of the Reformers <note>original: "Reformatorum"</note> and those who favor them in this matter. We shall attack the latter in what follows, but for the present, we shall address the former, and indeed, we shall deal with them quickly.

### PARADOXES CHAPTER IX. 61

...since they are fortified by a small and low rampart, and defended by light-armed soldiers. For this reason, we would almost not have moved our weapons against them, and would have neglected them as unworthy of assault. For we have always judged that someone whose mind is so abject and whose intellect is so warped that they can give assent with blind impulse to the fictions of frivolous men—which are not only destitute of all sound reason but moreover overturn it, and indeed in matters concerning eternal salvation—should cause no trouble to a noble and sincere lover of nourishing Truth. However, lest our **Exercise** <note>original: "Exercitatio"; likely referring to this specific scholarly work or "disputation."</note> be in any part incomplete or defective, we have decided it wise to spend this chapter on their refutation. They determine, therefore, as we also hinted above, that the right and power of investigating and explaining the sense of Scripture belongs to the Church, the Fathers, the Councils, and the Roman Pontiff. They cry out that these possess a certain and infallible Authority by which they can both interpret the Holy Letters and judge the interpretations of others: so much so that it is not permitted for anyone to resist or contradict their explanations or the judgments passed on the explanations of others. Against this **Tyranny**, all those to whom **Christian liberty** was so dear that they shook off this intolerable yoke from their consciences have opposed themselves with enough spirit and alacrity, and have reduced its defenders to great straits. For whenever the destroyers of this Opinion have taught that this right of interpretation by no means belongs to the Church, its advocates respond that by "Church," they mean the **Fathers** <note>The "Church Fathers" are influential early Christian teachers and bishops whose writings are considered authoritative in tradition.</note> or Doctors of the Church. But when the difficulties and absurdities of this escape-route are proposed to them, they retort that all these are easily and most certainly removed by **Councils**, especially General ones. When it is further demonstrated by others that this is also false, they finally bring forward their last refuge: the **Roman Pontiff**. They establish that Councils confirmed by him are the true and infallible interpreters of Holy Scripture; indeed, they claim that he alone meets all the conditions of a true and undoubted interpreter, as one elevated beyond all danger of error. Thus, **Bellarmine** <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Jesuit Cardinal and a central figure of the Counter-Reformation who argued strongly for papal supremacy.</note> himself does not blush to assert:

> *If the whole Council stood on one side and the Pope alone on the other, the Pope should be believed rather than the whole Council.*

Thus, that illustrious and splendid name of "Church" which they pretend to use—and by which they include the Fathers, the Councils, and the Pope himself—is ultimately limited to and resolved into this one man alone. Since all these things sufficiently show by themselves upon what a weak and unstable foundation they rest, and have been treated at length by our adversaries, we shall pass over them and show and demonstrate that this Opinion is erroneous with only one or two unassailable arguments.

2. The first is sought from the number of true Interpreters of the Holy Letters, of which we count only three according to our calculation: namely, either the [writers of] the New and Old

the resurrection of the flesh <note>original: "carnis resurrectione"</note>, is both repugnant to sound reason, as we have said, and can in no way be inferred from the cited passages of Scripture. Since no such arguments occur in the Word of God, it will remain firm and established in both Theology and Philosophy. As for the rest of the points we listed above, the reasoning is similar, as shown at length by Keckermann <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (1572–1609) was a German Reformed theologian and philosopher known for his systematic approach to logic and science</note> in Book 1 of *Philosophical Preliminaries*, the second to last chapter, and Alsted <note>Johann Heinrich Alsted (1588–1638), a prolific German polymath and theologian</note> in *Theological Preliminaries*, book 2, chapter 10. Instead, they must fit together most perfectly: *For Sacred Scripture (as the third Lateran Council, celebrated in the year 1180, session 8, holds, according to Keckermann’s reference) does not conflict with the truth of Nature, because truth does not contradict truth.*

## CHAPTER IX.

*1. The Church, the Church Fathers, the Councils, and the Roman Pope are not the infallible standard for interpreting Scripture. 2. They are neither guided by the Spirit of God, nor can they certainly extract and demonstrate the truths contained in the Sacred Letters. 3. They are subject to errors.*

<margin>1.</margin> Thus far, we have built up our own Opinion like a citadel; we have walled it around and fortified it with bulwarks, and then defended it from the insults and invasions of enemies, keeping it in good repair. Now, these enemies fall into two categories. The first is that of the **Papists** <note>original: "Pontificiorum", referring to the Roman Catholic Church</note>, the other is that of the **Reformed** <note>original: "Reformatorum", referring to the Protestant movement</note>, and those who favor them in this regard. # CHAPTER IX OF THE PARADOXES #
<note>The term "Paradoxes" here refers to theological statements that might seem contrary to common opinion or church dogma, but which the author intends to prove true through reason.</note>

...as if fortified by a small and low rampart, and defended by light-armed soldiers: for which reason we had almost not moved arms against them, and would have neglected them as unworthy of assault. For, we have always judged that it ought to give no trouble to a noble and sincere lover of nourishing Truth to realize whose mind is so abject, and intellect so warped, that he could give assent and rest with blind impulse to the fictions of frivolous men—which are not only destitute of all sound reason, but moreover overturn it, and indeed in matters concerning eternal salvation. Nevertheless, lest our **Exercise** <term>Exercitatio: a formal academic or theological treatise or discourse</term> be in any part maimed or defective, we have decided it wise to devote this chapter to their refutation. They establish, therefore, as we have also hinted above, that the right and power of searching out and explaining the sense of Scripture rests with the Church, the Fathers, the Councils, and the **Roman Pontiff** <term>Pontificem Romanum: the Pope</term>: and they cry out that these possess a certain and infallible Authority, by which they can both interpret Holy Letters and judge the interpretations of others: so much so that it is lawful and permitted for no one to resist their explanations or the sentences passed on the explanations of others. Against this Tyranny, all those to whom Christian liberty was so dear that they shook off this intolerable yoke from their consciences, opposed themselves quite courageously and eagerly, and reduced its defenders to profound straits. For whenever the destroyers of this Opinion <note>Referring to Protestant reformers and critics of Catholic dogma</note> taught that this right of interpretation by no means belongs to the Church, the builders [of the opinion] object that by "the Church," they understand the Fathers, the Doctors of the Church. But indeed, when the difficulties and absurdities of this evasion are subsequently proposed by them, these men retort that all those are easily and most certainly removed by the Councils, especially General ones. When it is further demonstrated by them that this too is false: these men finally bring forth their last refuge, the Roman Pontiff, by whom they establish that confirmed Councils are the true and infallible interpreters of Holy Scripture: indeed, that all the conditions of a true and indubitable interpreter belong to him alone, as one raised above all danger of error; so much so that **Bellarmine** <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Jesuit Cardinal and a leading figure in the Counter-Reformation who defended papal authority</note> himself does not blush to assert: *If the whole Council stood on one side, and the Pope alone on the other: the Pope ought rather to be believed than the whole Council.* Thus, that illustrious and splendid name of "the Church" which they pretend, and by which they include the Fathers, the Councils, and the Pope himself, finally terminates and resolves into this one man alone. Since all these things show of themselves on what a weak and unstable foundation they rest, and have been treated at length by our Adversaries, we, passing over them, will show and demonstrate by only one or two unassailable arguments that this Opinion is erroneous.

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter IX, specifically refuting the claim that the institutional Church, Church Fathers, or the Pope hold infallible authority over the interpretation of the Bible. The author is moving from a general critique to a point-by-point rebuttal of various ecclesiastical ranks.</meta>

-> **OF THE EXERCISE** <-

The authors of the Testament—that is, while they were still living—disclosed and explained their own intentions regarding any ambiguous speech occurring in their works, whether through writing or by the living voice, according to that well-worn proverb: *Everyone is the best interpreter of their own words* <note>original: "Optimus interpres verborum quifque fuorum"</note>. This authority also belongs to those whom the Holy Spirit of God inspires extraordinarily and immediately with His divine power for the task of interpreting; or finally, to those who can with certainty draw out the truths of the divine Utterances and demonstrate that they have done so. However, into this rank of interpreters—specifically the middle and last orders, since the first have long since departed from the living and thus no longer hold a place in Christendom—neither the Church, nor the Fathers, nor the Councils, nor the **Roman Pope** <term>Papa Romanus: The Pope, viewed here as a human official rather than an infallible authority</term> can be admitted.

For the visible Church (of which we speak here; for the invisible Church, being the assembly of all the **elect** <term>Electos: those chosen by God for salvation</term> for eternal life who have been, are, and will be from the beginning of the world, cannot explain its opinion regarding the interpretation of Scripture in this life) is composed of both the **reprobate** <term>Reprobis: those rejected by God or destined for damnation</term> and the elect. Now, the reprobate can neither be led by the Spirit of God, nor can they infallibly understand and teach the truths of His oracles: therefore, neither can the whole Church. 

But if the Roman Catholics <note>The author uses "Pontificii," a term for those loyal to the Pope</note> wish this to be said of their own Church: then surely, if that Church is not entirely reprobate, it must contain very few of the Elect, since not only are all the **laity** <term>Laici: common church members who are not clergy</term> utterly ignorant of Holy Letters, but even most of the clergy and the priests themselves. Add to this that the office which they claim belongs to the Church cannot be granted to the laity at all; for among the Roman Catholics, the laity constitute the largest part of the Church, yet they are not even permitted to read the Sacred Books—much less interpret them—but are interdicted and prohibited from doing so under the severest penalties.

Furthermore, that the Fathers, the Councils, and the Pope were neither extraordinarily inspired by divine power in interpreting, nor certainly extracted and demonstrated the truths of the passages contained in the sacred pages, is shown by their own interpretations, which clash with one another. Regarding the Fathers, we have **Bellarmine** <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Cardinal and influential defender of Catholic doctrine during the Counter-Reformation</note> himself admitting this, as he frequently cites opposing expositions of one and the same passage of Holy Scripture in his books of *Controversies*. Thus, in Book 2, Chapter 2 of *On Christ*, he says: *We do not follow the Fathers when they defend their own singular opinions that contradict others. Let the Blessed **Cyprian** <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage and influential Church Father</note> be an example, whom we do not follow when he teaches that baptism is void if it is administered by a heretic; for we know that the other Doctors did not agree with Cyprian in this matter.*

It will also become sufficiently manifest to anyone who reads and compares them that the General Councils fight amongst themselves in the explanation of many passages, so that this point requires no laborious proof. Finally, that the Popes have handed down many contradictory explanations of Holy Scripture and issued contrary decrees, even in matters of faith, is witnessed more than enough by **Canon Law** <term>Jus Canonicum: the body of laws and regulations made by Church authority</term>. Thus, **Innocent I** <note>Pope from 401 to 417 AD</note> in his 3rd Epistle concluded from John, chapter 6, verse 53—*Unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink His blood, you have no life in you, etc.*—that the **Eucharist** <term>Holy Communion</term> must also be given to infants. However, later [Popes]...

# CHAPTER IX OF THE PARADOXES.

...taught the opposite, and still teach it. Thus ***Gregory*** <note>Gregory the Great, Pope from 590–604 AD</note> wished for marriage to be restored to Priests; however, his successors denied it. He also established that he who would assume the title of Universal Bishop is a precursor to the Antichrist; a title which, nevertheless, his successors did assume. More examples of this kind may be seen in ***Chamier***, *Panstratia Catholica, Volume 1, Book 3, Chapter 13* <note>Daniel Chamier was a French Reformed theologian; this was his major work against Roman Catholic doctrines</note>. To these can also be added, regarding the Popes—to overturn that enormous authority which ***Bellarmine*** <note>Robert Bellarmine, a Jesuit Cardinal and a leading figure of the Counter-Reformation</note> attributes to them, even over Councils—as if they were two corollaries: that many were Heretics, and many were impious, profane, and wicked. Many among the Roman Catholic writers themselves admit that there were heretics among the Popes. Chief among others were ***Eleutherus***, ***Liberius***, ***Felix***, ***Anastasius***, ***Honorius***, and ***John XXII***, whose heretical dogmas ***Chamier*** extensively reviews in the *aforementioned place, chapters 9, 10, 11, 12, & 13*, and defends against the exceptions of the Romanists, who try to cleanse the heads of their Church by any means possible. And the **Canon Law** <term>Jus Canonicum: The body of laws and regulations made by Church leadership</term> itself confesses that a Pope can fall into heresy, as not a few Papists have clearly pronounced; and ***Chamier*** proves the same with arguments in the same book, chapter 8. That they were also wicked and impious, ***Platina*** <note>Bartolomeo Platina, a 15th-century historian who wrote "Lives of the Popes"</note> himself in the *Lives of the Popes*, and many other historians, testify. Such men were—to bring at least some onto the stage—***Stephen VI***, ***Sergius III***, ***John XII*** (numbered by most as such, by some as the ***XIII***), ***Sylvester II***, ***Boniface VIII***, ***Benedict XIII***, and finally ***Alexander VI***, whose crimes and impieties are listed in ***Chamier***, *loc. cit. cap. 6*. Whether the Spirit of God can shine upon men of this sort in interpreting Holy Scripture, or whether the faculty of investigating and demonstrating the truths of divine utterances can reside in them—and whether such authority as ***Bellarmine*** exaggerates can belong to anyone who has a clear mind and a **sound mind in a sound body** <note>original: "mens sana in sanô corpore"</note>—we leave and permit to the reader's judgment.

3. Thus was the first point; we derive the second argument from the Conditions which theologians state are required in a true Interpreter, from which we make the following **syllogism** <term>Syllogismus: A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</term>. An infallible Interpreter of Holy Scripture can neither err, nor be led by partisan zeal, nor leave room for appeal; but can compel assent and imbue men with certain knowledge. But the **Church**, the **Fathers**, the **Councils**, and the **Roman Popes** can err, be led by partisan zeal, etc. Therefore, they are not infallible Interpreters of the Holy Scriptures. Regarding the **major premise** <term>Majore: The first part of the syllogism</term>, we spoke in Chapter 4, Section 10. But for the confirmation of the **minor premise** <term>Minoris: The second part of the syllogism</term> (besides those things we brought forward in the proof of the first Argument, and that the Church and Councils consist of men, and the Fathers and Popes are men, from whom these conditions may be absent), we will proceed piece by piece. And indeed, first, that the **Church** can err is clear from the fact that the **Eucharist** <term>Eucharistia: The sacrament of the Lord's Supper</term> was even administered to infants...
