->OF THE DISCOURSE<-

among the Ancients, about which **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers</note> and others have much to say. Furthermore, shortly after the time of the **Council of Nicaea** <note>The first ecumenical council in 325 AD, which defined the nature of Christ against Arianism</note>, the greater part of the Christian world swore an oath of betrayal <note>original: "perfidiam juravit"; referring to the temporary triumph of the Arian heresy</note>, as we read in **Sulpicius Severus** <note>A Christian historian (c. 363–425 AD)</note>, book 2. So also in the times of the **Arians**, almost the entire world was infected by the poison of that heresy, as witnessed by **Vincent of Lérins** <note>Latin: "Lirinensis"; a 5th-century monk and author</note>, who even admits that it is not impossible for the entire Church to deviate from the faith at the same time. More on these matters may be seen in **Chamier**, *Panstratia Catholica*, book 2, chapter 16 <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621) was a prominent French Reformed theologian</note>. But if the first condition <note>the purity of faith</note> fails in the Church, the remaining conditions will also fail, for they are all founded upon it, as we have shown above. Furthermore, it becomes clear that the **Fathers** <note>The early influential theologians of the Church</note> could err from the fact that they frequently failed to grasp the true sense of Scripture, as **Bellarmine** <note>Robert Bellarmine, a Jesuit Cardinal and leading Catholic controversialist</note> very clearly teaches in book 3, *On the Word of God*, chapter 3, sections 9 and 10. There he says: *The Fathers, in explaining the Holy Scriptures, occasionally slipped, taking figuratively what should have been taken literally, and literally what should have been taken figuratively.* He brings forward examples from **Origen**, **Papias**, **Justin**, **Irenaeus**, **Tertullian**, **Lactantius**, and others. **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> teaches the same thing when he writes to **Theophilus** in Volume 3 of his Letters:

> *I know that I regard the Apostles in one way, and the other Commentators in another. The former always speak the truth; the latter, being human, wander from it in certain matters.*

So he also says that certain of his own commentaries on the sacred books escaped him—that he poured them out rather than wrote them. And the Fathers did not only err, but many were moreover led by partisan zeal. For they often say things out of benevolence and love; for example, **Augustine** in his *Manual to Laurence* <note>The "Enchiridion"</note> regarding **Purgatory** and **Prayers for the Dead**, admits in a way that he and others erred out of an excess of love, such as when he thinks of his friends and his dearest mother, **Monica**. Often they remain silent about other things out of fear of certain people, as the same man testifies in *Letter 119 to Januarius*, chapter 19, in these words:

> *I do not dare to more freely disapprove of many such things because of the scandals of certain persons, whether they be holy or turbulent, that must be avoided.*

Often, through the excessive reverence of some, or through the tyranny of certain Popes and Emperors, they were driven into absurd interpretations of the Holy Books, as the history of the **Council of Rimini** and of **Berengarius** abundantly testifies <note>The Council of Rimini (359 AD) was a victory for Arianism; Berengarius of Tours (11th century) was forced to recant his views on the Eucharist</note>. But to these things the **Papists** <note>A contemporary, often polemical term for Roman Catholics</note> object, saying they do not deny that this or that individual Father was occasionally liable to error; but they argue that all the Fathers consenting together have always taught the truth and never hallucinated, and only to that extent do they hold them as infallible interpreters. But we reply, first, that this exception is futile: for if one can err in the interpretation of this passage of Scripture, and another in that one, why could they not all err together? Furthermore, very few passages are found—indeed, perhaps none—concerning whose meaning absolutely all the Fathers have pronounced an opinion. Moreover, all, or certainly almost all, erred together in explaining the passage in **Proverbs 8** <note>Proverbs 8:22, a key text in the Arian controversy regarding whether Divine Wisdom was "created" or "begotten"</note>. Finally, it is false that the "Consensus of all the Fathers" is the rule of interpretation for the followers of the Pope, since all their writings testify that they sometimes follow the opinion of no one, and sometimes only one or the o-

### CHAPTER IX OF THE PARADOXES

and follow the exposition of only one person, even if other and better **Fathers** <note>The early influential leaders and theologians of the Christian Church</note> disagree with it, as **Polanus** <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610), a German Reformed theologian</note> shows at length in all the chapters of his *Catholic Harmony* and his *Syntagma Theologiae*, book 1, chapter 45, question 7. Furthermore, that **Councils** can err is made clear by the **First Council of Nicaea** <note>The first ecumenical council held in 325 AD</note> itself: when it condemned soldiers who, having once professed Christianity, later returned to military service (even if legitimate); when it enacted certain canons of penance which became the causes of many superstitions in the Church and diminished or obscured the merits of Christ; and when it wished to forbid marriage to sacred persons, had **Paphnutius** <note>A bishop from Egypt who famously argued at Nicaea that marriage should not be forbidden to the clergy</note> not stepped in to prevent it, etc. Finally, that the **Roman Pontiffs** <note>The Popes</note> can err is taught by their grave and frequent slips in interpreting the Word of God. For example, **Boniface VIII**, in his decree *On Superiority and Obedience* <note>Referring to the bull *Unam Sanctam* (1302)</note>, chapter 1, in order to prove that the supreme Secular and Ecclesiastical power belonged to him, brought onto the stage the passage from the Evangelist Luke, chapter 22, verse 38: *Behold, here are two swords* <note>original: "Ecce hîc duo gladii"</note>, etc., and interpreted it in his own favor. Likewise, they claim **Cephas** <note>The Aramaic name given to the Apostle Peter</note> means "head" <note>original: "caput"; a play on the Greek 'kephale'</note>, and therefore the Pope is the head of the Church—whereas John, chapter 1, verse 43 <note>Usually John 1:42 in modern Bibles</note>, says that *Cephas* means a "rock" <note>original: "petram"</note>. Similarly, **Pope Siricius** in his first Letter to Himerius of Tarragona, chapter 7, and **Innocent I** in his third Letter to Exuperius, Bishop of Toulouse, interpreted the passage from 1 Peter 1:16 (*Be holy because I am holy* <note>original: "Estote sancti quia ego sanctus sum"</note>) and Paul’s Romans 8:8 (*Those who are in the flesh cannot please God; but you are not in the flesh, but in the spirit* <note>original: "Qui in carne sunt Deo placere non possunt"</note>) in such a way as to conclude that Priests must be barred from marriage. Yet Peter speaks there of all the faithful, and Paul speaks of those who have not yet put off their carnal nature through the Spirit of Christ. One could bring forward six hundred such examples, but these will suffice; for it is tiresome to linger longer on such trifles. Anyone who desires more, as well as the solutions to the arguments by which the **Papists** <note>A term used by the author to refer to Roman Catholics</note> establish and confirm their opinion, should consult the already frequently praised **Chamier**, **Polanus**, etc.

***

Against them, there will be need for a stronger heart and a longer struggle, for they are vigorous in war, distinguished in stratagems, well-equipped with the protection of arms and strongholds, and—what is most significant—they have, as far as I know, never yet been [opposed...]

### CHAPTER IX. OF THE PARADOXES (continued)
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: oppu-</meta>

and follow only the exposition of one person; even if other, and better Fathers dissent from it, as **Polanus** <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf (1561–1610), a prominent Reformed theologian</note> shows at length in all the chapters of his *Catholic Symphony* <note>original: "Symphonia Catholica"</note> and in his *Syntagma Theologicum* <note>A systematic arrangement of theology</note>, book 1, chapter 45, question 7. Furthermore, that Councils can err is made plain by the first **Council of Nicaea** <note>The landmark council of 325 AD that defined core Christian doctrines</note> itself: when it condemned soldiers who, having once professed Christianity, later returned to military service (even if lawful); when it established certain canons of penance which became the cause of many superstitions in the Church and diminished or obscured the merits of Christ; and when it wished to prohibit marriage to those in holy orders, had **Paphnutius** <note>An Egyptian bishop who famously argued at Nicaea that "marriage is honorable" and successfully blocked a mandate for clerical celibacy</note> not intervened to prevent it. Finally, that the Roman Pontiffs <note>The Popes</note> can err is shown by their grave and frequent slips in interpreting the Word of God. For example, **Boniface VIII** <note>Pope from 1294–1303, known for asserting supreme papal authority over secular rulers</note>, in the section *On Superiority and Obedience* <note>original: "de Majoritat. & Obedient."</note>, chapter 1, in order to prove that the supreme secular and ecclesiastical power belonged to him, brought onto the stage the passage from the Evangelist Luke, chapter 22, verse 38: *Behold, here are two swords* <note>original: "Ecce hîc duo gladii"</note>, etc., and interpreted it in his own favor. Likewise, they claim that *Cephas* signifies "head"; therefore, the Pope is the head of the Church—even though John, chapter 1, verse 42 <note>The text cites verse 43</note>, says that *Cephas* signifies a "rock" <note>This refers to a common medieval linguistic error where the Aramaic 'Cephas' (rock) was confused with the Greek 'Kephalē' (head)</note>. Similarly, **Pope Siricius** in his first Letter (to Himerius of Tarragona), chapter 7, and **Innocent I** in his third Letter (to Exuperius, Bishop of Toulouse), took the passage from 1 Peter 1:16, *Be holy, because I am holy* <note>original: "Eſtote ſancti quia ego ſanctus ſum"</note>, and Paul in Romans 8:8, *Those who are in the flesh cannot please God; but you are not in the flesh, but in the spirit* <note>original: "Qui in carne ſunt Deo placere non poſſunt, vos autem non eſtis in carne, ſed in ſpiritu"</note>, and interpreted them in such a way as to deduce that priests must be barred from marriage. This was done even though Peter was speaking to all the faithful, and Paul was speaking of those who have not yet put off their carnal nature through the Spirit of Christ. One could bring forward six hundred <note>A common Latin idiom for an indefinitely large number, like "countless"</note> such examples, but let these suffice; for it is wearying to linger longer on such trifles. He who desires more, as well as the refutations of the arguments with which the Papists establish and confirm their opinion, should consult the already frequently praised **Chamier**, **Polanus**, and others.

# DISPUTATIONS

among the Ancients <note>original: "apud Veterēs"</note>, a matter on which **Augustine** and others have much to say. Furthermore, shortly after the time of the **Council of Nicaea** <note>Held in 325 AD, this was the first ecumenical council of the Christian Church</note>, the greatest part of the Christian world swore an oath to treachery <note>referring to the Arian heresy</note>, as we read in the second book of **Sulpicius Severus**. Likewise, in the times of the **Arians**, almost the entire world was infected by the poison of that heresy, as **Vincent of Lérins** <note>original: "Lirinensis," a 5th-century monk known for his definition of "Catholic" as that which is believed everywhere, always, and by all</note> testifies; he even admits it is not impossible for the entire Church to deviate from the faith at the same time. More on these matters can be seen in **Daniel Chamier’s** *Catholic Panstratice* <note>original: "Panstrat. Catholic.," a massive work of Reformed theology by the French pastor Daniel Chamier (1565–1621)</note>, book 2, chapter 16. However, if the first condition fails in the Church, the remaining conditions will also fail, for all are founded upon it, as we have shown above.

Furthermore, it becomes clear from the following that the **Fathers** <note>The influential early Christian theologians known as the "Church Fathers"</note> are able to err: they frequently failed to grasp the true meaning of Scripture, as **Robert Bellarmine** <note>A leading Jesuit cardinal and scholar (1542–1621) who defended Catholic doctrine against the Reformation</note> very eloquently teaches in book 3 of *On the Word of God*, chapter 3, sections 9 and 10. There he says: *The Fathers, in explaining Holy Scripture, were occasionally tripped up, taking figuratively what should have been taken literally, and literally what should have been taken figuratively.* He brings forward examples from **Origen**, **Papias**, **Justin Martyr**, **Irenaeus**, **Tertullian**, **Lactantius**, and others. **Jerome** teaches the same when he writes in his third volume, in a letter to **Theophilus**: *I know that I regard the Apostles in one way, and other commentators in another. The former always speak the truth; the latter, being men, wander from the path in certain matters.* <note>original: "Scio me aliter habere Apostolos..."</note> He also says that some of his own commentaries on the sacred books were lost to him, and that he had poured them out rather than written them down.

And the Fathers did not only err, but many were additionally led by partisan zeal. For they often say things out of benevolence and love; for instance, **Augustine**, in his *Handbook to Laurence* <note>original: "Enchiridiô ad Laurentium"</note> concerning **Purgatory** and his *Orations for the Dead*, admits in a way that both he and others have erred through an excessive abundance of love—such as when he thinks of his friends or his most dear mother, **Monica**. Often, they keep silent about other things out of fear of certain people, as the same author testifies in his 119th Letter to **Januarius**, chapter 19, in these words:

> *I do not dare to more freely condemn many things of this sort, on account of the need to avoid the scandals of certain persons—who are either holy or turbulent.* <note>original: "Multa hujusmodi propter nonnullarum..."</note>

Often, through excessive reverence for some, or through the tyranny of certain **Popes** and **Emperors**, they were driven into absurd interpretations of the Holy Books, as the history of the **Council of Ariminum** <note>A council in 359 AD where bishops were pressured into accepting an Arian-leaning creed</note> and the story of **Berengarius** <note>Berengarius of Tours (d. 1088), who was forced to recant his views on the Eucharist</note> abundantly testify.

But to these points, the **Papists** <note>A term used by reformers for supporters of the Roman Catholic Church</note> object that they do not deny that this or that individual Father was sometimes subject to error. However, they claim that all the Fathers consenting together have always taught the truth and never hallucinated, and that they are held as infallible interpreters only to that extent. But we reply, firstly, that this exception is futile: for if one can err in the interpretation of this passage of Scripture, and another in that one, why can they not all err together? Secondly, very few passages are found—indeed, perhaps none—concerning whose meaning absolutely all the Fathers have pronounced a judgment. Furthermore, all, or certainly almost all, erred together in explaining the passage in **Proverbs 8** <note>Proverbs 8:22 was a central text in the Arian controversy regarding whether Christ was "created" or "begotten"</note>. Finally, it is false to say that the consensus of absolutely all the Fathers is the standard of interpretation for the Catholics, since all their writings testify that they sometimes follow the interpretation of no one, or of only one...

<summary>This page argues against the infallibility of the Church Fathers and Councils, citing historical instances of widespread heresy (Arianism) and admitting that even figures like Augustine and Jerome were prone to error due to emotion, fear, or misunderstanding.</summary>

->OF THE DISPUTATION<-

attacked, much less conquered and subdued. For the opinion which they hold, however confusedly and obscurely proposed, nevertheless appears plausible enough, and is supported by many various arguments that even carry the appearance of weight. For this reason, we must linger upon it even longer, and a more lengthy discussion must be devoted to it. But this is the final labor, which once exhausted, a secure peace and tranquil rest awaits us. Come then, and without further wandering, let us attack the enemy with a spirited mind.

1. **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Reformed theologian</note> in his *Universal Catholic Panstratia* <note>original: "Panstrat. Catholic.", his most famous work defending Protestantism</note>, volume 1, book 16, chapter 6, defines the opinion of the Reformers thus, making no mention of the Holy Spirit: *"Catholics <note>meaning 'the truly orthodox', as Protestants often claimed the title</note> believe that the interpretation of Scripture cannot be held more securely, certainly, or truly than through Scripture itself."* More expressly, **Polanus** <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610)</note> in his *System of Theology* <note>original: "Syntagmat. Theolog."</note>, book 1, chapter 45, question 5, says: *"The norm of interpreting Scripture, and of judging whether the interpretation of Holy Scripture be true or false, is Holy Scripture itself, which is the voice of God."* But **Wendelin** <note>Marcus Friedrich Wendelin (1584–1652)</note> explains it a little differently in his *Christian Theology*, Introduction, chapter 3, thesis 6, explanation 1: *"The supreme right,"* he says, *"of interpreting the Scriptures belongs to the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scripture, or to the Scripture, which is the voice of the Holy Spirit."* In almost the same words, **Walæus** <note>Antonius Walæus (1573–1639), a Dutch theologian</note> says in his *Handbook of the Christian Religion*, in the section on the interpretation of Scripture: *"Holy Scripture is its own interpreter; or rather the Holy Spirit speaking in Holy Scripture."* And in his *Theological Commonplaces* regarding the interpretation of Holy Scripture: *"We say that God Himself speaking in the Scriptures is the supreme Interpreter of the Scriptures; and shortly thereafter: For we say that God so tempered Holy Scripture that it is not only the norm, but also its own interpreter."* In these passages, certain roles in interpreting Scripture seem to be assigned even to God and the Holy Spirit, just as **Whitaker** <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595), a leading English Protestant academic</note> asserts in his *Disputation on Holy Scripture* against the Papists of this time, Controversy 1, question 5, chapter 8: *"Our opinion is that the supreme judgment and authority of interpreting the Scriptures ought not to be granted to the Church, but to Scripture itself, and to the Holy Spirit both speaking openly in the Scriptures and secretly confirming the same in our hearts."* Still more clearly, more brilliantly, and more distinctly, **Tilenus** <note>Daniel Tilenus (1563–1633), a Silesian-born theologian</note> explains in his *System of Theological Disputations*, part 1, disputation 8, theses 1 and 2, in these words: *"The true Church of Christ claims the supreme right and authority of interpreting for the Holy Spirit alone speaking in the Scriptures: and it establishes that the true sense of Scripture, as far as internal <term>conviction</term> <note>original: "πληροφορίαν", meaning full assurance or certainty</note> is concerned, proceeds from Him alone; but as far as external persuasion is concerned, it is best taken from Scripture itself."* And **Scarpius** <note>John Sharp (c. 1572–1647), a Scottish theologian and professor in France</note> in his *Course of Theology*, in the section on Holy Scripture, much more at length in this manner: *"This is the opinion of the Orthodox: That the supreme right, authority, and judgment of interpreting the Scriptures belongs to the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scripture, and that the interpretation of Scripture is not tied to a certain seat or succession of men, as the Papists wish, but to the Holy Spirit secretly [interpreting] the Scriptures in the hearts..."*

->PARADOXES CHAPTER X.<-

ought to be attributed to the one who confirms the faithful. For these things are not at odds when we say this power resides with Scripture and with the Holy Spirit. For the Holy Spirit is said to be the supreme interpreter because, in order for us to be certainly persuaded of the true sense of Scripture, it is necessary for us to be illuminated by the Holy Spirit; otherwise, we would never attain that full assurance <note>original Greek: πληροφορίαν (plērophorian), meaning absolute certainty or conviction</note> which resides in the minds of the faithful, no matter what other means we might use. And this is the "internal persuasion" which affects us alone; for in this way, we do not compel others to accept the sense and judgment of the Holy Spirit. However, Scripture itself—or the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture—is called the supreme interpreter and judge with respect to "external persuasion." Therefore, in order to persuade others, we must turn to this external judgment of Scripture.

2. And I have chosen to bring these points forward at such length partly so that we might draw out and propose the opinion of the Reformers <note>The Protestant theologians of the Reformation</note> from them, and partly so that it might be clear how they themselves express and define it. In my judgment, they perform this quite obscurely and confusedly. For the earlier writers, making no mention of internal or external persuasion, sometimes name Scripture (which is the voice of God or the Holy Spirit), and sometimes God or the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scriptures. By these titles, they seem to me to understand nothing other than Scripture itself, and thus they establish that Scripture is its own interpreter. The later writers, however, by distinguishing between internal and external persuasion, assign the latter to Scripture and the former to the Holy Spirit. They seem to denote this by the phrase "the Holy Spirit speaking in the Scriptures," although they soon again confuse Scripture and the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture. 

But we, as far as we can follow from what has been said, think this is their actual meaning: namely, that Holy Scripture is a certain and infallible norm and rule for interpreting itself. By this rule—as an instrument obvious to the senses—the true and genuine sense of the divine word ought not only to be drawn out and demonstrated externally, but once drawn out, it ought to be weighed and explored. Yet truly, they believe no one among the faithful can be fully and clearly persuaded of this in their own mind unless the testimony of the Holy Spirit is added. This Spirit acts as an internal cause, imperceptible to the senses, who seals the truth of the interpretation in their hearts. 

If this is indeed their opinion, although it does not involve an open contradiction, it is nonetheless not without difficulty. For if the Holy Spirit is required *in addition* to Scripture itself to reach a full persuasion of the interpretation, then Scripture will not be that sufficient and infallible norm that they claim it to be—namely, a norm that possesses all the conditions required in a true interpreter which we enumerated above. For if Scripture cannot err in interpreting, nor leave room for appeal, but rather imbues men with certain knowledge and compels them to assent, there will be no further need for the testimony of the Holy Spirit. What more can anyone seek beyond those things? And if the testimony of the Holy Spirit is necessary, Scripture will not be able to 

<meta>catchword: compel,</meta>

<summary>The author analyzes the Reformed Protestant view that Scripture is its own interpreter, noting a distinction between external evidence (Scripture) and internal conviction (the Holy Spirit). He argues that if the Holy Spirit's internal testimony is necessary for certainty, then Scripture alone cannot be considered a sufficient or "infallible" norm for interpretation.</summary>

->D I S C U S S I O N S<-

to compel, nor to imbue men with certain knowledge, but it will leave room for challenge and be subject to error. But having set aside these things which I only wished to touch upon in passing, let us refute each part of this opinion separately; and first, indeed, that Scripture is not the norm for interpreting itself. <note>original: "norma seipsam interpretandi"</note>

### C H A P T E R XI.

*1. The opinion of the Reformers, by which Scripture is established as its own interpreter, is explained. 2. And its difficulties are presented. 3. The meanings of words and speeches become known not by Nature, but by human social interaction. 4. Common usage of speech is not the interpreter of Holy Scripture, 5. Nor is it established by the Reformers, nor is it even possible. 6. No passage of it is clear in itself; but all are ambiguous. 7. Nor is one passage always explained through another, or obscure ones through clear ones. 8. The difficulties raised are not removed by the rules commonly given by the Reformers, 9. Nor by the evasion brought forward by a certain person.*

1. Under the name of "Scripture," we must understand in this context the words, phrases, modes of speaking, sentences, and other things of that kind, pertaining to external speech and the languages used by the Sacred Authors. We do not, however, mean the "internal word" <note>original: "sermo internus," referring to the mental concept or divine meaning behind the words</note> or the intended sense of the speeches and sentences; for if this sense were already known and perceived, it would neither need interpreting, nor could it be the norm of interpretation. For who will try to extract the sense of a speech they have already grasped, or desire it to be extracted for them by another? Or who will attempt to explain an obscure and doubtful passage of Holy Scripture through the sense of another passage while neglecting and disregarding the actual words and ways of speaking? Surely no one who has correctly understood that an interpreter deals with words—and things concerning words—and hunts for and investigates the meaning from them. This is done by comparing and conferring the words and phrases of a speech already understood with the words and phrases of the speech they are still striving and struggling to understand.

Thus, the mind and opinion of the Reformers <note>The Protestant theologians of the Reformation</note>, and those siding with them, when they establish that Scripture is its own interpreter, can be nothing other than this: that the meanings of both individual words and entire speeches occurring in Scripture (whose sense is hidden) must be investigated and extracted by the help of other words and speeches of Scripture whose sense is already known and perceived. Antonius Walaeus <note>A prominent Dutch Reformed theologian (1573–1639) who helped draft the Canons of Dort</note> also confirms this same point in his *Handbook of the Christian Religion*, in the section on Scripture.

<summary>This page begins a formal refutation of the Protestant doctrine that "Scripture is its own interpreter," arguing that interpretation must focus on external linguistic elements rather than an assumed internal meaning.</summary>
<keywords>hermeneutics, Reformed theology, Antonius Walaeus, biblical interpretation, linguistic analysis, Protestant Reformation, clarity of Scripture</keywords>

This page picks up with the conclusion of that thought and moves into a detailed critique of the Protestant principle that "Scripture interprets itself."</meta>

### PARADOXES CHAPTER XI.

They interpret [this] with these words: *"Scripture makes manifest obscure places through comparison with clearer ones, just as an illustrious example of this exists in Nehemiah 8, verse 9."* <note>The author is citing a common Protestant defense of the principle known as the "Analogy of Faith."</note> So also, throughout the New Testament, Christ and the Apostles explain more obscure Scripture through clearer Scripture. And Danaeus <note>Lambert Daneau (c. 1530–1595), a prominent French Reformed theologian.</note> in his *Christian Introduction to Commonplaces*, part 4, book 3, chapter 6, says: *"The method of interpreting Scripture from Scripture is when—as Augustine says to Volusianus—passages are compared with other passages, Holy Scripture is compared with Holy Scripture, and the explanation of one obscure passage is brought forth from another easier passage of the same Scripture, etc."* Similarly, and indeed at length, Whitaker <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595), an influential English Protestant theologian who defended the Reformation against Catholic critics.</note> in his *Disputation on Holy Scripture*, against the Papists of this time, Controversy I, Question 5, chapter 13, which is titled, *"That Scripture must be interpreted from Scripture."* He says that Scripture interprets itself most clearly and transparently, if we are willing to pay attention to Scripture interpreting itself, even if not in every single place so that no doubt remains; nevertheless, it does so in most places and the most necessary ones, and in the highest articles of our faith. And we have examples of this interpretation in the Scriptures. For Scripture, where it speaks more obscurely, explains itself sometimes immediately in the same place, and sometimes abundantly in other places. And he illustrates both kinds with a few examples, and then subjoins: "Infinite are the examples of this kind, in which it appears that the Holy Spirit took care that what he seemed to have said a little more obscurely, he would soon explain clearly and free the reader from every doubt." And soon after: "And this is the custom of Scripture, that to achieve the true understanding of one passage, it sends us to another, and in this way we do not stay and rest in any one part of Scripture; but we embrace the whole body of the Sacred volumes by our reading and meditation." Therefore, passages must be compared with passages if we wish to rightly understand and hold anything in the Scriptures. And Basil <note>Basil of Caesarea (329–379), one of the great Greek Church Fathers.</note> also supports this in his *Ascetica* or *Shorter Rules*, Question 276:

> *"Those things which are ambiguous, and which seem to be said obscurely in certain passages of Holy Scripture, are illustrated by those things which are open and clear in other places."* <note>original Greek: ἀσκητ.</note>

2. This opinion, however, explained in this way—no matter how plausible it may appear at first glance—will be found, upon deeper inspection and more accurate examination, to suffer from many and great difficulties. For what connection does one passage of Scripture have with others? Or what right or prerogative does this one have over that one, so that it should be interpreted through or from it? Will those things which Matthew or Mark relate that Christ said, and which seem somewhat obscure to the reader, be illustrated from those things which Luke or John record, and vice versa? Could not any of those Holy Writers, if they were still alive, rightly and deservedly grieve that their writings either serve as an explanation for others or are made subject to them? Would they not argue with the greatest reason that these interpreters do violence to them and their writings, and use them improperly—they who recorded in writing what they held to be true, so that for posterity and their late-born descendants

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of the Protestant principle of "Scripture interpreting itself." The previous page ended mid-sentence while discussing how the Holy Spirit inspired the biblical authors to interpret and explain earlier texts. This page questions whether this inspiration implies that one specific book or author has the inherent right to dictate the meaning of another.</meta>

# OF THE DISPUTATION

...they might make [these things] known; but was it not rather so that they might either themselves explain the monuments <note>In this context, "monuments" refers to the written records or works of earlier authors.</note> of others, or have them explained to them in turn? Was not Paul equally inspired by the Divine Spirit when writing to the Romans as when writing to the Corinthians? Or was he [inspired] any less than Peter, or any other of the Apostles or Evangelists? Why then must this author be explained by that one, or that one by this one, or even by himself?

Indeed, for every passage of Holy Scripture, its own authority is established, its own truth is inherent, and its own proper sense exists, which neither depends on another nor ought to be determined by another. Nor do we ever read that any right of interpretation was given to the writings of one Apostle over those of another. But let us grant that one passage of Scripture has the right to interpret another; by what reasoning, I ask, can we be certain that the meaning of one passage is exactly the same as another? For although the same or similar words and modes of speaking occur in both, the sense can nonetheless be different, and words may be taken literally in one place and figuratively—or allegorically, or in some other way—in another. *For the same expression and the same phrase do not everywhere sound and mean the same thing in the Scriptures, but rather [mean] different or even contrary things,* as Stapleton <note>Thomas Stapleton (1535–1598) was a prominent English Catholic academic and controversialist who argued against the Protestant doctrine of the "clarity" of Scripture.</note> holds and shows with many examples in Book 11 of his *Controversies*, chapter 10.

Hence, along with Dannhauer <note>Johann Conrad Dannhauer (1603–1666) was a Lutheran theologian and hermeneutics expert. Despite being a Protestant, his logical rigor is used here to show the limits of biblical comparison.</note> in his *Idea of the Good Interpreter and the Malicious Calumniator*, part 1, section 3, article 11, *§ 95, we establish that a <term>comparison based on an analogy of pure and bare proportion</term> <note>original: "Collationem ex analogiâ proportionis meræ nudæque"; this refers to comparing two passages simply because they use similar words, without proof that they share the same theological intent.</note> is not argumentative, but only illustrative by probable reasoning. For it does not follow that because this is the sense of a parallel passage, it is therefore also the sense of this one. [Or] a word is taken this way elsewhere, therefore it is also [taken this way] here. For this would truly be an argument from a particular instance [to a general rule].*

Furthermore, very often something (these are the words of the same Stapleton in the same place) *seems to be said similarly or dissimilarly, not so much from the similarity and dissimilarity of the things themselves, as from the emotion and the prejudiced opinion of those performing the comparison.*

But let us again concede that the Sacred Authors intended to indicate and hint at the same thing in two or more different places. In that case, the sense of the passage that will interpret the others must necessarily be known and understood; otherwise, it would be unable to perform its duty. But from where is that sense known? If it is referred back to another [passage], I shall inquire the same regarding this one in turn, and thus proceed continuously until finally one or more are reached (for one cannot proceed to infinity since Scripture is finite, nor can a [vicious] circle be committed without manifest absurdity) upon which we may plant our feet, or which we may establish are not to be explained through others. Since the sense of these [passages] is applied to interpreting other places—and therefore must be understood—it will be permitted to ask again from where it became known. To this question, it seems to me that nothing can be answered except that it is known through itself. For Scripture is asserted by the Reformers to be perspicuous and clear in all chapters that are necessary to be known for salvation <note>This refers to the "Perspicuity of Scripture," the belief that the Bible's essential message is accessible to everyone.</note>, so much so that even the simpler and more unrefined Christians are able to understand it, according to that [saying] of Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Church Fathers.</note> in his third Epistle to Volusianus: *In those things that are necessary for salvation, in such great...*

<meta>Catchword: [in] eis (in them)</meta>

<summary>This page presents a logical and theological critique of the Protestant method of using parallel scriptural passages to interpret one another. The author argues that words can have different meanings in different contexts and that such comparisons often reflect the interpreter's bias rather than the text's inherent meaning.</summary>

### CHAPTER XI. OF THE PARADOXES.

...one does not arrive at them <note>The Scriptures</note> through difficulty. Likewise: *The style of speech in which Holy Scripture is woven together is accessible to all, yet penetrable by very few. Those things which it contains openly, it speaks to the hearts of the unlearned and the learned alike, like a familiar friend without disguise. But as for those things which it hides in Mysteries, it does not elevate them with proud eloquence—lest a slow and uneducated mind would not dare to approach, like a poor man approaching a rich one—but it invites all with humble speech, whom it may not only feed with manifest truth, but also exercise with secret truth, having the same quality in its obvious parts as it has in its hidden ones. Yet, lest the open parts be loathed, those same open things are desired again; once desired, they are in a way renewed; and once renewed, they are sweetly imparted.* <note>original Latin: "Modus dicendi, quo sancta Scriptura contexitur...". This is a famous passage from Saint Augustine’s Letter 137 to Volusianus, explaining that the Bible is written to be simple enough for everyone but deep enough for the greatest scholars.</note> And from *On Christian Doctrine*, book 2, chapter 6: *Those who do not find what they seek at all suffer from hunger. However, those who do not seek because they have it ready at hand often wither from boredom. In both cases, this lethargy must be avoided. Therefore, the Holy Spirit has magnificently and wholesomely moderated the Holy Scriptures so that he might meet hunger with the clearer passages and wipe away boredom with the more obscure ones. For almost nothing is pulled out from those obscurities that is not found to be stated most plainly elsewhere.* <note>original Latin: "Qui prorsus non inveniunt...". Augustine (354–430 AD) was a foundational Christian philosopher whose works are central to Western theology.</note> But truly, what do they call an address that is "clear through itself," or a sense that is "known through itself"? To tell the truth, I have not been able to find anything solid on this matter—anything that could withstand a sharper inquiry—among all the <term>Theologians</term> <note>Scholars of divinity and religious doctrine.</note> I have happened to see so far. Therefore, it is not necessary to linger on reviewing and rejecting their opinions. To counter this one exception (namely, that certain passages are clear through themselves), which we brought up above and which is the only one they can offer for themselves in this question, we will first recall to memory certain things explained by us at the beginning of this treatise.

3. We said above in Chapter 2 that <term>Words</term> <note>original Latin: "Vocabula"</note> are the marks and signs of concepts and things, and that every speech is composed of and consists of these. Therefore, things and concepts will be the "things signified," and every speech will signify several concepts and things either joined together or separated from one another. We said further in that same chapter that the entire denotation of words, and thus the sense of speech, did not arise from nature but from human institution, and even now depends on the usage of those humans, within whose power lies the judgment, the law, and the norm of speaking. <note>This is a reference to the Roman poet Horace’s "Ars Poetica," where he argues that language is a living thing governed by the "usage" of its speakers.</note> From which it follows that all meanings of words and the senses of speeches are not made known by Nature or by a natural investigation of light; rather, they must be learned by interacting with people using that language—namely by speaking and hearing—or learned from a teacher’s living voice, or drawn from <term>Lexicons</term> <note>Dictionaries.</note> and <term>Grammars</term>.

4. When, therefore, the <term>Reformed</term> <note>A term for Protestants, specifically those following the tradition of Calvin or Zwingli.</note> establish that certain passages of Holy Scripture are clear and transparent "through themselves," they cannot mean that transparency by which the principles of the sciences—commonly called the "first and common notions"—are said to be clear through themselves. For these latter things are understood by the light of nature without any demonstration, which we have just now shown cannot take place in those <note>Scriptural</note> passages. But to understand...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Reformed (Protestant) position on the clarity of Scripture. Having previously argued against the idea that Scripture is its own sole interpreter, the author now examines the claim that certain passages are "clear in themselves" based on common linguistic usage.</meta>

# EXERCITATIONS

...they seem to consider that clarity to be the way the meaning of a speech becomes known to anyone skilled and expert in the language in which it is expressed, without any interpretation or explanation of the words. In this view, the phrase *by itself* does not deny that one must first know the language (acquired through practice or instruction), but only denies the need for a further interpretation. If this is indeed their thinking, they are making the "common usage of language" the interpreter of Holy Scripture. For since they establish that obscure passages are explained by clearer ones, and that the sense of these clearer passages is known *by itself*—that is, as we have just said, through common usage—then, accurately speaking, Scripture will not be its own interpreter. Rather, only certain parts of it would interpret others, as they themselves acknowledge. Thus, for the whole of Scripture, common usage is a "remote" guide for obscure passages but a "proximate" <note>In logic, a "proximate" cause or guide is one that is immediate and direct, whereas a "remote" one is indirect.</note> guide for clear ones.

However, we believe it is manifest that this "common usage" is not a suitable or legitimate interpreter of Holy Writ. In fact, it is the primary source of almost all false interpretations. This is because the great mass of people, from whom common usage is derived, is prone to error and fosters perverse and monstrous concepts about God and divine matters. If anyone brings these popular concepts to the Holy Scriptures and applies them to understand and explain the text, what else, I ask, can they produce but expositions that are as far as possible from the meaning intended by God and the sacred writers?

5. Nor can the Reformed scholars mean this—although, no matter how diligently and attentively I have searched, I have not been able to find anything else they could mean by "clear in itself." If any are found so unrefined or thoughtless as to hold this view, its error is made plain by the fact that many passages occur in the Word of God which consist of words and ways of construction perfectly well known from common usage, and are thus "clear" in that sense. Yet, interpreters do not accept them in the sense that offers itself spontaneously to the reader at first glance; instead, they are dragged into a different meaning, sometimes far removed from the original. We produced some examples of this in Chapter 3, Section 7, to which the following can be added: *Do not swear at all: etc. and Let your word be Yes, Yes; No, No, etc.* <note>Matthew 5:34, 37</note>. *Do not resist an evil person: but if anyone strikes you on your right cheek, turn the other to him also. And if anyone wants to sue you and take your cloak, let him have your tunic as well. And whoever forces you to go one mile, go with him two, etc.* <note>Matthew 5:39, 40, 41</note>. There are many other such passages whose obvious and open sense, based on common usage, these scholars would surely not reject if they truly derived the clarity of speech from that source.

6. But indeed, what then is, or ought to be, a "passage clear in itself" to them? Above in Chapter 3, we treated at length the obscurity and clarity of speech, and I distinguish— <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "distinxi-" (I distinguished), to be continued on the next page.</note>

He points out that even Reformed theologians ignore the "obvious" meaning of clear commands, such as Christ’s prohibition on swearing oaths, when it suits their doctrine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical dissection of biblical interpretation. Having just discussed how common language usage is insufficient for interpreting holy texts, the author now distinguishes between the literal meaning of words (the "simple sense"), the author’s intended meaning (the "true sense"), and the factual "truth" of a statement.</meta>

### PARADOX CHAPTER XI.

...and we have distinguished each in respect to a twofold sense, namely, that which is **simply stated**, or that which is **true**, both of which we have further distinguished from the **truth of the speech** itself. This is so that every interpreter, in interpreting any speech, must accurately consider these three things: for one should not persuade oneself that as soon as one has found one of these, they have also discovered the others. For it is possible for the *sense* of a speech to be known without its *true sense* or its *truth*; yet there can be no *true sense* without a *sense*. Furthermore, the *sense* and the *truth* can be known while the *true sense* remains ignored. And finally, the *sense* and the *true sense* can be perceived while the *truth* remains unknown. All these things are manifest in themselves to anyone paying attention. 

When, therefore, a certain passage in the Holy Scriptures is established as clear and transparent in itself, we are either talking about the clarity of the *sense simply stated*, or the *true sense*. It is apparent that it is not about the former, both from what we have just said—namely, that in such a case the common usage of speech would be the Interpreter of the Scriptures—and because the question here is not about the *simply stated* sense, but about the *true* sense of the Holy Scriptures. For we are not debating any random interpretation, but only the genuine and authentic interpretation of Scripture. Since, according to their own opinion, this is done through passages that are clear in themselves, that clarity must belong not to just any sense, but only to the *true* one. For <note>original: "Non enim ex quôlibet ligno fit Mercurius"</note> "Mercury is not made from just any wood," nor is the true sense elicited from just any literal meaning. <note>The proverb about Mercury means that not every material is suitable for a high purpose; here, it means you cannot derive a divine truth from a mere literal reading of words without deeper scrutiny.</note>

Therefore, they are speaking of the clarity of the *true sense*. But in respect to this, we frankly confess that we cannot see how a passage can be transparent "by itself," and we heartily desire to be taught by them. For since in every passage there is that twofold sense (or at least there could be), a certain <term>κριτήριον</term> <note>Greek: *kriterion*, meaning a standard or benchmark for judging.</note> must necessarily be provided by which they can be infallibly distinguished—if two senses occur—or, if only one occurs, to prove that more do not reside within it. From this it is clear that it is of no importance if someone should object or argue that the *simply stated sense* and the *true sense* often coincide, and that only those passages where this happens (and which have only a simple sense) are considered by them to be "clear in themselves," rather than the passages where they differ. For since this does not always hold true (as is clear enough from the examples provided), a mark or rule must be brought forward by which one can explore with certainty whether it *does* hold true; and thus, those passages will be known to be such not "by themselves," but through that <term>criterion</term>, or that mark or rule—which is contrary to their hypothesis. 

Furthermore, that objection utterly collapses because there is no speech that does not admit multiple senses, as is clear enough from what we said in Chapter 3; and specifically, that there is no passage of Holy Scripture that cannot be explained in many ways, as asserted by a Reformed theologian of no small reputation, Daniel Chamier, in his *Panstratia Catholica*, Volume 1, Book 16, Chapter 1, Section 1, in these words: *"It is certain that nothing is so easy, so clear, or so certain that it cannot be distorted in various ways, even into contrary meanings. Thus experience has taught that all heresies have taken their origin, or certainly their growth, from the Scriptures being wrongly understood."* For what occurs more fre-

...runs through the disputes of the Holy Fathers regarding the resolution of passages objected to by Heretics? Finally, in these wretched times, it is not only Catholics who strenuously defend their faith from the Scriptures: but also the **Papists** <note>A term used in this period by opponents of the Roman Catholic Church to refer to its followers</note> (and I add, the **Lutherans**, **Anabaptists**, **Socinians**, **Arminians**, and all other Christians) dare to misuse them repeatedly, and display their authority as if it were their own. This could not happen unless different people understood the same passages in different ways. Since, therefore, there is nothing in **Holy Scripture** that is not found to be ambiguous, and not just any sense is true, but only that which fits the mind of the Writer—and this is not obvious in itself—then no passage will be clear in itself.

7. And to all this, add that there are many passages of Scripture which are spoken only once in the divine books and cannot be compared with other similar passages; many such examples are brought forward by **Stapleton** <note>Thomas Stapleton (1535–1598), an English Catholic controversialist</note> in book 11 of his *Controversies*, chapter 10. Nor is it always possible in the explanation of Scripture to arrive at the passages which they themselves call "clear"; rather, one must very often stop at the more obscure ones, such as many provided in the Old Testament by the writings of the Prophets, as well as the book of **Job**, and in the New Testament, the **Revelation** of John. Yet, when the meaning of these is explained by interpreters despite their obscurity, that meaning is not drawn from clear passages, nor from Scripture itself, but from elsewhere—unless they decide that the obscure can be explained by the obscure, and thus openly contradict themselves.

8. Finally, all these listed difficulties are not relieved, nor is the opinion of the **Reformers** supported by the Rules they bring forward for this purpose, such as: *One must not always stick to the bare letter, but the true literal sense must be extracted, which is sometimes proper, and sometimes figurative; often even an **Allegorical** sense must be sought. The proper literal sense must always be retained in the explanation of Scripture, unless it is false. And always (which is **Augustine's** rule) the propriety of words is to be kept, unless some good reason suggests a trope* <note>A figure of speech or non-literal use of a word</note>. *When the <term>rhetón</term> <note>original: ῥητὸν — the "literal word" or "the thing said"</note> breeds absurdity if taken literally, we must flee to the <term>diánoian</term>* <note>original: διάνοιαν — the "intention," "thought," or "deeper meaning"</note>. *What is said figuratively is not to be interpreted literally, and vice versa. Such subjects as are permitted by the predicates, and predicates must be taken according to the nature of the subjects; or, the determination of the predicate must be sought from the nature of the subject. If it is a prescriptive saying, either forbidding a crime or a wicked deed, or commanding utility or beneficence, it is not figurative; but if it seems to command a crime or wicked deed, or forbid utility or beneficence, it is figurative. In interpreting Scriptures, before all things, consider what befits God. Let the sense not be brought into the text, but born from it; and words are understood <term>according to their relation to the subject</term>* <note>original: ὡς ἔχει πρὸς ὅ</note> etc. For (besides the fact that if these Rules are to be applied in interpreting the word of God from outside the Scripture, then Scripture does not seem to be a sufficient norm for interpreting itself, which goes against their own opinion); I would like to ask the authors, supporters, and collectors of all such Rules: how will it be known what is in

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the idea that Scripture has a "self-evident" clarity. Having previously argued that ambiguity is inherent in the text, he now examines and dismisses a common theological rule used to distinguish literal from figurative meanings.</meta>

-> PARADOXES CHAPTER 11 <-

8. In **Holy Scripture**, what is stated literally, what figuratively, and what allegorically? From where can it be known which sense is false? What is a sound reason that would persuade one to use a trope <note>A figure of speech or a non-literal expression</note>? When does <margin>seeking</margin> <note>original: ζητεῖν</note> produce difficulty? What kinds of subjects are permitted to be predicated of others, etc.? From where will it be recognized which expression forbids a disgrace or a crime, or commands usefulness or beneficence? From where will it be understood what is fitting for God, whether the sense is innate, etc.? If they know, let them point it out, and at the same time, they will reveal the **Method** for explaining Scripture. But I have been able to find no such thing anywhere among them or others; except for this one thing, which I remember either having read in someone's work or heard from someone, and I thought it worth the effort to add and weigh it here.

9. Here, if my memory does not fail me, a person disputing *whether, for instance, Holy Scripture is to be explained according to right reason*, asserted that in no way should Scripture be subjected to our reason. Having made this assertion, he then asked: How then will it be clear that one passage is to be taken and interpreted literally, but another figuratively? He answers this question in the following manner. Whenever **Holy Scripture** clearly and intentionally teaches something, or <term>dogmatizes</term> <note>original: δογματίζει; to lay down as an established and undeniable doctrine</note>, and in another place it seems to establish the opposite by chance or as a consequence: then that clear passage is to be understood literally and according to the letter; while the other is to be interpreted figuratively and according to the first. For example, the **Holy Scriptures** clearly <term>dogmatize</term> <note>teach as a core tenet</note> that *God is one*, yet in other places God speaks in the plural number <note>Such as in Genesis 1:26, "Let us make man in our image"</note>, from which it seems to follow that He is not one. Since, therefore, the former is taught intentionally, while the latter is deduced as a consequence from those words, these texts are to be taken improperly <note>meaning metaphorically or figuratively</note>, and must be explained according to those which ought to be taken literally. Similarly, it is commanded that we must be careful not to imagine God as having a body, for it says in **Deuteronomy 4**: *Take good heed to yourselves (for you observed no likeness, etc.) lest you make for yourselves a carved image, the likeness of any idol, etc. And later: If you make a carved image, the likeness of any thing, etc. I call heaven and earth to witness against you this day, that you shall soon utterly perish from off that land, etc.* In these passages, it is clearly taught that God is incorporeal <note>without a physical, material body</note>. Therefore, we are bound by this precept, and not by reason, to interpret all texts from which it seems it could be gathered that God is corporeal according to this precept. This is the opinion of that man, by which he thinks the question is satisfied in every part.

But he is miserably mistaken. For what is said to be "clear" to him in the Scriptures? For the whole consists of words, and no words are so clear that they do not admit multiple senses, as we have just shown: and what is clear to him, another will judge to be obscure. As **Stapleton** <note>Thomas Stapleton (1535–1598), a prominent English Catholic theologian and controversialist</note> has it in Book 10 of *Controversies*, chapter 3: *The manifest passages of Scripture are not manifest to all. For what is manifest to one is obscure to another, either of slower wit or differently affected. "This is my body" is clear to us, but to **Berengar**, **Wycliffe**, **Lutherans**, and **Calvinists**...*

# OF THE EXERCISE

...opponents. "The Word became flesh" is clear to us, but to the Arians, Nestorians, Eutychians, and Apollinarians <note>These were various early Christian groups whose views on the nature of Christ were declared heretical by the central Church</note> it was obscure. Furthermore, although Scripture may seem to one person to <term>dogmatize</term> <note>original: δογματίζειν; to state as an official doctrine</note> that God is incorporeal <note>Meaning God has no physical body</note>, many others will be led to the opposite opinion through that same Scripture. Moreover, the text he cites proves nothing; for those stupid and dull-witted people who imagine a corporeal God for themselves will object that when Moses says, *Take heed, for you noticed no form, etc.*, he meant nothing other than that there is no body to which God can be compared or likened. But let us indeed grant that something is expressed clearly and transparently in the Holy Scriptures. If, then, they <term>dogmatized</term> and asserted that *nothing is something, or something is nothing*, should they be understood literally and just as they sounded, even if they were contrary to our reason? Truly,

->Let the Jew Apella believe it;<-
->not I,<-

nor would any person of sound mind, not even the opponent himself. For when Scripture seems to contradict itself, he will be forced to admit that it is permissible to use reason so that one can distinguish which passages are to be understood and interpreted literally, and which are to be understood improperly and figuratively. Such are: *God is not led by repentance* (Num. 23:23 <note>Usually cited as verse 19</note>; 1 Sam. 15:29, etc.) and yet that *the same God repented that he had made man* (Gen. 6:6) and *repented of the evil he said he would do to his people* (Exod. 32:14). And many other things of this kind are found everywhere. From this, I think no one would be so thoughtless as to want to cure the difficulty brought against the aforementioned Rules with that escape-clause, since nothing is more open in the Holy Scriptures, and especially in the New Testament—and more familiar to Christ—than to teach through parables and <term>tropes</term> <note>Figurative ways of speaking</note>. As the Evangelist Mark expressly testifies in chapter 4, verses 33 and 34, in these words: *With many such parables he (Jesus) spoke the word to them, as they were able to hear it. But without a parable he did not speak to them.* Therefore, other remedies must be summoned and applied to remove that difficulty. It is indeed true that when Reformed theologians <note>Protestant thinkers following the tradition of John Calvin and others</note> are engaged in explaining the context of the Holy Scriptures themselves and examining the interpretations of their adversaries, they call upon Philosophy as an aid (as we noted above). They borrow from it principles known by the light of nature and demonstrated conclusions, with which they strengthen and confirm their own exposition of an ambiguous passage. But, when in the treatment of commonplaces <note>Standard topics or themes of theology</note> a judgment must be offered regarding the interpreter of Scripture, they with unanimous consent strip this duty from Philosophy and claim it for Scripture. They deny with their mouths what they admit by their deeds, not noticing how many and how great the difficulties are (as we have seen) that weigh down this dogma, and with what weak and flimsy <note>original: siculneis; literally 'made of willow-wood,' implying something fragile or unreliable</note> arguments (as we shall see) it is built up.

<header>PARADOXES CHAPTER 12</header>

## CHAPTER XII.

->*That the opinion of the Reformers is not proved 1. from Nehemiah 8:9.*<-
->*Nor is it 2. shown from 1 Peter 1:20.*<-

Having sufficiently examined, in our judgment, the opinion of the Reformers itself, let us now pass to the Reasons by which they strive to support and fortify it. While I have been engaged in the examination of these, it has several times occurred to me to wonder that men who are most learned, and otherwise sufficiently accurate and discerning, can be so subtle and rigid when examining and dismantling the arguments of their adversaries, yet so lenient and coarse when proposing and strengthening their own—as if they were destitute of all foundation and stability. They prove by their own example that saying of Christ in *Luke 6:42: Men can see the speck <note>original: "festucam"</note> in the eyes of others, but do not notice the beam <note>original: "trabem"</note> in their own.* Even Theologians labor under their own prejudices (which they, above all others, ought to keep far away) while they embrace their preconceived opinions, received from their ancestors, and the arguments used to build them up, without any real examination of either the arguments or the opinion. For if they had undertaken even a slight investigation, they would have clearly detected the futility of the one and the weakness of the others; thus they would have searched for the truth, found it, and embraced it—which I still hope and trust they will do with me. But let us return from this detour to the main road.

The arguments, therefore, the refutation of which we must undertake here, we shall divide into two classes. The first will contain <term>inartificial arguments</term> <note>In classical rhetoric, these are "extrinsic" proofs, such as testimonies or documents, rather than those invented by the logic of the speaker</note>, namely the testimonies drawn from Holy Scripture which seem to the Reformers to support their opinion. The second will contain <term>artificial arguments</term> <note>Logical proofs "created" by the speaker through reason, the "analogy of faith," and other intellectual methods</note>, taken from Reason, the <term>Analogy of Faith</term> <note>The principle that individual Bible passages should be interpreted in a way that is consistent with the whole of Christian doctrine</note>, and elsewhere. Of the passages cited from Scripture, there are two principal ones: one from the Old Testament and the other from the New.

1. The passage cited from the Old Testament occurs in Nehemiah chapter 8, verse 9 <note>In modern Bibles, this is usually Nehemiah 8:8</note>. There it is narrated concerning Ezra and others *that they were reading that book, the law of God, clearly and by explaining the sense, they gave understanding through the Scripture itself.* From this it is inferred that if Scripture was already explained by itself in the past, the same method ought to hold true now. (See Chamier, *Panstratia Catholica*, Vol. 1, book 16, chap. 6, §10; Scarp, *Theological Course*, Topic on Scripture, Controversy 8; Alsted, *Precognition of Theology*, book 2, chap. 95, and others).

But we respond that this passage, based on the Hebrew original, admits other versions besides the one cited, which is that of Tremellius and Junius <note>Two famous 16th-century Hebraists and translators</note>. For the last words, in which the greatest force of the argument is situated, Piscator <note>Johannes Piscator, a Reformed theologian known for his Bible commentaries</note> translates as "while reading"; and in the notes on this chapter...

<sig>K 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...ter</meta>

<summary>The author begins a systematic refutation of the Reformed doctrine that Scripture interprets itself, starting with an analysis of Nehemiah 8:8/9. He argues that his opponents are blinded by tradition and that their favorite proof-texts are based on questionable translations.</summary>
<keywords>Reformers, Nehemiah 8:9, Biblical interpretation, Ezra, logic, inartificial arguments, artificial arguments, Piscator, Tremellius, Junius</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Reformed interpretation of Nehemiah 8:8. On the previous page, the author began arguing that the biblical text does not support the idea that Scripture interprets itself without external aid.</meta>

<header>OF THE DISCOURSE</header>
<page-num>78</page-num>

...[scri]ptures, he says that in the Hebrew it is "in the reading" <note>original: "in lectione"</note>. Thus also the old interpreter <note>The Vulgate, the standard Latin Bible translation</note>, following the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, did not translate it as *they gave understanding through the scripture itself*, but rather, *they understood when it was read*. Ravanellus <note>Petrus Ravanellus (1585–1662), a French Protestant theologian and author of the *Bibliotheca Sacra*</note> confesses in his *Sacred Library*, under the title "On Scripture," Distinction 7, Number 7, that this version also corresponds to the Hebrew context and can be admitted. Since therefore *it is permitted* <note>original: "fas sit"</note>, as the same Ravanellus says in the stated place, for an Interpreter to follow either of two interpretations when both fit the text without error, it follows that if the Reformers <note>Protestant theologians of the Reformation</note> wish to build their opinion upon this passage, they must clearly show that their interpretation alone is the true and genuine one. 

But so that it may be clear that no strength is added to their opinion from this source, let us examine the whole passage. The text is to be drawn from the Hebrew fountain thus: *They read in the book, in the law of God, unrolled, and with attention, and they understood the reading.* Vatablus <note>François Vatable (d. 1547), a distinguished French Hebraist</note> interprets the passage in almost the same way: *They read in the book itself, in the law of God, distinctly, and they applied their understanding, and they understood the scripture itself.* The Syriac Translation <note>The Peshitta, an ancient Syriac version of the Bible</note> almost agrees with these: *And while they read the book of the law of the Lord unrolled, they perceived it, and understood its reading.* Nor do the commentators on the Vernacular version <note>The *Statenvertaling*, the Dutch Authorized Version</note>—made by the order of the High and Mighty Lords of the States-General of the United Netherlands—differ much; from the original text they translated the last part of the passage in the margin thus: *And by applying understanding, they understood the reading*, that is, that which was being read, whether the Scripture or in the congregation. Yet (which seemed strange to me as I read it) in the main text itself, they followed the version of Tremellius and Junius <note>Immanuel Tremellius and Franciscus Junius, 16th-century scholars who produced a famous Latin translation of the Bible</note>, except for the final word. 

From all these, however, we gather this sense: namely, that Ezra and the others read in the book, which was the Law of God, and indeed (in my judgment) *unrolled* <note>original: "expansô"</note>, that is, opened and spread out—even though many hold it means *distinctly* <note>original: "explanatè"</note>. For the Jews did not use paper books, bound in our fashion and divided into leaves and pages, but rather rolling and folding ones. Indeed, like almost all the ancients, they wrote on "books of trees"—that is, on barks—which they closed by rolling together and opened by unrolling; for this reason, they were also called *volumes* <note>From the Latin *volvere*, meaning "to roll"</note>. Something similar still holds among modern Jews, who keep the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible (the Torah)</note> in their synagogues in this folding and rolling form. They read, therefore, in the book, unrolled and opened in this manner, and *they applied their understanding*, that is, they paid attention or were attentive—the readers as much as the listeners—and *they understood the reading*, or *that which was read*; that is, they perceived its mind and meaning, which again can be attributed to both readers and listeners. And if this was the genuine sense of this passage, as we believe, it is clear that nothing can be drawn from it that supports the cause of the Reformers.

2. The second passage, which is brought forward from the New Testament, is found in the Second Epistle of St. Peter, Chapter 1, Verse 20, and sounds like this: *Knowing this first, that no*

He emphasizes the physical nature of ancient scrolls ("volumes") to explain the term "unrolled" in the text.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the Reformed doctrine that Scripture is self-interpreting. He is specifically addressing the interpretation of 2 Peter 1:20–21, which was frequently cited by Protestant theologians to argue that "prophecy of scripture is of its own interpretation."</meta>

->## CHAPTER XII. OF THE PARADOXES.<-

**That the prophecy of scripture is of its own explanation.** By these words, the following authors believe the Apostle [Peter] teaches: Daniel Chamier in his *Panstratia Catholica* <note>original: "Panstrat. Cathol." — A massive work of Reformed systematic theology</note>, Volume 1, Book 1, Chapter 19, and Book 16, Chapter 3, Sections 1 and 2; Amandus Polanus in his *Theological System* <note>original: "Syntagm. Theol."</note>, Book 1, Chapter 45, Question 6; Joseph Mede <note>original: "Med."</note>; Antonius Walaeus in his *Commonplaces of Theology* <note>original: "Loc. Comm. Theol."</note>, in the section on the interpretation of Scripture; Antoine de la Faye in his *Handbook of Theology* <note>original: "Fayus Enchirid. Theol."</note>, Disputation 26, Theses 8, 9, 10; and others. They argue that the Scriptures ought not to be explained by one’s own wit or by a private sense which anyone might fashion for themselves and impose upon the Scripture. Rather, it must be explained by that same Spirit by whom they were first set forth—that is, according to the dictation of the Holy Spirit. They gather this from what follows, for the Apostle adds: *For prophecy was not brought in the past by the will of man, but holy men of God spoke as they were moved by the Holy Spirit.* And from this, they deduce that every interpretation must be according to the Scriptures. For because it is necessary (these are the words of Chamier) *that whatever "untying" <note>original: "Epilysis" — referring to the Greek word for interpretation or explanation</note> is finally brought forward must be from the Holy Spirit, it must be drawn from either ordinary or extraordinary inspiration. But no extraordinary inspiration is expected now, and the Church long ago bid farewell to the prophecies of the Enthusiasts along with the Comforter of Montanus <note>A reference to the Montanist heresy, which claimed new, "extraordinary" prophetic revelations</note>. There remains, therefore, ordinary inspiration, which no one can deny is sought from the Scripture.* This "ordinary inspiration" he later calls the "diligent comparison of passages."

But we respond: First, that some read the text, as Walaeus notes, not as <term>epilyseōs</term> <note>original Greek: *ἐπιλύσεως* — interpretation or explanation</note> but as <margin>of an impulse <note>original: *ἐπελεύσεως impetus*</note></margin> <term>epeleuseōs</term> <note>original Greek: *ἐπελεύσεως* — an onset or impulse</note>. Among these is Calvin himself (as Theodore Beza reports in his notes on this passage), who interprets it as "impulse or instinct," and writes that this interpretation greatly pleases him. Secondly, even if we retain the common reading of the word <term>epilyseōs</term>, it sometimes denotes an impulse. And thus, if either that reading or this meaning holds its place, the words of Peter sound a little differently and should be translated in this manner: *That no prophecy of Scripture is of one’s own instinct or impulse; that is, the Prophets did not speak or write the things they produced out of their own wit and impulse.* 

Niels Hemmingsen <note>original: "Nicol. Hemmingius"</note> unhesitatingly pronounces this to be the meaning of this passage in these words: *The word "epilysis" is ambiguous among the Greeks; for sometimes it signifies an impulse, and sometimes an interpretation. But the antithesis clearly indicates that it is taken here for the impulse by which men are carried or moved to say or do something. It is opposed to the Holy Spirit, who is the author of prophecy, which is shown from three things: namely, from the perpetual consensus of the Prophets, the certainty of their preaching, and the astounding miracles by which the truth of the prophecies is sealed; to this is added the approval of Christ and the Apostles.* Therefore, Peter is not speaking here about the interpretation, but about the certainty of prophecy. 

To these points, Augustin Marlorat <note>original: "Marloratus"</note> agrees in this place when he says: *There can be a double sense of the words. For "epilysis" is as much "motion and impulse" to the Greeks as it is "interpretation." And almost everyone accepts it thus, etc.* Yet the other sense seems simpler: that Peter says the Scripture was not produced by human means or human impulse. And a little later: *First, therefore, Peter commands us to have faith...*

<summary>The author challenges the Protestant interpretation of 2 Peter 1:20, arguing that the Greek term "epilysis" refers to the divine origin and impulse of prophecy rather than the method by which Scripture should be interpreted.</summary>
