<meta>This page continues the author's analysis of 2 Peter 1:20–21. Having previously discussed the Protestant view that Scripture interprets itself, the author now introduces various Catholic and scholarly interpretations of the word "interpretation" (epilysis) to show that it refers to the divine origin of the prophecy rather than the method of reading it.</meta>

<header>OF THE DISCOURSE</header>

...to the prophecies, as if to the indubitable oracles of God; because they did not flow from the personal impulse of men. This is what Hemmingsen <note>original: "Hemmingius" — Niels Hemmingsen (1513–1600), a Danish theologian</note> and Marlorat <note>original: "Marloratus" — Augustin Marlorat (1506–1562), a French Reformed reformer</note> say; and that this explanation fits perfectly with what comes before and what follows will also be clearly evident from those things we are just about to say. Thirdly, the word <term>ἐπιλύσεως</term> <note>original: "epilyseōs" — the Greek word for 'interpretation' or 'untying'</note> perhaps does not denote here "explanation" in the sense of the *investigation and pointing out of the true meaning*—a task in which the commentators of the Holy Books are engaged—but rather the *interpretation* which the Prophets used as ambassadors of God and stewards of the divine utterances in setting forth their prophecies. To the people, they preached and made known not their own opinions or the figments of their own brains, but the counsels of the Almighty; not what their own minds dictated, but what the Spirit of God suggested. And that this is the meaning of this word in this specific place appears sufficiently from the Apostle’s entire context.

For he had said above in verse 16: *That he, not following skillfully devised fables, had made known the power and coming of our Lord Jesus Christ, but as one who had seen his majesty with his own eyes; and had heard the voice brought down from heaven testifying to his honor and glory; and he held the most firm prophetic word, namely regarding the coming, power, and majesty of Christ, etc.* The firmness and certainty of this he proves from the fact that *no prophecy of Scripture is of its own interpretation*, that is, proceeding from the human brain of the Prophets, which is subject to errors and hallucinations. He himself declares and confirms this by adding: *For prophecy was not brought in the past by the will of man, but holy men of God spoke as they were moved by the Holy Spirit.*

Hence also Cajetan <note>original: "Cajetanus" — Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534), a prominent Catholic cardinal and philosopher</note> says on this passage: "He touches upon the difference," he says, "between the written sciences and written prophecies: it lies in this, that a learned man both teaches and writes those things which appear in the light of the active intellect according to his own interpretation; but a Prophet both says and writes those things which appear to him under the light of divine revelation, not according to the interpretation of his own judgment."

The Venerable Bede <note>original: "Venerabilis Beda" — an 8th-century English monk and scholar</note> does not differ from these when he comments on this passage thus: "None of the Holy Prophets preached the dogmas of life to the people by his own interpretation; rather, they commended to their listeners those things which they had learned from the Lord as things to be done. Whatever celestial secrets they had perceived in private, these they simply handed over to God’s people either by speaking or by writing; and they were not like the diviners of the nations, who brought forth things they themselves had fashioned from their own hearts to crowds of deceived people as if they were the decrees of a divine oracle."

Nor is Erasmus <note>original: "Erasmus" — Desiderius Erasmus, the famous Renaissance humanist</note> different in his *Annotations on the New Testament* on this passage: The Greek word (<term>ἐπίλυσις</term>) <note>original: "epilysis"</note> is derived from "untying," which is suitable for intricate things; or from "approaching," which more fits the things that follow. *For they did not undertake to publish prophecies by their own will, but they undertook them by the impulse of the Spirit.* Nor is Dionysius the Carthusian <note>original: "Dionys. Carthusianus" — a 15th-century theologian known as the 'Ecstatic Doctor'</note> on this passage, when he writes: "Every prophecy of Scripture, that is, every prophecy contained in Holy Scripture, does not happen by its own interpretation, that is, it was not made plain by human invention, nor was it discovered by the proper sense of the Prophets."

<summary>The author argues that "interpretation" in 2 Peter 1:20 refers to the divine source of the prophecy (revelation) rather than the reader's method of explaining the text. He supports this by citing major Catholic and humanist scholars who emphasize that prophets acted as mouthpieces for God, not as independent authors.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the author's critique of the Protestant interpretation of 2 Peter 1:20. He continues to argue that the phrase "no prophecy is of private interpretation" actually suggests that Scripture requires an external guide—such as philosophy or divine inspiration—rather than being self-explanatory.</meta>

# PARADOXES CHAPTER XII.

If therefore, either this or that, or both of the already cited meanings of <term>interpretation</term> <note>original: "ἐπιλύσεως" (epilyseōs) — a Greek term meaning an unfolding, release, or explanation</note> were intended by the Apostle, the Reformed <note>The "Reformed" refers to Protestant theologians, like those following Calvin, who argued that "Scripture is its own interpreter."</note> will be able to draw nothing from this passage to bolster their opinion. But indeed, let us be generous and grant them that the word *interpretation* here denotes the *indication of the true sense*; it still cannot be inferred from this that Scripture is its own interpreter; but rather the opposite. For when the Apostle writes that *no prophecy of Scripture is of its own interpretation*, the word *own* <note>original: "propriæ" — often translated as "private" in English Bibles, but the author argues it means "its own" or "internal to the text."</note> does not refer to the Interpreters; for no mention is made of them, nor does that word occur anywhere in the preceding text. Rather, it must be referred to the Prophecy, or to the Prophets, and thus the sense will be: *no prophecy of Scripture*, or (if by "prophecy of Scripture" we understand Scripture itself) Scripture, *is to be interpreted by itself, or by the Prophets*. This certainly conflicts head-on with their contrary opinion.

This sense of the passage seems to be favored by the Syriac Translation: *Knowing this first, that every prophecy is not the solution of its own Scripture.* <note>The author uses the Syriac version to argue that a prophecy cannot "solve" or explain itself.</note> And even more clearly the Arabic: *Knowing this first, that every prophecy of Scripture does not itself solve its own sense.* But indeed, even if we grant again that the word *own* refers to the Interpreters, and also that the Apostle wishes to teach in this place that the Scriptures ought not to be explained by the Interpreter’s own talent but according to the dictation of the Holy Spirit, it still does not follow from this (as they contend) that Scripture is *its own interpreter*. 

For, besides the fact that there is no logical consequence here—nor is it proven from those things we cited above from Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a prominent French Reformed theologian.</note> concerning the ordinary inspiration of the Holy Spirit to be sought from Scripture—it can also be said with the best right that he who interprets the word of God through true Philosophy does not explain it from his own talent and private sense, but by the dictation of the divine Spirit, as is clear from chapters 5 and 6. And thus, some light may be drawn from here for the proof of our own Opinion. But enough of these things.

We pass over the remaining passages of Scripture brought forward, as they are of such little weight that anyone can easily detect their triviality. Such are John 10, verse 34, where Christ, to defend himself against the Jews, cites a passage from the Old Testament; and Acts 17, verse 11, where the Bereans are said to have searched the Scriptures to see if the things Paul preached were so; and Acts 9, verse 22, where Paul is said (according to Beza’s version <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a primary successor to John Calvin; his Latin translation of the New Testament was highly influential.</note>) *to have confounded the Jews, demonstrating by comparing testimonies.* And other things of that kind.

## CHAPTER XIII.

<meta>This page continues the philological and theological debate regarding 2 Peter 1:20. The author is moving from his own logical arguments to citing established authorities (both Protestant and Catholic) to prove that "interpretation" in this context refers to the divine origin of prophecy rather than the reader's method of explaining the text.</meta>

->OF THE EXERCISE<-

prophecies, as the indubitable oracles of God, because they did not flow from a personal human impulse. This is the view of Hemmingsen and Marlorat <note>Niels Hemmingsen (1513–1600) and Augustin Marlorat (1506–1562) were influential Protestant theologians whose commentaries were widely read.</note>; that this explanation fits perfectly with what precedes and follows it will become clearly evident from what we are about to say. Thirdly, the word **interpretation** <note>original: "ἐπιλύσεως" (epilyseōs)</note> perhaps does not denote here the investigation and indication of the true sense—the work in which commentators of the Holy Books are occupied—but rather the "unfolding" or "delivery" which the Prophets, acting as God’s ambassadors and the stewards of divine eloquence, employed in putting forward their prophecies. Through these, they made known to the human race not their own opinions or the **figments of their own brains**, but the counsels of the Almighty; they preached not what their own minds dictated, but what the Spirit of God suggested. 

That this is the meaning of the word in this passage appears clearly enough from the Apostle’s entire context. For he had said above in verse 16 that he, **not having followed artfully composed fables, had made known the power and coming of our Lord Jesus Christ, but as one who had seen His majesty with his own eyes.** He had heard the voice brought down from heaven testifying to Christ’s honor and glory, and he possessed the **most firm prophetic word** regarding Christ’s coming, power, and majesty. He proves the firmness and certainty of this word by the fact that **no prophecy of Scripture is of its own interpretation**—that is, it did not proceed from the human brain of the Prophets, which is subject to errors and delusions. He declares and confirms this himself by adding: **For prophecy was not brought long ago by the will of man, but the holy men of God spoke as they were moved by the Holy Spirit.**

Hence, Cajetan <note>Thomas Cajetan (1469–1534) was a prominent Italian cardinal and philosopher known for his opposition to the Reformation and his commentaries on Thomas Aquinas.</note> says on this passage: *He touches upon the difference between the written sciences and written prophecies: in the former, a learned man teaches and writes those things which appear in the light of the active intellect according to his own interpretation; but a Prophet speaks and writes those things which appear to him under the light of divine revelation, not according to the interpretation of his own judgment.* 

The Venerable Bede <note>Bede (672–735) was an English monk and scholar, often called the "Father of English History."</note> does not differ from this when he comments on this passage: *None of the Holy Prophets preached the dogmas of life to the people by his own interpretation; rather, they commended to their listeners the things they had learned from the Lord to be done. Whatever heavenly secrets they had perceived in private, these they simply delivered to God's people by speaking or writing—unlike the diviners of the pagans, who brought forth things they had fashioned out of their own hearts to crowds of the deceived, as if they were the decrees of a divine oracle.* 

Nor is Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Dutch humanist who produced the first printed Greek New Testament.</note> different in his *Annotations on the New Testament* on this location: The Greek word (**interpretation** <note>original: "ἐπίλυσις" (epilysis)</note>) is derived from "loosening" or "untying," which is suited to intricate matters; or from "approaching," which fits better with what follows. *For they did not set out to produce prophecies of their own will, but they set out by the impulse of the Spirit.* 

Neither does Dionysius the Carthusian <note>Dionysius (1402–1471) was a Belgian theologian and mystic known as the "Ecstatic Doctor."</note> differ on this passage when he writes: *Every prophecy of Scripture—that is, every prophecy contained in Holy Scripture—does not happen by its own interpretation; that is, it was not made clear by human invention, nor found by the Prophets' own sense.*

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the Protestant principle that "Scripture is its own interpreter." Having previously discussed the Greek word epilyseōs (interpretation), he now analyzes the grammatical structure of 2 Peter 1:20 to argue that the "private interpretation" mentioned by the Apostle refers to the prophecy's origin or the Scripture itself, rather than the reader's personal judgment.</meta>

# CHAPTER XII OF THE PARADOXES

If, therefore, either one or the other, or both of the meanings of **interpretation** <term>epilyseōs: a Greek term meaning an "untying," "solution," or "explanation"</term> already cited were intended by the Apostle Peter, the Reformed <note>The "Reformed" refers to Protestant theologians, particularly those in the tradition of Calvin or Zwingli, who argued that the Bible's meaning is clear and self-interpreting.</note> will be able to draw nothing from this passage to support their own opinion. But truly, let us be generous and grant them that the word **interpretation** <term>epilyseōs</term> here denotes the *indication of the true sense*; even then, it cannot be gathered from this that Scripture is its own interpreter; rather, the contrary follows. For when the Apostle writes that *no prophecy of Scripture is of one’s own interpretation*, the word *own* <note>original: "propriæ"</note> does not refer to the Interpreters; for no mention is made of them, nor does that word occur anywhere in the preceding text. Instead, it must be referred to the Prophecy itself, or to the Prophets. Thus, the sense would be: *no prophecy of Scripture*—or, if by *prophecy of Scripture* we understand Scripture itself—*is to be interpreted by itself, or by the Prophets*. This certainly fights head-on against their [the Reformed] opinion.

This understanding of the passage seems to be favored by the **Syriac** Interpretation: *Knowing this especially, that every prophecy is not the solution of its own Scripture.* And even more clearly by the **Arabic** version: *Knowing this first, that every prophecy of Scripture does not itself solve its own sense.* But indeed, even if we grant once more that the word *own* refers to the Interpreters, and that the Apostle wishes to teach here that the Scriptures ought not be explained by the Interpreter’s own wit, but according to the dictation of the Holy Spirit: it still does not follow, as they contend, that *Scripture is its own interpreter*. For, besides the fact that there is no logical consequence here—nor can it be proven from the things we cited above from Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Reformed theologian known for his defense of Protestantism against Jesuit critics.</note> regarding the inspiration of the Holy Spirit to be sought from Scripture—it remains that he who interprets the word of God through true Philosophy can also be said, with the best right, to explain it not from his own wit or private sense, but by the dictation of the Divine Spirit, as is clear from chapters 5 and 6. Thus, some light for the proof of our own Opinion may be drawn from this. But enough of these things.

We pass over the remaining passages of Scripture brought forward [by opponents], as they are of such little weight that anyone can easily detect their triviality. Such are John 10:34, where Christ, to defend himself against the Jews, cites a passage from the Old Testament; and Acts 17:11, where the Bereans <note>Residents of Berea who, in the New Testament, are praised for checking Paul’s teachings against the Hebrew Scriptures.</note> are said to have searched the Scriptures to see if the things Paul preached were so; and Acts 9:22, where Paul is said (according to the version by Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a major Protestant Reformer and successor to John Calvin in Geneva.</note>) to have *confounded the Jews, demonstrating by the comparison of testimonies*; and other things of that kind.

# EXERCISES

## CHAPTER XIII.

### The principal arguments by which the Reformed strive to prove that Holy Scripture is its own interpreter are proposed and resolved.

We proceed, therefore, to the second class of arguments, which we said are sought from Reason, the "analogy of faith," the properties of Scripture, and elsewhere. Of these, let this be the:

**First:** All controversies concerning matters of faith ought to be settled and judged by Scripture: But every interpretation of Scripture is a certain controversy concerning matters of faith: Therefore every interpretation of Scripture ought to be settled and judged by Scripture. Thus argues Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621) was a highly influential French Huguenot theologian. His *Panstratia Catholica* was a massive defense of the Reformed faith against Roman Catholic claims.</note> in *Panstratia Catholica*, Volume 1, book 16, chapter 6. And he says the **Major** [premise] was extensively disputed and demonstrated in the first book. But the **Minor** [premise] is evident in itself. *Because, for example, if we have before us that passage, "This is my body" <note>original: "Hoc est corpus meum" (Matthew 26:26)</note>, to be explained, you cannot attach any sense to it which does not support some side of the contenders. If you understand it thus: "this is transubstantiated into the body of Christ," you take the side of the Papists. But if: "this bread is locally joined to the body of Christ," you cross over to the Lutherans. If: "this is a bare and empty sign of the body of Christ," you defect to the Anabaptists. If finally: "this bread is the body of Christ," or, "is the Sacrament of the body of Christ," then you receive yourself into the bosom of the Catholic Church <note>In this context, the author likely refers to the "Universal" or Reformed church as he defines it, distinguishing it from the "Papist" (Roman Catholic) view mentioned earlier.</note>. Therefore, the same method used to judge these controversies will show and confirm the true and legitimate sense of those words. But it is established that these controversies are judged by Scripture. Why then should this very passage not be explained by Scripture itself?*

To respond properly to this argument, a double distinction must be considered. The first is that the word "Scripture" may be taken either *formally* or *materially*. Taken **materially**, it signifies the words, phrases, modes of speaking, and discourses which the Prophets, Evangelists, Apostles, and the rest of the writers of the sacred Books used in composing the Divine utterances. But taken **formally**, it denotes the mind <note>The author uses "mind" (mentem) here to mean the intended meaning or the "spirit" of the text.</note> they wished to express by those words, and the sense which those same Holy Authors had in their souls while they were writing them down. The second [distinction] is that **Matters of Faith** may be understood either *broadly* or *strictly*. If taken **strictly**, they include only those things necessary to be believed for salvation, which are commonly called Articles of Faith, and are contained summarily in the Apostles' Creed. But **broadly**, everything that can in any way be referred to Faith comes into their meaning. Having prefaced these things, we grant the major premise of the argument under a distinction—namely, that all controversies concerning...

...regarding **matters of faith**, <note>original: "Rebus Fidei"</note> that is, things necessary to be believed for salvation, ought to be settled and judged by **Scripture**, that is, by the sense of **Scripture**; we consider this to have been demonstrated by **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621) was a prominent French Huguenot theologian whose work *Panstratia Catholica* was a standard defense of Reformed theology.</note> in his first book, and not in any other sense. However, we deny the **Minor premise**, <note>In logic, the minor premise is the specific case applied to a general rule. Here, the author is disputing the claim that "every interpretation of the Bible is a matter of faith."</note> namely that the **interpretation of Scripture**—that is, of words, phrases, and so on—is a certain controversy regarding **matters of faith**, understand this, **strictly speaking**. For neither the words and phrases of Scripture, nor their interpretation (as is self-evident), nor an article of Faith, is a controversy concerning faith; as far as I know, it does not go by that name, nor can it. For as is commonly admitted, all dogmas of Faith which are necessary for a Christian to know for salvation must be sought from the genuine and legitimate sense of Scripture alone. But interpretation precedes this sense, since the sense is known through it; therefore, interpretation also precedes those dogmas; and for that reason, it cannot be counted among them. 

Furthermore, a logical circle would be committed: for articles of Faith are drawn from the true sense of Scripture, and controversies regarding them are decided and settled by it, but that sense comes from the interpretation of words. If, therefore, this interpretation were either an article of Faith or a controversy concerning one, it would also have to be drawn from or settled by the true sense of Scripture. Since that sense itself arises from the interpretation of words, the interpretation of words would be drawn from or settled by itself—and what could be more absurd? 

Moreover, regarding the **proof of the Minor premise**, there is none. For although someone who has shown and demonstrated the genuine sense of this pronouncement, **"This is my body"**, <note>original: "Hoc est corpus meum" (Matthew 26:26). This phrase was the epicenter of the Reformation's debates on the Eucharist.</note> can from there settle and judge the controversy of how Christ's body is to be **received in the Holy Eucharist**, yet the interpretation of Scripture is not itself a controversy regarding matters of Faith. For these two questions must be distinguished: how Christ's body ought to be received in the **Holy Supper**, and in what sense this proposition, **"This is my body"**, is to be interpreted. The former, if it contains anything necessary to be believed for salvation, must be determined from the true sense of Scripture; the latter, however, is by no means a controversy regarding matters of Faith **strictly speaking**. And thus **Chamier** wrongly concludes that *the method for judging controversies regarding matters of faith strictly speaking is to show and confirm the true and legitimate sense of the words of Scripture: when, on the contrary, the true and legitimate sense of the words of Scripture is the sole norm and method for judging controversies regarding matters of faith.*

**Second**, *If Scripture is authentic, and perfect, and clear: Therefore it is its own best and most certain interpreter. But the former is true: Therefore so is the latter.* Thus **Chamier** argues again in the place cited, section 3. And **Whitaker** <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595) was an influential English Puritan theologian and Master of St John's College, Cambridge, known for his defense of the Protestant view of Scripture.</note> argues almost identically with him in his *Disputation on Holy Scripture*, controversy 1, question 5, chapter 8, argument 19. He says that the parts of the **Antecedent** <note>The 'if' part of a logical statement.</note> have been demonstrated to him through as many entire controversies: and that the **Consequence** <note>The 'then' part of a logical statement.</note> is manifest in itself. For indeed, if Scripture is perfect, it is necessary that whatever pertains to faith be contained within it. But whatever things are necessary for...

## CHAPTER XIII.

# The principal arguments by which the Reformed strive to prove that Holy Scripture is its own interpreter are presented and resolved.

We proceed, therefore, to the second class of arguments, which we said are sought from Reason, the analogy of faith, the properties of Scripture, and elsewhere. Of these, let this be the:

**First**: All controversies regarding matters of faith ought to be decided and judged by Scripture. But every interpretation of Scripture is a certain controversy regarding matters of faith. Therefore, every interpretation of Scripture ought to be decided and judged by Scripture. So argues **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621) was a French Huguenot theologian. His "Panstratia Catholica" was a massive defense of Reformed theology against Roman Catholic claims.</note> in his *Universal Catholic Defense*, Volume 1, book 16, chapter 6. He says that the **Major premise** <note>The first part of a logical argument (the general rule).</note> was discussed and demonstrated at length in his first book. But he claims the **Minor premise** <note>The second part of the argument (the specific case).</note> is self-evident. 

*Because, for example, if the passage "This is my body" <note>original: "Hoc est corpus meum," the words of Jesus at the Last Supper.</note> is at hand to be explained, you cannot assign any sense to it that does not support one of the contending parties. If you understand it this way, "this is transubstantiated into the body of Christ," you take the side of the **Papists** <note>A term used by Protestants for Roman Catholics.</note>. But if you understand it as, "this bread is locally joined to the body of Christ," you move to the **Lutherans**. If, "this is a bare and empty sign of the body of Christ," you fall away to the **Anabaptists**. If, finally, "this bread is the body of Christ," or, "it is the Sacrament of the body of Christ," then you receive yourself into the bosom of the **Catholic Church** <note>In Chamier's Reformed context, he uses "Catholic Church" to refer to the "True/Universal Church" of the Reformation, rather than the Church of Rome.</note>. Therefore, the same reasoning used to judge these controversies will also show and confirm the true and legitimate sense of those words. But it is established that these controversies are judged by Scripture. Why then should this very passage not be explained by Scripture itself?*

To respond conveniently to this argument, a twofold distinction must be considered. The **first** is that the word **Scripture** may be taken either *formally* or *materially*. Taken **materially**, it signifies the words, phrases, modes of speaking, and discourses which the Prophets, Evangelists, Apostles, and the rest of the writers of the Sacred Books used in composing the Divine utterances. Taken **formally**, however, it denotes the *mind* <note>The intended meaning or internal thought.</note> which they wished to express with those words, and the sense which those same Holy Authors had in their souls while they were writing them. 

The other distinction is that **Matters of Faith** <note>original: "Res Fidei"</note> are taken either broadly or strictly. If taken **strictly**, they include only those things necessary to be believed for salvation, which are commonly called **Articles of Faith**, and are summarized in the **Apostles' Creed**. Taken **broadly**, however, they encompass everything that can in any way be related to Faith. With these things premised, we concede the **Major premise** of the argument under this distinction: namely, that all controversies regarding <meta>matters</meta>

The author argues for a distinction between the "material" words of the Bible and its "formal" intended meaning.</summary>

<meta>On the previous page, the author began addressing arguments from Reformed theologians like Daniel Chamier. He started analyzing a syllogism that claims the interpretation of Scripture is itself a "matter of faith" and therefore must be judged by Scripture alone. -># PARADOXES CHAPTER XIII.<-

We consider it demonstrated by **Chamier** in his first book that *Matters of Faith*—that is, things necessary to believe for salvation—ought to be settled and judged by Scripture, meaning the true sense of Scripture; and we do not think it can be demonstrated by any other sense. *However, we deny the minor premise*—namely, the claim that the *interpretation of Scripture* (meaning its vocabulary, phrases, etc.) *is a type of controversy concerning Matters of Faith*, at least when understood strictly. For it is self-evident that neither the words and phrases of Scripture, nor their interpretation, nor even a controversy about them, is an article of faith; as far as I know, it has never been classified as such, nor could it be. For it is an acknowledged fact that all dogmas of Faith <term>dogmas of Faith: the core, non-negotiable doctrines of Christianity</term> which a Christian must know for salvation must be sought from the genuine and legitimate sense of Scripture alone. But interpretation precedes these dogmas, since the sense is recognized through interpretation; therefore, interpretation cannot be counted among those dogmas. Furthermore, this would lead to a circular argument: for the articles of Faith are drawn from the true sense of Scripture, and controversies regarding them are decided and settled by it, yet that sense arises from the interpretation of words. If, therefore, this interpretation were itself either an article of Faith or a controversy concerning one, it would also have to be drawn from or settled by the true sense of Scripture. Since that sense itself arises from the interpretation of words, the interpretation of words would be drawn from or settled by itself—and what could be more absurd than that? As for the *proof of the Minor premise*, it carries no weight. For although someone who has shown and demonstrated the genuine sense of this pronouncement, *This is my body* <note>original: "Hoc est corpus meum"</note>, can thereby settle and judge the controversy of *how Christ's body is to be received in the Holy Eucharist*, it does not follow that the interpretation of Scripture is a controversy regarding matters of Faith. These two questions must be distinguished: first, how Christ's body ought to be received in the Holy Supper <note>original: "S. Coena"</note>; and second, in what sense this proposition, *This is my body*, is to be interpreted. The former, if it contains anything necessary to believe for salvation, must be determined from the true sense of Scripture; the latter, however, is by no means a controversy concerning matters of Faith strictly speaking. Thus, Chamier incorrectly concludes that the method for judging controversies of Faith (strictly speaking) is to show and confirm the true and legitimate sense of the words of Scripture; whereas, on the contrary, the true and legitimate sense of the words of Scripture is the unique rule and method for judging controversies of Faith.

**Second**: *If Scripture is authentic, perfect, and clear, then it is the best and most certain interpreter of itself.* But the first part is true; therefore, so is the second. Chamier argues this way again in the place cited above, section 3. **Whitaker** <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595), an English Protestant theologian and Master of St John's College, Cambridge, known for his defenses of the Reformation.</note> argues almost identically in his *Disputation on Holy Scripture*, controversy 1, question 5, chapter 8, argument 19. He says that the parts of the premise <note>original: "Antecedentis," the 'if' part of the logical statement.</note> have been demonstrated by him through just as many complete controversies, and that the consequence <note>original: "Consequentiam," the 'then' part of the logical statement.</note> is self-evident. Truly, if Scripture is perfect, it is necessary that it contains whatever pertains to faith. But whatever things are necessary for—

<meta>On the previous page, the author was refuting a syllogism proposed by the Reformed theologian Daniel Chamier. The argument being dismantled claimed that since the interpretation of Scripture pertains to matters of Faith, it must be contained within Scripture itself. This page continues the systematic rebuttal of that logic.</meta>

-># OF THE EXERCISE<-

...its interpretation; those things pertain to Faith. Therefore, it is necessary that these same things be contained within it.

To this argument, we respond by granting the antecedent (since its greatest force here seems to regard the perfection of Scripture, as is clear from the proof of the consequence: for it could otherwise be denied regarding its perspicuity <note>perspicuity: the Protestant doctrine that the essential teachings of the Bible are clear enough for an ordinary person to understand</note>; see what we said above in chapter 11, § 6) and by denying the consequence. For the proof that follows, we speak from the distinction regarding *Matters of Faith* brought forward in the response to the preceding argument; that if by "whatever pertains to faith," one understands those dogmas which are necessary to be known for salvation—the only respect in which the Reformed assert that Scripture is "perfect"—then we grant the *Major premise*. However, we deny the *Minor premise*. For not everything necessary for the interpretation of Scripture, let alone the interpretation itself, can be referred to this category, as we have already shown in the refutation of the superior argument. But if one understands those things which in any way look toward Faith and can be reduced to it, we grant the *Minor* on the contrary; but we deny the *Major*, as it was proved in that sense neither by **Chamier**, nor by any theologian acting with him.

Third: If the interpretation of Scripture must be done according to the *analogy of faith* <note>original Greek: τὴν ἀναλογίαν τῆς πίστεως (tēn analogian tēs pisteōs)</note>, then it must also be done according to Scripture. But the first is true; therefore, the second is also true. This argument is again from **Chamier** in the place cited, § 4; and he says the antecedent is evident from **Paul’s** letter to the Romans 12:6: *Having different gifts, according to the grace that is given to us: whether prophecy, let us prophesy* according to the analogy of faith, *according to the proportion of faith.* <note>The "analogy of faith" is a hermeneutical principle suggesting that unclear passages should be interpreted in light of the core, clear doctrines of the faith.</note> Regarding the sense of this passage (rejecting the interpretation of others—namely, that one should hold the measure of his own revelation according to the proportion of his own faith, that is, for the measure of faith which each person possesses), he says this interpretation seems much more suitable: that everyone laboring in "Prophesying" (that is, in searching out the secrets of doctrine) should always remember the proportion of faith—that is, the doctrine of faith. Indeed, the consequence hardly needs illustration, except against the **Papists** <note>Papists: a derogatory term for Roman Catholics used in the post-Reformation era</note>, who perversely interpret the *Rule of Faith* <term>Rule of Faith: the standard of right belief, which Catholics linked to Church tradition and Protestants linked to Scripture alone</term> (he also understands this by the "analogy of faith") to include unwritten Traditions. Other theologians, however, do not create an argument from this passage of **Paul**, but a Rule or means of Interpretation, in this manner: *All interpretation of Scripture is to be directed and required according to the analogy of faith.* To them, the *analogy of faith* is the constant and perpetual sentiment of Scripture in open and least obscure places, from which the primary dogmas of the Christian Religion are sought; the sum of which is contained in the **Apostles' Creed**, the **Decalogue** <note>The Ten Commandments</note>, and the **Lord's Prayer**, which are handed down in the entire **Catechism**. See **Whitaker** in *Disputation on Holy Scripture*; against the Papists of this time, Controversy 1, Question 5, chapter 11, middle of part 7. **Scarpus**, *Theological Course*, section on Holy Scripture, Rule 9, second general. **Polanus**, *Syntagma*.

<summary>This page refutes the Reformed argument that the "analogy of faith" proves Scripture is the sole judge of its own interpretation, clarifying the distinction between essential dogmas and the process of interpretation.</summary>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's point-by-point rebuttal of the Reformed theologian Daniel Chamier. The previous page ended mid-sentence, arguing that if interpretation must follow the "analogy of faith," then specific logical problems arise depending on how that phrase is defined.</meta>

->### PARADOXES, CHAPTER XIII.<-

<note>See</note> Theologian, book 1, chapter 45, question 6, in the middle of those things which are to be applied in the meditation of Scripture itself. Zanchius, *Treatise on the Holy Scripture*, question 12, chapter 2, cause 6, on the obscurity of Scripture. Beza on the stated passage, and others.

But we respond: if by the "analogy of faith" <note>original Greek: τὴν ἀναλογίαν τῆς πίστεως (tēn analogian tēs pisteōs)</note> one understands the *Reason, Rule, and Canon of Faith*, which is Scripture itself, the Major premise <note>Major premise: the first part of a logical argument</note> appears ridiculous, as it is an identical statement <note>i.e., saying Scripture must be interpreted by Scripture</note>. But if it truly means the *proportion of doctrine concerning Faith* which is contained in the Reformers' Catechisms, the Antecedent <note>the first part of the logical "if/then" statement</note> is absurd, since that entire doctrine ought to be drawn from no other source than the true sense of the Holy Scriptures. If it means the constant and perpetual sentiment of Scripture in clear passages, this has already been refuted at length in Chapter 11. Finally, if it means the *Apostles' Creed, the Ten Commandments, and the Lord's Prayer*, it is partly absurd and has partly been refuted already; for the former must first be confirmed and derived from Scripture, while the latter parts are actually pieces of Scripture itself.

Furthermore, the passage from Paul <note>Romans 12:6</note> which is brought forward to prove the Antecedent proves nothing. For the word "let us prophesy" is not found in the Greek; rather, Beza—whose version Chamier followed—inserted it, and did so in the *subjunctive* mood to accommodate the rest of his interpretation. Thus, he made Paul either command or persuade, when Paul is actually only asserting simply in the *indicative* mood <note>indicative: a statement of fact rather than a command or possibility</note> that *we, being many, are one body in Christ (this is the translation of Erasmus, from which the Vulgate does not differ much). Individually, however, we are members of one another, but having different gifts according to the grace given to us: whether Prophecy according to the portion of faith, or ministry in administration, etc.*

From this, it is clear that by the "analogy of faith," Paul does not mean the *proportion of doctrine regarding faith*, nor the *Rule of Faith*, or anything similar, as Chamier wishes. Rather, it means what Chamier rejects: the *Measure of one's own faith, that is, what each person possesses*, as it is in the Syriac version and as Wolfgang Musculus judges in his commentaries on this passage. Or, it means the *measure of our knowledge by which we know God and divine things from Scripture*, as Piscator has it in his Notes on this passage, simultaneously disapproving of and rejecting Chamier’s superior exposition as spurious and not genuine. Even Beza and Pareus admit this interpretation, as does Peter Martyr (according to Pareus), and Chrysostom in the stated passage: *“Even if Prophecy is a grace, it is not poured out simply, but receiving its measure from the recipients, it flows in only as much as it finds the vessel of faith offered to it.”*

And Ambrose says on the same: *“This (Prophecy) is given according to the measure of the recipient, that is, as much as the cause for which it is given requires.”* And Jerome: *“According to the faith which has been given and granted to each by God.”* Likewise, Theodoret: *“He who is the supplier of good things measures out grace according to each person's faith.”* Hence Erasmus paraphrases <note>original: παραφράζει (paraphrazei)</note> this passage thus: *“Whether the gift of Prophecy happened in explaining the secret writings according to the measure of faith, which alone God regards—not other merits—without arrogance, sharing his good with others...”*

<sig>L 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: commu-</meta>

<summary>The author refutes the Reformed interpretation of the "analogy of faith" (Romans 12:6), arguing it refers to an individual's measure of spiritual insight rather than an external dogmatic rule. He cites various Church Fathers and contemporary scholars to support a reading focused on individual grace over systemic theology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's refutation of Reformed arguments regarding the self-interpreting nature of Scripture. Specifically, it finishes addressing a point by Daniel Chamier and begins addressing arguments from theologians Amandus Polanus and Johann Heinrich Alsted.</meta>

# EXERCISES

...communicates. But truly, even if Beza's <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a major Protestant Reformer</note> earlier version and the insertion are accepted to the highest degree, it must still be demonstrated that "prophecy" and "to prophesy" here do not denote the prediction of the future and to predict—as Musculus <note>Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563), a Reformed theologian</note> explains regarding this passage—but rather that they designate interpretation and to interpret, and that the "analogy of Faith" here designates Holy Scripture itself: a task which I am convinced is not so easily accomplished. Finally, something could be said regarding the logical consequence depending on the various interpretations of the Major Premise <note>The first part of a logical argument</note>; but I deem that I have already sufficiently broken the force of this argument.

The fourth argument: *Whatever explains divine matters most clearly and most manifestly, from that source the interpretation of Scripture must be sought: But Scripture itself explains divine matters most clearly and most manifestly: Therefore, Holy Scripture interprets itself.* In this manner argue Polanus in his *System of Theology* <note>original: Syntagm. Theolog.</note>, book 1, chapter 45, question 7, argument 1, and Alsted in *Theological Foundations* <note>original: Præcognit. Theolog.</note>, book 2, chapter 95. The former proves the minor premise <note>The second part of a logical argument</note> *because it is the voice of God, than which nothing can be clearer or more manifest;* while the latter declares it with this simile: *Just as there is no need for someone to lend light to the Sun, so there is no need for us to bring light from elsewhere to the most brilliant light of the Holy Scriptures.*

But we respond with a distinction: For the word *to expound* either denotes to propose, declare, and manifest; or to explain and interpret. Furthermore, by *divine matters* are meant either things necessary for a man to know for salvation (the dogmas of Faith themselves), or the Speech <note>original: Sermo</note> concerning them. If, therefore, "to expound divine matters" means to hand down and propose things necessary to be known for salvation, once the *minor premise* is granted, we deny the *consequence of the major premise*; for the most brilliant and manifest tradition and treatment of some matters does not include the interpretation of itself, or of the book which deals with those same matters. If, however, it means to interpret the speech about the articles of Faith or the Scripture (in which sense these words must be taken so that a conclusion proving the question can be legitimately drawn), once the *major premise* is granted, we deny the *minor premise*: For above in Chapter 11, we showed the contrary, nor is the proof brought forward here sufficient. For although we grant—albeit very improperly—that Holy Scripture can be called the *voice of God*, and that no book *deals with divine matters more clearly and manifestly* than it does, it does not follow from this that it *expounds divine matters most clearly and manifestly*, that is, that it interprets the speech about the articles of Faith or the Scripture; since the clear writing of any book does not prove that it is the interpreter of others, or of itself. Therefore, the proof only shows that Scripture is the clearest and most manifest exposition of Divine Matters (concerning which we stated our opinion in Chapter 11), but not the interpreter of the speech regarding the articles of Faith, which is the sole question here.

The fifth argument: *Because Scripture itself can make a man wise unto salvation, 2 Timothy 3:15.* Thus again Polanus and Alsted argue in the places cited.

<summary>The author continues his rebuttal of the Reformed idea that Scripture is its own interpreter, specifically critiquing the arguments of Polanus and Alsted. He argues that even if Scripture is the clearest book about God, that does not mean the text can interpret its own linguistic and dogmatic difficulties without external aid.</summary>

<keywords>Scripture, interpretation, Amandus Polanus, Johann Heinrich Alsted, analogy of faith, 2 Timothy 3:15, theology, logic, Wolfgang Musculus, Theodore Beza</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's point-by-point rebuttal of Reformed theologians Amandus Polanus and Johann Heinrich Alsted. The author is systematically addressing their arguments for why the Bible should be considered its own interpreter.</meta>

### CHAPTER XIII OF THE PARADOXES

But we **deny the logical consequence** <note>A formal term in logic where one accepts the premise but denies that the conclusion necessarily follows.</note>: for we are not so "Lyncean" <note>In Greek mythology, Lynceus was an Argonaut with vision so sharp he could see through the earth; the author uses this to mean "exceptionally clear-sighted."</note> that we can see how it follows that because Holy Scripture, when understood, can make a person wise unto salvation, it is therefore its own interpreter.

The sixth argument: *Because Scripture is divinely inspired* <note>original Greek: θεόπνευστος (*theopneustos*), often translated as "God-breathed."</note>, *it is useful in a divine manner for doctrine and reproof: Therefore, it is also useful for teaching its own true sense and refuting a false one.* Polanus and Alsted argue in this manner once again in the places cited above.

We again **deny the consequence**: For Scripture does not extract its own true sense, nor does it refute a false sense extracted by others, even though it is granted that, once understood, it is useful for teaching true dogmas of the Faith and refuting false ones; for the preceding statement must be restricted in this way. For if it were taken more broadly, so that this utility of Scripture extended to teaching every truth and refuting every falsehood, it would be false, as is quite manifest.

The seventh argument: *Because the sense of Scripture is the Scripture itself: as Jerome rightly wrote in his commentary on the Epistles to the Galatians: "The Gospel is not in the words of the Scriptures, but in the sense; not on the surface, but in the marrow; not in the leaves of the speeches, but in the root of reason."* This is also an argument of Polanus in the place cited.

To which we respond, once again **by denying the consequence**: for we cannot follow the logic of that argument in any way.

The eighth argument: *Because Scripture teaches the method of interpreting itself: it teaches it, moreover, by examples, just as there are two ways of teaching—one through rules or precepts, the other through examples.* Polanus, whose argument this is, brings forward examples of interpretation within Scripture itself in the place cited: *Matthew 2, verses 17, 18, and 23; and chapter 3, verse 3; and chapter 4, verses 13, 14, 15, 16; and chapter 5, where the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments</note> is explained; and chapter 8, verses 16, 17.* And others, which appear everywhere in the New Testament: indeed, he says the entire New Testament is an interpretation of the Old Testament. Thus Paul interprets his own words in Romans 10:8. Similarly, he interprets Scripture through Scripture in Romans 13:9.

To this argument **we respond by a denial of the consequence**; for a text that hands down a method for explaining itself does not immediately explain itself by itself: for it could hand down a method for explaining itself through something else. The argument would be pressing if Scripture taught either that it must be interpreted through itself, or taught a method of interpreting itself through itself—the author perhaps intended one or both of these, as the things he brings forward as proof (namely, that Paul interpreted Scripture through Scripture) seem to suggest. But then the **premise is denied**, nor is it proven by the cited examples: for the former examples report that certain prophecies concerning Christ were fulfilled; in the latter examples, certain passages of the Old Testament are explained, but not by others, which would have had to be the case for his intention to be demonstrated from there.

The ninth argument: *He who is the Author of Scripture, and the supreme legislator,*

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author continues refuting Protestant arguments for "Scripture interpreting itself," specifically targeting the logic of Polanus and Alsted regarding the Bible's divine inspiration and the use of the New Testament to interpret the Old.</summary>
<keywords>Amandus Polanus, Johann Heinrich Alsted, Sola Scriptura, biblical interpretation, Jerome, New Testament, logic, divine inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s systematic rebuttal of Reformed theologians (specifically Alting, Whitaker, and Walæus) regarding the "self-interpreting" nature of the Bible. On the previous page, the author was finishing the eighth argument; this page begins mid-sentence with the conclusion of the ninth argument and proceeds to the tenth.</meta>

-> OF THE EXERCISE <-

...and teacher of the Church, the same is the supreme and infallible interpreter of Scripture. God alone is the Author of Scripture, and the highest lawgiver and teacher of the Church. Therefore, etc. This argument is used by Alting <note>Henricus Alting (1583–1644), a German Reformed theologian.</note> in *Theological Writings*, Volume 1, part 2, location 2, "On the Form of Scripture," controversy 2; by Whitaker in *Disputations on Holy Scripture*, controversy 1, question 5, chapter 8; and by Walæus in *Common Places of Holy Theology*, in the section "On the Interpretation of Holy Scripture." The first of these says that the **major premise** <note>In a logical argument or syllogism, the "major premise" is the broad general statement used to reach a conclusion.</note> is evident because everyone is the best interpreter of their own words, and a lawgiver explains the meaning of a law most excellently. He says the **minor premise** <note>The "minor premise" is the specific statement that connects a particular case to the general rule of the major premise.</note> rests on the clear testimony of Scripture: 2 Timothy 3:16, James 4:12, Matthew 23:8, 10, and chapter 17:5.

We respond that this does not conclude what was intended to be concluded—namely, that Scripture is its own interpreter. For there is no controversy over whether God is the infallible interpreter of Scripture. But if it is insisted that *Scripture is the voice of God, and therefore by proving that God is the interpreter of Scripture, it is also proven that Scripture is its own interpreter,* we retort that this **logical consequence is void**. For an author can write two or more treatises, one of which will not immediately be the interpreter of the other, nor will they be mutual interpreters of each other.

The tenth and last argument: *The supreme and infallible interpreter of Scripture is one who never errs in interpreting, is led by no partisan zeal, and leaves no room for appeal. These things truly belong to the Holy Spirit alone, speaking in Scripture, and thus they belong to Scripture itself.* Therefore. This is the argument of Alting again, and Whitaker and Walæus follow him almost exactly in the places cited above. Alting asserts that the consequence of the **major premise** is firm. This is because one who errs or can err, or one who is led by partisan zeal (which blinds the mind), is not infallible; and one who leaves room for appeal has a superior, and thus cannot be supreme. Moreover, he proves the **minor premise** because the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture—and thus Scripture itself—is truth itself; it is **self-authenticating** <note>original Greek: ἀυτοπιστος (*autopistos*), meaning something that must be believed on its own authority without external proof.</note>, and has no superior, being itself the **Alpha and Omega, the first and the last.**

But we respond by **denying the minor premise**—namely, that the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture, or Scripture itself, is an "Interpreter" that never errs, etc. Regarding the proof offered, we say it only demonstrates that the Holy Spirit or Scripture never errs, is not led by partisan zeal, and leaves no room for appeal (all of which we freely grant). It does **not**, however, demonstrate that Scripture is an *Interpreter*, which is the only point in question here.

And we judge that the solutions to these ten arguments are sufficient to show how weak the reasons are by which the Reformed theologians strive to establish their opinion. As for any remaining arguments that might occur in other authors, they can be very easily answered following these examples. Hence, we move on to the other part of the Reformed position.

<summary>This page concludes a list of ten logical rebuttals against Reformed theologians who argue that the Bible is its own supreme interpreter. The author argues that while God is infallible, it does not logically follow that the written text of the Bible functions as an active interpreter of itself.</summary>

This page shifts the focus to the role of the Holy Spirit’s "internal testimony" in the process of interpretation.</meta>

<header>PARADOXES, CHAPTER 14.</header>
M
<meta>catchword: 2. But</meta>

## CHAPTER XIV.

->1. *What, and of how many kinds, is the internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit?*<-
->2. *What is "ordinary" persuasion? 3. That the kind of persuasion*<-
->*posited by theologians does not exist.*<-

<margin>I.</margin>
The Reformed <note>Followers of the Calvinist tradition.</note>, as we cited from them in Chapter 10, establish that besides Holy Scripture being its own interpreter, they also attribute a role—and indeed the most important part—in this business to the Holy Spirit. Specifically, they claim that His testimony internally seals the true interpretation in the minds of the faithful and most fully persuades them of it. They argue that Holy Scripture is merely an external norm of interpreting which paves the way to that internal <gloss>full assurance</gloss> <note>original: "πληροφορίαν" (plērophoria), a Greek term for complete certainty or conviction.</note>. However, so that the difficulties of this opinion may come into view (setting aside those we raised above), I wish to ask its patrons what they mean and denote by "internal testimony" or "internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit"; for those terms can be understood in many different ways. 

If they respond that it is their view that no true sense of the Holy Scriptures—from wherever it may be drawn or by whatever method it is investigated—can be fully certain unless the natural light of the intellect perceives its truth clearly and distinctly, and is intimately conscious of that perception within itself (which is required in all certainty raised above every doubt); and therefore that this clear and distinct perception, along with the consciousness of it, comes to them under the name of "internal persuasion" and the "internal testimony of the Holy Spirit," then they hold the same opinion as we do. This is easily seen from what we judged regarding Philosophy in Chapter 5, Section 6. 

But if they say they do not mean the natural light of the intellect, or what is built upon it, but rather a supernatural light of the Spirit—not innate to reason but arriving from without, not enclosed and acquired by the mind but infused and inspired, by whose rays the soul is illuminated and strengthened to acquiesce to the dictates it has received—then I wish to ask them again (since they establish a twofold inspiration of the Holy Spirit, namely "ordinary" and "extraordinary") whether they mean this one or that one. It is admitted by everyone that an extraordinary light of the divine Spirit shone upon the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles, and the other writers of the sacred books, for the purpose of understanding and interpreting the word of God. 

> *For to these, as Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf (1561–1610), a prominent Reformed systematic theologian.</note> has it, the Holy Spirit revealed the true sense of Scripture through immediate revelation, without labor or industry. Which method, in the words of Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Huguenot theologian known for his defense of the Reformed faith against Catholicism.</note>, is no longer expected today; the Church long ago bid farewell to the prophecies of the Enthusiasts <note>A derogatory term for radical reformers who claimed direct, private revelation from God.</note> along with the "Paraclete" of Montanus <note>Montanus was a 2nd-century figure who claimed to be the mouthpiece of the Holy Spirit (the Paraclete) and offered new prophetic revelations.</note>.*

Therefore, their discussion must only concern the Ordinary light.

<summary>The author examines the Reformed claim that the Holy Spirit provides an "internal testimony" to confirm the meaning of Scripture, distinguishing between natural intellectual clarity and supernatural inspiration. He argues that if this testimony is "extraordinary" revelation, it ended with the Apostles, leaving only the "ordinary" kind for discussion.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Spirit, internal testimony, Reformed theology, assurance, Amandus Polanus, Daniel Chamier, Montanism, inspiration, interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the author's rebuttal of Reformed arguments regarding Scripture as its own interpreter, specifically addressing the ninth and tenth logical proofs. <header>OF THE EXERCISE</header>

...and teacher of the Church, is the same as the supreme and infallible interpreter of Scripture. God alone is the Author of Scripture, and the highest lawgiver and teacher of the Church. Therefore, [He is the interpreter], etc. This argument is used by *Alting in Theological Writings, Volume 1, part 2, location 2, "On the Form of Scripture," controversy 2; Whitaker in Disputation on Holy Scripture, controversy 1, question 5, chapter 8; and Walæus in Commonplaces of Holy Theology, the section on the Interpretation of Holy Scripture.* <note>Henry Alting (1583–1644), William Whitaker (1548–1595), and Antonius Walæus (1573–1639) were prominent Reformed (Calvinist) theologians who defended the authority of Scripture against Catholic claims.</note> The first of these says that the **major premise** <note>The first part of a logical argument or syllogism.</note> is evident, because everyone is the best interpreter of their own words, and a lawgiver explains the sense of a law best. But he claims the **minor premise** <note>The second part of a logical argument that connects a specific case to the general rule.</note> rests on clear testimonies of Scripture: *2 Timothy 3:16; James 4:12; Matthew 23:8, 10; and chapter 17:5.*

We respond that what was supposed to be concluded is not concluded—namely, that Scripture is its own interpreter. For there is no controversy regarding whether God is the infallible interpreter of Scripture. But if it is insisted that Scripture is the voice of God, and thus by proving God is the interpreter of Scripture, it is also proven that Scripture is its own interpreter: we reply that this consequence is invalid. For an author can write two or more treatises, one of which will not immediately be the interpreter of the other, or others of others, and certainly not interpreters of each other.

Tenth and last: The supreme and infallible interpreter of Scripture is one who never errs in interpreting, is led by no partisan zeal, and leaves no room for appeal <note>The right to take a case to a higher authority.</note>. These qualities belong to the Holy Spirit alone, speaking in Scripture, and thus they belong to Scripture itself. Therefore [it is the interpreter]. Again, Alting argues this way, and Whitaker and Walæus almost exactly with him in the places cited above. Alting asserts that the logic of the **major premise** is firm. This is because anyone who errs or *can* err—likewise, anyone led by partisan zeal, which blinds the mind—is not infallible; and whoever leaves room for appeal has a superior, and therefore cannot be supreme. He then proves the **minor premise**: because the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture (and thus Scripture itself) is truth itself; it is <term>self-credible</term> <note>original: "ἀυτοπιστὸς" (autopistos); a theological term for something that is believed on its own authority.</note>, and has no superior, being himself the <term>Alpha and the Omega</term> <note>original: "Α & Ω"; the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, signifying the beginning and the end.</note>, the first and the last.

But we respond by denying the **minor premise**—namely, that the Holy Spirit speaking in Scripture, or Scripture itself, is an "Interpreter" that never errs, and so on. Regarding the proof, we say that it only demonstrates that the Holy Spirit or Scripture never errs, is not led by partisan zeal, and leaves no room for appeal—all of which we freely concede. It does *not*, however, prove that it is an **Interpreter**, which is the only question at issue here.

And we judge the solutions to these ten arguments to be sufficient, so that it may be clear how weak the reasons are by which the *Reformed* <note>Followers of the Calvinist tradition.</note> strive to establish their opinion. As for the rest, if any occur in other authors, they can be very easily answered following these examples. Hence, we pass to the other part of the Reformed position.

->CHAPTER XIV.<-

<summary>This page concludes a series of logical rebuttals against Reformed theologians, arguing that while God and Scripture are infallible, Scripture itself does not function as its own active "interpreter." It marks the transition to discussing the Holy Spirit's internal testimony.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from a critique of the Reformed view that Scripture interprets itself to a specific investigation of the "internal testimony of the Holy Spirit." The author begins Chapter 14 by defining and categorizing what theologians mean by this internal persuasion.</meta>

<header>PARADOXES CHAPTER XIV.</header>
M
<meta>catchword: 2. But</meta>

# CHAPTER XIV.

-> 1. *What is the internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit, and how many kinds are there?* <-
-> 2. *And what is the "ordinary" kind?* 3. *And that this kind, as* <-
-> *theologians define it, does not exist.* <-

I. The Reformed <note>Refers to Calvinist theologians.</note>, as we cited from them in Chapter 10, besides establishing that Holy Scripture is its own interpreter, also attribute to the Holy Spirit the chief roles in this matter. Specifically, they claim that His testimony internally "seals" the true interpretation in the minds of the Faithful and fully persuades them of it. In their view, Holy Scripture is merely an external norm for interpreting, which paves the way to that internal *full assurance* <note>original: "πληροφορίαν" (plerophoria), a Greek term meaning total conviction or certainty.</note>. However, to bring the difficulties of this opinion into view (setting aside those we raised above), I wish to ask its patrons what they intend to denote by "internal testimony" or "internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit"; for those terms can be understood in many different ways. 

If they answer that it is their own mind—meaning that no true sense of the Holy Scriptures (no matter where it is drawn from or by what method it is investigated) can be fully certain unless they have perceived its truth clearly and distinctly by the natural light of the intellect, and were intimately conscious of that perception within themselves (which is required for all certainty that is raised above every doubt)—then they hold the same opinion as we do. This is easily seen from what we argued regarding Philosophy in Chapter 5, Section 6. 

But if, on the other hand, they say they do not mean the natural light of the intellect (or what is built upon it), but a supernatural light of the Spirit—one not innate to reason but added to it, not enclosed and acquired by the mind but infused and inspired, by whose rays the soul is illuminated and strengthened to acquiesce to the dictates it receives—then I wish to ask them another question. Since they establish a twofold inspiration of the Holy Spirit, namely *ordinary* and *extraordinary*, do they mean the former or the latter? It is admitted by everyone that an extraordinary light of the divine Spirit shone upon the Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles, and the rest of the writers of the Sacred Books, in their understanding and interpreting of God’s word. 

> *For to these*, as Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610), a prominent German Reformed theologian.</note> says, *the Holy Spirit revealed the true sense of Scripture through immediate revelation, without labor or industry.* *This method*, in the words of Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a French Huguenot theologian.</note>, *is now not to be expected; the Church long ago bid farewell to the prophecies of enthusiasts along with the "Paraclete" of Montanus.* <note>Montanus was a 2nd-century heretic who claimed to receive direct, new revelations from the Holy Spirit (the Paraclete). Chamier is arguing that such direct "extraordinary" revelation ended with the Apostles.</note>

Therefore, the discussion with them will concern only the **Ordinary** persuasion.

<summary>The author begins a critique of the Reformed doctrine of the "Internal Testimony of the Holy Spirit," questioning whether this persuasion is a natural intellectual clarity or a supernatural infusion. He distinguishes between the extraordinary inspiration given to the Apostles and the ordinary persuasion claimed by contemporary believers.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Spirit, internal testimony, Reformed theology, persuasion, illumination, Amandus Polanus, Daniel Chamier, Montanism, certainty, biblical interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "internal testimony of the Holy Spirit." The author is examining how various Reformed theologians define the "ordinary" operation of the Spirit in helping a believer understand the Bible, contrasting it with Catholic critiques of the era.</meta>

<header>D I S P U T A T I O N S</header>
<meta>catchword: mony</meta>

2. But truly, what do they mean by the "ordinary" light, dictate, testimony, inspiration, and persuasion of the Holy Spirit? Chamier <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621) was a prominent French Reformed theologian; his *Panstratia Catholica* was a massive defense of Protestantism.</note> in his *Catholic Panstratia, Volume 1, Book 1, Chapter 19, Section 8*, asserts that "ordinary inspiration" must be sought from the Scriptures themselves, and he later writes in *Section 13* that this is the "diligent comparison of passages." Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf (1561–1610), a German-born Reformed theologian in Basel.</note> seems to establish nothing different in his *System of Theology, Book 1, Chapter 45, Question 4*. There, based on the fact that *Bellarmine* <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Jesuit Cardinal and the leading Catholic controversialist of the Counter-Reformation.</note> admits in *Book 3 of On the Word of God, Chapter 3, Section 11, that "Scripture ought to be explained according to the dictate of the Holy Spirit,"* Polanus concludes *that it ought to be explained according to the Scriptures, and not according to a private dictate of the Spirit outside of the Scriptures.* And those who attribute the interpretation of the Scriptures to the Holy Spirit speaking within the Scripture—or to the Scripture itself, which is the voice of the Holy Spirit—also seem to me to agree with these two; we reviewed many such authors in *Chapter 10*. If, however, they want this to be denoted by their "ordinary light" or "dictate" of the Spirit, it is clear that they are falling back into the question already refuted, proposing nothing different from it. Thus, it would have been sufficient to simply establish Scripture as its own interpreter.

But indeed, they seem to understand something else: specifically, a certain illumination of the intellect and an operation of the Holy Spirit by which the faithful are inwardly persuaded of the true and genuine sense of the Holy Letters. For *Chamier*, in his *Catholic Panstratia, Volume 1, Book 16, Chapter 1*, disputing against *Bellarmine* and others *concerning the Spirit by which the Scriptures are to be understood, establishes that it is not the impulse of any little man who, thrusting himself forward to read and interpret the Scriptures, fashions a sense by his own prowess according to his whim to attach to the sacred books; rather, it is that particular efficacy given to each of the faithful, which is from the Holy Spirit, and which brings it about that he understands the Scripture—that is, that he attains the true and genuine sense.* And *Scarpius* <note>John Sharp (c. 1572–1647), a Scottish theologian who taught in France.</note> in his *Course of Theology, in the section on Sacred Scripture, controversy 8, against the Papists, argument 2*, asserts that the perception of the true sense happens through the "illumination of the Holy Spirit," which (in *argument 1*) he seems to call that "internal testimony" by which the Scripture is recognized to be of divine authority.

This is also confirmed by what Chamier has in *Catholic Panstratia, Volume 1, Book 6, Chapter 1, Sections 5 and 7*. There, wishing to brush away the slander of the Papists—who cry out that the internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit (which carves the authority of Scripture onto hearts and surely persuades them) *is nothing other than a private spirit of pride common to all heretics today, and who babble much about particular and extraordinary revelations and "enthusiasms"* <note>original: "ἐνθουσιασμοῖς" (enthusiasmois), referring here to claims of direct, ecstatic divine inspiration.</note>—he responds that the Reformed "dream of nothing here outside the ordinary order." Rather, they recognize the very thing that applies to all other articles of faith: namely, that the internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit is absolutely necessary for us to assent to the sacred books with full faith. In this same vein, *Calvin* also says in his *Institutes of the Christian Religion, Book 1, Chapter 7, Section 4: "This persuasion must be sought from a deeper source than human reasons, judgments, or conjectures—namely, from the secret testi-* <note>The text ends mid-word as a "catchword" for the next page: "testimony."</note>

<summary>This page explores how Reformed theologians define the "internal testimony of the Holy Spirit" in interpreting the Bible. It contrasts the view that the Spirit works simply through the text itself with the view that the Spirit provides a specific, internal "illumination" to the individual believer's mind.</summary>

<keywords>internal testimony, Holy Spirit, illumination, interpretation, Reformed theology, Daniel Chamier, Amandus Polanus, Robert Bellarmine, John Sharp, John Calvin, biblical authority</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the internal testimony of the Spirit, building on the definitions provided by Reformed theologians in the preceding section.</meta>

<header>P A R A D O X E S C H A P. XIV.</header>

testimony of the Spirit. And shortly after: *I respond that the testimony of the Spirit is superior to all reason. For just as God alone is a fit witness of himself in his own word, so also the word will not find faith in the hearts of men until it is sealed by the internal testimony of the Spirit, etc.* <note>This is a famous passage from John Calvin’s *Institutes of the Christian Religion*, emphasizing that the Bible's authority is confirmed to the believer by the Holy Spirit rather than by human logic alone.</note> See furthermore Zanchius’s *Treatise on Holy Scripture*, question 2, part 4, proposition 7; Walaeus’s *Theological Commonplaces*, in the section on the Authority of Holy Scripture; Scarpius’s *Theological Course*, section on Scripture, Controversy 6; Alting’s *Theological Works*, part 1, place 2; and Polanus’s *System of Theology*, book 1, chapter 16, who defines this internal Testimony of the Holy Spirit as follows: it is *an internal revelation of the Holy Spirit, who through an internal breathing so teaches us in our hearts that he effectively persuades us, so that we firmly believe the Holy Scripture, which we have in the Prophetic and Apostolic books, to be truly and undoubtedly the word of God.* This definition—if the latter words were changed in this manner: *so that we firmly believe some sense of Scripture, whether investigated <term>by our own effort</term><note>The original Latin phrase is "propriô marte," literally "by one's own Mars," a classical idiom meaning to do something by one's own strength or industry.</note> or taught by another, to be undoubtedly true and genuine*—would, in my judgment, encompass what the Reformed theologians intend to indicate by the "internal persuasion of the Holy Spirit" regarding the true meaning of Holy Scripture.

3. But, to speak freely what I feel, I confess that I indeed hear the words of this definition, but I by no means grasp its mind or meaning. For it explains the obscure by means of the equally obscure, and the thing itself by itself. For what is "internal revelation"? What is "internal breathing"? What is "to be effectively persuaded"? What is "to firmly believe"? Furthermore, what is being defined? Is it an action of the Holy Spirit, or an effect? And if it is an effect, what is it in the souls of the Faithful, and how does it affect them? For nothing is offered here but external labels. If, therefore, by the "internal revelation of the Holy Spirit" and "internal breathing" they mean a clear and distinct perception produced by the Holy Spirit in the minds of the faithful through the natural light of the intellect; and by "effective persuasion" and "firm faith," they mean an intimate consciousness of that perception and a full acquiescence in it; then they do not feel any differently than we do, as we explained at the beginning of this chapter. If, however, they mean to designate something supernatural—something that exceeds the powers of Nature and surpasses its ordinary course, such as they are always clamoring about and advertising in this place—let them see whether they are not crossing over into the camps of the ancient <term>Enthusiasts</term><note>In this historical context, "Enthusiasts" (from the Greek 'entheos', meaning 'God within') refers to radical religious groups who claimed direct, private revelations from God that bypassed the Bible or Church authority.</note>, or those fanatics and "ill-rested" men who have recently emerged, whom they call <term>Quakers</term><note>The Society of Friends, or Quakers, emerged in 17th-century England; they were often criticized by contemporary theologians for their focus on the "Inner Light" over formal scripture and academic training.</note>, a camp they claim to avoid with such great zeal. For the Church long ago bid farewell to the prophecies and spiritual revelations of the Enthusiasts along with the "Paraclete of Montanus," <note>Montanus was a 2nd-century Christian who claimed to be the mouthpiece of the Holy Spirit (the Paraclete), leading a movement that emphasized new prophecy over established scripture.</note> as we have frequently cited from Chamier; and modern theologians rightly reject the deliriums of the Quakers concerning light and the Spirit, as well as their internal illuminations. For these are people who exalt their own spirit above the Holy Scriptures, and who [trample] under their feet all reason, all liberal disciplines, along with the original languages...
