<header>E X E R C I T A T I O N I S</header>
<meta>catchword: First</meta>

...they boast magnificently of trampling <note>The context from the previous page implies they are trampling human reason or logic underfoot in favor of divine inspiration.</note> underfoot. Indeed, they seem to me not to be far from such [fanatics]: since they bring forward many effects and additions regarding this Supernatural Testimony of the Holy Spirit, and babble many other things, while in the meantime they nowhere propose its essence and nature, nor explain it anywhere—and perhaps they are even ignorant of it. For no matter how much I have run through and unrolled their books with great diligence, and sought within myself with attention, and seriously asked many others (not only the learned, but also as many unlearned as possible) to search within themselves as well, all this labor has fallen in vain. I could find nothing that would satisfy my mind.

Quite a few have frankly confessed to me that they have found nothing in themselves except that clear and distinct perception, and a consciousness of it, and a restful satisfaction in it. They could find nothing else in others—however much they might boast of it—of which they could form a true and real concept. Indeed, some declared they had often felt something supernatural and heavenly, and still found it within themselves. However, when this was brought back to a precise and accurate examination, not only I, but sometimes they themselves clearly discovered that it was nothing but a natural <term>affection</term><note>In this context, an "affection" refers to an emotion or a state of mind rather than a physical illness.</note> of the mind, or the enjoyment of an imaginary good, and a joy in the soul smelling of such things. From all this, we judge more than enough on how fleeting and unstable a foundation this opinion rests, even if the arguments with which they strive to confirm it were not refuted. Yet, so that we might leave nothing untouched by which they might defend themselves, and so we do not neglect to remove what might cause a doubt in anyone, we will briefly explain those arguments in the following chapter and examine how much strength they have.

### C H A P T E R XV.

->*The arguments are examined by which the Reformed contend that the Holy Spirit is the interpreter of the Holy Scriptures.*<-

Only among the theologians I have happened to see, Whitaker <note>William Whitaker (1548–1595), an influential English Protestant theologian and Master of St. John's College, Cambridge.</note> in his *Disputation on Holy Scripture* (Controversy 1, Question 5, Chapter 8) and, following him, Scarpius <note>Johannes Scharpius, or John Sharp (1572–1648), a Scottish theologian who taught in France.</note> in his *Theological Course* (section on Scripture, Controversy 8), propose three arguments. With these, they attempt to prove that, besides Scripture itself, authority also belongs to the Holy Spirit to interpret the Holy Scriptures. And although they—as almost everyone does—join and mix together the right of judging all controversies of faith, we shall nevertheless set that aside and select and weigh only those points which regard the present question.

<meta>This page continues the author's systematic refutation of the Reformed position on biblical interpretation. Having previously critiqued the vague definition of "Internal Testimony," the author now addresses three specific logical arguments (syllogisms) used by his opponents to claim that only the Holy Spirit can interpret the Bible.</meta>

# PARADOXES CHAPTER 15.

The first argument, therefore, is this: *If Scripture is not recognized as such by any other means than through itself, and through the internal testimony of the Holy Spirit in individual believers; then the supreme authority for interpreting Scripture rests with Scripture alone and the Holy Spirit. But this was previously proven in the Controversy concerning the Authority of Scripture. Therefore [this authority belongs to them alone].*

But we respond, and indeed to the first part of the premise in stages: first, by denying that **Scripture is recognized as such through itself**. For nothing is known through itself except for primary and common notions <note>original: "primæ atque communes Notiones"; these are self-evident logical axioms, like "the whole is greater than the part."</note>. When Scripture is recognized as the Word of God from the marks of divinity that are found within it, it is not known through itself, but is recognized as such through those specific marks. Next, we must distinguish the meaning of the **internal testimony of the Holy Spirit**. If by this they mean a clear and distinct perception produced by the Holy Spirit through the light of nature, along with a conscious awareness of it and a peaceful satisfaction in it, then we concede that part of the **premise**. For this would not be against our own position, as is clear from what was said in the previous chapter. But if they intend some kind of supernatural illumination, we deny that such a thing is given, as it reeks of <term>Enthusiasm</term><note>In the 17th century, "Enthusiasm" was a derogatory term for the belief that one received private, direct revelations from God outside of reason or the Church.</note> and Quakerism. It has not been explained by any theologian, much less proven. Indeed, all the arguments usually brought forward for that purpose, either here or in the section on the authority of Scripture, can be easily resolved by what has already been said. As for the **logical consequence**, that too might be doubted: for even if *Scripture were recognized through itself and through the internal testimony of the Holy Spirit*, it does not follow from this that it must be interpreted *only* through itself and the Spirit's testimony. It could potentially be explained in another way—such as the one we have already proposed—which is infallible.

The second argument is: *That which alone has the power to generate faith also alone has the supreme authority to interpret the Scriptures. But Scripture alone, together with the Holy Spirit, has this power. Therefore, this supreme authority must be granted to them alone.* The first part of the proposition is proven from Romans 10, verse 17: *Faith is born from the correctly perceived meaning of Scripture; but the meaning of Scripture must be sought from Scripture alone—indeed, it is Scripture itself—and the perception of the true meaning occurs through the illumination of the Holy Spirit. The second part of the assumption is proven because the Holy Spirit alone can infuse faith.*

We respond first by denying the **consequence of the Major premise**. Indeed, we cannot wonder enough at the "proof" offered for it: for it belongs partly not to the Major but to the Minor premise—namely, when faith is said to be born from the *correctly perceived meaning of Scripture*. Furthermore, it assumes the very point in question: for instance, that *the meaning of Scripture must be sought from Scripture alone*, and that *the perception of the true meaning occurs through the illumination of the Holy Spirit.* Secondly, we respond to the **Minor premise** regarding what it contains about the Holy Spirit, either by conceding or denying it according to the distinction we brought forward in the response to the first argument.

Finally, the last argument is: *If the Scriptures must be interpreted and understood by the same Spirit by which they were written, then it is necessary that all those who [seek to] interpret them...*

->M 3<-

# DISPUTATIONS

*...or wish to understand, they should consult the Holy Spirit and rely on his persuasion. But the prior point is true. Therefore [the conclusion follows].*

We respond: If by "the same Spirit" they mean that same extraordinary inspiration of the Holy Spirit by which the holy men of God—the *Prophets* and *Apostles*—were driven to write, then the *Antecedent* <note>In logic, the first part of a conditional "if-then" statement.</note> is denied. For that inspiration was unique and granted to them alone, and no longer has a place in the Church today. If, however, they mean by the assistance of the same Spirit, but operating in a different manner, this could be granted and adapted to prove our own opinion—by saying that *Philosophy* is the very assistance by which the Holy Spirit now interprets the *Scriptures*. But if they wish to claim that the same *Holy Spirit* explains the *Sacred Writings* in a different way, yet not through *Philosophy* but through some supernatural revelation, then it is up to them to define what that is and prove that it exists, as we have already emphasized many times.

->Chapter XVI.<-

1. *The opinion of the Socinians is explained.* 2. *As is that of the Arminians.* 3. *How both of these groups, as well as the Reformed, agree among themselves regarding the clarity and obscurity of Scripture, but disagree with us.* 4. *Regarding the disposition of the Intellect: what the Reformed determine, and in what ways they differ from the Socinians, Arminians, and from us?* 5. *What the Socinians and Remonstrants <note>Another name for the Arminians, referring to the "Remonstrance" or protest they filed in 1610.</note> feel about the same matter, and in what ways they differ both among themselves and from the Reformed?* 6. *Regarding the rule of interpretation: what do the Socinians determine?* 7. *That the Reformed determine that Reason is not the rule of interpretation, and whether that opinion seems to be falsely attributed by them to the Socinians and Remonstrants?* 8. *The author's own opinion on the Rule is proposed, and its difference from the Socinians, Arminians, and Reformed is shown.* 9. *The argument against this same opinion is dismantled.*

Until now, we have moved our weapons against enemies; we have led our forces into their lands, and we have attacked and destroyed their citadels. Now, with victory won, it is pleasing to visit the supporters of our own side—those who act with us (though not entirely), and yet (as far as we can gather from their writings) do not wander far from our own opinion. We wish to confer with them, by way of a concluding flourish <note>original: "coronidis locô"; a Greek-derived idiom for a finishing touch or conclusion.</note>, to see how far...

# PARADOXES CHAPTER XVI.

...between us, and since we have already conquered our enemies <note>The author likely refers to the "Orthodox" Reformed/Calvinist theologians whose arguments were countered in previous chapters.</note>, let us consider whether we agree or disagree, and so that we might place our affairs in safety on every side, let us try and test whether they can be drawn to our side, reconciled with us, and joined by a firm treaty. These groups are either the **Socinians** <note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity and the deity of Christ, named after Fausto Sozzini.</note> or the **Arminians** <note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius who diverged from strict Calvinist views on predestination; also known as Remonstrants.</note>, whose opinions we will first investigate so that the whole matter may be more expeditiously completed.

1. The **Socinians**, therefore, to begin with the older group, do not seem to be consistent with themselves in this matter: for at one time they affirm that *the task of interpreting the Sacred Scriptures belongs to reason alone*, while at another time they additionally require the grace and help of the Holy Spirit. Thus, in the *Racovian Catechism* <note>original: "Catechef. Racov."; the primary statement of Socinian theology first published in 1605.</note> *chapter 3, when it is asked whence comes such great discrepancy over the understanding of Scripture; it is answered: from the fact that men either read the Holy Scripture negligently, or they do not bring a sincere heart, free from all impediments and desires; or they do not implore divine aid, that is, the grace of the Holy Spirit, which God has promised to all who invoke Him for it day and night.* On the other hand, **Ostorodt** <note>Christoph Ostorodt (d. 1611), a prominent Socinian leader.</note> in his *Institutes, chapter 4*: *If therefore reason—that is, talent or intellect—openly shows that the Trinity of Persons in God is false, how could it ever come into the mind of an intelligent man that it is true and can be proved by the word of God?* And **Smalcius** <note>Valentin Smalcius (1572–1622), a major Socinian theologian.</note> against Franz, *Disputation 4, On Justification*: *There is no particle of the Christian Religion which does not agree with reason: and an opinion which does not agree with reason can also have no place in Theology.* In the latter two of these passages, mention is made of reason alone, but in the former, the Holy Spirit is mentioned.

But the Author of the *Short Disquisition against Valerianus Magnus, on the Rule of Belief of Non-Catholics* <note>original: "Brevis Disquisitionis contra Valer. Magnum, de Acatholicorum credendi regulâ"</note> (whom the most celebrated and learned Professor of Theology at the University of Leiden, **Johannes Hoornbeek**, says and proves is a Socinian in his *Refutation of Socinianism, volume 1, book 1, chapter 6*) brings forward both **Reason** and the **Holy Spirit** in *chapter 4* with these words: *It must be denied that no one certainly attains the true sense of the sacred Writings unless he is inwardly enlightened and taught by the Holy Spirit.* And a little later: *Wherefore it is entirely necessary to attribute to it (he is speaking of sound reason) the faculty of understanding and judging the Sacred Scriptures. For the obscurity of the Sacred Scriptures is not everywhere so great. Indeed, some things are so clear that they are immediately understood by anyone hearing and reading them. Moreover, the explanation of more obscure passages is not necessary for everyone. But if anyone nevertheless seeks seriously, he will investigate by the help of the Holy Spirit. And this very thing which he understands—whether it be from the Holy Spirit or not—cannot be established except by the judgment of sound reason. For that gift of the Holy Spirit does not take away reason, but raises it up and illuminates it; and what it either could not do by itself (as appears in unraveling certain more obscure passages of Scripture) or could not do without difficulty, it may do, or do more easily, by his help. For the Holy Spirit employs as a medium for perfecting His operations that which He finds in us suited to those operations. Just as the eye for seeing, and my ear for hearing...*

### OF THE EXERCISE

employs it, so for understanding and judging, certainly in my view, it uses **reason** alone as the single instrument for understanding and judging. Furthermore, just as anyone can read the **Sacred Words** with their eyes and hear them with their ears without the inner help of the **Holy Spirit**, or without a peculiar illumination of the eyes and ears; so no less is it possible to understand and judge those same **Sacred Words** by the benefit of **sound reason**, even if the illumination of the **Holy Spirit** has not yet arrived. Especially in those matters which are simply necessary for salvation. And in chapter 7: *To discern the authority and genuine mind of the Sacred Scriptures, even those principles called Philosophical must be summoned.* And a little later: *These and similar things, which are unknown to no sane person, must be established as the foundations of our knowledge, both regarding the authority of the Sacred Scriptures and regarding their true sense.* And again afterward: *Those things which are necessary for salvation he shall judge from the Scriptures with no trouble; provided he applies diligence, and least of all allows himself to be deceived by passions and prejudiced opinions. Especially indeed if he also seeks the gift of the Holy Spirit from God. What of the fact that by the help of these principles, even he who either cannot read the Sacred Scriptures, or has never seen them, or does not know they exist—provided he hears the sum of the Christian Religion from someone else—can learn as much as is necessary for him for salvation.* So says that **Anonymous writer**. <note>The author is citing an unnamed Socinian or Remonstrant source to demonstrate the radical reliance on reason over supernatural revelation.</note>

But **J. Schlichting** <note>original: "J. Slightingius"; Jonas Schlichting (1592–1661) was a Polish Socinian theologian and biblical commentator.</note> in his book *On the Most Holy Trinity* against **Balthasar Meisner** <note>original: "Balt. Meisnerum"; Meisner (1587–1626) was a Lutheran theologian who argued against Socinian rationalism.</note> on page 67, mentions **Reason** alone in this business, with the **Holy Spirit** entirely omitted, in this manner: *Either it concerns things recorded and expressed in the Scripture in the clearest words, so that no one of sound mind can doubt their sense; or it concerns obscure things, about which it may rightly be doubted whether they are read as signified or not signified in the Scripture. If it concerns the former, we concede this assertion: that the Holy Scripture is the most perfect rule of faith and morals; and this primarily because it is most certain that Scripture contains nothing that is repugnant to manifest reason, or involves a true contradiction. If indeed it concerns obscure things, anyone sees that the matter cannot be settled without reason; which nevertheless is not to be employed as if it could oppose anything to the Scripture affirming or denying something, but only to declare whether the Scripture contains something: if it is established to contain it, whatever reason may finally contradict, it is necessary that reason be deceived.* And a little later: *For we do not recur to reason whenever the Scripture openly testifies to the contrary; but whenever the mind <note>The "mind" or intended meaning of the text.</note> of the Scripture is doubted.* And then: *The judicial parts of faith do not belong to Reason if the mind of the Scripture is already established. But when it is doubted whether the Scripture has defined a matter in that way or not, to wish to reject reason, what else is it than to extinguish light in the darkness, and moreover to pluck out one's own eyes? Meisner's arguments are true whenever reason opposes itself to the Scripture, or to the Holy Spirit speaking in men or in the Scriptures, and contradicts it; but not when it helps and directs us in perceiving the mind of the Scripture or of the Holy Spirit.* The same author in the same book, page 79: *Then we never flee to the judgment of reason, whenever*

## CHAPTER 16 OF THE PARADOXES

*...is certain regarding the mind of Scripture. For why should we oppose truth to truth? But only when there is doubt either about the sense of Scripture, or outside of Scripture about the truth of some dogma. And on page 84: It is false that right and manifest reason is a foreign and alien principle in divine matters, as Meisnerus* <note>Balthasar Meisner (1587–1626), a Lutheran theologian who argued against the Socinians.</note> *affirms here, especially when the question is whether Scripture says something or does not say it, as is the case here. From all of which we briefly gather this opinion of the Socinians* <note>A rationalist Christian sect that prioritized reason in theology.</note> *concerning the understanding and interpretation of Scripture: namely, that those things necessary to be known for salvation are so clearly proposed and expressed in the Sacred Letters that anyone of sound mind, and free from prejudices and passions, can immediately understand them; the obscurer matters, however, are indeed not necessary for all, yet their mind and sense can be drawn out and judged by **reason**, that is, as much by the soul's own faculty of judging and reasoning as by the dictates, decrees, and discoveries of that same faculty: and indeed more expeditiously and easily if the grace of the Holy Spirit should be added, although it is not simply necessary.*

2. The Arminians <note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius, also known as Remonstrants, who argued for a more moderate view of predestination and the clarity of Scripture.</note> declare their opinion on this matter in their **Confession**, chapter 1, *and specifically regarding **Understanding** in Section 14, and regarding **Interpretation** in Section 16. Concerning **Understanding**, they speak thus: Furthermore, although these same Books (namely, the Sacred ones) are in some places (especially to the unlearned and less practiced) quite obscure, there is such clarity and <term>perspicuity</term>* <note>The theological concept that the Bible is clear in its essential teachings.</note> *especially in those senses necessary to be understood for eternal salvation, that all readers, not only the learned but even <term>unlearned commoners</term>* <note>original: *idiotæ*; meaning laypeople or those without formal education.</note> *(provided they are endowed with common sense and judgment), can attain their meaning as much as is sufficient: provided they do not allow themselves to be blinded by prejudice, vain confidence, or other perverse passions: but search this Scripture piously and diligently, etc. And this opinion Episcopius* <note>Simon Episcopius (1583–1643), a leading Dutch Remonstrant theologian.</note> *also acknowledges and defends as the view of his people in his **Defense of the Remonstrant Catechism against Heidanus**, page 32, and further declares it in **Disputation 3 on the Perspicuity of Scripture**, Thesis 3: To attain this understanding, nothing else is necessary than the power of apprehending and perceiving the sense of those words which are clearly and significantly proposed; which power is natural, and natural to all endowed with reason in every state. Hence, neither scholarship sought over a long time, nor laborious and elaborate consequence, nor any other supernatural light infused into the power [of the mind] and elevating it, is required to elicit this understanding. For intervening passions, which sometimes seem to cloud and overwhelm the power of understanding, should be said to hinder not the power of understanding itself, but the will, or the act of understanding.* Regarding **Interpretation**, however, they establish this: *The best Interpretation of Scripture is that which most faithfully expresses its native and literal sense, or at least approaches it as nearly as possible: since that alone is the true and living word of God, from which, as from an incorruptible seed, we are born again to the hope of eternal life. The sense, however...*

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Arminian (Remonstrant) view of Scripture. It specifically addresses how they define the "literal sense" and the role of "Right Reason" in understanding the Bible, distinguishing their position from both the Socinians and the Reformed orthodox tradition.</meta>

-> # D I S P U T A T I O N S <-

[the sense] we call native and literal, is not so much that which the words strictly taken suggest (as indeed very often happens), but that which—even if the words rigidly accepted do not imply it—is nevertheless most suitable to <term>right reason</term> <note>Latin: *recta ratio*; the human capacity for logical thought, which theologians debated was either sufficient or insufficient for divine truths after the Fall</note> and to the very mind and intention of the one who spoke the words, whether it was expressed properly or figuratively. This can and ought to be discerned from the scope and occasion of each passage, the subject matter, the preceding and following context, the comparison of similar passages, as well as from the palpable absurdities that would otherwise follow, and other arguments or signs of things of that sort. Now, what they understand by "right reason" is explained in the *Apology* <note>The *Apologia pro Confessione Remonstrantium* (1629), a key defense of Arminian doctrine against the Synod of Dort</note>, in this same section, in this manner: <note>original Latin: "Recta ratio hic non significat vim, quâ homo ratiocinandô ex se ipsô excogitare..."</note> *Right reason here does not signify a power by which a man, by reasoning from himself, can devise or find out what is right and consistent with the divine will; but only the power by which he can apprehend a sense clearly and plainly revealed by God, or can correctly and suitably elicit from the circumstances of the words, from the preceding and following context, etc., the intention of the one who spoke.* And further down: <note>original Latin: "Vis rationis rectæ, id est, convenientem & rectum verbis Jesu Christi sensum aptantis."</note> *The power of right reason; that is, fitting a suitable and right sense to the words of Jesus Christ.* Many more things of this kind may be seen in the *Apology* in the cited place. From this, the opinion of the <term>Remonstrants</term> <note>The Arminians, so called because they drafted a "Remonstrance" (a document of protest) regarding five points of Calvinist doctrine</note> seems to us to be this: namely, that in the Scriptures some things are clear and transparent, while others are obscure. In those things necessary for eternal salvation, <term>Understanding</term> <note>Latin: *Intelligentia*; here referring to the immediate grasp of clear truths</note> takes place, but in the others <term>Interpretation</term> <note>Latin: *Interpretatio*; referring to the more laborious process of explaining obscure passages</note> [is required]; and both can be completed without any internal illumination of the Holy Spirit, by the power and faculty of right reason alone—provided that, regarding the former, it is not hindered by prejudice, vain confidence, or other corrupt affections, but searches the Scripture piously and diligently; and regarding the latter, it further considers and attentively examines the scope and occasion of the passage, the subject matter, the context, etc.

3. Thus far we have investigated the opinions of the <term>Socinians</term> <note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity and emphasized reason's role in judging the truth of revelation</note> and Arminians concerning the interpretation of Scripture; let us now see in what points both of these agree with the <term>Reformers</term> <note>The mainstream Protestant movement (Calvinist), to which the author belongs</note> and with our own view, and in what they differ, so that their opinions may be perceived and held even more distinctly and clearly. It must be noted here in advance, however, that we are not having a discussion about the understanding and interpretation of the things treated and handed down in the Sacred Letters—by which we judge or dispute their truth or falsehood, goodness or malice—but only about that understanding and interpretation by which we track down and draw out the sense of the words and the mind of the speaker, and having drawn it out, certainly show and demonstrate that we possess it. In this matter, however, some things regard the one who understands and explains (namely, the man or the human Intellect), and some things regard that which is understood and explained (namely, the Word of God). In those things which [regard] the Holy Scriptures...

Having previously defined "right reason," the text now turns to whether the Holy Spirit's internal illumination is a prerequisite for understanding Scripture.</meta>

### CHAPTER XVI OF THE PARADOXES

Regarding matters concerning the Scriptures, the <term>Reformed</term> <note>The orthodox Calvinist tradition prevalent in the Dutch Republic</note>, as well as the <term>Socinians</term> <note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity and emphasized reason in interpreting Scripture</note> and <term>Remonstrants</term> <note>Followers of Arminius who disagreed with strict Calvinist views on predestination</note>, agree that certain passages are clearer and some more obscure. They also agree that everything required for eternal salvation is contained in the clearer passages, and that an understanding of the more obscure ones is not necessary for everyone. This we do not deny, as is evident from what we said in chapter eleven. However, in those matters regarding the human intellect, there is a very great discrepancy, both concerning the **disposition** required for one to understand and interpret, and the **rule** according to which interpretation ought to be conducted.

4. Regarding the **disposition**, the Reformed establish that no one, unless internally illuminated by the Holy Spirit, can achieve the true sense of the Holy Scriptures, whether in the clearer or the more obscure passages. This is clear from the *Censure of the Remonstrant Confession* <note>original: "Censurâ in Confess. Remonstrant."; a critique published in 1626 by the theology faculty at Leiden against the Remonstrants' statement of faith</note>, chapter 1, sections 14 and 16. There, among other things, the **Leiden Professors** speak thus: *If by "right reason" the Remonstrants here mean the use of human reason not yet illuminated by the Holy Spirit, they are no less insane—either with reason or against reason—than the Socinians, etc. But if they speak here of a person illuminated by the Holy Spirit, we indeed acknowledge that his right reason is capable of judging the genuine sense of Holy Scripture; provided, however, that in this inquiry he does not follow his own natural reasoning, but the dictation and guidance of the Holy Spirit.* Similarly, that distinguished and most Reverend man—who was both a most vigilant and eloquent preacher at Leiden and a most profound Professor of Holy Theology—**Abraham Heidanus**, in his noble work *The Cause of God* <note>original: "de Causâ Dei"; Heidanus was a prominent Cartesian-influenced theologian who defended the Reformed position against the Arminians</note>, against Episcopius, book 1, chapter 6: *The clarity of Scripture is a property that is in Scripture itself, and which it always retains whether a person is illuminated or not; although the unilluminated person does not perceive that clarity, etc.* And later: *God has so tempered the Scripture that a Christian, from its whole body, can most excellently, perfectly, and clearly learn and grasp its own mind and certain will in all necessary doctrines of the Faith, provided the illumination of the Spirit is added.* You may see more in the same place and elsewhere in others. Furthermore, compare what we reviewed above in chapter 10. However, both the <term>Arminians</term> <note>Another name for the Remonstrants</note> and the Socinians deny this opinion of the Reformed—and we subscribe to their view. It is clear that the Arminians deny it from their *Apology* against the *Censure of the Leiden Professors*, chapter 1, section 14. There, among other things, these words occur: *If you say this light of the Holy Spirit is a prerequisite so that the true sense may be understood, you increase the absurdity.* And a little later: *The Remonstrants establish that the senses of Scripture necessary to be known can be understood by a man without the internal and special illumination of the Holy Spirit.* And in section 16, after the author of the *Apology* had reported the words just cited from the *Censure*, he asks: *What is it to be insane with reason, if this is not? etc.* So too **Episcopius** <note>Simon Episcopius, the primary theologian of the Remonstrants after Arminius</note> in his *Defense of the Remonstrant Catechism* against Heidanus, page 32, confesses that the Remonstrants teach that each and every person, etc. without the internal [light] of the Spirit...

# DISPUTATIONS
### CHAPTER XVI OF THE PARADOXES

...by that light, *one can understand the true sense and mind of Holy Scripture*. It is clear that the **Socinians** <note>A rationalist Christian sect that emerged during the Reformation, known for denying the Trinity and emphasizing the use of human reason in theology</note> side with the **Arminians** <note>Also known as Remonstrants; followers of Jacobus Arminius who disagreed with strict Calvinist views on predestination</note> here, as seen in the *Brief Disquisition against Valerius Magnus concerning the Non-Catholic Rule of Faith* <note>original: "Brevi Disquisit. contra Valer. Magnum de Acatholicorum credendi regulâ"; Valerius Magnus was a prominent Capuchin monk and polemicist</note>, chapter 4, where the following is found:

> It must be denied that no one can certainly attain the true sense of the Holy Scriptures unless they are internally illuminated and taught by the Holy Spirit. And a little after: Just as anyone can read the sacred words with their eyes and hear them with their ears without the internal aid of the Holy Spirit, or any special illumination of the eyes and ears; so it is no less possible to understand and judge these same sacred things by the benefit of sound reason, even if the illumination of the Holy Spirit has not yet occurred.

The same is also made evident from the cited passages of **Schlichting** <note>Jonas Schlichting (1592–1661), a leading Socinian theologian and biblical commentator</note>, where, in disputing against **Meisner** <note>Balthasar Meisner (1587–1626), a Lutheran theologian who defended orthodox views against the Socinians</note> regarding the interpretation of Scripture, he always recurs to **Reason**, and never to the illumination of the Holy Spirit.

5. Against the **Reformed**, the Remonstrants and Socinians maintain that a sound mind and the Light of the natural intellect are sufficient for a human being to understand the Holy Scriptures in the clearer passages, provided they are free from prejudices and not overwhelmed by wicked passions, if they apply diligence. For the **Remonstrants** say in their *Confession*, chapter 1, section 14:

> Not only the learned, but also the unlearned <note>original: "idiotas"; in this context, it refers to laypeople or those lacking formal theological training, rather than a lack of intelligence</note> (provided they are endowed with common sense and judgment) can grasp their meaning—they are speaking of the clearer passages—as much as is sufficient; provided they do not allow themselves to be blinded by prejudices, vain confidence, or other wicked passions, but piously and diligently scrutinize this Scripture.

And the Socinians, in the *Racovian Catechism* <note>The defining statement of faith for the Socinian movement, first published in 1605</note>, chapter 3, when giving the reason for discrepancies in the understanding of Holy Scripture, assign this: namely, that people either read Holy Scripture negligently, or do not bring a sincere heart, free from all impediments and desires. See more things which relate to this, cited by us above. 

Moreover, in interpreting the more obscure passages, the Remonstrants assert in express words in their *Confession* and in the *Apology*, chapter 1, section 16, that the true sense can and ought to be discerned from the scope and occasion of each subject matter, from the antecedents and consequences, likewise from the comparison of similar passages, as well as from the palpable absurdities that would otherwise follow, and other arguments of that kind or indications of the facts. The Socinians, however, call **Reason** to their aid in these places, as is evident from their testimonies cited above, and indeed most clearly of all from Schlichting, when he says:

> If the matter concerns obscure things, anyone sees that the business cannot be completed without reason; and a little after, "for we do not recur to reason whenever Scripture openly testifies to the contrary, but whenever there is doubt about the mind of Scripture."

We have brought forward more things looking to the same end from him above, which can be seen. What truly is meant by the name of "reason" for these people in this place—whether it is the discursive faculty of reasoning and judging itself, or its dictates and findings discovered and unearthed through discourse and experience or by any other way—they do not expressly declare or pronounce; although they seem to understand both, indeed they will understand them both, if the Author of the *Brief...*

# CHAPTER XVI OF THE PARADOXES

The *Brief Disquisition against Valerius Magnus regarding the Non-Catholics' Rule of Faith* <note>original: "Brevis Disquisition. contra Valerian. Magn. de Acatholicorum credendi regulâ"</note> is a Socinian work and puts forward the Socinian opinion, just as the most celebrated theologian **Johannes Hoornbeek** <note>A Dutch Reformed professor (1617–1666) known for his exhaustive catalogs of theological "errors"</note> maintains, as we stated above in the place cited.

6. Thus, they also do not openly explain whether they use **Reason** in the interpretation of the Scriptures merely as a *means* or *instrument* by which the sense of the Sacred Letters is sought and drawn out, or whether they use it additionally as a **norm** <note>A standard or "measuring stick" by which truth is judged</note> according to which every interpretation must be directed and judged; except for that same writer, who seems to assert this latter point throughout his booklet, and especially in Chapter 7. There, when about to set forth the true opinion concerning the norm, he writes as follows:

> *Therefore, it must be denied that there is no other way of certainly knowing the truth in matters of faith than by consulting the Holy Spirit from the sacred letters after offering prayer. It must be established that the Sacred Letters are entirely the norm for controversies of Faith. And so far they are the only norm, insofar as Unwritten Traditions—by whatever name they may eventually go—are utterly excluded. Furthermore, to distinguish the authority and genuine mind of the Sacred Letters, those principles which are called **Philosophical** must also be called upon.*

And after he had summoned Metaphysics, Logic, Mathematics, Ethics, and History to aid the understanding of Holy Scripture, he concludes thus:

> *These and similar things, which are unknown to no sane person, must be established as the foundations of our knowledge regarding the authority of the Sacred Letters and regarding their true sense. Therefore, one who is even moderately equipped with these, whether by natural goodness of wit or experience, recognizes from those principles that the Scriptures themselves are sacred, and will judge those things which are necessary for salvation from the Scriptures with no trouble.*

7. The **Reformed** <note>The Calvinist tradition, which emphasizes the sovereignty of God and the necessity of grace</note> however (to return to them) do not deny that a sound mind—free from prejudices, not blinded by wicked affections, and diligently searching the word of God—is required for the understanding of the clearer passages of Holy Scripture; but they deny that it is sufficient unless, as we just said, the **illumination of the Holy Spirit** is added. Thus, besides that illumination, they certainly concede that in the interpretation of more obscure passages, there is a place for considering the **scope** <note>The overall aim or purpose of a text</note> and the occasion of each passage, as well as the subject matter, the things preceding and following (the context), and the comparison of similar passages. Finally, they allow for the observation of palpable absurdities that would otherwise follow, and other arguments and indications of things from which the sense of the passage is discerned. Furthermore, they grant that **Reason** must be employed, whether by that term one means the faculty itself or its dictates and findings. But they admit the faculty only as the *subject* or *instrument* that perceives and understands the Sacred Letters; and all the rest as the *means* or *instrument* by which the sense of the Sacred Scriptures is sought and drawn out: but in no way do they admit the one or the other as a judge-

# DISPUTATION

...by that light <note>The internal light of the Holy Spirit</note> one is able to understand the true sense and mind of Holy Scripture. It is clear that the **Socinians** <note>A rationalist Christian sect that denied the Trinity and emphasized reason</note> side with the **Arminians** <note>Also known as Remonstrants; they broke from strict Calvinism over the issue of predestination</note> here, as seen in the *Brief Disquisition against Valerius Magnus regarding the Non-Catholics' Rule of Faith* <note>original: "Brevi Disquisit. contra Valer. Magnum de Acatholicorum credendi regulâ"</note>, chapter 4, where these words are found: *“It must be denied that no one certainly attains the true sense of the Holy Scriptures unless they are internally enlightened and taught by the Holy Spirit.”* And a little later: *“Just as anyone can read the sacred words with their eyes and hear them with their ears without the internal help of the Holy Spirit or a peculiar illumination of the eyes and ears; so it is no less possible to understand and judge these same sacred things by the benefit of sound reason, even if the illumination of the Holy Spirit has not yet arrived.”* The same is also made plain from the cited passages of **Schlichting** <note>Jonas Schlichting (1592–1661), a leading Socinian theologian</note>, where, disputing against **Meisner** <note>Balthasar Meisner (1587–1626), an influential Lutheran theologian</note> concerning the interpretation of Scripture, he always recurs to **Reason**, and never to the illumination of the Holy Spirit.

5. Against the **Reformed** <note>Calvinist theologians</note>, the Remonstrants and Socinians establish that a sound mind and the **Light of the Natural intellect** <note>The innate human capacity for logic and reasoning, independent of divine grace</note>, provided it is free from prejudices and not overwhelmed by depraved affections, is sufficient—if diligence is applied—to understand the Holy Scriptures in their clearer passages. For the Remonstrants say in their *Confession*, chapter 1, section 14: *“Not only the learned, but even the unlearned <note>original: "idiotas"—meaning laypeople or those without formal theological training</note> (provided they are endowed with common sense and judgment) are able to attain their meaning (speaking of the clearer passages) as much as is sufficient: provided they do not allow themselves to be blinded by prejudice, vain confidence, or other depraved affections: but search this Scripture piously and diligently.”* And the Socinians, in the *Racovian Catechism* <note>The definitive statement of Socinian belief, first published in 1605</note>, chapter 3, when giving the cause for the discrepancies in the understanding of Holy Scripture, assign this: namely, that men either read Holy Scripture negligently, or they do not bring a sincere heart, free from all impediments and desires. See more things that pertain to this, which we cited above. 

Furthermore, they assert in express words in their *Confession* and in the *Apology*, chapter 1, section 16, that in interpreting more obscure passages, the true sense can and ought to be discerned from *“the scope and occasion of each passage, the subject matter, the antecedents and consequences <note>The context of what comes before and after a verse</note>, likewise from the comparison of similar passages, as well as from the palpable absurdities that would otherwise follow, and other arguments of that kind or indicators of things.”* The Socinians indeed in these places call **Reason** to their aid, as is clear from their own testimonies cited above, and most openly of all from Schlichting, when he says: *“If the matter concerns obscure things, anyone can see that the business cannot be settled without reason.”* and a little later: *“For we do not recur to reason as often as Scripture openly testifies to the contrary: but as often as the mind of Scripture is in doubt.”* We have brought forward more things looking toward the same point from him above, which can be seen. However, what is meant here by the name of "reason"—whether it is the discursive faculty of reasoning and judging itself, or its dictates and findings discovered and dug out through discourse and experience or in any other way—they themselves do not eloquently declare or pronounce; although they seem to understand both, and indeed they do understand both, if the Author...

# CHAPTER 16 OF THE PARADOXES

### The *Brief Disquisition against Valerius Magnus regarding the Non-Catholics' Rule of Faith* is Socinian <note>The Socinians were a rationalist Christian group that rejected the Trinity and emphasized human reason as the ultimate judge of religious truth.</note> and proposes the Socinian opinion, just as the most celebrated theologian Johannes Hoornbeek <note>A leading 17th-century Dutch Reformed theologian known for his polemics against various sects.</note> maintains, as we have stated above in the place cited.

6. Thus they also do not openly explain whether they use **Reason** in the interpretations of the Scriptures only as a means or instrument by which the sense of the Holy Letters is investigated and extracted, or indeed additionally as a norm according to which every interpretation must be directed and judged; unless it is that same writer, who seems to assert this latter point here and there in his booklet, and especially in chapter 7. > *Therefore, it must be denied that there is no other way of certainly knowing the truth in a matter of faith than by consulting the Holy Spirit from the sacred letters after offering a prayer.* <note>The author here quotes a rationalist opponent who argues that divine intervention is not strictly necessary to understand the Bible.</note> *And it must be established that the Sacred Letters are entirely the norm for controversies of Faith. Therefore, whoever is instructed in these, whether by natural goodness of talent or by experience even moderately, he both recognizes from those principles that the Scriptures themselves are sacred, and will judge those things which are necessary for salvation from the Scriptures with no trouble.*

7. The **Reformed** <note>Mainline Calvinist theologians who emphasized that human reason is corrupted by sin and needs divine grace to understand spiritual truth.</note> indeed (to return to them) do not deny that for the understanding of the clearer passages of Holy Scripture, a sound mind is required—one freed from prejudices, not blinded by depraved affections, and diligently searching the word of God; but they deny that it suffices unless, as we just said, the illumination of the Holy Spirit is added. Moreover, they also grant that **Reason** must be applied, whether the term refers to the faculty of the mind itself or to its dictates and findings. But they only admit the faculty as a subject or instrument that perceives and understands the Holy Letters; all the rest they admit as a medium or instrument by which the sense of the Holy Scriptures is investigated and extracted; but by no means do they admit these or that as a judge—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "norm" or standard of biblical interpretation. On the previous page, the author explored the views of the Socinians and Remonstrants (groups often accused of rationalism), noting how they were criticized by Reformed theologians for supposedly making human reason the judge of Scripture. This page analyzes whether the Reformed position is truly different from their opponents' and begins to introduce the author's own radical thesis: that Philosophy is the only infallible norm for understanding the Bible.</meta>

# OF THE EXERCISE

...instrument, canon, or norm, according to which interpretation should be established, or against which it should be tested. But they attribute this duty and function to **Holy Scripture** alone, so that it might be its own interpreter and the norm of interpreting (as we have explained at length in chapters 10 and 11), striving with all their might to show that the Socinians <note>A rationalist Christian sect that rejected the Trinity and emphasized reason in interpreting the Bible.</note> and Remonstrants <note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius, known for emphasizing human free will; their views often clashed with strict Calvinist Reformed orthodoxy.</note> assign this role to reason, as is clear from the *Censure of the Remonstrant Confession*, chapter 1, sections 14 and 16, and Hoornbeek's <note>Johannes Hoornbeek (1617–1666), a prominent Dutch Reformed theologian and polemicist.</note> *Refutation of Socinianism*, volume 1, book 1, chapter 6. 

However, I have neither found this expressly in their writings, nor been able to gather it from them—not even from the *Brief Disquisition against Valerius Magnus* <note>original: "Brevi disquisitione contra Valer. Magn." – a polemical work often attributed to the Socinian Joachim Stegmann.</note> cited just now. For Amandus Polanus <note>A significant early Reformed (Calvinist) systematic theologian.</note> himself, a writer of no small reputation among the Reformed, in his *Syntagma of Theology*, book 1, chapter 45, question 6, dealing with the *Means of finding the true sense and use of Sacred Scripture*, lists in the fifth place among those things to be applied in the study of Scripture itself: *The order and method in investigating the sense and use. Of which the first consideration is the purpose; the second is the Theme being discussed; the third is the arguments by which the Theme is explained or confirmed, along with the circumstances—namely of persons, occasion, place, time, antecedents, and consequences, etc. And in the seventh place, the Comparison of one passage with other parallel and similar passages—of a more obscure passage with clear and open ones dealing with the same matter. And in the eighth place, the Collation <note>The act of comparing or bringing together.</note> of the passage to be explained with other dissimilar passages, even those appearing to conflict, and their reconciliation. And finally, in the twelfth place, the knowledge of the Arts and disciplines, and especially of Grammar, Rhetoric, Dialectic <note>Logic.</note>, and Physics.*

And similar points occur here and there among others as well. If all these things go by the name of "Means" among the Reformed, why should they not also go by the same name among the Remonstrants and Socinians? Indeed, they even seem to go by that name in a similar way as they do among the Reformed, as is clear from Smalcius <note>Valentin Smalcius (1572–1622), a leading Socinian theologian.</note> against Frantsius in the preface, when he says: *where there is need for interpretation, we do not say that reason is to be followed principally, but we teach that the interpretation of the Holy Letters is to be sought from the letters themselves, and thus we hang entirely upon the mouth of the Master, the Holy Spirit.* Thus, these things provide no firm foundation for the Reformed to conclude that those groups establish Reason as the norm of interpretation—a point they nevertheless strive to prove with such great zeal and effort. Whatever the opinion of the Arminians and Socinians might be, it is all the same to us; our intention is to investigate not what this or that person thinks, but what the truth dictates.

8. Therefore, to finally set forth our own opinion regarding the norm of interpretation—which we judge to be true—and to compare it with the preceding ones: we say that no one can understand the true and genuine sense of a clear passage of Scripture, nor extract and explain the sense of an obscure one, unless he has done so through or according to true Philosophy, as an infallible norm.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the "norm" or standard for interpreting Holy Scripture. The author is distinguishing his own radical position—that Philosophy is the infallible standard for understanding the Bible—from the positions of the Socinians, Arminians (Remonstrants), and the Reformed (Calvinist) theologians. He has just finished explaining that while others use reason as a tool, he elevates "True Philosophy" to a decisive rule of truth.</meta>

### CHAPTER XVI OF THE PARADOXES

...has investigated or explored it, it is certain: nor can one demonstrate such a thing to others except through the same norm. Thus, we establish **true Philosophy** as the **Lydian stone** <note>original: "Lydium lapidem" — a touchstone used to test the purity of gold; here, a metaphor for an infallible standard of truth.</note>, by which it may be infallibly examined and most certainly demonstrated whether a given sense of Holy Scripture—whether understood with no effort or unearthed with great labor—agrees with the intention of the writer, or whether it deviates from it.

From this, it is clear that our opinion differs from that of the *Socinians* <note>A rationalist sect that emphasized reason but, according to the author, lacked a formal philosophical system for interpretation.</note> and *Arminians* <note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius who argued for human cooperation in salvation; also known as Remonstrants.</note> in three ways:
1. Regarding the **certainty and infallibility** of both understanding and interpretation; they say not a word about this, while we express it explicitly.
2. Regarding the **clarity of Scripture** in matters necessary for salvation and things clear in themselves; they acknowledge this clarity, while we deny it.
3. Regarding the **interpretation of obscure passages**; they seem to want to employ *Reason*—the consideration of circumstances, what comes before and after a text, and the comparison of similar or dissimilar passages—only as *means* or tools. We, however, make **Philosophy** the *norm* and boldly declare it so.

In this last point, we also disagree with the **Reformed** <note>The mainstream Calvinist theologians of the era.</note>, who establish Scripture as its own norm of interpretation. We also differ from them on the second point, since the Reformed do not hold a different view from the *Socinians* and *Remonstrants* regarding the clarity and transparency of Scripture; both of these views we have refuted at length above in Chapter 11. Finally, as far as the illumination of the Holy Spirit is concerned, we have already asserted—against the Reformed and alongside the *Socinians* and *Remonstrants*—that it is simply not necessary for understanding the Word of God, as we proved in Chapters 6 and 14. Thus, we believe nothing more remains to be said concerning the opinions of the *Socinians* and *Arminians* and their agreement or disagreement with the Reformed and our own views; we consider what we proposed to examine at the beginning of this chapter to be entirely settled.

9. However, before we **take our hand off the tablet** <note>original: "manum de tabulâ tollamus" — a Latin idiom meaning to finish or complete a work.</note> and bring our Exercise to an end, we must resolve an absurdity here that the most learned **Hoornbeek** <note>Johannes Hoornbeek (1617–1666), a prominent Dutch Reformed theologian who wrote extensively against "heretical" groups.</note> brings onto the stage in his *Refutation of Socinianism* (Volume 1, Book 1, Chapter 6, Section 2, Argument 4). He directs it against the *Socinians*, but it could also be adapted against our own opinion: namely, *“If Philosophy, or reason, is the infallible Norm for interpreting Holy Scripture, then Scripture and its interpretation are subject to reason (or Philosophy). This is against the dignity of Scripture and against the truth; and one should rightly shudder to claim such a thing for one’s own judgment.”*

For this reason, the judgment of the Holy Spirit and the testimony of Holy Scripture are elsewhere so carefully distinguished from, and even opposed to, the testimony of men, as in the First Epistle of John, Chapter 5, verse 9: *“If we receive the testimony of men, the testimony of God is greater.”* And likewise in the Gospel of John, Chapter 5, verse 34: *“But I do not receive testimony from man.”* Not unlike this is what the same author says in the same chapter, Section 1, page 91: *“That Reason (or Philo-”*

<meta>catchword: sophy)</meta>

<summary>The author concludes his comparison between his philosophical method and the theological schools of his time, asserting that Philosophy is the only infallible rule for interpreting the Bible. He then begins to address an objection from the theologian Johannes Hoornbeek, who argues that making reason the standard for interpretation undermines the divine authority of Scripture.</summary>

He is arguing that while they claim to rely on Scripture alone, they all use various "means" or tools of logic and reason, which sets the stage for his own argument that "True Philosophy" should be the formal standard.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>OF THE EXERCISE</header>

...instrument, canon, or norm, according to which an interpretation must be established, or against which it should be tested. Instead, they attribute this office and duty to **Holy Scripture** alone, so that it may be its own interpreter and norm of interpretation (as we explained at length in chapters 10 and 11). They strive with all their might to show that the *Socinians* <note>Followers of Fausto Sozzini who emphasized a rationalist approach to the Bible, often denying the Trinity.</note> and *Remonstrants* <note>Another name for Arminians, followers of Jacobus Arminius.</note> assign this role to reason, as is evident from the *Censure of the Remonstrant Confession* (chapter 1, sections 14 and 16) and Hoornbeek’s *Socinianism Refuted* (Volume 1, book 1, chapter 6). 

However, I have neither found this expressly stated in their writings, nor could I gather it from them—not even from the *Short Disquisition against Valerianus Magnus* <note>Valerianus Magnus was a Capuchin friar and diplomat who engaged in famous polemics with Protestants and Socinians.</note> in the passage just cited. For Amandus Polanus himself <note>Amandus Polanus von Polansdorf (1561–1610), a significant Reformed theologian known for his systematic approach.</note>, a writer of no small repute among the Reformed, in his *Syntagma of Theology* (book 1, chapter 45, question 6), when dealing with the *Means of finding the true sense and use of Holy Scripture*, lists in the fifth place among those things to be used in the meditation of Scripture: *The order and method in investigating the sense and use. The first consideration of which is the purpose; the second, the Theme being discussed; the third, the arguments by which the Theme is explained or confirmed, along with the circumstances—namely the persons, occasion, place, time, and what comes before and after [the text], etc. And seventh, the Comparison of one passage with other parallel and similar passages—the more obscure with the clear and open ones dealing with the same matter. And eighth, the Collation [comparison] of the passage to be explained with other dissimilar passages, even those that seem to clash, and their reconciliation. And finally twelfth, the knowledge of the Arts and disciplines, especially Grammar, Rhetoric, Logic <note>original: "Dialecticæ"</note>, and Physics.*

And similar points occur here and there among other writers as well. If all these things come under the name of "Means" among the Reformed, why should they not also come under the same name among the Remonstrants and Socinians? Indeed, they do seem to be used in a similar way as they are among the Reformed, as is clear from Smalcius <note>Valentin Smalcius (1572–1622), a prominent Socinian theologian and writer of the Racovian Catechism.</note> in his work against Frants in the preface, when he says: *where there is need for interpretation, we do not say that reason is to be followed principally, but we teach that the interpretation of the* **Holy Scriptures** *must be sought from the Scriptures themselves, and thus we hang entirely upon the mouth of the Master, the Holy Spirit.* 

Thus, these things provide no firm foundation for the Reformed to conclude that those groups establish Reason as the norm of interpretation—a point which the Reformed nevertheless strive with such great effort and labor to prove. Whatever the opinion of the Arminians and Socinians may be, it is all the same to us; our intention is not to investigate what this or that person thinks, but what the truth itself dictates.

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of Philosophy as the standard for biblical interpretation. He is responding to the theological objection that using Reason to judge Scripture is a "metabasis eis allo genos"—a logical fallacy where one applies the rules of one field (like logic or nature) to a completely different field (like divine revelation).</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>OF THE EXERCISE</header>

Philosophy) in such doubts, and to hear it as an argument or a norm according to which a decision is made concerning the mind of Holy Scripture, is a most prodigious <term>shift into another category<note>original Greek: μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος (*metabasis eis allo genos*). In logic, this is the error of jumping from one subject area to another where the same rules do not apply.</note></term>, and as much unworthy of Scripture as it is wicked in itself, that Reason should dare to claim this for itself. However, to dissolve this difficulty, one must simply recall the distinction of the word "Scripture" brought forward above in Chapter 13, Argument 1: namely, that Scripture is either taken **materially**—denoting the letters, syllables, words, and the speeches composed from them—or **formally**, signifying the senses and the things expressed and designated by those words and speeches.

Now indeed, we think it in no way unworthy that the letters, syllables, words, and speeches (which must come under the name of "Scripture" when its interpretation is discussed) should be subject to **sound Reason** or **Philosophy**. For both Right Reason and True Philosophy are, as we have shown above—and no man of sound mind will deny—excellent and singular gifts granted by God to the human race. The letters, syllables, words, and speeches, however, are nothing other than "talking sounds," or the mute signs of these sounds, which signify concepts or things by human institution and will; therefore, they are not to be compared in any degree of dignity even with those aforementioned noble gifts of God. 

Wherefore, it is neither wicked, nor should anyone rightly shudder, to claim the interpretation of the words of Scripture for one's own sound judgment, or right reason, or true philosophy. It would indeed be wicked, and one would rightly shudder, if one were to subject the divine eloquence itself and the oracles uttered in those words to these things; for that would indeed do violence to the Divine mind itself. Truth can in no way be subject to truth—much less opposed to it—whether nature has dictated it or Scripture has revealed it, since it always has God Himself as its author, just as we taught in Chapters 5 and 8. 

From this it also follows that every interpretation of Scripture adorned with the help of right Reason or true Philosophy does not lean upon human testimony, but **Divine** testimony; and thus the passages cited from John in no way hinder our opinion. This is even less the case with that other objection brought forward: namely, that this is a "shift into another category." For already previously, against Balthasar Meisner <note>A Lutheran theologian known for his "Philosophy Sobriety," who argued against the over-use of reason in theology.</note> in the book *On the Holy Trinity*, etc., page 84, Slichtingius <note>Jonas Schlichting (1592–1661), a leading Socinian theologian and biblical commentator.</note> denied that right and manifest reason is a "foreign" or "alien" principle in divine matters, especially when the question is whether Scripture says something or does not say it, as is the case here. 

Nor do we see why—since Reason can be used as a "subject and instrument" (as Hoornbeek <note>Johannes Hoornbeek (1617–1666), a prominent Dutch Reformed theologian.</note> admits in many places in the cited chapter) and the disciplines of Philosophy can be used as a "medium" (as we just reported that Polanus <note>Amandus Polanus (1561–1610), an influential Reformed systematic theologian.</note> establishes)—why it should not be permitted to assign this task to the former as much as to the latter as a **norm**, without leaping from one category into another. This is even more true if what Hoornbeek says in the cited chapter, section 21, is correct: namely, that the axioms of universal and perpetual truth...

<meta>This page concludes the author’s argument that logic and philosophy are not "foreign" to theology, but rather the essential tools for settling religious disputes and establishing a firm understanding of divine truth. <page-num>105</page-num>

->P A R A D O X E S  C H A P.  X V I.<-

such as, *that the same thing cannot both be and not be at the same time*, etc. Theology does not adopt these from nature or reason as a prior principle; rather, they are so much a part of common truth, and so rooted in divine truth, that in these matters neither does reason accuse Scripture, nor Scripture accuse reason. Instead, they flow from the same fountain on both sides, and hold a place universally everywhere. To all this, add that the pronouncements of that sort—of which <term>Peripatetic Philosophy<note>The philosophy of Aristotle and his followers, which dominated medieval and early modern universities.</note></term> has produced a massive clutter—are not to be held in high regard. In the search for Truth, they are not so much helpful tools (the name under which they are thrust upon the youth) as they are hindrances by which progress is interrupted.

For what reason stands in the way of using an argument sought from Mathematics to solve a question in Physics, or using a physical conclusion to support a medical thesis? I, for one, see none. I do not consider that anxious distinction between the <term>Liberal Arts<note>The traditional academic subjects, including logic, grammar, and rhetoric.</note></term>, devised by the Peripatetics and usually observed with such zeal—on which that <gloss>shift into another category</gloss> <note>original Greek: μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος (*metabasis eis allo genos*). In logic, this is the error of jumping from one subject area to another where the same rules do not apply.</note> seems to rely—to be of such weight that, because of it, those things which are connected to each other in the nature of things (and of which one can thus even be known from the other) should be separated from one another, and one of them should not be proven from the other.

But now we have reached the finish line of our course. For we have considered and refuted the opinions differing from our own view, held by the <term>Arminians<note>Followers of Jacobus Arminius who argued for human free will in salvation.</note></term> and <term>Socinians<note>A rationalist sect that denied the Trinity.</note></term>, as well as the <term>Reformed<note>Calvinist Protestants.</note></term> and <term>Papists<note>original Latin: *Pontificiorum*. Referring to the Roman Catholic Church.</note></term>. Moreover, we have kept our view safe and sound <note>original: *sartam tectam*, a legal idiom meaning "in good repair."</note> from the accusations of adversaries and the charges with which it might be attacked. Furthermore, after the sufficiently detailed and extensive explanation provided before, we have proven it to be true and beyond doubt. Let Philosophy, therefore, be and perpetually remain the infallible norm for interpreting Holy Scripture, since reason both persuades and dictates this, and the practice of theologians of every age confirms it. Whatever difficulties are raised against it can be cleared away with little effort. Having rejected all other norms of interpretation which we have weighed and refuted, let this one live and flourish hereafter in the Church of Christ. May it help to settle the disagreements that have arisen among Christians, so that the uncorrupted doctrine of heavenly truth may be more and more established and spread, and our souls may at last enjoy eternal salvation and become sharers of everlasting bliss.

## ## PARADOX XVI.

->PARADOX XVI.<-

Of such a kind as they are, one thing seems possible: that there is both one and the other; unless it is a vain and merely philosophical notion, such as that which is derived from first principles. But it seems this cannot be done, since we see it happening in other things—for instance, those where a whole result arises from <term>matter and form<note>The Aristotelian theory of hylomorphism, which suggests all physical substances are a combination of 'matter' (the underlying stuff) and 'form' (the essence or structure that makes it what it is).</note></term>, though they are diverse and distinct, and one is a part of the other; yet each truly possesses its own inviolable nature. Add to all these not a few things which seem to be held by those recently mentioned, whose genius has shone before them and who prefer Philosophy. Among these is the fact that in Aristotle's investigation, they have not so much found the same bonds as they have thrust forward things found in their name, so that the defenses of the <unclear>interpreters</unclear> are removed. Certain <unclear>trifles</unclear> stand in the way of things too much—those, of course, arising from the things he says; for he says those very things within them. For regarding philosophical questions, he says in the Conclusion: "And that teaches what the Master <note>original: *Magister*—a traditional title for Aristotle in university settings.</note> said"; for he seems anxious about that discipline. Meanwhile, having been freed, he has somewhat veiled the obstructing surface; it seems to be an <unclear>existing</unclear> <unclear>rather</unclear> <unclear>than</unclear> something that is a whole within itself, and it is clear the name fits it. # E P I L O G U E .

I have deliberated long and hard, my Reader, whether I should submit this offspring <note>original: *foetum*; the author uses a common biological metaphor for his intellectual work.</note> of mine—already mature, yet likely to appear untimely and monstrous to very many theologians—to the press and bring it forth into the light; or whether I should truly suppress it within myself and condemn it to eternal darkness. The advantages and disadvantages which seemed likely to arise from its publication provided the occasion for this deliberation, and held my mind in suspense, not knowing which way to turn. Since I think it would be to your benefit to know what these are, and of what kind and scale, I have judged it not foreign to my purpose to set them briefly before your eyes here.

The disadvantages, then—to speak of those first—were two, and indeed quite heavy ones: one concerns me, and the other concerns the Christian Churches. For I feared that disturbances would be created in those churches by this treatise, since not only are almost all new things accustomed to rouse them; but, what is primary, the issue being aired and examined here is not some trifling <term>dogma<note>A core principle or tenet of a religious system.</note></term> of little weight in Religion, but one of such importance that, after the authority of the Sacred Scriptures <note>original: *S. Literarum*</note>, it undoubtedly holds the first place in Christianity. It is the very foundation and basis on which all other articles of both faith and morals lean, and from which, as if from a fountain, they flow and are derived. In this matter, since we have shown that all the Churches which have taken the name of Christ and come to our knowledge are wandering in error <note>original: *hallucinari*; literally "hallucinating" or being deluded.</note> and are far removed from the truth, it seems hardly possible but that some within them—acknowledging and laying aside their error and embracing the truth demonstrated by us—will change their opinion; while others, who stubbornly cling to their accepted opinions out of a hope for profit or honor, or by blind impulse or a settled mind, will take it most poorly. Among these groups, therefore—with the former championing our opinion (to which they have recently come) and the latter, by contrast, attacking it while defending their long-held views—the rope of dissension and contention would be pulled. As is customary in religious affairs, the battle would be fought most fiercely, as if for "altars and hearths" <note>original: *pro aris & focis*; a Roman idiom for defending one's most sacred and vital interests.</note>, and with the greatest force and impulse, not without the greatest damage and disgrace to the Christian name.

For me, truly, the disadvantage that looms is the hatred of the theologians who disapprove of and reject my opinion; I will undoubtedly stir up against myself a hatred of the most bitter kind, such as they are accustomed to burn with. For since it is almost always the case with these men that they exalt themselves above all the learned—as those who are most firmly persuaded that the oracles of God were entrusted to them alone to be interpreted, and the divine worship committed to them—they immediately flare up and take offense if anyone else dares "to put his sickle into their harvest" <note>A metaphor for interfering in another's professional territory or expertise.</note>. And, since each of them [believes] nothing [he says] is not perfectly in harmony with the truth and most distantly divided from any company with error, any one of them...

He argues that his work challenges the very foundation of Christian faith, which he believes all existing churches have misunderstood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's list of "disadvantages" or risks associated with publishing his work. # EPILOGUE.

(even though they fight among themselves on many opposing fronts) they believe with a firm mind that they teach the truth. They think that something is taken away from Divine majesty and glory if a core **dogma** <note>original: *statuminatum dogma*; a firmly established tenet.</note> already settled by them is uprooted, and they contend that another ought to be substituted in its place. Hence, each one also thinks it is his duty and his zeal to stand in the way and prevent his religion from suffering any decrease or detriment. And so, out of anger, they conceive a hatred, and they pursue whoever does not subscribe to their opinions and who does not fear to propose to the theological world for examination something that contradicts their decrees even in the slightest. 

And so that they may vomit out the poison of this anger and hatred, they render that person hated by the common people, among whom their authority carries the most weight. By traducing his name in unworthy ways and loading him with slanders and insults, they warn and command that he should be avoided "worse than a dog or a snake" <note>original: *cane pejus & angue fugiendum*; a classical idiom for something to be avoided at all costs.</note>, as the worst of heretics and one who fosters abominable and most ruinous opinions. If they succeed in this, it follows that the man, as if "marked with a black coal" <note>original: *atrô carbone notatus*; a Roman expression for being marked as wicked or unlucky</note>, is avoided by everyone, deserted, and badly treated. Thus, little by little, he is deprived of his business (if he practices trade) or his profit (if he practices a craft), and finally, he is stripped of his family property and the comforts by which he sustains his life. He is reduced to the height of poverty and unhappiness, forced to spend the rest of his life most miserably. 

And so that nothing is lacking from his misfortune, and all pity is shut out, they later cry out that these evils they procured for him are a "most just punishment," which he pays to the Most High for his impiety and execrable opinion—clear signs which they say clearly convict and condemn him of heresy. Our "little ship" <note>original: *navicula*; a common metaphor for the author's life's work or his personal safety.</note> could also be swept away by a similar storm—since we live among men and very often need their help and labor—dashed against the rocks, overwhelmed by the waves, and sunk in the deep, if we were to commit it to such an impetuous sea, subject to such great tempests and threatening most certain shipwreck.

And these two disadvantages seemed to me of such weight that I have already delayed the printing of this treatise for several years, and have quite often decided in my own mind that I would never permit it to be set in type or sent out into public. This was especially because, besides these things, having been taught by experience, I also noticed and held for certain that the <term>Prelates<note>High-ranking church officials, such as bishops or abbots.</note></term> and <term>Clergy<note>The formal leadership and ordained ministers of the church.</note></term> of all **Sects** in the Christian religion, as well as the common people and <term>Laity<note>Ordinary church members who are not part of the professional clergy.</note></term> themselves, are so tenacious of their opinions—imbibed both from infancy and from their training—that you could sooner "touch the sky with your hand" than pluck even one of those opinions from their minds, even if it had never been seriously examined, much less understood. And thus, I feared I had not only "wasted oil and labor" <note>original: *oleum atque operam perdidisse*; an idiom meaning to waste time and effort on a fruitless task.</note> in writing this little work, but would also waste it in publishing it.

He expresses deep skepticism that people can ever be convinced to change their long-held, unexamined beliefs.</summary>
