# E P I L O G U E .

Yet, despite all these things, the advantages and the fruits that I hope the Christian religion will abundantly gather from this work have finally prevailed and moved my mind from its previous opinion. To these reasons was added the fact that I had initially imagined the first of those listed disadvantages to be more severe, and likely to bring greater evils upon the Church, than I later discovered to be the case in situations similar to—or even more serious than—my own. For while I was keeping this writing private, other works appeared in which **dogmas** <note>original: *dogmata*; the established principles or tenets of the faith.</note> of much greater importance—indeed, the very foundation of the entire Church—seemed not only to be weakened, but torn up and overturned. This occurred without any great outcry, except among a few of the more learned scholars. Furthermore, I can avoid the second disadvantage by hiding behind the suppression of my name. I realize this will somewhat damage the reputation of my **Treatise** <note>original: *Tractatui*</note> and my opinions among those who judge the value of a writing by the author’s name, or who are in the habit of condemning anything published anonymously as mere trifles or "toothed" writings <note>original: *scripta dentata*; a phrase referring to biting or satirical works</note> unworthy of being read. However, I did not think this was so important that I should either risk the dangers mentioned before, or—treating such petty censors with indifference—fail the zeal and utility of more fair-minded readers by denying them the benefits that will flow to them from this work.

The first and greatest of these benefits is that, although for several centuries the interpretation of **Holy Scripture** <note>original: *S. Literarum*</note> has been cast into doubt, and interpreters have been unable to either infallibly draw out its true meanings or demonstrate that they have done so—whence so many and such great dissensions and contentions have sprouted among Christians, and still flourish and sprout new ones daily—by the **Method** <note>original: *Methodô*</note> and rule established in this little book, an interpreter of the Divine word need no longer wander in uncertainty. Instead, he may lean upon and advance with a firm and unswerving foot. Thus, the "saw of disputes" <note>original: *disputationum disceptationumque serra*; a metaphor for the back-and-forth grinding of endless arguments</note> which is pulled so soulfully and sharply, may be taken away, and tranquility and peace may be won for the whole Christian world and established forever.

The second benefit is that, according to this opinion of ours, the task of the interpreter will not struggle with such great difficulties as before. Since we have shown that all truths found in the Scriptures are also true meanings, and that multiple meanings can exist in any single passage, some of these many meanings will be easier to investigate and find, and more obvious than if only one single meaning had to be extracted. It is less work to reach a destination where there are many paths than to think out the one and only way that exists. And **Chamier** <note>Daniel Chamier (1565–1621), a highly influential French Reformed theologian.</note> himself acknowledges this ease added to the interpretation of Holy Scripture by our opinion in his *Universal Panoply of Catholic Doctrine* <note>original: *Panstrat. Cathol.*; short for *Panstratia Catholica*, a massive work of Protestant theology.</note>, Volume 1, Book 15, Chapter 15, Paragraph 31, in these words: *“If there were [multiple literal senses], Scripture would seem easier because of it. For the difficulty of Scripture, if there is any, consists...”*

<sig>O 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: consists</meta>

<summary>In this epilogue, the author justifies publishing his work anonymously to avoid persecution, arguing that his new interpretive method will end religious conflict by proving that a single passage of Scripture can have multiple valid, "true" meanings.</summary>
<keywords>hermeneutics, religious peace, anonymity, Daniel Chamier, interpretation of Scripture, theological disputes, literal sense</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s transition from his personal fears of persecution to a defense of his work's utility. He has just finished explaining that he chose to publish anonymously to avoid personal danger while still providing a valuable service to the Church. He now begins to list the specific benefits (commoda) of his interpretive method.</meta>

-> # E P I L O G U E <-

...consists in finding the true sense. Moreover, things that are many are usually found more easily than things that are unique, as experience teaches in every kind of matter.

**Third**, this Method of ours provides a shortcut for interpretation, in that it will not be necessary to consult the theologians and the interpreters and commentators of the Divine Word from every age and place, or from every sect, to weigh their opinions on the meaning of any particular passage. Instead, equipped with this rule we have assigned, anyone will be able to investigate and draw out the truth by their own strength <note>original: *propriô marte*; literally "by one's own Mars," an idiom meaning by one's own efforts or resources</note> and by the mind of God, and to explore what has been investigated and drawn out by others to discern without a doubt whether it be genuine. No one will fail to see how much time is gained and how much labor and trouble is avoided by this, if they are willing to consider how many and how great are the volumes written with much late-night study <note>original: *elucubrata*; referring to "lucubration" or work produced by candlelight</note> upon the **Holy Codices** <term>Codices: ancient manuscript volumes of the Bible</term> that are now worn thin in the hands of Christians.

And indeed, these three advantages regard the Interpretation itself—namely, that it may be carried out more safely, more easily, and more concisely. The following three, however, concern other matters: namely, the corruption of the **Sacred Letters** <note>original: *S. Literarum*; the Holy Scriptures</note>, the **variant readings**, and finally, the Translation or version. But let us examine each one.

**Fourth**, therefore, is that this Method of ours for interpreting can also serve to detect the mutilations and corruptions in both the Hebrew and Greek **Codices**. Of these, almost none have had the good fortune—if we believe that **Most Distinguished Man** <note>This likely refers to a contemporary scholar of textual criticism, such as Louis Cappel or perhaps Erasmus, who was well-known for identifying errors in biblical manuscripts.</note> who is most skilled in these matters—not to have suffered under the "left-handed" <note>original: *sinistras*; meaning malicious or unlucky</note> hands of ill-wishers, and to have been contaminated and defiled by a great many errors.

**Fifth**, that it helps in exploring the **variant readings** of **Holy Scripture** and in discerning which are spurious and which are genuine. There is a most fertile harvest of these in the books of both Covenants, to such an extent that the **Rabbis** <note>original: *Rabbinos*; Jewish scholars and teachers of the law.</note> themselves confess that in the **Old Testament** *there is such a great abundance and confusion of them that it is difficult to distinguish the true from the false; and if anyone were to compare and examine all the written manuscripts of the **New Testament**, he would find almost as many discrepancies as there are words.* That same man, who is by far the most experienced in every kind of this learning and literature, does not fear to say this in plain words.

**Sixth** and finally, that it carries no small weight in detecting false translations of the **Holy Books**, which are prepared either from corrupted texts or spurious readings, for those who are ignorant of the **Original Languages** and lack the opportunity to learn them. Moreover, that there can be—and indeed are—versions of this kind is proven by the slips observed here and there in a great many places.

And these three advantages rest upon the same foundation, and flow in the same way from our rule of interpreting. For since nothing is contained in the Scriptures that is not in harmony with the truth, and since that truth can undoubtedly be drawn out by our Method and demonstrated once it is drawn out, by that same method every corrupt text...

...a false reading or a bad translation that teaches something untrue will be exposed, and will be caught and refuted for being of that character. Thus, the danger of falling into falsehood and seizing or accepting it as truth will be removed. This danger hangs over all other interpreters, who lack the ability to escape it as long as they are without the **Lydian stone** <term>Lydian stone: a "touchstone" or dark siltstone once used to test the purity of gold; here used metaphorically as a standard for testing truth</term>, by which they might explore with certainty which passages are pure and which are corrupted; distinguish without doubt which readings are genuine and which are spurious; and infallibly discern which translations are correct and which are perverse or bad. I confess I cannot conceive how they will manage for themselves without it.

These are the reasons that swayed my mind from the suppression of this writing toward its printing and publication. When these reasons were placed in a fair balance against the two disadvantages I have reviewed, and were weighed with careful examination, they far outweighed them. Therefore, as soon as a suitable opportunity presented itself, I set myself to the work and applied the **final hand** <note>original: *extremam manum*; a common Latin idiom for the finishing touches on a work</note> to it, just as it is now presented to the literary world. And although, besides those two mentioned disadvantages, another of perhaps no small weight did not escape me—namely, that many people, and especially theologians, would rise up against me and my opinion and exercise, "unsheathing their pens" <note>original: *stylumque stricturos*; a martial metaphor comparing the pen to a sword</note> against me—it was necessary for me to engage with them and pull the **saw of dispute** <note>original: *disputationis ferra reciprocanda*; a metaphor for the back-and-forth nature of a heated debate</note>. Yet this never had enough power over me that I ever considered not publishing this writing, much less did I decide upon it.

For if anyone deigns to spend a few hours unrolling this little treatise of ours and examines what they find written in it with the scale of their own judgment, and discovers with certainty—and demonstrates solidly in a public writing—that the proof of our opinion is built on a false foundation, or is wrongly and poorly deduced from true principles; or that it is not properly defended and protected from the insults and accusations with which it might be attacked by others; or that the opinions of those who disagree are not well-opposed and refuted, and their arguments not correctly diluted and dissolved; then I will also offer that person my special thanks in a public writing, after a candid confession of my error. I would do so because they considered it worth the effort to expose my mistakes to me and to prevent others from being entangled or led astray by them. I hold nothing dearer, nothing more ancient <note>original: *antiquius*; used here in the sense of "more important" or "a higher priority"</note>, and I desire nothing more earnestly than to be taught the truth and to be led back to the right path if I have turned aside from it.

But if, on the other hand, someone else who has not yet correctly grasped our meaning should bring forward such things and raise such difficulties that might delay them or place a barrier for others, preventing the force and evidence of our Demonstration from being understood in every part, or preventing a full vindication of our opinion from the twisted arrows of objections hurled at it, or [preventing the vindication] of other opinions from ours...

...consists in finding the true sense. Moreover, those things which are numerous are usually found more easily than those which are unique, as experience teaches in individual categories of things.

Thirdly, our Method serves as a shortcut for interpretation in that it is not necessary to consult the theologians and interpreters or commentators of the Divine Word from all ages and places, or from all sects, regarding the meaning of any given passage, nor to weigh their opinions. Instead, equipped with this rule we have provided, anyone can, by their own effort <note>original: *proprio marte*; literally "by one's own Mars," a common Latin idiom meaning by one's own strength or initiative</note> and with the mind of God, track down and draw out the meaning. They can also examine meanings found and drawn out by others to explore whether they are genuine and distinguish them beyond doubt. No one will fail to see how much time is gained and how much labor and trouble is avoided by this, if they consider how many massive volumes of commentary on the Holy Books are worn thin by the hands of Christians.

And these three benefits concern interpretation itself—namely, so that it may be carried out more safely, more easily, and more concisely. The following three points, which also make up a set of three, concern other matters: namely, the corruption of the Holy Scriptures, variant readings, and finally, translation or version. And these three benefits rest on the same foundation and flow in the same way from our rule of interpretation. Since nothing is contained in the Scriptures that is not consistent with the truth, and since by our Method that truth can undoubtedly be drawn out and demonstrated, by that same method every corrupted text...

...a spurious reading or a bad translation that teaches falsehood will be uncovered; it will be detected and exposed for what it is. This is a danger that hangs over all other interpreters, who lack the means to escape it as long as they are without a touchstone <note>original: *Lydiô lapide*; literally a "Lydian stone," an ancient tool used to investigate the purity of gold, here used as a metaphor for a standard of truth</note> with which they can reliably explore which passages are pure and which are corrupted; distinguish between genuine and spurious readings beyond doubt; and infallibly recognize which translations are correct and which are perverse or poor. We must confess that we cannot conceive where they might acquire such a tool for themselves from any other source.

These, indeed, are the reasons that shifted my mind from suppressing this writing to printing and publishing it. When weighed in the just scales of my judgment against the two disadvantages I previously mentioned, and considered through careful examination, these reasons far outweighed them. Therefore, as soon as a suitable opportunity arose, I set myself to the task and applied the finishing touch to the work as it is now presented to the literary world. And although—besides those two mentioned disadvantages—another of perhaps no small weight did not escape me (namely, that many people, and especially theologians, would rise up against me and my opinion and take up their pens against me, with whom I must clash and engage in the back-and-forth of debate <note>original: *disputationis serra reciprocanda*; literally "the reciprocating saw of dispute," a vivid metaphor for the grueling, repetitive nature of academic or theological argument</note> unless I wish to abandon my cause), this was never so powerful as to make me think of not publishing this writing, much less to make me change my plan.

For if anyone should deign to spend a few hours reading this little treatise of ours and examine what they find written in it with the balance of their own judgment; and if they should certainly find and solidly demonstrate in a public writing that the proof of our opinion is built on a false foundation, or is incorrectly and poorly deduced from true principles; or that it is not properly defended from the insults and accusations with which it might be attacked by others; or that the opinions of those who disagree are not well-opposed and refuted, and their arguments not correctly dissolved and answered—then we will give that person special thanks, also in a public writing, after a frank confession of our error. We would consider it a worthwhile effort that they uncovered our mistakes and prevented others from being entangled and led astray by them; for we hold nothing dearer, nothing more important, and desire nothing more earnestly than to be taught the truth and to be brought back to the right path if we have strayed from it.

But if, on the other hand, someone else who has not yet correctly grasped our meaning should bring forward such things and raise such difficulties that might delay them or place an obstacle for others—preventing the force and evidence of our Demonstration from being understood in every part, or a full vindication of our opinion from the twisted arrows of objections from being seen, or [the differences] of others from our own...

-> ### EPILOGUE 5 <-

...so that a prepared refutation of disagreeing opinions might be perceived, and thus people might rush into it wholeheartedly <note>original: *manibus pedibusque iretur*; literally "to go with hands and feet," a Latin idiom meaning to support or pursue something with all one's might</note>: we will immediately strive, to the best of our ability, to remove those scruples from them and to dispel those clouds that obstruct the light of nourishing Truth. We consider our time, and all that we are, to be most excellently spent if we have in some way held out a torch to our neighbor in attaining the truth—which is indeed of such great importance—and have brought some assistance.

But if anyone, finally—being fixed like a post to an opinion instilled in him since childhood or by his teachers, or swallowed with blind impulse—should rush at us, and if he should gnaw at our opinion and this exercise (which he cannot weaken with solid arguments and reasons) with stupid fabrications and quibbles, and rave against the Author with insults and slanders, simply because he thinks whatever differs from his own view is immediately false and worthy of his hatred and rejection before he has even examined it; or if he should heap up and accumulate many passages of Holy Scripture, and from them, without drawing out or demonstrating their true meaning, should draw up a large battle line and oppose it to our opinion without actually engaging or attacking our arguments—as is customary for certain Theologians: to him and those like him we shall not deign to reply anything. For I, who am a student uniquely dedicated in this writing to unearthing the Truth, have no desire to contend with a brawler using insults, or to fight, or to throw back the slander to be swallowed again <note>original: *resorbendam calumniam*; suggesting the slanderer should be forced to "eat his words"</note> (for it deserves no other response). My mind has always been most foreign to that trivial and mocking intemperance by which many writers—as if seized by an unbridled madness—receive and tear each other apart with the harshest and most bitter words, as if with whips and bites. They sharpen their pens, which (as the eminent Philosopher of this century <note>likely referring to a contemporary thinker like Francis Bacon or Hugo Grotius, who advocated for civil discourse</note> says) ought to be instruments of humanity, like the points of swords, with which one person most petulantly insults and attacks another's reputation. Furthermore, I do not have so much leisure that I would wish to spend—and thus basely squander—that time which I have consecrated entirely to studies and searching for truth, by responding either to a quibbler or to a most trifling accumulator of Scripture passages. In deciding this controversy, such passages can provide no light or help, seeing as this is a dispute in which the very *rule* <term>Norma: a standard or method used for measurement or judgment</term> for investigating and finding the meaning of these passages is being discussed. Therefore, let everyone into whose hands this exercise of ours comes know in advance: if certain writings come out against it (as perhaps not a few will), to which he sees no response, I consider all those things to be of that same flour, or rather, of that same bran <note>original: *furfuris*; the author is using a pun, comparing high-quality "flour" (substantive arguments) to "bran" or husks (worthless scrap)</note>.

And so I also want anyone who busies himself with writing against me and attacking my opinion and exercise to be forewarned and asked to attack the matter itself, and to abstain and restrain himself from all quibbling and slander, if he wishes for anything to be written back by me to his writing. And furthermore, so that all things may be handled more distinctly, let not this our [method] concerning the Rule...

-> P <-

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

One should not confuse or mix the question of interpreting Holy Scripture with that of the Judge of theological controversies, or any other related issue that might arise, as is the custom among theologians. Instead, one should accurately separate and distinguish them. Take up only the first question for examination and discussion, letting all the others go. If a reader does this, I pledge my word in return that I will receive him with equal kindness and courtesy. I will satisfy the doubts and difficulties he raises to the best of my strength, hoping and trusting—indeed, having no doubt—that he will eventually reach the truth with me and, shaking hands, will leave the arena <note>original: *arenâ cessurus*; a metaphor from gladiatorial combat or athletic games, meaning to concede the debate honorably</note>.

However, to ensure this proceeds more easily and successfully, and to prevent (as far as it lies within us) any lingering doubt that might open a path to contradiction, we will strive to remove one particular obstacle. In our judgment, this is a matter of great weight; we are firmly persuaded that once it is removed, everything else will be smooth and clear. The objection is this: if *Philosophy* is the norm for interpreting Holy Scripture in the manner we have established, then Holy Scripture itself would seem useless, written and transmitted to us in vain. For if the truths of all the meanings of *Scripture* (which are to be unearthed and explored by the aid of *Philosophy*) must be understood *before* they are unearthed and explored, what need is there to approach *Scripture* or consult it? Why draw truths from it or learn them there? Why explain or expound it at all, since those truths—already known—are not made better known or even confirmed by those explanations? Therefore, since in this way *Scripture* serves neither to teach nor to confirm the truth, it seems to be of no use. This, however, contradicts the unanimous consensus of the Churches of all places and ages, which with one voice have continually preached and declared its utility to be of the highest and greatest importance.

To solve this knot conveniently and explain it clearly, we believe we must revisit this entire matter from a deeper level. All Words can be considered in two ways: either *absolutely* or *relatively*. When considered *absolutely*, they are again twofold: either as they are uttered by the voice or as they are written by the hand. Insofar as they are uttered, they are sounds and voices produced by the mouth and carried through the air; insofar as they are written, they are characters and marks drawn or depicted on paper or other material. In both ways, they can be called *real beings* <term>entia realia: things that have a physical or objective existence in the world</term>. But they are viewed *relatively* when we attend not so much to this physical nature, but to their relationship and connection—specifically, that they are signs of other things. These are not "natural signs" (as they are called), which have a connection with the thing signified rooted in nature itself; rather, they are "arbitrary signs," which signify based on human institution and choice, as we said above in *Chapter 2*. And since the entire essence of a sign depends not on nature but on reason, to that extent they are only *beings of reason* <term>entia rationis: things that exist only within the mind or through mental designation</term>.

-> # EPILOGUE <-

and indeed, they can be known by themselves alone, insofar as they are viewed absolutely; but only through the mediation of others, namely the things signified, insofar as they are accepted respectively and as signs: since it is a property of <term>Related things</term> <note>Latin: *Relatorum*; in logic, these are things like "father" and "son" which cannot be defined without each other.</note> to *be simultaneous in knowledge*. <note>original: *esse simul cognitione*; a philosophical principle stating that you cannot truly know a sign without knowing what it points to, and vice versa.</note> Thus, for example, the words ***man*** and ***tree***, when they are considered as sounds produced by the mouth or pictures formed by letters, can be understood through themselves; but when they are viewed as signs of concepts or things denoted by those names, they cannot be known without those things. And since absolute knowledge comes before relative knowledge, the concept or thing, and the word viewed absolutely, must both be known before the word can be known as a sign and the thing as the signified. For we first know the ***man*** or the ***tree***, and these words by themselves; and afterwards we establish among ourselves that these words shall signify those concepts or things, or we learn that it has already been established by others that they signify them.

Since, however, many kinds of knowledge occur among Philosophers—**intellectual**, **sensory**, or another more basic type, if such exists—it is not necessary for the knowledge discussed here to be accurate and intellectual, although it could be; rather, **sensory** knowledge, or another lesser type if any is found, is sufficient. For it is not necessary, in order for me to know what is denoted by the word ***man*** or ***tree***, that their inner nature be understood by me; but it is enough that I have seen them once or several times, or perceived them with another sense, or arrived at some kind of knowledge of them in another way, as is well known to everyone.

From all this—as well as the fact that words contain no analogy and nothing of the concepts or things signified within themselves—it is not difficult to gather how much help they can provide to the mind in acquiring knowledge of things. First, no words, if taken separately and if their meaning is unknown, can ever produce ideas in the intellect of the things they denote. For even if you hear or read these words, ***God*** and ***Omniscience***, a hundred times or more, you will by no means be led by that into a knowledge of God or Omniscience.

Second, even if they are proposed together in a sentence, and we have some sensory or more basic knowledge of the things they designate, they are nonetheless in no way able to produce **intellectual** knowledge; for that [sensory knowledge], or whatever is placed below it, is not sufficient to generate intellectual knowledge. Thus, for example, if someone who knows only through a basic knowledge, and not accurately, what **God** and **omniscience** are, should come upon this statement: ***God is omniscient***, he will not thereby attain a true and intellectual understanding of this entire sentence, so as to perceive clearly and distinctly in his mind that God is omniscient. Finally, however clear and distinct the ideas of the things signified by the words—for example, God and Omniscience—we may have represented in the mind, nevertheless this sentence, ***God is omniscient***, if it is read or heard, cannot be the cause in itself of your understanding, as you ought, that God is omniscient; it is only an **occasional**, **impulsive**, or some similar cause. <note>The author is using "occasional cause" in a technical sense: the words don't "force" the knowledge into your brain, they merely prompt your mind to activate the knowledge it already has.</note> For those words cannot, by any true **causality**, bring it about that you have a certain and evident understanding...

# EPILOGUE

P 2

...that **Omniscience** belongs to the nature of God: but they <note>Referring to the words or signs discussed on the previous page.</note> are only an occasion or incentive by which the intellect is raised and led to contemplate the ideas of **God** and **Omniscience** more attentively, to compare them with one another, and thus discover that the latter is necessarily included in the former.

Now indeed, since all books consist of and are forged from interconnected and joined speeches, it follows most evidently that the greatest and highest utility they can offer for truly and accurately knowing and understanding things consists in this alone: that they suggest a thought to the reader and excite him to contemplate those **clear and distinct ideas** <note>This is a technical term from Cartesian philosophy; "clear" means the idea is present and accessible to an attentive mind, and "distinct" means it contains nothing but what is clear.</note> which he already has formed in his mind and which are denoted by the words in those books, to compare them, and to examine whether one is included in or connected to another. But by no means can books lead the intellect into a true knowledge of things through or by themselves; much less can they plant, infuse, imprint, or in any other way generate clear and distinct ideas in the mind if they were not previously infused or implanted there.

Having prefaced these things to remove the difficulty raised above, we say that **Sacred Scripture**—since it agrees with other books in that it is composed of speeches—must also agree with them in the manner of teaching the truth. And so, it serves only to excite and compel its readers to think about the things discussed therein, to inquire into them, and to examine whether the reality matches what is proposed. To accomplish this, <term>Philosophy</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Philosophy" often referred to the use of natural reason, logic, and the study of the natural world, rather than just abstract ethics.</note> and whatever the reader already knows and perceives must be called to aid, so that it may be seen how the predicate fits the subject, the truth of the statement may be unearthed, and at the same time the true sense may be elicited and drawn out.

From this it is clear that in the interpretation of Scripture through Philosophy, the truth does not always have to precede the sense. Thus, it is by no means superfluous to approach and consult Scripture; not indeed so that it may generate truth in our minds by itself, or make it clearer, more distinct, or more firm, but so that, as we have said, it may suggest an occasion and material for thinking—and indeed about such things as perhaps we would never have thought of otherwise. Because these matters concern the <term>highest happiness</term> <note>Latin: *summam beatitatem*; referring to the ultimate fulfillment or "blessedness" of the human soul.</note> of those who behold them, they are more conducive to our well-being than all other things. Therefore, the utility of Scripture is not based on its ability to "teach" truth in a vacuum, but its value is the greatest of all and far to be preferred to other books, just as the utility of any book is valued according to the excellence or worthlessness of the subjects it treats.

And from these things it appears even clearer than the midday sun how far they wander from the path of truth, and how miserably those **Theologians** err who have persuaded themselves (and are persuading others) that a solid knowledge of **Sacred Theology** can be drawn from the reading of the **Sacred Letters** <note>original: *S. Literarum*; a common way to refer to the Bible or Holy Writ.</note> alone, without any other consideration or contemplation of things.

# EPILOGUE

...recommend only the reading of it, and thus they draw away and deter people from the study of Philosophy and other noble disciplines. They do this even though not a single thing can be perfectly known from reading alone, nor can a **clear and distinct idea** <note>A hallmark of Cartesian philosophy; an idea is "clear" if it is present to the mind, and "distinct" if it is not confused with other ideas.</note> be acquired from it. Indeed, false and perverse views cannot be corrected by reading alone; for if they could, then everyone who reads the **Sacred Scriptures** <note>original: *S. Codices*</note> would necessarily hold the same concepts regarding the matters discussed within them. Since this does not happen, another path must be attempted and trodden to achieve this: namely, by employing a serious rejection of prejudices and an attentive contemplation of things themselves, and from these, the emergence of a true knowledge of them, which is **Philosophy**.

Therefore, we wish to advise and request all who devote themselves to **Theology**, and who will follow and embrace the truth of our opinion (for we do not believe everyone is so blinded that no light will shine upon them, or so obstinate that it will draw no one to its side): that they should apply every nerve of their intellect and direct all their studies toward acquiring true **Philosophy** for themselves, if they are not yet imbued with it. With philosophy as their guide and leader, and having rejected prejudices and dogmas admitted into Theology without a certain and unshakable foundation, they should seek to discover and establish new truths, or confirm and demonstrate old ones.

And although [Philosophy] is still confined within a very brief and narrow scope, and thus cannot provide many things conducive to this end—since among the ancients, if you except the **Mathematicians**, almost nothing solid or evidently demonstrated occurs among the Philosophers; and only a few things of this kind have been thought out and discovered by the moderns—nevertheless, we judge it better and more satisfactory to know a few true things with certainty than to persuade oneself and thrust upon others many false and doubtful things as if they were true and certain. To these things add that a significant hope smiles upon us: that the <term>boundaries of Philosophy</term> <note>original: *pœmœria*, a term referring to the sacred boundary of a city</note> in these times—in which that greatest restorer and propagator of it, **René Descartes** <note>original: *Renatus Des Cartes*, the 17th-century French philosopher whose work revolutionized science and logic.</note>, has shone a torch before the literary world and led the way by his example—will be extended far and wide by others who wish to follow in his footsteps. Such things regarding **God**, the **Rational Soul**, man’s highest happiness, and other matters of this kind pertaining to the acquisition of eternal life, will be brought to light. These will clarify every page in the interpretation of the Scriptures and will pave and prepare a level and straight path by which the **Church of Christ**—hitherto divided and torn apart by continuous disagreements—may sweetly unite and flow together in friendship. Bound by these closest and sweetest bonds, may it flourish, thrive, and grow on this earth hereafter, united and of one mind, and draw and entice nations still foreign to it into its bosom, and finally triumph in blessedness in the heavens.

---

->THE END.<-

<summary>This epilogue argues that reading Scripture without the aid of philosophy leads to disagreement and error, urging theologians to adopt Cartesian methods to find certain truth. The author envisions a future where rational philosophy reconciles a divided Christendom and leads to a universal understanding of God and the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Theology, René Descartes, Sacred Scripture, Rational Soul, Church Unity, Cartesianism, Prejudices, Certainty</keywords>
